In the twenty first century it is thought to be difficult to define art. It does not always exist in a gallery. It is not always a solid object or a flat image. It can be imagined, an action, a pledge, existing only temporarily, it can be made by one people or many, it can be an idea which accumulates over time. It can exist in other realities and will exist in realities of which we do not yet know. It is thought not to be the servant of the state, of religion or of ritual, but to exist purely for its own sake. This subjective freedom is highly valued and often disputed. The one constant is that art is made by artists and that it is made for its own sake. It was not always this way. 

!!THE STORY OF ART.

The story of art is the story of the human urge to represent the world. This began before humans had acquired verbal language with the simple drawing of a line in the sand with a finger. In 6000 BC the indigenous groups of prehistoric humans in the continents of Europe, Africa and Asia, the Middle East, the Far East, Australasia and the Americas had not developed any forms of verbal or visual communication. There was no form of representation in the world. But as these peoples evolved and began to acquire language, conceptual and cognitive skills, they began to create tools. The first tool may have been a stick used to draw in the sand, or it may have been a stone used to crack a nut or to kill an animal. 

!!PREHISTORIC ART.

Tools and visual representations are the first evidence we have of the existence of early civilisations. The earliest examples of these have been found in Africa, the home of the Neanderthals from whom all humans are descended. Prehistoric people were developing forms of representation before the discovery of fire. We know this because some of their drawings and tools have survived. Art, or the evidence of people’s urge to record their life, is the oldest surviving evidence of human endeavour. It may be the case that music, probably in the form of rhythm, is an older form of human expression but only drawing, artefacts and objects have permanence. This is why they are assigned such great value in western culture where linear history is the primary means of self definition. Many other cultures do not place the same emphasis on history. Their artefacts and art are different. In the beginning art may have been pure representation. We do not know whether there was a purpose to art other than pure self expression because there are many examples of prehistoric art whose purpose is unknown to us. There are lines drawn in the desert in South America that form geometric patterns visible only from the air, patterns scratched and carved into rock, and the countless temporary forms of expression that have been washed or blown away. 

!!THE STONE AGE.

As humans evolved, their visual representations became part of the rituals they created around the most important aspects of life, fertility, birth, death and the hunting for food. Art began to have a specific function. The earliest surviving images are scratched into stone, and consist mostly of lines, for example the cave paintings at Lascaux in France. These and others like them depict people hunting animals, in the case of Lascaux a buffalo like creature. The paintings are drawn in outline on the wall using paints made of earth and soot based pigments, in ochre, reddish brown and black. There are groups of lines too which are unexplained but seem to function as a way of counting or as a visual dynamic. As prehistoric people developed more sophisticated tools, they discovered a greater variety of ways of making marks. Flints could be chipped to make sharp blades with which to cut animal skin, animal hair, and to pulverise plants, earth and clay. The discovery of fire meant that substances for drawing, painting and modelling could be dried, heated, reduced, distilled and fire provided soot, ash, charcoal and light. 

!!THE IRON AGE.

Iron age people had a much greater range of tools and implements. They had begun to fashion utensils and to make personal adornments from metal, as well as tools and weapons and with these came the development of decoration. Objects began to have greater value and the degree and type of their decoration began to be significant. At the same time Iron Age people also began to acquire language. One of the first groups to produce metal vessels for eating and drinking was the Beaker people. Often these objects were decorated with images of animals, vitally important as a source of food. 

!!ABORIGINAL ART.

In Australasia, the aboriginal people developed an entirely different form of representation. Early aboriginal art was a language of symbolic representation in which pattern and symbol is used to represent the world. Drawings were made using different colours of sand and earth straight onto the ground. These described the land and its spirits, and depicted time as a journey. The drawings were temporary and not always shown to everybody, sometimes even being made in isolation. Different cultures developed different views of the world according to their geographical location, the climate and the food that was available to them. During the Iron Age people from other continents developed along different paths leading to a material expression of cultural difference. 

!!THE BRONZE AGE.

The smelting of metals led to the discovery of a range of alloys, the first being bronze. These metals could be fashioned more intricately and finely allowing for greater precision and decorative effect such as repeating patterns, beaten metal and thin wire. 

!!EARLY CIVILISATIONS.

The earliest civilisations were formed as people began to travel further in search of food and hospitable land and to encounter other groups both aggressively and peaceably. Humans had previously been nomadic but some groups began to make permanent settlements and with these came buildings and monuments. Cave painting and rock carving had been the only permanent sites for art until the construction of permanent dwellings. Settlement gave rise to the construction of stone buildings, burial chambers and monuments all of which could be decorated or contain representation. For nomadic people portability had been a factor but now larger and heavier objects were produced and people began to make figurative sculptural representations from stone, clay and wood. Stonehenge, the great stone circle in Britain which was built in the Stone Age to celebrate the solstice, is a construction which is not decorated but is not a dwelling. It is an early church. Subsequently buildings that were the focus for ritual whether pagan or religious became the most highly decorated anywhere. Art and architecture began to be closely linked. After the Bronze Age the great early civilisations of the world developed in isolation, reaching their peaks in terms of population, claims to territory and cultural attainment at different periods in history. 

!Pre Columbian art.

The pre Columbian people in south America made some of the most sophisticated early sculpture. Their stone male and female figures have distinctive flattened and squared forms, with large featured faces. Later in South America the Mayan Aztec and Inca civilisations became very skilful in the use of gold. In Africa, the Sumerians and Abyssinians and many other cultures developed widely different forms of visual expression, both in the adornment of the body and dwellings, and in the production of artefacts. 

!!TRIBAL ART.

In all the continents of the world large numbers of people continued to live as tribes, some settled and some nomadic, rarely coming into contact with other peoples and each with its own cultural specificity. Native Americans, semi nomadic peoples who lived off the plains of America, used leather, beads, feathers and wood, and are today customarily symbolised by the Totem Pole, a ritual sculpture. Melanesian and Polynesian peoples that depended on the sea for their livelihood used wood, shells and pounded tree bark to make the tapa cloth which they printed with symbolic designs. Their boats were adorned with the work of their most skilled artists. The Inuit who inhabited the frozen north of America carved from soft stone. The African subcontinent yielded the largest number of distinct tribes, for example the Nuba, Yoruba, Dogon, Masai and Zulu. Africa was one of the culturally richest parts of the world. 

!!EMPIRES.

The earliest civilisations soon built cities and with cities came armies and battles for territory. Soon empires were formed, the earliest being the Byzantine, Ottoman, the Roman empire, the Japanese and Chinese empires which were ruled by successive dynasties. Greece is often referred to as the cradle of civilisation and early Greek culture was the most sophisticated in the world. The development of religions was very significant in terms of the history of art was religion became the focus of a large proportion of artistic activity. 

!!ART AND RELIGION.

Religions divided the world and generated an unprecedented quantity of visual art. Many parts of the world remained pagan, worshipping local gods with their own iconography and ritual. The Greeks, for example, had a complex family of pagan gods and myths. Larger religions began to spread: in the east, Islam, the religion of the Ottoman empire predominated, and in the middle east Christianity. In Asia Hinduism and Islam co existed although not necessarily in harmony and in China and Japan Buddhism and Taoism were predominant. Many Northern Europeans such as the Celts, Norse and the Vikings, were pagans. A burial ship found at Sutton Hoo in Britain contained many examples of Viking art. The ancient Egyptians had their own gods. Art had been an important part of ritual. It now became a servant of the state and of religion and in the process the works that have become known as some of the greatest works of art were created. Artists became the producers of religious iconography but of course in Islamic areas no figurative representation appeared anywhere since Islam does not permit it. 

!!EARLY WRITTEN TEXT.

Writing is a very important influence on art during the period leading up to the birth of Christ and afterward. It is thought that writing was developed by the Sumerians and that Sanskrit may be the oldest written language. The earliest surviving artefacts are the Dead Sea scrolls, written in Sanskrit on papyrus, and the Rosetta stone, an inscribed tablet. Writing was a new way of describing and recording the world which had previously been exclusively visual and for centuries writing was a skill learned only by the very few. Visual imagery remained the primary means of communicating and recording events, but as writing began to tip the balance, art was led to fulfil new functions. 

!!EGYPTIAN ART.

The ancient Egyptians developed an early writing system called hieroglyphics. They were a wealthy trading civilisation with a highly sophisticated system of government and constructed some of the greatest buildings and monuments ever made, the Pyramids, the Sphinx and the statues of the pharaohs in the Valley of the Kings. The Egyptians worshipped deities such as Isis and Osiris and held cats, certain birds and scarab beetles to be sacred. Egyptian art is seen at is most spectacular in the tombs of the pharaohs. They believed in the afterlife, therefore the art that has been found in Egyptian tombs is a good indicator of the life of the living. Egyptian artists were skilled in carving, casting and gold and silver smithing. They had a style of drawing that is very distinctive – all figures are shown with their body facing forward and the head in profile. Hieroglyphs use this style too in the creation of long vertically written texts consisting of pictograms. Egyptian two dimensional art emphasises flatness and uses no perspective but in contrast Egyptian sculpture is very realistic. A cat has been found carved from black stone and wearing a jewelled collar, for example, and countless sarcophagi for mummified pharaohs bare lifelike effigies. The sphinxes and the pharaohs carved on a monumental scale, forty metres high, are in proportion and physiologically accurate. 

!!ETRUSCAN ART.

Etruscan pottery is known for the quality of its form and decoration. 

!!BYZANTINE ART.

The Byzantine empire was one of the largest empires in the world. Byzantine art is valued for the quality of figurative drawing and painting that had developed physiological accuracy, realism and early use of perspective. 

!!GRECO ROMAN ART.

Around 400 BC the Greeks had developed a civilisation that was pre eminent. The Greek system of government was the model for all modern nations. The Greeks had a written language, and a flourishing culture. Greek sculpture was the most realistic in the world, and it was at this time that the idea that art should aspire to realism began to take root. Greek sculptures were generally of white marble or other stone, and often painted in bright colours. The best known Greek sculptures are the Elgin marbles, subject of a protracted battle between Britain, who has possession of them and Greece, who wants them back. The marbles once adorned the frieze of one of the most famous buildings of antiquity, the Parthenon in Athens. The Venus de Milo, a classical Greek sculpture, is perhaps the best known sculpture of the western world, considered to represent perfection of female form and becoming a byword for artistic skill and beauty. It is made in white marble and at some point since antiquity has suffered the loss of its arms. Greek vases are very finely decorated by incising into the black glaze which covers the red clay from which they are made. They are renowned for the quality and realism of the drawing. Greek art was for centuries to be seen as the benchmark for all art, and Classical Greece provided the model for the Roman Empire, whose cultural achievements dominated Europe for centuries after the Empire fell. The Romans built cities, roads and infrastructure on a vast scale and created art to decorate their empire and venerate their leaders. The carved stone Roman portrait bust is a good example, of which there are countless examples surviving. Many archaeological sites have revealed the variety of Roman art, for example Herculaneum, Pompeii, two towns that were destroyed but preserved for centuries by the eruption of the volcano Vesuvius. Mosaic wall decorations and paintings revealed after excavation in the nineteenth century show mosaic floor designs featuring food and fish, and murals depicting sports such as leaping over a bull’s horns and dolphins. Knossos in Crete also has excellent murals, whilst Volubilis and Lullingstone Villa in Britain contain similar but smaller scale work. Roman art spread so widely that it became a dominant style, Romanesque, as artists, artisans and craftspeople adapted and personalized it. Many cathedrals and churches in Europe were built in this style for the next 200 hundred years as Catholicism developed. 

!!ISLAMIC ART.

At the same time in the middle east and in Asia, Islam spread, bringing its own cultural identity to the towns and cities of the Ottoman empire. Islam demanded that there be no figurative representation of any kind, whether in architectural decoration, textiles, book, fabric, painting or sculpture. This was because the word by which the teachings of Mohammed had been handed down, was itself sacred, and so the word became the only permissible imagery. Written text was rendered as decorative design, carving and patter, and calligraphic patterns of remarkable complexity were carved into stone to adorn mosques for example. Carpets for which the area then known as Persia became so famous, depicted animals, plants and buildings symbolically. For centuries there was no art produced in Islamic parts of the world that represented a human face. 

!!CHINESE ART.

By contrast in the far east, home of Buddhism, representational painting and sculpture featured amongst the highest achievements of artists. Japan and China have produced the finest clay sculpture in the world, for example the glazed clay horses of the Tang dynasty and the terracotta army found buried in the late twentieth century. This consists of hundreds of statues of warriors buried in a shallow underground tomb. Chinese writing is not pictographic but the characters have a visual element. Chinese art therefore has a strong tradition in calligraphic art. Chinese watercolours have calligraphic brushstrokes made quickly which represent plants, landscape, particularly mountains, and sometimes animals and birds. Chinese paintings were made on silk that was readily available rather than canvas. Painting on silk required soft small brushes and thin paint. Since Buddhism did not depend on graphic illustration, religious iconography never became an intrinsic part of daily life. However Buddha was widely represented in statue form, from the smallest pocket carving to the largest stone or metal figures such as the reclining gold Buddha that is still famous today. 

!!JAPANESE ART.

Clay and porcelain modelling is an important element of Japanese art, seen in the ceramics of the Sung and Ming eras which reached a pinnacle of excellence which was not surpassed anywhere else. Japanese ceramics are famous all over the world and their influence continues to be seen in contemporary ceramics and mass produced porcelain both in the form and in the decoration. Japanese ceramics were an intrinsic part of the tea ceremony, an important daily ritual in Japanese life. Japanese watercolours and woodblocks are well known too. The Japanese were skilled in the making of paper and the production of silk. Although Buddhism has its place in Japan, Confucianism was widely followed, and this was more a philosophical teaching than a religion. No religious iconography was attached to Confucianism. The opposing but complementary and essential principles of yin and yang influence much Japanese art. They can be seen in the many representations of landscape and weather, in particular rocky mountains with stylised swirling wind and cloud, and in similar sculptures which are large chunks of rock with especially appreciated forms. Japanese art has a flatness to it, a lack of deep perspective, that has prevailed for centuries. The famous willow pattern, a Victorian interpretation of traditional Japanese art, shows a never ending landscape that exists all on one plane. 

!!CHRISTIAN ART.

After the spread of Christianity a new kind of art began to emerge. Christianity was an iconographic religion based on the worship of a god with a human form, his son Jesus, a human, and Mary his mother. The depiction of these three has given rise to some of the greatest and most influential art in the western world. For a period of several hundred years, from the Medieval period to the seventeenth century and the Enlightenment, most significant art had a religious theme or was commissioned by the Church. The rulers of Europe were considered to rule by divine right, putting art also into the service of the state. Early Christian art, such as the Coptic tapestries, the earliest surviving woven textiles that depict Christ and Mary, are simple and show images of the face of Christ. Wherever Christianity spread, local artistic styles were put to the service of the church, resulting in multiple representations of the same three people with local regional and ethnic features. But there are constant elements – God is usually shown as an old man with a white beard, Christ as a young man with long brown hair and a beard and Mary as a young woman with long black or brown hair. Judaism, like Islam, did not permit representation in any form, its teachings being transmitted through words and closely prescribed laws governing everyday life. So for a time early representational art was essentially a Christian endeavour. In the early medieval period many churches had fresco paintings in them and these frescos are some of the earliest surviving paintings that we have today. During the period in which Islam, Christianity and Judaism were expanding, very few people were literate apart from the clergy and members of the ruling classes. Visual imagery was still the primary means of mass communication. The Bayeux tapestry, as late as the eleventh century was made as a piece of news reportage after the fact to tell the story of the battle of Hastings through images. These images acted as a prompt for the spoken word – a storyteller would stand in the hall in which the tapestry hung around all four walls and tell the tale. The diagonal marks in the borders of the tapestry are thought to add some kind of rhythmic dynamic to the spoken word. With the invention of printing and the dissemination of the Bible, literacy improved. The dissemination of visual images through print was more democratic. 

!!MEDIEVAL ART.

In the Middle Ages, the influence of the Romans was still pervasive. Romanesque and Gothic art and architecture dominated northern Europe, but towards the south the influence of the moors was seen in Spanish and Portuguese art. In the east Islamic art and architecture mixed with pockets of Christianity and Judaism led to a non representational art, but in the far east in China, Japan, and in Russia, visual art was representational. 

!Lucas Cranach.

The work of Lucas Cranach, a German medieval artist working in the 1300s is a good example of northern European religious art. Cranach painted an altarpiece that was shocking at the time since it accurately depicted the misery suffered by Christ on the cross, the pain and the physical degradation. Cranach also showed the plight of ordinary people of the time by painting them with festering sores, deformities and other afflictions. He painted very realistically in quite bright colours. 

!Matthias Grunewald.

Grunewald, a German medieval artist working around 1350, is also known for his crucifixion scenes and for the gritty realism of his paintings, extraordinary for the time, and for their use of colour. 

!Hieronymous Bosch.

Another German painter, Bosch, painted a version of heaven called The Garden of Earthly Delights. This extremely well known painting is a small triptych approximately 70 cm tall with a square central panel and two rectangular side panels. Bosch's painting depicts paradise as an extraordinary place, with dreamlike creatures and objects, some beautiful pink glass spheres, some castles and towers. It is an early precursors to fantasy and science fiction illustration, being full of fantastical creations of Bosch’s imagination. The painting is very detailed in its composition and execution. 

!Peter Breughel the Elder.

Breughel’s work depicts medieval everyday life. A Dutch painter working in the 1400s, Breughel is known for his instantly recognisable paintings of the seasons, for example Hunters in the Snow. In each case the painting shows a village scene, with a large number of peasants, children and domestic animals going about their everyday life inside and outside buildings and dwellings. There is activity in every corner of the painting, down to the smallest detail such as a person urinating, or a dog stealing some meat, a couple kissing, children being naughty, someone sleeping. The scenes are realistically painted and are notable for their depiction of the lives of ordinary people with no imposed narrative, no moral or religious overtones, no theme other than the representation of life as it was. The colours, the drawing of landscape, vernacular, architecture, the detail of the clothing, of tools and household items makes these works extraordinary documents of their time. Above all Breughel’s paintings of the seasons present four very different atmospheres and moods. The paintings are so famous as to be instantly recognisable whether or not the Breughels’s name is known, and are used on calendars, merchandise and in advertising the world over. 

!Peter Breughel the Younger.

A Dutch painter related to Breughel the Elder. In the south of Europe, especially in Italy, painters were developing new ways of working. Many such as Piero di Cosimo, Antonello da Messina, Fra Angelico and Piero della Francesca were making frescos or wall paintings in monasteries and churches. 

!Antonello Da Messina.

Da Messina was an Italian renaissance painter who made a painting of Saint Jerome in his study. This shows the room as a box like structure that sits on its own in a landscape, giving the viewer access through the open front wall. What we are seeing is monk’s cell consisting of two linked rooms made of dark coloured wood in one of which the saint is seated at a desk. 

!Piero Della Francesca.

This Siennese painter is perhaps the best known of the Italian late medieval period. In the 1450s Della Francesca painted a number of notable frescos in churches in central Italy, in particular an image of the Virgin Mary, pregnant and standing in a landscape with a tree, that can be seen significantly restored in a tiny village near Siena. His painting of the baptism of Christ shows Christ being baptised by John the Baptist who is standing in a stream wearing a cloth round his loins. The Italian countryside, representing the Middle East, stretches away in the background. Artists from this period understood how to use foreground and background to create a sense of three dimensional space. 

!!THE RENAISSANCE.

The fifteenth century saw a flowering of western culture centred around Italy. The dominance of the Catholic church and the wealth of the ruling families like the Medicis, led to a great number of artists being commissioned to make religious paintings for countless churches as well as many portraits. The artists of this period are acknowledged to be among the greatest in the Western world. Italian artists such as the painters Lorenzo de Ghiberti, Fillipo Lippi, Lorenzo Lotto, Lorenzo Ghiberti and Simone Martini made many paintings depicting scenes from the life of Christ set in the Italian countryside and with Italian architecture and clothes. 

!Lorenzetti.

Lorenzetti’s Good and Bad Government can be seen in Siena. This painting is the width of a room, situated high up near the ceiling and it shows a fortified city on a hill in the distance with its people going about their daily lives. A man on a horse is riding into the image from left in the foreground, the horse has a white coat on and the man is wearing armour. Good and Bad Government is unusual for its depiction of an entire community and its attempt to represent democracy in action in a painting. 

!Giorgione.

Giorgione was an Italian artist who painted The Flagellation of St Jerome, depicting the middle aged saint with a thin sagging body, long grey hair and a beard, alone in penitent pose in his room, naked save for a red cloth around his loins. Jerome is surrounded by symbolic objects and is in the act of scourging himself with a long leather whip. During the Renaissance artists began to study techniques and materials with a scientific approach. New pigments, painting techniques and materials were discovered and casting techniques for sculpture were refined. 

!!THE AGE OF PATRONAGE.

The renaissance was the great age of patronage. Artists who were commissioned by the Church or the State were in a position to afford assistants and to establish studios. They took on apprentices who were employed to draw out paintings, mix colours, prepare canvases, under paint, and often to paint backgrounds – including trees, grass, or patterns on fabric. Many old master paintings are in fact the work of a number of people which has sometimes made attribution difficult. An artist’s assistant might also go on to set up their own studio, and to paint in a very similar style to that of their master, so that it has often been difficult to tell who painted what. If the painter of a particular work could not be established it was often attributed as being painted in a style similar to, or ‘after,’ a certain master painter. 

!Andrea Mantegna.

Mantegna, an Italian renaissance painter, is known for his many great paintings and for his invention of the painting technique grisaille. 

!!PERSPECTIVE.

The discovery during the renaissance which had the most significant effect on painting was that of perspective. This was a landmark in the history of visual representation since it enabled artists to depict three dimensional space for the first time. There were many theories of perspective but the most significant element was the establishment of the vanishing point. This was an imaginary point in the vertical centre of the canvas, about one third down from the top, at which all sightlines from the position of the viewer converged, giving an illusion of three dimensional space. In particular, perspective enabled a far greater accuracy in the depiction of the built environment. Complex theories tried to show that there could be a perspective that enabled the illusion to be effective from every viewing angle. Perspective was essentially an illusion constructed through geometry but in the Renaissance it was thought to be a two dimensional replication of what the human eye actually saw. 

!Leonardo Da Vinci.

One renaissance artist blended science and art more successfully than any other. Leonardo da Vinci is often seen as the quintessential Renaissance Man who defined the era. He worked as a court artist for the Medici family in Venice and did not paint only religious subjects. Da Vinci is arguably the most famous artist in the world, or has certainly made the most famous painting, the Mona Lisa, which has been reproduced by the billion, forged and stolen many times and still draws huge crowds to its site on the walls of the Louvre museum in Paris. This painting depicts a woman shown head and shoulders turned very slightly to one side. She has on a simple yellowish gold dress, a plain necklace and has brown hair. She does not look particularly wealthy. There is a landscape in the background. There is an inscription LHOQ below her on a painted scroll. What made the painting so famous is the woman’s expression, in particular her smile. She seems to be looking straight at the viewer, and has a look of cool calm appraisal, the hint of a smile on her lips. However her eyes seem to be fixed on the viewer wherever they stand in relation the painting, leading to the myth that the Mona Lisa is actually following the movements of her viewers, that she is somehow real. This is an extraordinary achievement for a painting that has come to symbolise great beauty. The term Mona Lisa has almost become an adjective to describe a beautiful woman. The myth attached to the painting has led to many copies being made and to the work featuring in other artists work, in advertising, in newspaper cartoons and is in fact an image that has currency everywhere in the Western World and often beyond. Mona Lisa copies appear in fairgrounds with holes cut for heads so that people can be photographed as her. Automatic photo booths have the Mona Lisa as a backdrop or as a body for the sitter’s head shot. Copies of her with the eyes cut out have been used in films for comedic effect – someone behind the painting will look through the eye hole so that it appears that she is following a person around the room and Marcel Duchamp famously graffitied a moustache onto a reproduction of her. The author of this image succeeded in painting a very beautiful portrait, but it is only one of his achievements. He made many religious paintings and altarpieces. A cartoon for a Madonna and child is particularly well known. He did a vast number of drawings from the models, paying endless attention to hands and feet, eyes or other parts of the body, drawing them separately to get them right. Da Vinci was a polymath and his interests extended to anatomy and engineering. He performed many dissections and recorded what he saw in drawings, expanding his sense of how to draw the human form this way as well as his medical knowledge of how the human body worked, even drawing a foetus still inside a woman’s uterus. Another famous work by Da Vinci is a pen and ink drawing called The Golden Section. A male figure with flowing hair is drawn standing with both arms stretched out horizontally and one leg pointed outwards at an angle. Measured lines radiate from the figure, which represents the proportions taken from the human body used to make perspective and on which architectural construction is based. A copy of this drawing was placed on the Moon by the crew of the Apollo in the 1960s. Da Vinci was also an inventor; he was so interested in the myth of Icarus that he built a flying machine. 

!Sandro Botticelli.

Another well known Italian painter working around 1540 is best known for his painting The Nativity and The Birth of Venus, and also for his illustrations to Dante’s Inferno. Botticelli’s The Birth of Venus is one of the most recognised images in the western world due to its frequent use in advertising. The very beautiful Venus is standing in a giant clam shell which floats on the sea, just away from the shore. She is naked but her long hair covers her pubic area. She is surrounded by cherubs who pull the clam shell along by blue ribbons. Pale pink roses fall around her from the sky. The whole painting is rendered in luminous pale shades of blue, pink and sand colours. The Nativity is a small painting about fifty cm tall and is certainly one of the best known nativity scenes ever painted. The stable is set in a forested landscape, with a timber and straw shelter in the centre of the picture which houses the Holy family. The shelter has no walls. The Mary is seated with Jesus on her lap. Joseph kneels beside her. The shepherds are there. The ox and ass are in the shadows at the back of the stable. Above the thatched roof are angels, arranged in a row, some blowing trumpets or playing harps, their feet dangling, their softly coloured red blue and purple robes falling in folds around them, with gilded belts and halos hovering. Above the angels heaven can be seen though breaks in the clouds, and there are cherubs everywhere. Below the stable is a twisting path that leads to the bottom of the painting crossing horizontally, turning again and again, until the flames at the mouth of hell are reached. All along the path are devils poking out of burrows enticing lost souls; they have black skin, horns and forked tails. A similar but smaller work, The Birth of Ezra, predates this painting. Botticelli made a series of drawings to illustrate Dante’s Inferno. These show the different levels of purgatory and the sins associated with each level and graphically illustrate the particular torture or punishment inflicted on the lost souls. The route to heaven is then shown culminating in the entry in o heaven, which is a beautiful garden. The drawings are made for a book, done in dark brown ink and are very detailed. 

!Van Eyck.

Around the same time, Van Eyck made a painting called the Arnolfini Marriage which is particularly well known. The painting shows a wedding couple standing side by side. The woman is wearing a long dark green dress and holds one hand up, palm toward the viewer. There is a landscape in the background. Sculpture also developed rapidly in the renaissance. Artists began to measure their models with callipers and develop tools, metal alloys and casting and carving methods that enabled them to create realistic simulations of the human form. Sculpture studios had enough employees to enable the handling of large blocks of stone and the construction of foundries. 

!Donatello.

Donatello is one of the greatest renaissance sculptors. 

!Benvenuto Cellini.

An Italian renaissance sculptor and goldsmith, Cellini was working around 1540. His made a bronze statue of David with which he attempted to rival Michelangelo and also made a bronze of Medusa. Cellini was well respected for his skill as a goldsmith and his work was much in demand with the Medici family. An extravagant gold salt cellar in the form of an ornate clam shell containing Neptune and many sea nymphs is particularly well known. The salt cellar was stolen in 2003. Cellini wrote an outspoken and lengthy autobiography which details the rivalry between artists’ studios. 

!Michelangelo.

The greatest renaissance sculpture is generally acknowledged to be a figure of the biblical David which was made by the sculptor and painter Michelangelo to represent ideal male beauty. This white marble figure of a nude man is larger than life, about six metres high, and was radical at the time for its naturalism and the physiological accuracy with which it was modelled. Michelangelo applied the lessons of perspective to the sculpture, making the feet proportionally larger than they should have been. Several smaller versions were made from bronze. This sculpture was much copied and inspired much debate in the studios of Florence. Michelangelo was an Italian artist working in Florence who is universally known as one of the greatest artists of all time, his name often being used to invoke the idea of the highest achievement in art. Michelangelo is as well known for his fresco on the ceiling of the Sistine Chapel showing the life of Christ and the ascension into heaven. He was a very skilled draughtsman and created very lifelike figures using colour in a realistic way and painting flowing drapery. The Sistine chapel ceiling was an enormous undertaking and to paint it Michelangelo and his assistants spent a year lying on their backs on scaffolding towers. Images from the ceiling are known all over the world, often used by advertisers, and have become almost generic. One part of the fresco is most frequently reproduced, the image of Christ’s outstretched hand reaching out to the hand of God, their fingers nearly touching. This is an image of great tension and portent, and it seems as if an electrical charge passes from one to the other through their fingers. In the late 1990s the ceiling was cleaned, provoking a great deal of controversy since for centuries the fresco had been darkened and the colours muted. The fresco had been known to visitors and to those looking at reproductions as an antique, parts of which were even obscured from view by centuries of dirt and the breath of millions of visitors. By contrast the cleaned ceiling seems very brightly coloured, even garish. Michelangelo also made a marble sculpture of the Pieta. By the end of the fifteenth century the fame of Italian masters had spread throughout Europe. Artists began to look beyond religious and political subjects to the myths of classical Greece which were sometimes used as metaphors for contemporary events. Classicism was the dominant theme for artists in the sixteenth century such as Poussin, El Greco, Raphael and Titian. Very few artists were women. Artemesia Gentileschi is a rare exception although there may have been more of whom there is no record. 

!Artemesia Gentileschi.

An Italian artist who is the best known woman painter of the classical period, Gentileschi’s most frequently cited work is a portrait of a woman and a painting of the Martyrdom of St Catherine. 

!El Greco.

Most of the well known artists of the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries are Italian or Spanish. El Greco, ‘the Greek’, is an exception. He made paintings using loose brushstrokes with bold colours. 

!Caravaggio.

Caravaggio is another classical Italian painter. He painted in a more precise and well worked way than Titian, for example, and was known for his complex compositions. Caravaggio invented chiaroscuro, a way of painting using only the lightness and darkness in a scene. One painting by him shows a scene of bacchanalian revelry, a number of semi clad young men wearing ivy wreaths in their hair. 

!Nicholas Poussin.

A French classical artist who made precisely drawn and richly coloured paintings which have a theatrical atmosphere. The figures in Poussin’s paintings are painted in very bright colours and posed to look as if they are immobile and the scenes he depicts often look staged rather than natural. For this reason he fell out of favour for some time and not much was known about him. Poussin was rediscovered in the early twentieth century by the art historian Anthony Blunt who wrote a catalogue raisonné. 

!Bernini.

An Italian baroque painter who was commissioned to make a great number of altar paintings for churches in the north of Italy. 

!!CLASSICAL REALISM.



!Raphael.

An Italian sixteenth century painter who was influential for his loose and impressionistic way of painting and use of colour, unusual for the period. Raphael was commissioned to make many paintings for churches in Florence. He made a lot of working drawings and drew cartoons from which his paintings were traced. 

!Titian.

Titian is a very influential Italian painter who made many altarpieces for churches in Florence and Rome but also used classical themes which gave him greater freedom of interpretation and therefore the space to experiment. Titian’s style was much emulated, leading to many works being wrongly attributed to him in the twentieth century. He developed a style of painting that, like Raphael’s, was looser and more impressionistic than his contemporaries. Titiian began to show that the surface of the painting and the way the paint is used could be just as important for the viewer as the subject of the painting. It became important that the individuality and singularity of the painter’s touch should be celebrated. Artists began to make paintings that were far more than a presentation of a narrative subject realising that the way paint was applied could reveal as much of the psychology of the subject as could the literal visual interpretation. Titian was one of the pioneers of this way of working, displayed in paintings like ‘The Flaying of Marsyus’, a four metre high painting which shows Marsyus tied to a tree as his flesh is cut off by several men in a crowd. Dogs at his feet feed on his flesh. Titian was also a great colourist and used daring and vivid combinations as can be seen in a crucifixion painted for the cathedral in the Italian city of Ancona. This shows his compositional skill too – like many painters of the time Titian controlled the direction of the viewer’s eye by carefully considered compositional elements, such as the twist of a body, the direction of the gaze of the subjects, the direction of a pointed arm, the flow of drapery and by the use of colour. Diagonal trajectories were popular, so that the viewer might be led into the centre of a painting, and sometimes these became spirals that take the viewer’s eye in several steps to the focal point of the composition. The use of red or another bright colour often indicated the focal point. Titian often depicted Japanese or Chinese ceramics in his interiors, evidence that these were newly imported and popular in Italy at the time. 

!!REALISM.

During the sixteenth century as the European nations vied for power, their monarchies became the main patrons of art. Court artists such as Velasquez painted the ruling families of Europe and had the time and financial backing to break new ground. Artists such as Velasquez, Chardin, or Van Dyck, for example, began to depict real life rather than fictional scenes, mythical or religious subjects, going beyond the formal portrait to paint events, objects and people that interested them. History painting and still life were explored by an increasing number of painters. 

!Diego Velasquez.

A painter by appointment to the Spanish court, Velasquez painted some of the best known works in the history of painting. One of his most popular works shows the King Philip, the queen and the Infanta standing in an informal group with various servants in a room in the royal household looking out at the viewer. Velasquez chose to show the family at home in intimate surroundings, several rooms beyond the room in which they stand visible through a door behind them. The family look out of the painting as if the viewer has trespassed upon them in their private quarters yet they regard the viewer with a challenging cool assurance. The use of perspective and light and shade focuses the viewer’s eye on the Infanta. This painting does not have a literal narrative but neither is it a straight forward family portrait. Velasquez has taken a psychological approach to his subjects which was unusual at the time. Velazquez also painted many portraits including one of King Philip on a rearing horse, and a portrait of the Infanta wearing a lacy dress. The detail of the spectacular red dress, the lace, embroidery and the jewels on it, is painted with thick squeezes of paint which viewed close up seem no more than rapid impressions and the viewer is very conscious of the physical properties of paint on canvas. Viewed from a distance however the apparently crude application of paint is astoundingly like the fabric it portrays. The Rokeby Venus is one of Velasquez’s best known works. It shows a reclining nude with her back to the viewer, her face, which stares directly at the viewer, visible in a mirror held in front of her on ribbons by a cherub with red and white feathered wings. This painting, like the one of the royal family, addresses the psychology of looking at paintings. It gives the viewer access to the subject but ensures by the choice of pose, composition and facial expression that the subject is portrayed as being aware that they are being observed. This ensures that the viewer cannot avoid acknowledging their own subjectivity. 

!!THE STILL LIFE.

Artists had begun to explore new subjects and the still life became an important genre of painting which reached its height in sixteenth and seventeenth century Europe. The still life had many forms, the most common being a table bearing an array of food and drink, a vase of flowers, or an arrangement of objects. The still life allowed the painter to display skill in the way the various items are painted and can be seen essentially as a close 0up view, in contrast to a painting of a scene or a person. The skin of a lemon, the shine and reflection on silver ware, the pattern on a pheasant’s feathers, the weave of a rug, the petals of flowers all gave ample opportunity for displays of virtuosity. A typical food still life might have fruit piled casually on a plate, silver pitchers or goblets, a dead game bird, a candle, knives, glasses, some meat or fish. The food would usually have been chosen to present an impressive display so that anyone who saw the picture would be impressed by how the owner was able to live. Zubaran and Chardin were early exponents of the still life. 

!Zubaran.

A Spanish sixteenth century artist who is known chiefly for his still lifes, Zubaran made several paintings of vegetables such as cabbages and pumpkins. In one such painting an array of vegetables is shown hanging from a line, tied on by string of varying lengths. The vegetables are set against a plain background. 

!Chardin.

Like Zubaran, Chardin usually painted flowers and vegetables and was rigorous in his choice of subject and composition, often making very simple arrangements of one or two items and concentrating on painting them as realistically as possible. Similarly, paintings of flowers were a signal of wealth and many artists added flowers to combinations of other items. Later on the Dutch flower painters concentrated on the flowers alone, filling the canvas with vases of exotic mixed floral arrangements. These were also intended as displays of wealth, for only the richest could afford cut flowers. Some still life painting depicted only objects, perhaps a rustic plate and cup, a book and a lamp, or richly decorated precious items. Kalf, a Dutch artist, painted a suit of armour cast onto a chair by a table covered with gold goblets and plates, the metal glows and shines and reflects fire and lamplight. Kalf’s virtuosity is revealed by a close inspection of the painted shine, made from the tiniest and most carefully applied touches of a range of yellows, whites and ochre. Whereas most still life paintings have no narrative, the vanitas painting is a form of still life which depicts objects with an allegorical or symbolic significance. The vanitas might be painted to celebrate a particular person’s interests and achievements through objects alone, or combined with a portrait of the person. The symbolism in vanitas painting is not always evident and some knowledge is required to interpret them. Skulls make an obvious and frequent appearance to represent mortality, as do books to represent learning, musical instruments, globes, sextants and telescopes to represent travel and the outside world. However more obscure and personal items would have been significant only to the subject or commissioner of the painting and those that knew them. One of the best known vanitas paintings is Holbein's The Ambassadors. 

!Holbein.

An English artist working during the reign of Elizabeth the first, Holbein, like most artists of the time mostly painted to commission, recording the members of and activities of the royal Court. He made several distinctive standing portraits of the Queen including one in which she is wearing a white embroidered dress decorated with pearls, one of her father Henry VIII, and many other people, usually between fifty cm and one metre high. He also painted Sir Thomas More. The Ambassadors is a very well known painting which shows two bearded dressed in doublet and hose with cloaks edged with ermine and hats, standing either side of a sideboard and table. On the furniture are casually arranges various items, tropes, that represent elements of learning and knowledge which are prerequisite to public life. Some of these are obvious, like the globe and compasses, the bible, others more obscure to us today. In the centre of the painting is a curious trompe l’oeil feature. Invisible to the viewer standing in front of the painting but for a smear like element, viewed from a specified angle a skull can be seen. It is speculated that Holbein merely wanted to display his virtuosity, or that the painting was intended to be hung on a stairway where it would be reliably encountered at the requisite angle. Elizabethan artists often specialized in miniature painting. Miniatures were around ten cm high, often oval shaped very detailed head and shoulders portraits. They were used like photographs to carry a likeness of a person around. The miniature had a strategic function too – portraits of possible marriage partners could be conveyed to each party. This was important since marriages between members of the royal family and the nobility were often arranged between different nationalities for political reasons, and the couple would rarely have met before the marriage. During the Elizabethan period, Europe underwent great political upheavals as the Protestant and Catholic churches vied for power. Alliances were forged and broken and the borders of modern Europe were drawn. By the seventeenth century Europe had entered a phase of prosperity. Amsterdam was the centre of the trade in goods from overseas and became a very wealth city. Artists were employed to represent and display the wealth of the new merchant class. Venice was also a wealthy and powerful centre of trade, and it was painted many times by Canaletto. 

!Canaletto.

Canaletto is world famous for his scenes of Venice in panoramic format showing the grand canal and its palazzos with water traffic passing, painted in very accurate detail and bathed in a golden sunlight. Canaletto also spent some time in London and painted the Thames river front, including the Royal Naval College at Greenwich and St Paul’s Cathedral. Canaletto's painting are among the most frequently stolen in the world. The influence of Italian classical sculpture spread and statuary was an essential element in architectural design, interiors and gardens. There were many popular sculptors. 

!Canova.

An Italian classical sculptor who made a work called The Three Graces a two metre high white marble sculpture of three women in flowing robes holding hands dancing in a circle. This sculpture has been stolen once. 

!!DUTCH PAINTING.

Dutch seventeenth century painting depicted domestic interiors inhabited by the merchant class. This was the first time people other than the ruling classes had been able to commission artists because of the new found wealth brought by the import and export business. Painters such as Van Dyck, Rembrandt and Vermeer were not presenting images of authority and power, they were depicting the calm serenity of the domestic sphere, a feminine world inhabited temporarily by men. The paintings reproduced the architecture of the time and some artists such as Saenredam made this their subject. 

!Pieter De Hooch.

De Hooch painted the life of the Amsterdam merchant class in intimate detail. He was painting at a time when this newly wealthy class wanted to display its wealth to the world, but women were still largely confined to the home. Windows were always open with light streaming in to spaces which were otherwise candlelit, and food was plentiful, even fruit (although this was evidently a luxury). De Hooch’s paintings portrayed a range of subjects, for example in one a toddling child reaches up to the mound of apples on a woman’s lap. The woman is sitting on a stool on the stone flagged floor and there is a door leading into a room with a window in it behind her. The child is rosy cheeked and blonde. Another shows a back yard with red bricks laid and plants growing along a wall. The architecture of the houses, the brickwork, the construction of doors and windows, the flooring and soft furnishings are faithfully reproduced. 

!Johannes Vermeer.

Vermeer is famous for his painting The Card Players, which shows a group of people sitting round a table at night, the scene lit by lamp and candlelight. The light glows on the faces of the card players but this is a very dark painting. One of the men in the painting stares out at the viewer. Vermeer painted numerous self portraits showing himself both as a young and old man, but like de Hooch he is best known for his paintings of domestic interiors, for example Woman at a Window and another painting of a woman playing the harpsichord. His interiors usually show a room lit from one side by a window, with a door in an opposite wall. The furnishings are comfortable, the floor is often black and white stone tiles, and the walls panelled. If a man is present he is placed by or looking out of the window. The woman is often seated, and looking at the viewer or into the room although the woman playing the harpsichord is in profile. Vermeer wanted to symbolise the different realms inhabited by men and women in the seventeenth century, the outside world and the domestic space. In Woman at a Window a map hangs on the wall behind the woman, as if to amplify this difference. Woman with A Pearl Necklace is a very well known portrait of a young woman wearing a simple yellow and blue dress with fabric wrapped round her head and a peal necklace at her throat. 

!Rembrandt van Rijn.

Rembrandt is acknowledged to be one of the greatest artists of all time. His paintings are celebrated for their treatment of light and for their psychological approach. He tried to show the personality and mood of the sitter, giving the viewer the opportunity to make an assessment about the kind of person they are looking at. Rembrandt used light to great effect, not just in the way he used painting to represent it but also in his composition, in how he places figures in relation to light sources. One of Rembrandts best known works is The Night Watch a painting of a group of people sitting in the dark with candlelight. Another is Girl at a Window which shows a young girl seated at a table looking up to a window through which light is streaming into the very dark interior. As with most of Rembrandt’s work, light is the subject of this painting. 

!Pieter Saenredam.

Saenredam is noted for his church interiors which are generally quite small. One shows the architecture of the Osterkerk in Amsterdam, with tall stone columns, a floor of black and white squares of marble, and people dwarfed by the height of the building. The composition is deceptively ordinary, apparently showing an everyday view of an interior, but the treatment of light in the painting is skilfull, producing an effect of great serenity. The predominantly black and white painting has its focal point in the black diamond shaped board hanging from one of the columns, concentrating the viewer’s eye on the pattern of black and white, vertical lines and perspective planes in the composition. During the seventeenth century Europe saw much political upheaval and many significant events took place. Artists recorded these events and began to take on a new role as commentators. 

!!HISTORY PAINTING.



!Delacroix.

The iconic image of the revolution is Delacroix’s Liberty at the Barricades. This shows Liberty, the female symbol of France, astride the barricades, a crowd swirling around her, and tricolour flags waving. Delacroix is one of the first artists to represent current events and his work prefigured the realism of the nineteenth century. The French Revolution was a rich source of inspiration for artists and one of the chief exponents was David. 

!Jacques Louis David.

David made one of the first topical political paintings, The Death of Marat. The painting was shown at the time it was made and was a direct interpretation of a recent and controversial event. It was also an innovative composition, showing Marat, dead in his bath by his own hand, the knife with which he had stabbed himself on the floor nearby. Only Marat’s head and shoulders and arm are visible, his arm trailing over the side of the stone bath which is carved from a single block on a pedestal base. The dead man wears a white cloth wound like a turban around his head which lolls on the side of the bath. In his other hand he clutches a book or a letter. His skin is cold and blue. The date is, unusually, inscribed in the corner of the painting, drawing the viewer’s attention to the fact that it was recent history. 

!Theodore Gericault.

Another French artist who made topical history paintings, Gericault is known best for The Raft of the Medusa, a very large (2 metre high) painting which shows a shipwreck scene with the survivors clinging to a raft in a stormy sea. This is a depiction of a real recent event in which sense it was a news picture, conveying very vividly the terror of the survivors so that people could imagine being there. 

!Francisco Goya.

One of the greatest painters in history, Goya is well known for his political paintings including The Disasters of War, The Execution of Maximilian (1814) and a series of works known as The Black Paintings. His paintings are mostly small, around 150 cm high at most. Goya’s intention was to reveal to a wider public the cruelty of war and the harshness of life for ordinary people. He was also an innovator in terms of his use of paint and colour. The Execution of Maximilian (an emperor) shows a blindfolded man on the right of the canvas facing a firing squad on the right side. The scene takes place in front of a hill with some buildings behind it. Smoke is coming from the soldiers’ rifles. The painting depicts the actual moment of execution and was made very soon after the event. Thus it is news reportage, depicting the deposition and execution of a ruling monarch. The Disasters of War are a series of around 30 etchings (about 40 cm high) showing scenes of carnage from the battlefield. Soldiers lie dismembered and disembowelled on the ground, they hang from trees, decapitated or genitally mutilated. Goya intended to show the gross cruelty of war and because he chose to use etching his work reached a wide audience that might otherwise have been able to ignore the black paintings show the life of ordinary Spanish people eking out a subsistence living on the land, living in one room. The paintings are dark and inky, there is no light. They show the harshness and ugliness of life. There is one in the series that shows a dog in quicksand, an ochre sand hill filling most of the canvas with the dog’s head sticking out of it. Eighteenth century Britain was a rural economy ruled by landed gentry. Artists were in demand to record the ownership of land and houses for display by the new English landed gentry !Thomas Gainsborough. One of the best known eighteenth century British artists, Gainsborough, made a painting showing a couple and their children with a hunting dog under a tree with a house in the background. The man wears breeches, a tail coat and a hat, and is leaning on a shotgun; the woman has on a fine low necked dress which is light blue and white with a straw hat. They are dressed in their country clothes, but there is no pretence – this is a painting intended to display the couple’s wealth and social status, showing them confidently engaging the viewer from the rural beauty of their large estate. 

!Joshua Reynolds.

Another English painter of the landed gentry, Reynolds made portraits and landscapes. English landscape painting was particularly important during a period when land ownership was the key to wealth. People commissioned paintings to show how much land they owned. 

!George Stubbs.

Stubbs' name is synonymous with his paintings of horses which are extremely realistically painted, in profile, on a plain background. Other aspects of eighteenth century life are well represented by Rubens and Hogarth. 

!Peter Paul Rubens.

Rubens is known for his paintings of buxom rosy cheeked naked women. Rubens showed groups of women relaxing together, often out of doors, naked or semi clothed. Their flesh is almost expressionistically painted and close to the range of colours used to paint the skin tones can clearly be seen. Rubens draws on the classical nude as inspiration but his nudes are ordinary women. 

!William Hogarth.

From Hogarth, a British artist and political commentator and satirist, we have a comprehensive documentation of eighteenth century London life in his highly detailed drawings and engravings. His engravings of Bedlam, the notorious London hospital for the insane, are famous for their accurate depiction of a horrific place which few people had seen. Hogarth showed the seamy side of life, the injustices and inequities, but contrasted it with images of debauchery, gluttony and indulgence. 

!Franz Hals.

Dutch Eighteenth century painter notably of portraits, Hals’ painting The Laughing Cavalier, a portrait of a man in a large hat with a feather is instantly recognisable. 

!William Blake.

English poet and artist William Blake is known for his visionary drawings and for his illustrated poetry books the Songs of Innocence and the Songs Of Experience. Blake made a drawing with watercolour of god measuring the world with a pair of compasses which has been much reproduced. In the eighteenth century in Europe and America neo classicism and the study of Greek sculpture was a central part of any artist’s education. The highest accolades were given to those who could best emulate, interpret and appropriate classical Greek art, taking Greek literature as its narrative theme. Artists were expected to become skilful at drawing in a realistic style, and to be able to draw from life, model and carve the human form with accuracy. But by the nineteenth century romanticism was beginning to take a hold and landscape and nature were idealised. 

!Anton Watteau.

A French artist working in the nineteenth century, Watteau made paintings of people in landscapes, often with classical allusions – as was the fashion of the day the aristocracy was fond of acting the peasant (Marie Antoinette, for example) or dressing as mythological characters. The paintings were often portrait commissions. Watteau was particularly good at painting trees – at the time trees in paintings were rendered in much the same way by many artists, often by assistants rather than by the artist. Watteau drew and painted virtually every leaf making the trees in his work very realistic and quite distinctive. 

!Fragonard.

Fragonard is known for his paintings of women and children in the countryside or in a garden. Fragonard’s many portraits depict an idyllic life, as in Girl on a Swing and his many paintings of society women and children in beautiful dresses. Fragonard’s paintings are full of trees, flowers and grass in the background, and the detail of the subjects’ clothes is given great attention. They are painted in a soft, feathery delicate style. 

!Arnold Bocklin.

During the 1960s Bocklin painted romantic landscapes and idyllic pastoral scenes showing shepherdesses and their swains in ribboned clothes with the occasional piece of statuary. Like Watteau he painted trees in great detail. 

!Boucher.

Boucher also painted romantic landscapes. 

!!THE SUBLIME.

The worship of nature found its culmination in the German concept of the sublime, which described nature as vast and never ending, true beauty in which man is almost an irrelevance. The chief exponent of this was Friedrich. 

!Casper David Friedrich.

Freidrich was a romantic painter many of whose works were painted from nature on an island in the Baltic sea off the coast of Germany. He was chiefly known for his interpretation of the Romantic sublime. Friedrich tried to create a rendering of nature so beautiful as to seem almost like heaven. This deeply subjective approach was of great interest to many critics and writers struggling with the idea of authorship, subjectivity and the purpose of art after Kant. The sublime is also dealt with by Mary Shelley in Frankenstein and by English poets such as Wordsworth and Shelley. Friedrich's paintings are usually less than 200 by 100 cm in size. They nearly always feature a solitary figure in a landscape. A typical painting depicts a ruined folly on a hilltop seen on a moonlit night with a figure on another peak in the foreground. One painting shows a figure in a rain and windswept landscape with barren trees, another a figure in a moonlit garden with a balustrade, a classical statue and a broken urn. Friedrich painted in a cool reduced palette and his many dramatic moonlit scenes are rendered in shades of blue and grey. 

!John Martin.

An English artist working in the late nineteenth century, Martin painted a kind of inversion of the idea of the sublime. His visionary scenes depicted humankind struggling with the natural world. An example is a painting of a towering dark black and brown mountain at night in which a huge chasm opened up, a volcanic eruption spewing flames, sparks and lava into the darkness. Dwarfed by this landscape many people are struggling and fleeing from the lava flow and fires. English artists did not engage with the sublime but focused on the rural landscape around them and its inhabitants. 

!John Constable.

Constable was an eighteenth century artist who made one of the most famous English paintings, The Haywain, which shows a country scene in which a cart loaded with hay is travelling down a lane. The cart and the farm workers that it carries is very small in relation to the picture as a whole. The painting consists mostly of trees and bushes, predominantly green, brown and ochre coloured with a blue sky. It is a peaceful image of a summer day at harvest time. Flatford Mill is almost as well known. This painting shows a house on a millpond by a river where trees and vegetation predominate. Constable’s paintings convey specific weather conditions and the time of day very well. They seem to represent an idealised and valued vision of the countryside as a peaceful haven. Constable always added an element of red to each painting, a tiny dot or brushstroke of red paint that served to draw the viewer’s eye into the part of the painting that he wanted them to focus on. It might be the roof of a distant cottage or, as in the Haywain, a red garment on a person on the cart. Other British painters such as Wilson Steer, Joseph Wright of Derby, John Everett Millais and Samuel Palmer explored both landscape and portraiture during the nineteenth century. In the second half of the century the industrial revolution changed life in Europe dramatically and the rural idyll was no longer given centre stage. Although critics such as John Ruskin held up the values of traditional arts and crafts skills, and the study of classical art, artists on the whole discovered new challenges. 

!Joseph Mallord Turner.

One of the best known British artist, often described as a British impressionist, Turner made semi abstract watercolours and oil paintings of the river Thames and London. He was most interested in depicting weather and light conditions as opposed to landscape or architectural detail. Turner’s paintings are often brightly coloured and made with blurred brushy strokes. He had made one of the river Thames with a rainbow and one of a cloudy sky painted in white and grey. Whistler was very critical of Turner, describing his work as mere daubing. 

!James McNeil Whistler.

A painting of fireworks over the river Thames was said by Whistler’s rival Turner to look as if someone had thrown a pot of paint at the canvas. Whistler made many partings of London, but he also had a house in the Lake District in the north of England and many of his landscapes are painted there, as well as domestic interiors and portraits. Whistler also painted murals, for example one at Plas Newydd, the home of the Marquis of Anglesey, in Wales. He prided himself in being able to paint very fast. During the nineteenth century the styles of historical periods were being appropriated by contemporary artists. Classicism, realism and romanticism were broad spectrums that included artist working in diverse ways. Some movements were more specific in their aims and distinct in their visual output. 

!!THE PRE RAPHAELITES.

The Pre Raphaelites were a group of artists working in England who were inspired by poetry and medieval history. They painted people dressed in clothes inspired by medieval costume but in contemporary fashion, women and men characterised by their full lips and long flowing hair worn parted in the centre and loose. The style of painting was exact and detailed, everything rendered with great realism, the intention being to convey ideal beauty and sentiment. Often the paintings had a decorative border or architectural elements. Pre Raphaelite painting and sculpture was highly stylised and extremely popular. 

!Dante Gabriel Rossetti.

Rossetti was a leading figure in the pre Raphaelite Brotherhood, the society formed by artists and writers interested in working together. These artists produced paintings that looked to medieval art and legend for their inspiration. Rossetti’s Lady of Shallot is a world famous painting of a drowned woman, the lover of King Arthur, floating in a stream. She is face upwards and looks as if she is merely asleep, wearing a long green dress with her hair floating in a cloud around her head and her hands clasped on her breast. Flowers float in the water around her. 

!Ford Maddox Brown.

One of the British pre Raphaelite painters, Maddox Brown made less stylised paintings than Rossetti, for example a painting of a goat in a barren landscape lit by a lurid setting sun, painted in orange, purple and green. The poetic aesthetic extended to other artists. 

!Henry Wallis.

A British nineteenth century painter who is best known for his painting of Henry Chatterton, a boy poet who committed suicide. The boy is shown on a bed with a view of a garden through a window behind him, wearing a white shirt which is loose. 

!Gustav Klimt.

An Austrian artist working at the turn of century in Vienna, Klimt painted highly decorative images of women with particular attention to the reproduction of the patterns on textiles and clothes the models are wearing. He also painted landscapes, giving the same attention to the ‘pattern’ of leaves and flowers, such as in his series The Four Seasons, paintings of woodlands and gardens in seasonal colours. Klimt had a very distinctive style of drawing the figure, giving his almost exclusively female subjects a kind of languid beauty. They are often draped in decadent poses or staring straight at the viewer giving an impression of forthrightness and unabashed hedonism. One example is a painting of a standing woman, her head almost resting on one shoulder, wearing an extraordinarily ornate dress and cloak which has a pattern of multicoloured and gold squares. In fact Klimt's trademark was his use of tooled and flat gold leaf applied to the patterns on fabric in his paintings. He also tended to delineate the shape of the body by the outline of the clothes, so that the figure often appeared to be a solid block. These attributes give Klimt’s work a graphic effect. Another example is a painting of four women who are lying together on a bed. Klimt’s paintings often show women together in intimacy, lying together in an embrace. In this painting the four are intertwined and seen in a kind of false perspective as if from above. It is hardly possible to tell which limbs belong to which head. The fabric of their clothes are mixed together, so that the composition is a swirl of heads, limbs and pattern, creating a kind of round shape within the frame of the canvas. Portrait painters were in great demand in a new social milieu ushered in by the industrial revolution and its resulting new wealth. 

!John Singer Sargent.

Sargent's paintings show fashionably dressed society figures, the fabric of their clothes very realistically painted, skin glowing and hair shining. 

!Berthe Morisot.

French artist Morisot is best known for her portraits and drawings. 

!Gwen John.

John's portraits are subtle and she used a sombre palette. 

!!ORIENTALISM.

Art from Japan, China and the far east, both traditional and contemporary, began to appear in Europe and attracted a great deal of attention. 

!Hokusai.

A Japanese eighteenth century artist who is best known for his painting The Wave, was a master of the woodcut and pen and ink drawing and painting. The towering wave in this work is about to crash down on the surrounding sea, representing the power of nature. Hokusai worked in traditional style but his subject matter was innovative. The Wave has become an instantly recognisable image. 

!Utamaro.

Utamaro’s series of hand coloured woodcuts, the work for which he is best known, depicts a series of explicit sexual acts between men and geisha women dressed in elaborate kimonos. The women and men perform a range of often gymnastic sex acts and could not be shown in public since they were considered indecent. These works are unusual at the time for their representation of enthusiastic and playful sexuality. Overt sexuality was not a subject of art in the eighteenth century. Religious beliefs in Europe would not tolerate the depiction of explicit sexual acts and Victorian Britain did not even permit allusive or veiled reference to sex. A number of artists nevertheless made work on the subject, notably Beardsley. 

!Beardsley Aubrey.

Beardsley was a graphic artist whose finely executed and intricate art nouveau style has become instantly recognisable. Beardsley illustrated Oscar Wilde's book of the tale of Salome, and made a large number of explicit sexual images. Influenced by the art of China and Japan as were many at the time, Beardsley's characteristic flat highly decorative black and white pen and ink drawings made use of patterns on textiles and clothing of his subjects to great effect, and he was an extremely skilled draughtsman. Many artists were influenced by contemporary developments in the new discipline of psychoanalysis, as well as other therapeutic and diagnostic methods of the day such as phrenology. Sigmund Freud was beginning his research into the unconscious which was to have a profound influence on artists in the late nineteenth and early twentieth century. 

!Messerschmidt.

Messerschmidt was a German artist who in the 1890s made a series of small modelled heads cast in bronze, about ten cms across, a male head with a number of different expressions, often the features are contorted. The heads are titled by the mood or emotion the face is displaying. Messerschmidt was influenced by contemporary developments in psychology. 

!!REALISM.

French realism profoundly influenced European art. Painters like Courbet, Corot, Greuze and Daumier began to try to represent landscape and the figure as objectively as possible. 

!Gustav Courbet.

The most influential of the early realists is Courbet who is often referred to as the father of realism. Both Manet and Cézanne acknowledged his influence. 

!Corot.

A contemporary of Courbet, Corot is known for his still calm scenes of the French countryside, often featuring roads and tracks and always trees, which he was particularly good at painting and some notable snow scenes. Corot’s paintings are small, no more than sixty cm high. 

!Camille Pissarro.

A French landscape painter who spent some time in London painting the river Thames. Pissarro liked the cool grey light of London. 

!Ingres.

Ingres made a painting of a seated nude which is known all over the world. It is a simple, natural and intimate portrait of a woman seated on a bed with her back to the viewer, a white sheet draped round her hips, and a red patterned scarf wound round her head with her face just visible in profile. It has been remarked that that drawing of the figure is anatomically inaccurate and that if the woman were to stand up her body would be very out of proportion. 

!Auguste Renoir.

Children and women are Renoir’s favourite subjects. His paintings are light bright celebrations of youth, beauty, the countryside, gardens and flowers. He painted himself often, the best known self portrait is one in which he is wearing a white painter’s smock and a brown wide brimmed hat with glasses. Renoir’s nudes are fleshy and sensual, the paint applied to create a fuzzy, slightly out of focus effect. A particularly well known painting shows a little girl with very blonde hair in a dark blue dress standing in long grass and poppies. 

!Gustave Caillebotte.

Caillebotte's landscapes and people are characterised by a richness in the way the paint is applied. A painting of men scraping and polishing a wooden floor is a well known example of his work. The men, wearing dark trousers and white shirts, some wearing caps, are on their knees in a horizontal line across the canvas, working, and the floor has a bright shine on it, created by the daylight streaming through the windows at the back of the room. The painting is mainly dark brown and wood colour, with the windows and the shine on the floor painted in a bluish white. 

!Puvis de Chavannes.

French painter of the early nineteenth century who made a painting of a thin young man with dark hair and eyes who is standing in a small boat on a lake. The man is meant to make us think of Christ. 

!!THE INVENTION OF PHOTOGRAPHY.

The realist painters were working just before photography. There was still no way of picturing the world other than to paint it. When photography was invented it had an immediate effect on painting because of the way a photograph defined a definite edge to an image. In a painting the edge was always mutable and subjective. Manet and Degas began to compose their paintings in a different way, framing what they painted in a way that was closer to photography.
