!Edouard Manet.

The French artist who is often described as the father of impressionism, Manet painted in a realistic way but used paint expressively. Manet took great interest in the way a photograph cropped a scene and noted the difference for a painter, who can choose what to include and where. Manet’s work began to have a new look which was at first shocking but immediately influential. Manet was a prominent member of the community of artists in Paris and was friends with Courbet, Degas, and Monet. Manet painted two very well known works, Olympia, and Dejeuner Sur L’Herbe, as well as Bonjour Monsieur Courbet and the Bar at the Folies Bérgères. This last is a very original painting which depicts a bar at the Folies Bérgères night club, visited and painted by many of his contemporaries, including Toulouse Lautrec, behind which a woman is serving. On closer inspection what the viewer sees is actually the reflection in the mirror behind the bar. Manet has taken this mirror as the edge of his painting and thus we see the woman’s back, some of people at the bar and the bottles which are in front of the mirror. What was striking at the time was that this was a painting of a reflection, and was not what it seemed to be at all. Manet's paintings were startlingly new and inventive. His Olympia (which owes much to Velasquez’s Rokeby Venus) was considered shocking, provoked storms of protests and was rejected from the Paris salon, the official body of recognition for artists. The painting was said to be pornographic and the public was outraged. Olympia is depicted nude, reclining on a couch, with her maid and a cat behind her. She wears a ribbon choker and earrings and seems to be a prostitute. However it was the look on the woman’s face that was controversial – she stares out of the canvas at the viewers, actively acknowledging their presence and thus making them the subject of the work. This was revolutionary, and brought the whole history of the depiction of the female nude in art into question – here a woman meets the viewer’s gaze rather than allowing the viewer unchallenged access to her body in passive acquiescence. Dejeuner Sur L’Herbe, also a reworking of a historical painting, provoked a similar response but soon these two paintings were acknowledged as groundbreaking works. Dejeuner Sur L’Herbe shows a group of young men and women picnicking in the woods, one of the women is naked, reclining casually and staring out at the viewer from the centre of the canvas. The gaze of some of the others is also focused out of the picture plane at the viewer. Bonjour Monsieur Courbet is a painting in which Manet acknowledges his debt to Courbet. He shows himself walking along a country lane, respectfully greeting the passing Courbet who carries a walking stick and wears a hat. 

!Edgar Degas.

Degas is a contemporary of Manet’s who also made use of the photograph in his compositions. Degas is known all over the world for his scenes of ballet dancers rehearsing, which are instantly recognisable and often reproduced in particular as calendars. His paintings are cropped – much like a photograph a figure will be half painted and cut off by the edge of the canvas. He mainly painted women, including several paintings and drawings of women bathing. One of these shows the back of a woman with red hair as she crouches in a tin bath. There is a blue towel next to her on the floor. Degas made many bronze sculptures of ballet dancers, most are quite small ones around fifty cm high, as well as some of curled up dogs. His best known and very popular sculpture The Little Dancer is larger, around two metres high. The little girl is realistically modelled and wears a net tutu, a bodice and ballet shoes. It is rare to find a bronze sculpture which has added clothes. 

!Paul Cézanne.

Known as the father of modernist painting, Cézanne was a French painter working in the mid nineteenth century. He took the ideas of Manet and Courbet and developed a completely new style of painting. Many see Cézanne as the pivotal figure in late nineteenth century painting who laid the ground for Cubism. Cézanne lived in Paris and the south of France in a house called Jas de Buffon in Provence. The Provencal countryside featured in much of his work and Cézanne liked to work out of doors as much as he could. He also painted still lives and portraits. Cézanne was working around the same time as the impressionists, but his ideas took him on a very different route. He had no interest in creating an impression, in depicting light, in optical fragmentation of the image. Rather he was concerned with how the three dimensional world could be rendered on a two dimensional plane. Cézanne was one of the first artists to paint a representative image giving as much importance to the picture plane as to the illusory space of the painting. In his landscapes and still lives he considered how much of an object he could paint, seeing no reason not to paint the parts of an object that could not be seen, to bend perspective, to remake nature. He composed his paintings so that the picture plane seemed to come towards the viewer, using judicious touches of white to control the path of the viewer’s eye. It is in this that he was revolutionary and laid the ground for Picasso and Braque to develop Cubism, for as young artists they were astonished by what Cézanne had done. He gave them the idea that a three dimensional object could be rendered in two dimensions not by creating an illusion, but by unfolding its various planes and presenting them with no relation to the actual physical integrity of the object. Cézanne was also daring in his use of colour. He never covered the ground of the canvas completely, always leaving parts of it bare to function as a part of the painting as a whole. He applied colour with an abstract sensibility, for its own sake as part of the painting, rather than as a part of a faithful depiction. He used the juxtaposition of pure colours to create shadows of other colours. A number of paintings are immediately associated with Cézanne. The Bathers (a number of works bear this title) are a group of female nudes in a variety of poses, their bodies represented as planes in a landscape. The painting is brown and green, the bodies flesh coloured. This is Cézanne’s best known and least understood work, and it is the work which has the most influence on other artists. His still lifes of oranges and apples are also characteristic, as are some of his portraits, for example the woman with a green face (the green representing the shadow cast on her face by the hat she was wearing. Most of his paintings are fairly small, under a metre high. He painted the land around his country home endlessly, often making repeated versions of the same view of trees and hillside. He made a small landscape that includes a rectangular water cistern in which there is a mirror image, many paintings of trees including a small painting of a single almond tree in blossom, and views from the house. He often painted his wife, including a notable painting of her in the bath under greenish water. 

!!IMPRESSIONISM.

Whilst Cézanne was investigated the depiction of three dimensional space, a number of younger artists who were influenced by photography were experimenting with a new way of painting. The photograph made realistic and accurate representations by artists redundant, since the photograph could do this quicker and more accurately. This fact, combined with a new acknowledgment of the subjectivity of art and an interest in the psyche, freed artists to try to make impressions of what they saw rather than try to replicate. Broadly speaking, the artist’s role became one of subjective interpretation rather than of objective representation. The most dramatic example of this is the work of a number of painters working in France in the 1880s and 90s. 

!Claude Monet.

Monet is the best known of the Impressionists, and indeed made the painting which gave the movement its name, Impression: Sunrise, a view of the houses of parliament and the river Thames in London. This is a small painting of the river at sunrise, the water reflecting the oranges and pinks of the sun, which is painted as a small orange circle of relatively thick paint. This painting was remarkable for its vagueness in terms of detail, and for the consideration of the nature of the painted surface, so that the sun could be read both as a sun and as a painted circle. Monet was trying to paint an impression of what he saw, as if with eyes half shut, and it is possible that he got this idea through seeing out of focus photographic images. After all the idea of a deliberately blurred image was a radically new one at the time and Monet’s idea changed the aims of painters everywhere. Monet took from Cézanne the idea that an artist might paint what was in front of him but paint less than he actually saw, aiming to reproduce the essence of the view. To this end he developed a very distinctive painting style breaking the scene he was painting into patches of colour, applied without covering the ground of the canvas, using the uncovered white ground to create a shimmering effect. He used colour to paint light as well as solid forms. Along with Matisse and Picasso, on whom he was influential, Monet is considered by many to be one of the greatest artists in the world and one of the founders of modernism and twentieth century painting. Monet made several important groups of works. The Water Lily paintings that he made at his country home in Giverny, a series of façades of Rouen cathedral, and a series of Haystacks. The water lilies series are largely three by four metre semi abstract canvases which depict a pond or lake with water lilies on it using shades of emerald, ultramarine and violet, with bright pink and red highlights on the usually white lilies. The way the paint is applied to construct the image is almost of as much importance as the image itself. A series of paintings of the façade of the cathedral at Rouen was a detailed exercise in and demonstration of impressionist technique. In these works the surface of the painting is encrusted with multicoloured paint, which is visible as a façade only at a distance. There are an immense variety of paintings which represent and exaggerate different light conditions. Monet was unique in his preference for continual repetition of views of the same subject. He also painted a haystack series, notable for their pale blue and lilac shadows, a houses of parliament and Thames series. Impressionism did not impress the Paris salon, leading to the formation of the Salon des Refusés, a place for all those artists rejected by the Salon to exhibit. It soon became recognised that these rejected artists were changing painting forever. 

!Georges Seurat.

Known as the inventor of pointillism, Seurat made a number of large paintings in this style, the best known of which is L’Ile de la Grande Jatte, as well as landscapes and coastal views. Pointillism is a way of painting using only tiny dots of separate colour. These dots merge at a certain distance to create an optical illusion of solid colour although there is a grainy quality to the painted image. L’Ile de la Grande Jatte shows people enjoying themselves on a summer day on an island. In the foreground is a boy in swimming trunks wearing a red hat. Women and children walk in the gardens and under the trees and a group of picnickers sit on the grass. 

!Alfred Sisley.

Sisley was an English painter who spent much time working in Paris. Sisley was the major exponent of the Impressionist style in Britain, painting London and the English countryside. 

!Walter Sickert.

Like Sisley, Sickert painted London in an impressionistic style, although he is not an Impressionist. Sacker’s paintings are all made in a gloomy palette of grey, ochre and brown, the paint thickly applied, showing scenes of London and portraits. Not all French painters worked in the impressionist style. Many artists developed individual ways of working that placed them outside any movement. 

!!SYMBOLISM.



!James Ensor.

Belgian symbolist artist who lived in Ostend where his former home can still be visited. During the 1890s Ensor made a painting of a crowd of people including several in clown masks, one white masked person wearing top hat standing out in the foreground. The painting has a slightly nightmarish effect. 

!Odilon Redon.

Belgian symbolist artist who made paintings, drawings and prints. One of his best known works is a drawing of a black spider. The Dutch artist Van Gogh was not part of any movement. His style was idiosyncratic and he made his work in relative isolation, not knowing success in his own lifetime. 

!Vincent Van Gogh.

Van Gogh came from a poor family and he was supported financially throughout his life by his brother Theo who became an art dealer and bought all of Van Gogh’s early work. Their relationship is well documented by letters. Without Theo, Van Gogh would not have been able to paint and as it was he lived much of the time on very little money. Van Gogh began by drawing and painting what he saw around him, rural scenes from his home village showing people working in the fields. He made his famous charcoal drawing The Potato Eaters, which shows a poor family eating a meagre meal crammed into one room in the semi darkness. Van Gogh moved to his own lodgings in Paris but continued to paint rural life. He also painted his own surroundings, producing another seminal work The Artist’s Bedroom, which shows a small bed, a chair with a straw seat, a pair of boots by the bed. The perspective is exaggerated and the drawing chunky. He outlined the shapes of things, developing what was to become his unique style. He also painted his own boots, which amounted to a self portrait. During his time in Paris and later on in Arles, Van Gogh met many other artists and became particular friends with Paul Gauguin who had a great influence on him. Van Gogh began to paint still life, and in particular flowers, influenced by the fashion for oriental decorative arts which had recently begun to arrive in Paris. He even made a painting of a cherry tree which has Japanese characters round its borders. Van Gogh’s most famous work, also one of the most famous paintings in the world, is Sunflowers, a painting of a number of sunflowers in a vase, the whole done in a palette of ochre, yellow and brown with Van Gogh’s usual thick encrustations of paint and impasto. This work is now priceless. A similar work, Irises, is worth billions. Van Gogh and his close friend the painter Paul Gauguin rented a house in Arles in the south of France where the light was very good for painting, where they lived and worked, both drinking heavily. Van Gogh made many of his best works here but it was in Arles that his increasing depression began to take its toll. He painted the portraits of many local people, including the school master, the postman, a local prostitute who modelled for him, and notoriously his own self portraits. One of these shows him as a red headed bearded man with pale green eyes wearing a suit. But one self portrait is notorious for showing the artist with a bandage round his head covering his ear. He is reputed to have cut off his own ear when drunk and sent it to a woman with whom he was angry. Van Gogh sold only to his brother in his lifetime and his talent was not recognised. This probably contributed to his increasing drinking and ill health. Towards the end of his life he painted a number of landscapes that popular myth supposes presage his death, cornfields with brooding dark skies and black crows flying, the birds painted with black paint squeezed straight from the tube. He often used paint in an unmediated way which was unusual at the time, and it was his use of paint that first brought him to attention, although his subject matter on the whole was conventional. The myths created around Van Gogh’s life have been enhanced by a Hollywood film made in the 1960s in which Kirk Douglas portrays Van Gogh as the ultimate tortured soul putting his life into his art. 

!Paul Gauguin.

Gauguin’s early works were landscapes and portraits. Gauguin was an outspoken person and influential among the artists of Paris. He was a very close friend of Van Gogh and they lived together in Arles in the south of France for a time. Gauguin supported Van Gogh and encouraged him, helping him through periods of madness including the ear dismembering incident. Gauguin’s paintings are mainly bold and colourful figurative works. Towards the end of his life Gauguin moved to Tahiti (where he later died) and painted the works with which he is most readily associated (although they are not necessarily seen as his best), showing Tahitian people, often women, in native dress, with the interiors of traditional homes and beach scenes. The colours he uses are very strong, bold and sun drenched. 

!Pierre Bonnard.

Bonnard’s domestic interiors are painted in warm and subtle shades with soft dabbing brushstrokes, and his attention to the pattern and detail of the textiles in the room is an important part of the whole composition. In one painting a woman is seated at a table at the top of the canvas, the table extending towards the bottom, the perspective elongated which is designed to draw the viewer in and give the impression both of welcome and of distance. Much of Bonnard’s work presents people in solitary and restful states. 

!Edouard Vuillard.

Like Bonnard, Vuillard painted landscapes and domestic interiors. 

!Henri de Toulouse Lautrec.

Toulouse Lautrec was a graphic artist who was friends with many of the artist of fin de siècle Paris. He is best known for his posters for the Folies Bérgère in particular for one showing a row of can can dancers. He was a very good draughtsman and was influence by Chinese and Japanese art. 

!Henri Rousseau.

Rousseau’s painting of a tiger in a jungle has become extremely well known. The jungle is shown as a mass of enormous green leaves and stalks that cover the canvas, below which stalks a partially obscured tiger. There are a number of other animals, birds and exotic blooms. The painting is executed in a faux naïve illustrative style. Rousseau also made a painting of a parade in a French town, he often represented French civic life and was a Republican. 

!!FAUVISM.



!Andre Derain.

Another group of French artists led by Andre Derain and influenced by both Cézanne and Monet painted in a style that they called Fauvism, meaning literally ‘wildness’. They worked in very bright pure colours, using larger areas of colour than the impressionists and plenty of white space, not attempting to create a recognisable image but to reduce the image to a wild approximation of its essential qualities. Although Impressionist painting became very popular, it was the work of Cézanne that inspired the next generation of artists, notably Matisse, Picasso, Gris and Braque to break new ground. 

!!CUBISM.



!Georges Braque.

A French artist born around 1880, Braque, along with Picasso, was the major exponent of Cubism, a new style of painting that fragmented and distorted the image in a radical way. Although he began as a painter of landscapes and portraits, in 1911 Braque made a painting on which he pasted a piece of newspaper, Still Life with a Guitar. It showed a table, a bottle and a guitar but treated all objects as if conventional perspective did not apply, representing them instead with flat planes, areas and shapes. The newspaper signified the presence of a newspaper in the still life, but was not a newspaper and did not look like one. The act of sticking something onto the canvas initiated a revolution in painting, because it destroyed the integrity of the picture plane by acknowledging its physicality as a surface. This had the effect of allowing a painting to be seen as an object as well as a flat representation. No one had ever stuck anything onto a canvas to make part of the image before. Braque’s achievement was to find a new way of representing form. He began to include collage, using old tickets and printed paper (in one painting he uses paper printed with a rattan pattern to signify a cane chair), in another a piece of wood effect paper is used for a guitar for example. He made many such collages often depicting bottles, tabletops and wine glasses with the structural planes of the objects opened out and repositioned, before going on, as did Picasso, to attempt the same thing exclusively with paint. Cubism was a way of representing all the planes of three dimensional objects on a two dimensional plane, to think of every object in terms of the surfaces of a series of cubes for the purposes of painting. This way of looking at the physical world had been initiated by Cézanne. Such a radical restructuring of pictorial space resulted in paintings made in browns and greys initially. Braque sometimes used an elliptical canvas. The paintings gave the impression of crystalline forms with recognisable elements attached, such as a head, an eye, part of a guitar, the side of a bottle, the front of a table, the back of a chair. Gradually Braque began to make a different kind of image becuase the effect of cubism had been to free the picture plane from the constraints of realistic representation and pictorial space. The surface of the canvas became the pictorial space, so that the painting was both a representation and an exploration of surface. 

!Juan Gris.

A painter working at the same time and in a similar way to Braque. It is possible that Gris was the first to use collage on a painting, although Braque is given the credit for this. Gris tended to make small paintings, sometimes using an oval format. He also went on to make Cubist paintings. 

!Pablo Picasso.

The Spanish artist Picasso is considered to be the greatest modern artist in the world. Picasso’s work progressed through a number of different styles, including Cubism, and he made work in a variety of media. His skill at drawing is consistently evident, but he was equally good at sculpture. From Picasso’s early career, his Blue period is the best known. This describes a number of figurative paintings that have a blue light cast on them, for example a boy with a dove in his hand, and a young boy dressed as a Harlequin balancing on a ball. The dove and the clown, particularly Harlequin and Pierrot, were recurring motifs throughout Picasso’s life. In the early 1900s Picasso, along with Braque and Gris, would have seen African sculptures for the first time when a collection was exhibited in Paris. This, combined with Cézanne’s experiments with the picture plane, led the three artists to make the first Cubist works. Although Cubism is most readily attributed to Picasso, it was Gris who in fact made the first Cubist collage. Picasso made a number of these collages featuring bottles, guitars, as well as some small sculptures including one of a shelf with some domestic objects on it made from rough bits of wood and painted with a bobble braid tacked along the front. Picasso made a great number of Cubist paintings, which are rarely more than a metre high and predominantly brownish or greyish. During this period Picasso began to develop his mature style to reach its greatest achievement in two paintings, Guernica and Les Demoiselles D’Avignon. Guernica was painted in 1917 to commemorate General Franco’s massacre of a Spanish village. In it Picasso wanted to show the horror of war and suffering, and he was conscious of making a painting which would get attention. He made the painting immediately after the event and it was intended to be given to the town of Guernica. It is a very large painting, three meters by six, which shows a number of figures based clearly on African sculpture (for example Dogon masks) painted in black, purple, white and grey with everything outlined in black. Guernica is an image of horror, of death and suffering, of weeping women, corpses and maimed bodies. The limbs of the figures are intertwined so that the whole painting operates as an abstract composition as well. This was the first time a modern artist had made a painting that was such an explicit criticism of the government of the day. Guernica was shocking and reproductions of the painting were instantly printed in newspapers all over the world. Les Demoiselles D’Avignon is composed along similar lines but depicts a more pleasant scene of naked women in the countryside, painted in flesh tones and greens. However, the women have the same mask like faces as the figures in Guernica. A film was made of Picasso making this painting, showing how he drew out the figures with continuous lines never once correcting himself or erasing anything. Picasso painted many portraits, often of the various women in his life, including Dora Maar, one of his lovers. These were never flattering, as the Cubist style which Picasso had made his own led to features being rearranged so that an eye might appear on the side of the head and the face be cut in two. Black painted lines delineate everything and colour is entirely unrealistic. Picasso made a great deal of carved and assembled sculpture including a bull’s head made from a bicycle seat with a pair of handlebars for horns. Drawing underpinned everything that Picasso did, and he made a large number of them, in particularly a number of black line drawings of the Minotaur, a myth which fascinated him. Later in he made coloured lithographs that look like pastel drawings. These which are very well known, for example a simple hand holding a bunch of flowers and a dove with an olive branch in its beak. In his exploration of every medium Picasso included ceramics, producing a great many plates, vases, and other objects, that he painted on and he also made a series of tooled and embossed silver platters. 

!Juan Miro.

Picasso’s compatriot Miro took a very different route, making abstract paintings that drew on a variety of sources. Miro uses amorphous shapes reminiscent of microscopic life forms that are painted on a thinly prepared raw linen ground. Thin black lines and spidery nodes connect amorphous shapes in red and blue. Miro borrowed compositional elements from comic strip drawings in particular the American Krazy Kat. He also made collages using images cut from cutlery, tool and machinery catalogues that were often mixed with elements from cartoon strips and then abstracted to produce his paintings. In some ways Miro’s approach had an affinity with the work of the surrealists. Few artists have come close to achieving Picasso’s huge output in such a variety of media. But one French artist made paintings that were as influential as Picasso’s. 

!Henri Matisse.

Matisse is considered one of the greatest artists in the world. His paintings include the famous Dance, an 8 metre by 3 metre panoramic painting of dancing female nudes, radical for its semi abstraction. The pink figures become rhythmic arabesques on the swirling blue ground. Matisse later made the figures into paper cut outs, a form in which he was prolific in later life, using ultramarine paper collaged onto a white ground. With the Snail, a large 4 by 3 metre collage made of a spiral of squares of different brightly coloured pieces of paper within a yellow collaged frame, these cut outs became Matisse's best known work, their graphic simplicity lending itself well to printed reproduction. The blue dancers, their heavy limbs creating a calligraphic pattern, are one of the most recognisable works by a twentieth century artist. Matisse also made a large number of drawings; he had an assured hand, creating very simple drawings often using one continuous line. In his paintings it is Matisse’s use of drawing which sets his work apart. He allowed a drawn edge to define the pictorial space and function both as part of an object or structure and part of the composition, and similarly he left areas of the canvas unpainted, allowing the white of the ground to function as part of the image. Matisse painted restful, joyous and domestic subjects in an entirely unsentimental, intellectual way. His composition was daring, for example the Piano Lesson is a predominantly green and red painting composed of large blocks of colour, the entire painting bisected by a diagonal line, the head of a boy emerging from behind a piano very small in relation to the very large canvas. The painting is figurative but it verges toward abstraction. It is this abstract sensibility and his use of colour which singles Matisse out. A head and shoulders portrait of a woman with a green face shows her with the shadow of her woman’s hat over her face painted in a swoop of emerald green. Matisse made literal translations of what his eye saw, so that if a shadow looked greenish, he painted it green, and if an edge could be made by not painting the canvas, he left it bare. For example, another painting depicts a goldfish in a bowl on a table by a French window opening onto a tiny balcony. The window and room are boldly outlined in black; the goldfish bowl and much else is half painted. The Red Studio is a daring work, showing the furniture and objects in his studio outlined in white (made simply by leaving the canvas white where he wanted the line to be) and some colour, with minimal perspective, resulting in a painting that is a red square of canvas with objects, and some architectural elements such as a pair of shutters floating on it. Matisse also made a number of bronze casts, the best known of which is the triptych The Back, an abstracted human torso viewed from the back, carved in relief as semi cylindrical shapes to represent the two sides of the back and the buttocks, leaving a space in the middle to represent the division caused by the spine. The sculptures were commissioned for a specific interior and were thus made as three bronze wall reliefs. At the turn of the century, the influence of photography on painting was clear. It was also having an effect on sculpture. This period saw some of the greatest sculptors emerge, leaving behind the tradition of copying classical sculpture and measuring from life. 

!!EARLY TWENTIETH CENTURY SCULPTURE.



!Auguste Rodin.

Rodin, a French artist who is considered to be one of the greatest modern sculptors, made his best known works at the turn of the century. Two of these, The Kiss (1901) and The Thinker are known all over the world. Rodin’s achievement was to use traditional stone and bronze cast sculpture techniques to represent movement, and to draw attention to the physical material property of the sculpture as a block of stone. Rodin did not want to make sculpture that looked lifelike but was closer to the impressionists and cubists in his desire to acknowledge surface and material as an important part of the work for its own sake. Rodin saw the plinth as an integral part of the sculpture to be acknowledged rather than seen as an unfortunate necessity, again drawing attention to the sculpture as an object rather than something that should try to replicate reality. He often left parts of the plinth unfinished or made his figures emerge from the stone of the plinth. Although Rodin was able to carve and model the human form with great accuracy he often exaggerated proportions to enhance the impression he wanted a particular work to make, using the tricks of classical sculpture such as perspective and foreshortening. This can be seen very clearly in The Kiss, a white marble sculpture that shows a seated male and female nude entwined in a kiss. The figures are about one and a half times larger than life, their bodies are carved without excess of detail, and the feet and legs are exaggerated. The couple grow out of the stone block on which they sit which is left roughly carved. The Kiss has become a world famous representation of romantic and sexual love. Rodin made several copies and a number of smaller bronze versions. There is a similar bronze of a girl lying on a low plinth, her body at points disappearing into the plinth as though it has not yet been formed. In this work Rodin wanted to express the state of sleep. Rodin’s Thinker is an iconic work, a slightly larger than life size seated male nude with one elbow on a knee, the hand made into a fist on which the bent forward head rests. The sculpture is made in bronze, with a very dark almost black patina and the modelling is relatively rough. It grows out of a plinth about one metre high, raising the seated figure to the standing viewer’s eye level. The Thinker is a representation of existential angst, and gained its reputation by succeeding in the three dimensional representation of a state of mind. It is frequently reproduced, used in advertising or merely invoked in conversation by mimicking the physical gesture made by the figure. This quotation of Rodin’s pose is both a reference to the actual sculpture and more generally to illustrate the idea of intense thought. Walking Man is Rodin’s solution to another challenge – how to represent motion in figurative sculpture. This is a life size dark brown bronze sculpture of a naked striding man who that on the floor on a minimal plinth so that the viewer is on the same level. Rodin made public commissions, the best known of which is The Burghers of Calais, which shows an event in French history in which the civic leaders of the town of Calais were imprisoned. Rodin’s sculpture shows them chained together, standing front to back and forming a melancholy circle, which draws the viewer to it. The sculpture itself forms a cage made by the bronze circle of figures. A statue of the French writer Balzac is a radical departure from contemporary portraiture – Balzac is shown standing, wrapped in a floor length cloak so that only his head is visible. He has wild hair and a fierce expression and the figure leans back at an almost unrealistic angle. A commission for a pair of monumental bronze gates for a public building in Paris resulted in The Gates of Hell, a bronze relief that occupied Rodin for years. It is a densely modelled depiction of purgatory covered with figures, for which Rodin did a large number of sketches and studies in bronze. 

!Constantin Brancusi.

Another artist who considered the plinth to be a part of the sculpture was Brancusi, who lived and worked in Paris at the end of the 19th century. Brancusi’s works were often no more than elaborated plinth shapes, with simple forms placed on them made in plaster, bronze, wood and marble. One of his best known works is Endless Column, a polished bronze column on a wooden plinth made of truncated three dimensional diamond shapes placed one on top of the other, about 10 metres high. He also made a small carved head of a sleeping person which is so smooth and almost featureless as to almost appear to be a large stone. 

!Alberto Giacommetti.

In contrast Italian artist Giacommetti had little interest in the plinth. Giacommetti’s bronze sculptures are extremely elongated usually standing figures, sometimes single figures, sometimes pairs or groups which often imply movement, or seem to be about to walk. They are solitary and isolated figures, reduced to essences of the human form. The sculptures are not more than human height and often much smaller, the heads are tiny, the legs and arms no more than rods of metal, the bodies agglomerations of bronze that seem to serve merely to join the limbs, not much more than 30 cm wide. The sculptures are modelled roughly so that the metal looks as if it has been dripped, dragged and pinched. Giacommetti made a large number of working drawings. 

!Gaudier Henri Brzeska.

Gaudier Brzeska made stone and bronze sculptures, mostly small and medium sized, for example a small dark stone carving of a curled up sleeping fawn. For the first time artists were entirely free to make whatever they wanted, the only constraint being financial. Photography began to be seen as part of an artist’s practise, abstraction was on the horizon, and the old traditions ceased to hold sway. Several major movements carried art into the twentieth century and the period known as modernism. These are surrealism, futurism and dada. 

!!SURREALISM.

Surrealist art was made in the context of the publishing of Freud’s work on the subconscious and of the liberation of art from straightforward narrative or representation of the world. The subconscious mind offered new worlds to explore. 

!Salvador Dali.

This Spanish artist, born in 1900, is with Magritte the most famous of the surrealists and a candidate for best known artist in the world. Dali’s paintings or even details of his paintings are instantly recognisable such as the melted clocks which from a small part of one painting but which are instantly associated with Dali and surrealism. Dali’s surrealism is fantastical, he created dreamlike landscapes filled with distorted objects, nightmarish scenes and apocalyptic wastelands and religious iconography. One painting shows mutant human bones that seem to have a life of their own in a grey green no man’s land, the aftermath of a war. Another shows the crucifixion from Christ’s point of view, showing what he would have seen looking down from the cross, the whole image a luridly coloured hallucination. A landscape with dead trees has clock faces draped on the trees and on the ground apparently melting and dripping. A giant red rose floats in the air above a desert. A telephone receiver is replaced by a lobster. Where Dali’s use of obvious symbolism and his lurid hallucinatory images have made him enduringly popular, his personal eccentricity has only increased his popularity and is difficult to separate from his work. Dali dressed as a dandy, often wearing white and sporting a very long waxed moustache that extended beyond his face and pointed up. He lived with his wife Galia in a house in Spain at Figuera da Foz where the couple were host to many of their contemporaries. Dali is not considered to be the intellectual force behind surrealism, however well known his work has become. This role is given to Breton. 

!Andre Breton.

The most outspoken member and leader of the surrealists in Paris at the beginning of the twentieth century, Breton made paintings and sculpture, and wrote the Surrealist manifesto that set out to define a new kind of art for new times. His best known work is a clothes iron with spikes set into the sole plate. 

!Rene Magritte.

Magritte is the most famous of the Surrealists, and indeed is one of the best known artists in the world. A French painter born in 1850, Magritte’s images are instantly recognisable and attributable, and have been much copied. They have influenced advertising imagery for several decades and continue to do so. Magritte was interested in both visual and textual puns, for example, ‘Ceci n’est pas une pipe’ (this is not a pipe) is inscribed on a painting of a pipe. Many of his paintings feature both words and objects and often the objects are apparently named but the names do not make sense. In this way Magritte's text paintings are both object and subject in the sense that are what they are but the viewer is then told that they are not, leading them to a new consideration of what they are seeing and of what art might do. Many of Magritte's paintings are very well known: a train emerging from a fireplace; an apple obscuring most of the front of a bowler hatted man’s face; an inverted mermaid with a fish’s head and woman’s legs and pelvis; an easel by a window with a painting on it, the painting showing the view through the window; two grey spaceship like objects titled Floating Spheres; and a man’s face which is also a female torso in which the pubic hair is also the man’s beard. These are painted in a highly realistic but flat and stiff style, with a muted palette, smooth, flat and few brushstrokes. The ludicrous juxtapositions of objects and people are nonetheless poetic and leave the viewer hoping that they might actually happen, that the everyday might mix up and become magical, in other words, surreal. 

!Man Ray.

A French surrealist sculptor, painter and photographer working in Paris in the early twentieth century, Man Ray experimented with photography to create unique images, using various methods including placing objects on light sensitive plates and then exposing them to get an image. Man Ray took a well known photograph of Duchamp with a star shaved out of the back of his head smoking a pipe. 

!Meret Oppenheim.

Oppenheim made a sculpture of a fur covered cup, saucer and spoon, as well as one of a pair of white high heeled shoes upside down on a silver salver trussed with string and paper frills on the ends of the heels. She also made a pair of leather gloves, the conventional stitched ridges on the backs of each made to resemble the veins on the back of a hand. 

!Hans Bellmer.

Bellmer made photographs of dolls and parts of dolls arranged in tableaux, as well as sculptures. His work deals with sexual taboo. 

!Lazlo Moholy Nagy.

This Hungarian artist was a contemporary of the surrealists and is best known for a number of pioneering semi abstract films and photographs using architectural form, light and shade. Surrealism provided the context for an avant garde group of artists, performers and writers to produce work that they collectively titled Dada. 

!!DADA.

The dada artists were based in Berlin but drawn from a number of different nationalities. They worked in all media and also made performance and musical work, performing regularly under the name Cabaret Voltaire. The name comes is a combination of a French word for hobby horse but was intended to have no specific meaning. 

!Marcel Duchamp.

Known in the twentieth century as the father of conceptualism, the French artist Marcel Duchamp was among the artists escaping Europe for America to avoid censorship and persecution as Jews. He founded Dada and became notorious for his controversial and usually sculptural works, in particular the ones that became known as ready mades. The notorious Fountain, exhibited at the Armoury show in New York in 1913 was in fact a gents’ ceramic urinal placed on its sided so that it could be said to have the shape of a Buddha or a wall fountain. Duchamp signed the urinal on the front, R. Mutt, (one of the invented punning names that Duchamp used to sign his work). Fountain created a furore because it was a bought object, to which nothing had been added, that Duchamp claimed to make into a work of art merely by the act of signing it and proclaiming authorship. The radical step taken by Duchamp with his ready mades, a cycle wheel attached to the seat of a stool for example or a metal bottle dryer, was to have far reaching implications for art. Duchamp employed pseudonyms and puns as part of his work. He created a female alter ego, Rrose Selavy (a pun on ‘C’est la vie’), who authenticated and signed many of his works including the urinal. He photographed himself dressed as Rrose, a rather unconvincing manifestation. Rrose produced a perfume named La Belle Haleine, literally ‘beautiful breath’, intended as an allusion to its opposite, bad breath, and a pun on ‘La belle Helene’, Helen of troy. Inframince or infrathin was a concept involving the declaration of an invisible or intangible and banal act or action to be a work of art. For example the static friction and the sound of velvet trouser legs rubbing together and the warmth left on a recently vacated seat. Almost as notorious as Fountain was Duchamp’s defaced reproduction of the Mona Lisa. Making a pun on the letters LHGC which appear below the face of the Mona Lisa he wrote that ‘elle a un grand cou’ which translates as ‘she has a fat arse’ and drew a moustache on her face. This re appropriated image became one of the best known images in twentieth century art. Duchamp made a number of bronze and wax sculptures which were casts of male and female sexual parts, taking, for example, a mould from female genitals which gave him a negative from which he cast in bronze rather than make a positive from it. One of Duchamp's last works was L’Origin du Monde, a large peepshow containing a wax female torso, laid on an apparent mossy bank, the head out of view, with the genitals exposed. There is a drawing by Courbet of a nude in a similar pose. The apparently innocent tableau with its celebratory title became due to the voyeuristic way in which the viewer was required to look at it tainted with an atmosphere of illicit pleasure, abuse or even murder. The Large Glass, also known as The Bride Stripped Bare by her Bachelors Even, is a large sculpture comprising a screen print onto two pieces of glass held in a metal freestanding frame which was made in an edition. This is Duchamp’s least understood work and it has always resisted interpretation. The images printed on the glass are geometric rays, lines and a coffee grinder with three buckets that was a motif Duchamp repeatedly returned to and had made several drawings of. The buckets on the grinder are sometimes supposed to represent the bachelors, but in general the meaning of the work remains opaque. Duchamp also made several paintings of the coffee grinder that appears in the Large Glass. Duchamp was photographed by Man Ray with a star shaved out of the back of his close cut hair. He played chess every day of his life. 

!Kurt Schwitters.

Schwitters, a leading Dada artist and poet, invented the concept of Merz, a way of working and making art that he applied to everything he did. The most significant manifestation of this is the Merzbau, the interior of a house of which he transformed in its entirety by constructing organic and crystalline protrusions and projections from timber, wire, plaster and papier maché. The rooms became like white caves or cubist grottos. Schwitters also made the (unfinished) Merzbarn during the time he spent in the Lake District in England, a small stone cottage that he transformed with cement and plaster reliefs. Schwitters began his career as a graphic designer and typographer and produced innovative work using unusual combinations of typefaces. He developed an interest in type as a visual form and much of his later work incorporates type in the form of cut out and collaged letters and drawn text. Schwitters wrote poetry, which he performed at Dada performances and at Cabaret Voltaire. His tone poems consisted mainly of sound rather than words. The best known is titled Caravane, which Schwitters intoned through a megaphone. He regarded his poems as aural images. Schweitzer’s small collages are made from coloured paper, printed paper, cut out printed letters, found images, drawing, paint and other materials. He made a great number of these, mostly postcard sized which just predate Braque’s and Gris’s use of collaged text as part of an image. Schwitters also made three dimensional objects, reliefs and assemblages from found materials, timber, wire and paint. 

!Johannes Baader.

Baader was a German artist involved in Dada and Cabaret Voltaire performances. Baader also worked on his own and carried out a number of extreme public performances, often involving physical risk to himself. He made a lot of pamphlets and distributed them in mass public leafleting actions for which he was frequently arrested. 

!Hugo Ball.

Ball was a leading Dada performance artist and a prominent member of Cabaret Voltaire, instigating many of the collaborative performances and writing many of the poems and scripts for them. 

!Luigi Russolo.

The Art of Noise, Russolo’s orchestra, played at many Cabaret Voltaire performances. Russolo was an experimental musician and composer; there were no conventional instruments in his orchestra, which consisted of household appliances and especially made instruments often producing a deliberate cacophony. Italy produced an avant garde at the turn of the century which bears comparison with Dada but had entirely different stated aims. There had been no Italian surrealism but de Chirico’s painting presented a reflexive view of Italian culture and civic life. 

!Giorgio De Chirico.

The towns and architecture depicted in de Chirico’s work are very distinctive for their deserted atmosphere, as if time stands still and the buildings cast long shadows in a strong sunny light. One painting of an empty town square has a statue of an unspecified man, a tower and an arched arcade or cloister. The shadows cast by the buildings and statue on the yellow ochre ground and under the arcade are black. Another painting has the fallen head of a classical marble statue, very like Michelangelo’s David, seen lying on its side on the ground, with a broken off hand in the background, offering a critique of Italy’s reliance on the culture of its past rather than of the present. 

!!FUTURISM.

In this environment of stultification and Mussolini’s dictatorship, the Futurists, who were based in Milan, wrote a manifesto declaring that speed was their god and that the Future was the only thing worth making art about. They were reflecting the times, in a period when manufacturing design was changing rapidly, people and news could travel fast, and moving pictures were available to entertain everyone. 

!Frederico Marinetti.

The leading member of the Italian Futurist movement, Marinetti was an eccentric didactic who produced many paintings and published texts include several versions of the Futurist Manifesto. He wrote a Futurist Cookbook, in which he declared pasta to be the most important (and futurist) of all food. The book contains many edible recipes including several for pasta, but also some very inedible and often ironic ones involving rose petals and entirely green banquets, for example, with complicated presentation methods. It offers a glimpse into the surprising romanticism of the futuristic movement. Marinetti made several performance works including one in which a set of dining chairs were made to dance on and off the stage. 

!Giacomo Balla.

Balla pursued an obsession with speed in his paintings, trying to find the ultimate way to depict people, animals and objects in motion. A portrait of a dachshund walking with spoke like lines emanating from each of its legs is one example. 

!Umberto Boccioni.

Boccioni is considered to be the best futurist painter and sculptor, much of his work dealing with how to represent speed in two or three dimensions. A bronze sculpture about one metre high called simply ‘Speed’ depicts a striding man, with apparently aerodynamic fin like structures representing trails of movement leading backwards from his limbs and the back of his head. The streamlined helmet shape of his head and the armour like fins give the sculpture a sinister warrior like quality. Boccioni made a head of Mussolini by drawing a profile copied from a photograph and using this as a guide to a piece of wood. The result is a solid object which has the shape of a vase but the unmistakeable profile of a man from whichever angle it is regarded. He also made many paintings of his native Milan usually with lines radiating to represent movement of people and vehicles. 

!Frances Picabia.

Picabia’s work did not follow Futurist prescription but he did have an interest in technological development. Picabia’s work does not fit into any movement but he was very influential and is best known for paintings that depict human machine like figures, abstract paintings and his use of text. Picabia also worked as a graphic designer and designed typefaces. 

!Tamara de Lempicka.

In France, Lempicka painted the newly liberated women of the time. Lempicka appears in her own work, distinctive for her straight bobbed hair with low fringe and fashionable clothes. In the early twentieth century many artists made work that depicted a futuristic world, either utopian or dystopian. Some reflected on the increasingly urban, industrialised way of life. 

!Fernand Léger.

Léger, a French painter, had a distinctive style, painting figures as if their bodies were composed of tubular elements, in shaded greys on a whiter ground with occasional additions of primary colours. Léger’s figures look like metal puppets or robots. Other artists concentrated on the traditional forms of portraiture and still life. 

!Ernesto Modigliani.

The name Modigliani is always associated with a certain type of portrait, typically of a seated woman with an elongated body and stylised face. Modigliani's portraits are contemplative and painted in a sombre palette with black outlines. 

!Giorgio Morandi.

Most of Morandi’s still life paintings depict the simplified forms of domestic objects such as jugs, bottles and vases on plain coloured grounds. 

!Nicholas De Stael.

De Stael also painted simple still lifes using flat bright colours. 

!!EARLY PHOTOGRAPHY.

The work of American photographers in the first few decades of the twentieth century was very influential. They recorded both the social conditions of the depression and the opening up of the vast landscapes of the west. Among them were Edward Weston and William Egglestone who photographed city life. 

!Walker Evans.

Evans took photographs of rural America during the thirties Depression that showed the extreme poverty and suffering of ordinary people. 

!Ansel Adams.

Adams was a pioneer of landscape photography known chiefly for his images of American national parks and wildernesses, such as Yosemite and Yellowstone. When he made these photographs the wildernesses were not well explored and many parts were remote and inaccessible, necessitating many days trekking with equipment to get the images he wanted. The photographs capture the enormity and grandiosity of the landscape and came to represent the American wilderness to the rest of the world. Because of their huge scope and precise depth of field they seem to be photographic records of time passing, although of course they only record a moment in time. An example is a photograph of the central valley in Yosemite which shows a huge waterfall cascading down the opposite side of the valley. In Europe, photographers like Eugene Atget and Bill Brandt were recording events, and Brassai was beginning to make abstract images. 

!Andresz Kertesz.

The Eastern European photographer Kertesz made images that became instantly familiar, including one of the Arc de Triomphe in Paris. Seen from a bird’s eye view there is snow on the road surface and the tracks of the few vehicles on the streets have inscribed black parabolas around the arch on the white ground. 

!Claude Cahun.

Cahun was a French photographer who made a number of self portraits in which she is dressed as a man without disguising her sexual identity. 

!!THE MOVING IMAGE.

Artists were trying to represent movement in different ways. The Futurists wanted to represent speed and Rodin to create a sculpture of the human form that conveyed movement. The invention of moving pictures was less of an early influence on them than the work of one photographer. 

!Eadweard Muybridge.

At the turn of the century, Muybridge, an American photographer, made a series of photographs of a horse and a man in motion. He took a series of exposures at close intervals, producing an image for every second of movement. The resulting images had a great influence on artists. Muybridge's sequential exposures showed a specific action, for example the components of the movement of a naked walking man and of a galloping horse. In the days before moving pictures, no one had foreseen that movement could be represented photographically, reduced to a sequence of still images, nor had they been able to study movement. Design and architecture were changing rapidly in Europe and America as a new style emerged, known as International Modernism. This was a sleek, unadorned way of building championed by Le Corbusier among others. Modernist architects were, like the futurists, enthusiastic about the future, proclaiming that in the future everybody would have a home of their own, a modular unit or machine for living in. Design schools were established to train a new breed of architects and designers. The Bauhaus and De Stijl are the best known. 

!!THE BAUHAUS.

The Bauhaus was in Berlin and was a multi disciplinary school with a distinct ethos. Students studied across all areas, including theatre design, textiles, ceramics and graphics, as well as architecture and industrial design. Some Bauhaus students went on to become artists. 

!Oskar Schlemmer.

Schlemmer studied at the Bauhaus as a theatre designer and he made a number of sculptural designs for the Bauhaus productions, later becoming an artist. De Stijl was the Dutch design school at which Gerrit Reitveld, the designer of the famous Red Blue Yellow chair, studied. The influence of the painter Mondrian and Russian constructivism can clearly be seen in De Stijl design. 

!Piet Mondrian.

Mondrian was a Dutch artist who began by painting his local countryside, trees and canals before abstracting these scenes and simplifying his palette. Mondrian also experimented with canvas shape, making a series of abstract works on oval canvases that were based on trees in a snowy landscape. These were conveyed as geometric grey and brown marks on a white ground and Cézanne was influenced by them. Mondrian made a series of works on small square canvases painted on a white ground, dividing the canvas into irregular blocks with black horizontal and vertical lines and colouring in some of the resulting squares and rectangles with red, yellow and blue. These works are amongst the most famous abstract paintings in the world. Later Mondrian began to paint patterns using smaller units and areas, for example Who’s Afraid of Red, Yellow and Blue? in 1910, and one titled Subway, which are an homage to the architecture and the new jazz scene of New York. Mondrian's abstract geometric compositions owe something to contemporary Russian constructivists but are significant for their abstraction of landscape into pure geometric form. 

!!CONSTRUCTIVISM.

In Russia, artists began to make paintings and sculpture that were based on architectural form. This was a reaction to the prevailing socialist realist art sponsored by the state, which often amounted to little more than Soviet propaganda. Art served the state by representing rural agricultural life as idyllic but productive, and portrayed the worker as the hero of the day. Artists rebelled by finding a way of working that was entirely different, and most importantly, abstract. Vladimir Tatlin made his spiral staircase monument. Constructivism used geometry, sharp edged straight lines and primary colours, and the artists laid down the principles on which works could be made. It had a great influence on contemporary graphic design and typography, and continued to do so throughout the twentieth century. Constructivism was later taken up by Europeans such as the French painter Victor Vasarely. 

!!SUPREMATISM.



!Kasimir Malevich.

A Russian artist called Malevich developed a form of constructivism that he named suprematism and made primary coloured paintings on white grounds. He painted the famous Black Square in 1910. This work was the logical if daring conclusion of his systematic attention to constructivist principles and is often attributed with marking the beginning of abstraction. It is also a stark political statement that there is nothing left to paint. Black square is simply a square canvas painted black all over. Square canvases were very unusual since they did not lend themselves well to the composition of traditional subjects of painting, landscapes and portraits. The square drew attention to the work itself, not the subject of the work, so that the painting became an object about painting rather than a depiction of something became important. Mondrian soon began to use the square format. Malevich's painting was entirely black which drew attention to the only possible details of his work, the colour of the black and the brushstrokes. The viewer was directed to regard only the physicality of the paint in this work. This may have been more than Malevich intended. 

!Wassily Kandinsky.

Another Russian artist, Kandinsky, is also credited with making some of the first abstract paintings. Whereas Malevich had made a minimal painting, Kandinsky used bright colours and black on a white ground to make expressive gestural flowing marks, giving rise to the term abstract expressionism. He was interested in the relation of music to sound and some of his paintings are attempts to ‘paint’ music. He painted some stage backdrops for experimental orchestral works. 

!!GERMAN EXPRESSIONISM.



!Ernst Ludwig Kirchner.

Kirchner was an early German expressionist painter. His paintings are brightly coloured, with angular stylised figures, and lots of brushstrokes. Kirchner was the founder of an artists’ group called die Brucke (The Bridge), which included Kandinsky among its members. Die Brucke published a magazine of critical texts called Die Blaue Reiter (The Blue Rider). The First World War had a profound effect on artists. Oscar Kokoschka, Otto Mueller and others registered their horror at what had happened in their work. 

!Max Beckman.

Beckman was a German artist who painted in an expressionist style, choosing to portray the people and social spaces of the day in Berlin. A self portrait shows him full length, standing glaring at the viewer while wearing a large black overcoat and a hat with a wide brim. Beckman used a lot of black to outline his figures. 

!Otto Dix.

Dix painted the First World War atrocities, including limbless and horrifically injured soldiers, the best known showing two veterans sitting at a café table, one of whom has had his leg blown off, the other has an eyeball hanging out. 

!George Grosz.

Grosz painted the decadence of the Weimar republic at the beginning of the twentieth century and the aftermath of war. 

!Paul Nash.

One of the few British artists who made paintings about the war, Nash showed barren landscapes of the trenches and no mans’ land in a slightly surrealist light, favouring greens and browns and populating his landscapes with skeletal shapes to create an atmosphere of death. 

!Egon Schiele.

Although Schiele did not directly paint about the war, his paintings are uneasy and cheerless portraits that were in tune with the times. The young women and men in Schiele’s work are usually emaciated and have hollow faces and large eyes. His portraits usually show the whole body, sometimes more than one figure, usually naked or semi clothed on pale backgrounds and they are painted with rough brushstrokes and a palette of white with brown, grey and black. Throughout the early twentieth century there were plenty of artists who ignored political events and continued to make art that represented romantic, spiritual or domestic subjects. 

!Balthus.

Balthus is renowned for his domestic interiors and the dreamlike quality of his paintings of young women and girls. He used a restrained palette and the features of his female sitters are flattened and idealised. Balthus continued to paint until he was a very old man. 

!Marc Chagall.

Chagall is a French painter who also designed stained glass. He is usually associated with images of lovers and animals who are sometimes flying. His narrative paintings are executed in a faux naïve style, with soft colours and a sentimental quality. Chagall often painted images of love and marriage, for example one painting shows a bride and groom resting on a giant hen in the sky, with symbols of the artist’s Jewish faith in the background. As well as hens and roosters, Chagall liked to paint horses, for example one painting features a white horse with large green eyes and long eyelashes. Chagall was commissioned to design the stained glass windows for a chapel designed by le Corbusier in the south of France. 

!Raoul Dufy.

Known for his idyllic seaside scenes, Dufy used bright colours and quick gestural brushstrokes. He was commissioned to paint a mural for the head quarters of the national French electricity generating company celebrating the benefits of electric power. He made a large curved panorama showing people going about varying activities in the countryside, the city and at the seaside with electricity pylons linking the different scenes. 

!Robert Delaunay.

Delaunay made semi abstract paintings using bright, luminous colours, usually in small squares. He was married to Sonia Delaunay. 

!Sonia Delaunay.

As well as making light bright coloured semi abstract paintings, Delaunay is known for her designs for woven and printed textiles. 

!Jean Arp.

Arp was a Dutch artist who worked in mixed media and painting, making a lot of wall hung reliefs and constructions using primary colours and white, and simple shapes, usually curved. Arp cut out simple shapes from sheets of timber and glued them to a board, adding other elements and painting the whole thing. 

!Kees Van Dongen.

Van Dongen made semi abstract colourful paintings, applying the paint quite thickly. Nolde and Munch made a new kind of expressionistic landscape painting. 

!Emile Nolde.

The Belgian artist Nolde's expressionistic landscapes and portraits are characterised by very bright colours and impasto. 

!Edvard Munch.

One of the most famous modern paintings, The Scream, was painted by the Norwegian artist Munch. This iconic painting has often been used in advertising and has been stolen several times. Munch had a very distinctive painting style, which gives the appearance of a woodcut at times. He outlined his figures, often painting the background to follow the contours of the person in the painting to add to the outlining. He used strong and often acidic colours in later works, but many of his earlier landscapes use soft colours and monochrome. In The Scream, Munch wanted to suggest both the sound of the scream and the emotion that engendered it. The figure of a screaming person dressed in black, with shocked eyes and hands to open mouth, is seen on a jetty, surrounded by concentric lines of bright colours that seem to represent the sound of the scream. Munch's subjects were either the rural Norwegian landscape or people. There is often a dreamlike quality to his work, as if nothing were quite real; the sun is often a strange colour, shadows are distorted, and elements are coloured unnaturally. The people in Munch’s work always seem to be or to feel that they are alone. A painting of a lake has a setting sun, the reflection of which is a thickly applied vertical yellow stripe on the water. In a lithograph called The Singer a woman dressed in black stands in a room with a piano. The only bright colour in the painting is in the woman’s red hair. At the time The Scream was painted Freud’s theories of hysteria and the unconscious were just becoming known and also influenced the work of the surrealists. Some artists experimented more literally with the subconscious mind. 

!Henri Michaux.

Michaux was a French artist who made small obsessive drawings and written works, dense with scribbling, extremely intricate patterns and landscapes and letters to anonymous recipients written in the smallest of hands, sometimes blanked out after writing. Michaux mainly used pen and ink, often brown ink, and made his drawings under the influence of mescaline, which Michaux believed allowed him fuller access to his subconscious. 

!MC Escher.

The German artist Escher made extremely detailed and precise drawings of fantasy interiors and architecture, using impossible perspective or optical tricks thereby making something appear to change from convex to concave continuously. His work has been frequently reproduced, for example an interior with a staircase descending around four walls that never goes anywhere, a pattern of black and white doves that becomes something else, and a hand drawing itself which becomes a hand drawing itself. The endgame of Esher’s work is characteristic, everything appears to return to itself. Some artists made work in a naïve or faux naïve style. 

!Paul Klee.

The French painter Klee made a large number of small paintings and an enormous range of works on paper, using both drawing and printmaking. Klee habitually used thin drawn lines to make geometric patterns, often repeated triangles or squares with dots, which were coloured and often resemble patchwork. Sometimes these remained as abstract patterns, at other times Klee would draw figures, trees, buildings or animals. 

!Jan Dubuffet.

Dubuffet used sand to roughen the texture of his paint. He painted large simple figures and faces which owed something to African art but also to graffiti. 

!Auguste Maillol.

Maillol's bronze and stone sculptures have solid and rounded forms, as if they cannot escape from the block from which they are carved. The bodies are distinctively simply formed, and even the smaller works have a gentle monumental quality. Artists’ groups were sometimes centred on a particular location. The St Ives group is named after a town in the southwest of England to which they migrated to avoid the Blitz and because the quality of light was very good for painting. Many of them stayed. 

!!THE ST IVES GROUP.



!Barbara Hepworth.

A British sculptor who began working in the 1940s, Hepworth made the work for which she is best known in the artist colony of St Ives in Cornwall, south west England, where she spent most of her working life, along with Ben Nicholson and others. She was also a contemporary of Henry Moore. Hepworth worked in stone, wood and metal, as well as making working drawings. Her sculptures were smooth, often oval forms, which sometimes have holes (or voids) through them. The voids might be spanned by string or wire strung across in parallel lines or fanning out like spokes, as if to create some kind of tension in the object. Hepworth favoured highly polished black or white marble, granite, wood or bronze. The sculptures ranged from small table sized ones, for example three perfectly smooth elliptical forms on a shallow plinth all made of white marble, or an elongated bronze shape standing on its end, to medium sized plinth based works such as the stringed elliptical voids, to large outdoor sculptures that sat on the ground, on stone boulders, or bronze plinths. Hepworth made several large scale public commissions and her work can be seen in London on the façade of John Lewis, a London department store, in various public parks, for example on Hampstead Heath and in Battersea Park, and towering over the piazza of the United Nations building in New York. In most cases these works feature an oval shape often with strings, either in stone or bronze. For the UN building she made a large oval flat shape with a small hole through it, reminiscent of a stone standing on its end, which is placed next to an ornamental water feature. The standing stones and other Stone Age monuments and monoliths that are common in Cornwall were part of her inspiration and Hepworth wanted her sculptures to sit in the landscape in the same way. 

!Ben Nicholson.

Nicholson was a leading member of the St Ives group and a contemporary of Barbara Hepworth who pioneered a style of work that is a cross between constructivism and geometric abstraction. His paintings often have raised areas made by shapes cut from plywood. He used white and grey and added lines and smaller areas of brighter colours. He also made representational works, most of which feature the Cornish land and seascapes. 

!Winifred Nicholson.

St Ives painter. 

!Terry Frost.

A younger British artist who was a member of the St Ives group in the 1950s and 60s. Frost made abstract pattern paintings, using irregular squashed circular shapes in a repeated pattern, in black and white with red, dark green, blue and other colours. Sometimes the paintings would consist of a part of a large solid black circle on one side of the canvas, and a part of a red circle on the opposite side. The clean lines, minimal forms, bright colours and black and white of Frost’s paintings were very in tune with contemporary fashion and design. 

!Naum Gabo.

An eastern European artist who lived and worked in Britain, Gabo made sculptures and maquettes for proposed sculptures from wood, metal, acrylic, wire and thread. Gabo’s formal abstract structures have a strong architectural element in their reliance on apparent tension and cantilevering for their structural strength. They are clearly influenced by Russian constructivism and Suprematism, but they also relate to the sculptural forms of Barbara Hepworth, in which voids and solid form are often given tension by thread or wires stretched across in parallel lines. Most of his work is very small, around 40 cm high. Gabo’s use of very new materials and his architectural forms made him unique in his time, and he developed a body of work that is singular in its devotion to an aesthetic and to material. He also made preparatory drawings and sketches. 

!Peter Lanyon.

A St Ives painter who painted semi abstract landscapes using a lot of green, brown and white. 

!Alfred Wallis.

Walllis painted the harbours, fishing boats and the sea around St Ives, often using an eccentric perspective that combined aerial and frontal views. 

!!THE COBRA GROUP.

This was a group of artists working in Holland who made semi abstract paintings during the 1940s and 50s. 

!Karel Appel.

Appel was the leader of the CoBRA group. His paintings of figures with smiling toothy faces are made with bright colours and lots of impasto. 

!!ABSTRACTION.

Malevich and Kandinsky had given the fullest expression to early abstraction. By the 1940s abstraction was developing fast both in Europe and most particularly in America. Many painters who had begun their careers painting figuratively turned to abstraction, de Kooning being one example. Some made works that were minimal in style, others worked towards abstract expressionism. 

!Willem de Kooning.

An American artist who worked from the 1950s to the 1990s, de Kooning made paintings in a great variety of styles, from figurative to abstract. He is best known for two periods of his work, the first made in the 1950s and consisting of a series of small portraits of women grinning with lots of teeth, painted in an almost impossibly thick and encrusted impasto, the surface of the canvas heavy with blobs of multi coloured paint that project from its surface producing an overall brown colour in combination. Like Rothko, de Kooning did not subscribe to the notion of the artist as knowing subject but to the Kantian idea of pure art. De Kooning’s second well known group of works are the controversial late paintings, made after the artist was diagnosed with Alzheimer’s disease. These are very large paintings made with thin bright primary colours of paint applied in single brush strokes that barely cover the white primed surface of the canvas giving the impression of a white painting roughly covered in gestural marks. Some critics considered them to be an extraordinary late flowering, others that they were inconsequential in de Kooning's oeuvre due to his mental state. De Kooning is vilified by American artist Paul McCarthy in a film in which McCarthy appears dressed as a paint tube, squirting paint everywhere and chanting ‘de Kooning, de Kooning’ in an infantile way. 

!Josef Albers.

An American painter born around 1900, Albers made geometric abstractions usually based on the square, using dull versions of primary colours. He was interested in the optical effects of colours on each other. Albers was a lifelong teacher of art and as is discussed in Eva Hesse’s posthumously published diary as her professor. 

!Yves Tanguy.

Tanguy was a French artist who made semi abstract paintings showing organic sculptural forms. Tanguy also made kinetic sculptures (the best known of which is in Paris outside the Centre Pompidou) consisting of similar forms that were constructed so that they would be animated by cause and effect, such as wind moving a vane, or water flow. Every movement of the sculpture creates sounds. 

!Ad Reinhardt.

An American artist who made minimal abstract paintings, mostly in grey and black, from the 1960s to 80s. 

!Clyfford Still.

Still’s abstract paintings often have large areas of flat colour to which he adds a contrasting area, the rough edge of which encroaches on the predominant colour. 

!Antoni Tapies.

A Spanish painter who made abstract paintings that resemble doors and walls. Tapies used textural materials and built up the surface of his canvas to create paintings that are almost reliefs, in predominantly natural colours such as dark brown, black and white. 

!Robert Ryman.

Ryman is known as the white on white painter; he only used white paint (although he often used a bright coloured ground). Ryman’s paintings are mostly small, they are neatly made but with quite thick paint and lots of blobs and brushstrokes. 

!Robert Motherwell.

An American abstract expressionist painter who made very big paintings often using a lot of black paint applied with large brushes and leaving other areas as bare canvas. 

!Leon Golub.

American artist born in the 1930s who made abstract paintings. 

!Elizabeth Murray.

An American artist who made wall reliefs and constructions that she called three dimensional paintings. 

!Victor Pasmore.

British artist who made abstract paintings and works on paper. 

!Eileen Agar.

A British artist who made sculptures and reliefs, both abstract and symbolist, as well as some small objects. 

!Mel Bochner.

Bochner’s characteristic works are dull coloured semi abstract cityscapes in a geometric hard edged style. 

!Morris Louis.

Louis is an American artist who is known for his abstract pour paintings made in the 1960s and 70s. Louis developed a technique for pouring paint onto raw canvas and allowing the paint to pool and spread, soaking into the canvas yet allowing the colours to remain distinct from one another. The resulting works usually consist of brightly coloured stripes emanating from one side of the canvas, spreading and blending on the edges, with the texture of the surface of the canvas always visible. 

!Frank Stella.

An American artist who made wall drawings and reliefs, which consisted of painted cut outs arranged as an abstract assemblage on a panel fixed to the wall. 

!Lucio Fontana.

The Italian artist Fontana’s paintings from the 1950s and 60s are abstract, plain and flat coloured canvases with slits cut right through the canvas. Sometimes Fontana added some textural detail, such as painted gravel or wool thread. He used a range of colours from dark reds and blues to bright copper green. Sometimes his paintings are made on egg shaped canvases. 

!Mary Heilman.

An abstract painter born in the 1940s, Heilman makes small paintings of carefully considered abstract shapes, lines and forms exploring endless possibilities of form and colour. 

!Al Held.

Twentieth century American painter. 

!Howard Hodgkin.

Hodgkin is a British painter born in the 1940s who makes abstract paintings. He uses very bright jewel like colours, applied in large, often round, repeated splodges, the paint continuing right off the edge of the canvas onto and round the frame. Hodgkin’s paintings are never larger than 2 metres high. He sometimes uses round and oval shaped canvases. Hodgkin has made work for public commission including a large photomural of brushstrokes that fills the circular facade of an Imax cinema in London. 

!Bridget Riley.

Riley is famous for her Op Art black and white paintings made in the 1960s, work which endures as one of the most recognisable images of the 1960s.
