She made these paintings by drawing and distorting grids and filling them in with black and white. The distortions create illusions of curves, of shimmering and of motion making the paintings hard to look at. Riley used grids of squares, triangles, as well as dots and ellipses and parallel lines. One painting, one metre high, has a grid of squares that gradually decrease in width as they move toward the centre of the painting, creating the illusion of two vertical chequered cylinders. Another painting, one and a half metres by two metres, has parallel horizontal wavy lines, each line fractionally further to one side of the painting than the next, creating an optical shimmer. A large, two metre square painting has black elliptical dots placed about 30 cm apart on a grid, the angle of each ellipse changing gradually, giving an impression of motion. A triangle pattern creates waves as each of the triangles on the square canvas is progressively tilted along a curved line. Riley made a large number of black and white works before beginning to add one or two colours, for example yellow, red and blue, to the black and white paintings. A horizontal wavy line painting has colour added. In the 1980s Riley began to explore the optical colour blending effects she had created, and made the first of a large number of pure colour works. She ceased to use black and white and has never returned to it. She continued to study the work of Cézanne and to refer to his paintings in her geometric abstractions, many of which draw directly on historical painting. Works from this period are mostly a grid of multicoloured parallelograms, the choice of colour influenced by the source image. These images predate but later seemed to anticipate pixellation. Riley has made many public commissions including applying bands of coloured stripes to the corridors on several floors of a London hospital. During the late 1990s and early 2000s Riley continued to use colour but began to draw much larger shapes. The shapes in her paintings remain hard edged but she ceased to use a grid. The tendencies evident in early twentieth century abstract painting developed into two distinct but complementary movements, abstract expressionism and minimalism. 

!!ABSTRACT EXPRESSIONISM.

The critic Clement Greenberg argued for Abstract Expressionism as the only logical way forward for painting. He supported many of its best exponents, including Pollock. 

!Jackson Pollock.

The best known of the American abstract expressionist painters and arguably the best known modernist painter of all time. Pollock was born in the 1920s and died in the early 70s in a car accident. Pollock began working in the 1950s and at the end of the decade he started to experiment, placing the canvas on the floor and using a brush to pour house paint, not touching the canvas at all. This was a radical step, since no one had ever made a painting without touching the canvas and with so little control over the result. Pollock’s drip paintings caused a sensation. His new method became known as action painting. The photographer Hans Namuth made a seminal film of Pollock showing him pouring paint on the canvas, which included a section filmed from below through glass. The action paintings are up to 2 metres high and very wide and are all composed of an all over ‘pattern’ of drips, applied in a dynamic but highly controlled way, so that a design is evident. Far from relinquishing control, Pollock became master of the technique. The combinations of colours are subtle, usually involving black and white with one or two other colours. One of the best known is Blue Poles, which has two vertical blue stripes amidst the swirling drips. Another, titled One, is a largely black and white composition. Pollock made many public commissions and his work was taken up by the American government as representing the pinnacle of modernist achievement in art. His work was seen as symbolising the freedom of the American way of life. Unwittingly Pollock’s work was used as US propaganda against the Soviet Union, when the CIA covertly funded an international touring exhibition of the work of the abstract expressionists designed to be compared directly and unfavourably with soviet socialist realist art. Pollock was married to Lee Krasner, also an abstract painter. Krasner saw Pollock through many years of increasing alcoholism and infidelity, taking him to live and work in a house on Long Island to help him stop drinking, until she felt compelled to leave him although she continued to care for him. Krasner subjugated her own career to Pollock’s and it seems reasonable to suppose that he was unlikely to have become successful, or to have even made the action paintings, without her support. 

!Susan Rothenstein.

American abstract expressionist painter. 

!Franz Kline.

American Kline’s enormous abstract expressionist paintings usually feature large black gestural marks made with a big brush on a white, or even un primed ground. Much of the ground is left untouched but it becomes an active part of the surface of the painting. 

!Lee Krasner.

American figurative painter who turned to abstraction. She established a foundation in her own and her husband Jackson Pollock’s names, which through its grant making activities contributes to the careers of many artists. 

!Helen Frankenthaler.

American abstract expressionist painter. 

!Barnett Newman.

American painter Newman’s minimal paintings are known for ‘the zip’, a line of a contrasting colour running vertically down the canvas. Newman invariably used one or two solid colours in blocks or bands, often dark blue, black and maroon, but also golden ochre colours and aquamarine, and the zip (red on a dark brown ground for example) animated these, setting up an optical resonance. He spent his whole career painting these and like Rothko was held up as a spiritual painter. One group of paintings on a single theme fills a whole room; they are white on white paintings with elements of black. 

!Mark Rothko.

Rothko is an American painter who is one of the most famous exponents of abstract expressionism. Rothko’s works are simple colour fields, predominantly one or two shades of the same colour or two contrasting colours, often arranged as a wide border with a central section and usually in portrait format. Although the colour is solid, it is roughly painted with an uneven edge so that the colours affect each other optically. Rothko’s paintings are quite large, up to 3 metres high and he sometimes made four works to be shown together. One such installation is a dark red group of paintings consisting of blurred rectangles within borders. This installation provokes a spiritual or contemplative response in many viewers, some of whom say they are moved to tears. These works are often used as exemplification that art can provide a transcendental experience. Rothko referred to his paintings as parts of himself, describing them as skins hung on the wall. 

!!MINIMALISM.

In the late 1950s and early 60s minimalism was an emerging trend in both painting and sculpture. Minimalist sculpture was groundbreaking in it refusal of any form of modelling, and in its use of prefabricated materials. 

!Robert Morris.

An American sculptor who was known for his use of fabricated steel and other metals. Morris made abstract minimal sculptures, which were nearly always cube shapes, about one and a half meters high, open at the top. His series of copper plated boxes shown as a group are particularly well known, as well as a similar series made of mirrored glass. The copper boxes emit a glowing pink reflected light from their interiors. These are contradictory works in terms of their representation of a heavy solid mass by means of emptiness and light. Morris also made sculpture with pieces of heavyweight felt that he scattered on the floor. He collaborated in a live performance with Carolee Schneeman in the 1970s titled Olympia. 

!Richard Serra.

An American artist who is well known for his monumental sculpture in steel, Titled Arc, which was installed in a public plaza in the 1980s. Tilted Arc was a curving steel wall that bisected the plaza and was tilted at a steep angle along its length. It became notorious for the degree of public resistance to it (the sculpture created a detour making the walk across the square longer), and for the fact that a worker was killed when a part of the sculpture fell on him during installation. Serrra’s work consisted of mostly welded steel sculpture, but not always on such a scale. An early video work, Hand Catching Lead, shows the sculptor’s hand trying repeatedly to catch a piece of falling lead, a difficult task which is rarely successful. 

!Donald Judd.

American Donald Judd is one of the most important of the minimal sculptors. He made steel boxes, shelf like structures and repeated units, fabricated with a high attention to detail and immaculately spray painted in a variety of colours. Some sculptures are made of polished metal such as brass, so that they are extremely shiny. The structures and units are ambiguous, suggesting function but obviously non functional. They are either mounted on the wall, in a horizontal line or one above the other, sometimes in ascending sizes, or placed with precision on the floor. The I Beam is one of Judd’s favourite materials; he pioneered the use of pre fabricated steel and modular construction in his sculptures creating simple works that often had a monumental quality. Judd set up a studio and a foundation in Marfa, Texas. 

!Carl Andre.

Andre is another of the leading artists in the generation of American minimalist sculptors that includes Judd and Morris. He is notorious for his ‘pile of bricks’, purchased by the Tate gallery in London in 1980, which was derided in the press for being nothing but a pile of ordinary house bricks. This work, an arrangement of house bricks to form a plinth two bricks thick, was similar to his other works, chiefly floor works consisting of metal tiles arranged in rectangles and lines. It was important for the viewer’s experience of the work that they were free to walk on the sculpture. These works are both self effacing in their near invisibility and grandiose in their ambition. Andre said that they represented footprints of much larger structures. He wanted to imply monolithic structures through minimal and economic means, creating a tension between the monolithic and the invisible. Andre also made a number of text works, some with ‘x’s and ‘o’s typed in rows, and others consisting of concrete poetry made by fitting words into some kind of geometric system. 

!Brice Marden.

American painter of minimal abstract works who made large (often at least 2 by 3 metre) canvases which are painted in muted colours, often grey with one or two contrasting coloured stripes on the edge of the canvas. Marden's work has similar formal qualities to the work of Barnett Newman, although where Newman's work is painterly, Marden’s is hard edged and painted with flat solid colour. 

!Agnes Martin.

Martin’s minimal abstract paintings are usually based on a grid and they are mainly white with pencil thin lines of bright colours. The detail of the painting is barely perceptible from a distance; they are cool, calm and full of light, and rigorous in their execution. 

!John McCracken.

A British artist who makes minimal abstract sculptures in the form of planks and cubes with a high gloss lacquered finish in solid colours. The planks (about 3 metres long, 50 cm wide and 10 cm deep) typically lean against the gallery wall, while other sculptures are hung from the wall. 

!Yvonne Rainer.

An American choreographer who worked with artists such as Robert Rauschenberg and Jasper Johns, as well as musicians such as John Cage on collaborative performance works, for example The Mind is a Muscle, made in 1971. These works were developed largely at the Black Mountain College summer school and at the Judson Church Centre in New York and were unusual in their day for being multidisciplinary. Rainer choreographed dance works based on the repetition of everyday movements eschewing classical dance form and skills. This emphasis on the everyday had its parallels in both minimalism and Pop Art. Rainer later went on to become a filmmaker and in the 1990s made her first feature film. 

!!TOWARDS POP ART.

Artists such as Rauschenberg, Johns and Lichtenstein took the logic of abstraction and minimalism and applied it to everyday objects and images. Their innovations paved the way for pop art. 

!Robert Rauschenberg.

Rauschenberg began his career in the late 1950s and was part of New York pop art in the 60s. He worked in all media and is particularly known for his paintings to which objects are attached. The object would be made part of the painting or in some cases a painting be made part of a sculpture by extending the paint from canvas to object. Rauschenberg’s innovation was to use screen printing as a medium, transferring images onto his canvases even before Warhol did, in particular photographic images from newspapers, cityscapes and street signs, before adding paint and even found objects. One of his best known paintings features a multi layered image of a city with a stop sign, a sign that reads ‘one way’ and architectural elements and abstract areas of colour. Another has a photographic transfer of President Kennedy. Rauschenberg made many works from found images and objects. One painting features a wooden chair attached to the canvas, and in another a single mattress with sheets, pillow and blankets becomes the canvas and is hung on the wall and painted on. Rauschenberg's conceptual painterly abstraction was important in the early development of pop art. He also made many drawings, including notably one called ‘Erased de Kooning’, which was simply that: a drawing by de Kooning erased by Rauschenberg. Rauschenberg worked with Merce Cunningham, the dancer and member of the Judson Church collective of artist and dancers which including choreographer Yvonne Rainer, designing sets, lighting and costumes for dance performances. He would often paint the set and the costumes himself. For one dance work he made a canvas backdrop painted with spots and matching spotted bodysuits for the dancers. 

!Jasper Johns.

American artist born in the 1930s, Johns was one of the artists who made the move from figuration to abstraction in the 1950s, and then in the 1960s began to use recognisable symbols in his paintings, the best known being the American flag. Johns and his contemporary Rauschenberg paved the way for Pop Art. Johns’s target paintings use a circular target motif (the kind used for archery or shooting) of black and white, or red yellow and blue. The paintings are portrait format so that the circular target is placed in the picture plane and there are sometimes other elements in the composition. Johns’s American flag paintings were innovative for their use of a pre existing symbol to make an abstract image; they are simply painted American flags, the stars and stripes covering the canvas edge to edge. These paintings were also significant because at the time the American flag was being burnt by protesters against the Vietnam War. Johns went on to make a proliferation of flag paintings, drawings and lithographs usually in red, white and blue. Then he began changing the colours culminating in a series of black on black and white on white flags, and even an edition of bronze flag table sculptures. Johns also made a series of number paintings, simply painting the numbers zero to nine, drawn using a stencil to fill a large canvas edge to edge. These were made in many versions including white on white. The idea to make an ordinary object into an art object led Johns to make some bronze sculptures, the best known of which are the Beer Cans and the Can of Brushes. These life size replicas are painted in true to life colours. 

!Roy Lichtenstein.

Lichtenstein’s work is among the best known of the American pop artists. He made paintings using images from printed cartoon magazines by taking single frames or even a detail from a frame and enlarging it, initially to about 1 metre high but eventually to a much larger size. He then made a painting from the drawing, reproducing exactly what he saw including any text. Initially the paintings were black and white but Lichtenstein soon moved to use colour. The paintings nearly always feature a person, a part of a vehicle and a graphic or textual depiction of fire, speed or force; they are part of a larger narrative that we are left to guess at. Eventually Lichtenstein began to paint the dots made by the printing process, the dots which are obvious on billboards when they are viewed close up. By making the dots part of the painting Lichtenstein was reproducing both the reproduction and the means of its reproduction and actually painting the printing process. He used the cyan magenta and yellow process colours on a white ground with black. Certain of Lichtenstein’s works are iconographic, for example one of a fighter pilot in a cockpit of a plane that has a yellow flash coming from the fuselage. The text announces the pilot’s thoughts at that moment. Another is of a glamorous woman crying, again with text revealing her thoughts. His style was, and still is, copied, plagiarised and borrowed. 

!!POP ART.



!Andy Warhol.

Warhol is one of the most significant artists of the late twentieth century. He was born in the 1930s and began his career as a graphic designer in his twenties. He was very accomplished at drawing and became an artist. Warhol is best known for his Campbell’s Soup Can paintings and his Marilyn paintings, but these are only a small part of his extremely prolific output. Warhol’s early graphic work included advertisements for shoe shops, clothes shops, children and adult book illustrations, book jackets and packaging. He used pen and ink, colour and sometimes added gold leaf. He began to make screen prints. Warhol became an artist and turned his studio into a gathering place and workshop called The Factory. He made his own work at The Factory but by virtue of his eccentric personality and arresting style began to gather around him a coterie of colleagues, assistants and admirers. Warhol’s hair was bleached white and he was never seen without dark glasses on and in stylish clothes. He was close to the most inspiring people in the fashion, music and art worlds. It is often said that Warhol himself and The Factory are productions of Warhol’s art; certainly Warhol’s persona was unique and The Factory was an unprecedented example of collaborative practice. At The Factory Warhol worked with photographer Billy Name, who documented everything that happened there, models such as Viva and Edie Sedgwick (among others), who were really his muses, actors and any young and beautiful people who interested him. The force of Warhol’s personality and the mystique that he created around himself meant that people were very keen to work with him or to commission him to make a portrait. Warhol was obsessed with fame. Sedgwick and Viva starred in Chelsea Girls, Warhol’s only feature film. Many other short experimental films were made at the factory including Empire State, a single static shot of the Empire State building 24 hours long, that documented the changing light on the building. Warhol said he wanted to make a film that people could watch when they were stoned. The Screen Tests are a series of head and shoulder shots of just about anyone who passed through the doors of the factory, including many who years later became well known, those who didn’t, and those who were well known at the time. Warhol would simply ask the person to sit and look at the camera. Warhol published a groundbreaking fashion, music and art magazine called Interview and made a series of television chat shows for which he interviewed people. Underpinning all this activity was the continuous production of Warhol’s paintings. Warhol took the most ordinary mass produced object and represented it as art, making a photographic silk screen of the label on a Campbell’s soup can. He did this as an anti art gesture, believing like other pop artists that art did not need to have noble subject matter and that art was a commodity like any other. To this end he used images of coke bottles, Brillo pad boxes and cornflake boxes among others to make his paintings, repeating the colour images in rows to cover the canvas, robbing the image of its original meaning. He produced these in multiples, denying them any exclusivity. He also made sculptures of plywood replica Brillo boxes in the same way. Warhol made a screen print from a photograph of Marilyn Monroe to which he added paint by hand on the lips, eyes and hair. He made as many of these as he felt like, painting each one differently. Multiples of the Marilyns, each one always the same size (1 metre high), are often hung in rows and blocks and they have become so famous as to be almost ubiquitous, exactly as Warhol would have wanted. He was attracted to Monroe because of her fame and glamour and made a vast number of paintings of her including a pair of round canvases painted gold, with her image printed in black on one and the other left blank. As well as the Marilyns, Warhol made a large number of paintings of Chairman Mao in the same way. He also made a number of images of an Electric Chair, again made in a wide variety of colour schemes, as well as a Last Supper, and repeated images of Jesus on one canvas. His very numerous portrait commissions, which were a mainstay of the Factory’s finances, included Jackie Onassis, Joan Collins, Elvis and many society and business figures. Warhol also made a number of self portraits, including one with sticking up hair. Towards the end of his life Warhol made some collaborative paintings with Jean Michel Basquiat. He died in the 1980s. 

!Claes Oldenburg.

This American artist is known for his soft sculptures that he began to make in the 1960s. Along with Warhol, Oldenburg was the main pop art sculptor. He worked in a number of different media and also made a large number of working drawings. The soft sculptures are made of canvas or vinyl, sewn and stuffed in the manner of soft toys, and then painted. The sculptures represent vastly enlarged objects such as a light switch made in white vinyl, a drum kit made in painted canvas and a hamburger. Each are accurately scaled up and constructed, but stuffed loosely so that they sag and collapse. The light switch (about one metre square) hangs drooping on the wall, the drum kit (3 metres across) flops in a heap on the floor. Oldenburg also made many painted clay sculptures, roughly modelled and painted, some cast in bronze, of which one of a hamburger is the best known. In the 1970s Oldenburg began to make public sculpture and also made a large number of designs, plans and sketches for monumental works, which were not always realised. These include a 30 metre high lipstick monument, a building based on the head of Mickey Mouse and one based on an electrical plug. 

!Ed Ruscha.

Ruscha became well known in the late 1960s for his paintings of words. Ruscha took the sign and the billboard as his inspiration and made the painted word the subject of his work; he is best known for his painting of the Hollywood sign. Ruscha used a variety of typefaces and three dimensional letters in his work, and also made a series of paintings of words as substance, for example the word ‘oil’ painted to look like oil. He also made landscape paintings to which words are added. Sometimes Ruscha used a (literal or imaginary) diagonal line in his paintings that he attributes to the compositional elements in classical painting. 

!James Rosenquist.

Rosenquist has made some of the largest paintings of any contemporary artist. As a pop artist he is distinguished by his skill for painting on a vast scale acquired during his years as a professional billboard painter, painting images on hoardings above Times Square in New York for example. Rosenquist is known for two paintings in particular, made in 1961, both of which are 4 or 5 metres high and around 25 metres long. F111 shows an American military aircraft, the painted lady on its side, and various other military items alluding to the contemporary Vietnam War protest movement. The other work shows a collage of items of Americana of varying relative sizes, such as hugely enlarged tinned spaghetti, celebrating the emerging consumer culture in tune with the spirit of the times. Rosenquist’s paintings often include the image of an idealised woman. Rosenquist was a contemporary of Andy Warhol and shared much of the same subject matter, but the two artists were unaware of each other’s work until late in the 1960s. Rosenquist continued to work on the same scale throughout his career refining his technique with new digital imaging technology to help it became very far removed from his original billboard style. In the late 1999s onwards, he made a return to the way he had worked in the 1960s. 

!Patrick Caulfield.

On of the best British pop artists, Caulfield is known for his very distinctive hard edged representative paintings of domestic interiors. He chose to paint interiors or elements from interiors such as furniture, lighting and ceramics, and developed a way of making paintings that he never deviated from. Caulfield copies the most basic structure of a scene from a photograph, rendering the forms with the minimum of line possible. He treats light and shadow as solid object and draws both in the same way so that the light from a table lamp will be represented as a solid yellow shape, the shadow behind its shade as a black shape. All the objects in a painting are given equal importance, since every element is treated the same. The outlines are painted in flat acrylic using a schematic, dramatically simplified black outline with the objects, walls, floors, light fittings, furniture being filled in with bright colours. These black lines are vaguely reminiscent of the lead in a stained glass window, yet they also call to mind Matisse’s painting. Caulfield had several favourite subjects, among them the lamp and the vase. He made many small paintings each around 50 cm high of one or two items, as well as many of entire interiors on a larger scale usually 2 metres high or more. His paintings can be seventeenth century Dutch interiors and still lifes; he painted the aspirations of an equivalent social group in his own day. Caulfield’s best known works are some of the simple still lifes, for example a single lamp, a goldfish in a bowl, an ashtray with a cigarette, a vase, an oval mirror which somehow conveys the idea that it is reflective even though it is painted in black and white. The schematic, iconographic way these things are painted makes them deceptively simple to look at. Of his larger works, perhaps the interiors of a Swiss bar, is the best known. This interior is painted mainly in blue (with the usual black outline), which signifies the quality of light in the place rather than the materials of which the furniture is made. In this work Caulfield uses trompe l’oeil, another characteristic technique: the photo mural on the wall behind the banquette seating is painted realistically in that it looks like a view of an actual mountain scenery rather than a schematic representation. Another example in which Caulfield paints a piece of wood grain realistically makes the direct acknowledgment of Braque’s work more explicit. Caulfield never painted a person. 

!Richard Hamilton.

Hamilton is another well known British pop artist who made paintings that are figurative and usually have a realistically painted element to them, perhaps a figure, combined with an abstract, more painterly element. Parts of the paintings are often blurry and watery, painted on un primed or thinly primed canvas. Like many of the pop artists, Hamilton used the techniques of graphic art, such as air brushing and photographic reproduction, in combination with oil painting. This can be seen in the painting of the IRA hunger striker Bobby Sands during the period of his dirty protest made in the 1970s which shows the long haired, bearded Sands in his prison cell in front of a wall smeared with his own excrement. Hamilton used an airbrush to paint the excrement as blurry brown squiggles. Hamilton’s collages are made as sketches for paintings but sometimes are presented as works in their own right, such as Hamilton’s arguably best known work, A Plan for Everyday Living. This shows a montage of a modern domestic interior, an apartment with cantilevered stairs descending into a double height living area with picture windows that are being vacuumed by a body builder dressed only in a frilly apron. 

!Eduardo Paolozzi.

Paolozzi is a British artist who made works on paper and sculpture that could be considered as pop art. Paolozzi specialised in patterns based on a grid of small coloured squares, which are complex and highly decorative, referring both to historical architectural decoration and textile design as well as to 1960s graphic style. He used the same patterning to create cast metal sculptures, often made from aluminium. In the 1980s Paolozzi was commissioned to design the mosaic tiling for the walls of Tottenham Court Road underground station in London. This commission made Paolozzi’s work one of the most frequently encountered public art commissions in the world. 

!Peter Blake.

British pop artist who is known best for his the sleeve design of the Beatle’s Sergeant Pepper album. Blake’s paintings are dense, detailed figurative collage style works. They often contain elements of patterning, decorative borders and are meticulously painted. He often chose to portray British national identity in some way. He included a self portrait in many paintings – he always appears with long hair and a long beard. 

!Jim Dine.

A contemporary of Rauschenberg and Johns in New York in the 1960s, Dine's best known work features his trademark hearts, drawn in outline or painted often in red or pink on white. 

!Robert Longo.

Longo is an American artist who made paintings, drawings and sculptures in the 1970s and 80s. The sculptures are usually complementary to, or even shown as a part of a painted work. Longo made a number of works in two and a half metre high panels arranged in threes and fours, one panel showing a man clothed in a suit apparently falling to the ground (perhaps having been shot), although he could almost be dancing, the other panels blank white or with photographic cityscapes. The pencil and graphite drawing on the panels is very realistic. In some cases there is a bronze cast of a similar male figure placed in front of the wall panels, the best known being of a man ‘falling’, the balance of the sculpture precarious. 

!Robert Indiana.

A screen print and sculpture made by Indiana became the motif for a generation. He placed the letters of the word ‘love’ one on top of the other to form a cube shape with a distinctive slanted letter ‘O’. Pop art was a major movement in twentieth century modern art. Conceptual art had been developing from its roots in dada and surrealism, notably in the work of European artists who had been influenced by these movements such as Klein and Broodthaers. 

!Yves Klein.

Although he cannot be classed purely as a conceptual artist, French artist Klein’s subject was the condition of art, and he made several works that gained him notoriety, in particular the Blue Paintings. These were made as a performance in a gallery in Paris. Klein invited female models to act as human brushes. He applied ultramarine blue paint to their naked bodies and then directed them to position themselves and move their bodies in certain ways, making body prints onto paper he had placed on the floor and walls. Klein’s idea was to use the nude to paint the nude. The resulting body prints were exhibited, but it is the photographic documentation of the event that has become better known. Later on he made some paintings by setting fire to canvases, singeing them and marking them with smoke. Ultramarine blue was Klein’s trademark colour that he used for all of his painted work and sculpture. He made a large number of paintings in the 1950s and early 60s using natural sponges stuck to canvas and painted blue. He also made simple plain blue canvases. Similarly he made sculptures with the sponges, creating tableaux of his flower or seaweed like creations. Klein worked briefly in ceramics, making small sculptures that drew on porcelain table ornaments, lumpen thrown together and glazed versions that nevertheless made their sources clear. The other strand to Klein’s work is more conceptual. He made many works with found objects and works that were events and documentations of actions. He made a work with a light bulb and several works with furniture. The best known of these works is Leap into the Void, a staged photograph in which Klein is seen apparently leaping off a high wall in a street and falling through the air. There is a house on the left and a lamp post behind him. Paris Air is a sealed glass vial containing nothing but air, to which Klein accorded a status as an artwork and a monetary value. In a performance work Klein sold gold leaf which he had authenticated to collectors and then invited them to throw the work they had just bought into the river Seine. He also printed his own bonds, and sold these as works of art. The work that summed up Klein’s interests most completely was The Void, a staged event for which Klein rented an empty gallery. He sent out invitations to the opening of his exhibition, provided champagne and hired a commissionaire to stand on the door. No one was allowed to enter the gallery without an invitation and when they did so they found an empty gallery – the void of the title. 

!Marcel Broodthaers.

Like Klein, the Belgian artist Marcel Broodthaers cannot be defined as a conceptual artist but he draws both from this and surrealism. Broodthaers occupies an eccentric personal world that centres around his Musée des Aigles, and the mussel shell. Broodthaers was something of a surrealist, with his use of puns and irony, and juxtaposition of unlikely objects. He created his own, sometimes impenetrable, lexicon of signs and symbols, and indeed signs were part of his work. He made pressed sheet metal signs displaying diagrammatic objects such as a pipe, with ‘fig 1’ and ‘fig 2’ written underneath. He made a number of small sculptures and wall reliefs using mussel shells stuck together. The Musée des Aigles (Museum of Eagles) contained things that the artist had collected which bore eagles' wings, such as military crests. 

!!CONCEPTUAL ART.



!Hans Haacke.

American Haacke became one of the best known of the 1970s’ conceptual artists in America and Europe. His practise was political in the sense that his subject matter was a critique of multinational corporations and advertising. Haacke made a number of installations consisting of text panels with photographs detailing the activities of multinational companies such as William Morris, often subverting advertising design used by the company. Haacke's earlier sculptural work includes a piece called Condensation Cube (1965), a Plexiglas cube about 50 cm square with naturally occurring and accumulating droplets of condensation inside it. 

!Alighiero Boetti.

The Italian artist Boetti made quite a lot of wall hangings using embroidery, chiefly maps of the world, but also tapestry panels showing the letters of the alphabet. His interests were naming, writing and maps. He often made work using embroidery and tapestry, as well as the equally time consuming method of using a ballpoint pen to colour large areas. Boetti made a series of drawings in ballpoint pen of invisible words. Reading from a key to the drawing along the top and sides, it is possible to discern words hidden in the drawings by referring to inverted commas on a grid. The whole paper is coloured by intensive use of the blue or red ballpoint, leaving only the inverted commas white. 

!On Kawara.

A Japanese artist who began working in the 1960s, Kawara is known for a number of conceptual projects and endurance works. I Met, I Went is a year of postcards written and mailed to friends and acquaintances by Kawara every day for a year. The work with which Kawara is usually associated is a constantly accumulating series called the Date Paintings, of which he has made one a day since the 1960s. Each identical 50 cm high painting has the day’s date painted in white numerals on a black canvas. 

!Michael Craig Martin.

Craig Martin is British American artist born in the 1940s who makes wall paintings depicting ordinary objects – furniture, paperclips, tools, bicycles, lamps, globes and fittings are juxtaposed without reference to relative scale at various angles. The objects are placed on very brightly coloured grounds painted with matt emulsion paint and outlined in black plastic tape, except for a period in the 1980s when Craig Martin used square sectioned steel rods bent to shape and welded to create the outlines. The wall paintings may fill a whole gallery or museum, or just one wall. Craig Martin’s earlier work made in the 1960s and 70s is conceptual. His best known work is the Oak Tree, which consists of a glass of water placed on a glass shelf above the viewer’s head and an accompanying framed text which explains to the viewer why the glass of water is an oak tree, by means of a question and answer between the artist and a fictitious interlocutor. The artist claims that it is by the authority invested in him as an artist that the glass of water is an oak tree, thus this is a work about authorship and subjectivity. He also made a work with a row of clipboards and plumb lines hung on a wall, a series of boxes with hinged lids placed on the floor, the lids taking progressively larger parts of the box until the lid was more box and the box was more lid. 

!Sol Le Witt.

An American conceptual artist who made installation and drawings, Le Witt is best known for his wall drawings made in pencil directly onto the wall which is painted either black or white. He devised complex geometric patterns of lines – parallel, radiating or on a grid – which are drawn in coloured pencil, forming a barely perceptible drawing which nevertheless has a strong effect on the space and adds glowing areas of colour. Le Witt also made a number of sculptures based on the grid. These are three dimensional grids formed from 30 cm cubes stacked in rows on the floor. the whole sprayed with white lacquer. Each layer has a different formation and may be up to 40 cubes long. The cubes are not solid, so that the structure has a complex appearance. 

!Daniel Buren.

Buren painted lozenge shapes on buildings and architectural structures, making site specific works that use the lozenge shapes as a visual intervention to prompt a reconsideration of the scene presented to the viewer, and attempt to alter the reading of the picture plane, treating an environment as if it were no more than a photograph. The lozenges allude to signage and logos are entirely without function. Buren's work is as much to do with reconsidering landscape painting as it is do with installation. 

!Victor Burgin.

An American artist who made conceptual work from the 1960s onwards with a strong theoretical slant which is evident both in the work and in his writing about art. 

!James Lee Byars.

One of Byars’s sculptures is a clear acrylic case, containing white sand, a white book, and a cream coloured (almost white) snake. This work can be recreated anywhere as long as the artist’s instructions are followed: he specifies the white sand, the dimensions of case and book, which must be made by a bookbinder, and that the snake must be as close to white as is available. Early conceptualism implied a form of resistance to subversion of the cultural norm. This gave the work of these artists a political edge until at the end of the twentieth century conceptualism became the norm. One artist who is identified with a subversive intention is Beuys, who made conceptual and political art between the 1950s and the 80s. 

!Joseph Beuys.

Born in early 1930s Beuys is one of the most significant German artists of the second half to the twentieth century. He was a professor at the Dusseldorf academy for many years, where he had a huge influence on several generations of younger artists. Beuys was an eccentric character who always dressed in the same canvas hat and black canvas shooting waistcoat with multiple pockets. He was extremely prolific, working in all media but chiefly known as a sculptor, performance and installation artist who proclaimed that ‘life is art’, thus making his every action a part of his work. Beuys is notorious for the myth of a near death experience that surrounds him and although long since discredited has proved impossible to ignore. It was said that he had been shot down when flying for the Luftwaffe in the second world war over snowy mountains. His life was saved by local villagers who found him and kept his body temperature up by smearing him in pig fat and wrapping him in felt. Whether apocryphal or not, this event would seem to have had a profound influence on most of Beuys’s work, given that his favoured materials are felt and fat. However, Beuys’s personal lexicon is often impenetrable and inaccessible. One of his best known works is Portrait of the Artist Talking to a Dead Hare, which was made for his first solo show in New York in 1979. Beuys sat in the gallery, his head covered in honey and gold leaf (also highly important symbolic substances in his work), cradling a dead hare in his arms and talking to it. As a declared socialist and an anti capitalist, politics are a major feature of Beuys’s work. He was sacked from his academic post at Dusseldorf for inciting student riots, although the exact truth is subject to speculation. In his second New York show he made a work with a live wolf, titled I Like America and America Likes Me. Beuys sat in the gallery behind a chain link fence separating him and the viewer, talking to the wolf for a week. The wolf symbolised the plundering and appropriation of the American wilderness and by his interaction with its spirit Beuys was trying to heal the wounds caused to the indigenous people. Beuys’s best known work is Fat Chair, a wooden chair with lard piled at a slant towards the back of it. He made a number of works from stacks of plate steel and felt which he described as batteries, a kind of life preserving power source. A number of vitrines containing an array of mysterious but apparently utilitarian metal, wood, fat and felt objects. A series of performances given by Beuys took the format of lectures often conducted in a studio, in which he used blackboards to illustrate a talk, usually about the possibility of art being life, and in particular to make his famous claim that ‘Art is a Social Sculpture’. By this he meant that art could not exist without the interaction of the viewer, it was therefore subjective, and thus a social event and by extension political. The blackboards with their drawings, diagrams and writings remain as a document of the lecture. Beuys made himself a felt suit and various other garments and items, which usually had a cross cut from cotton fabric stitched onto them. In 1985 Beuys installed rolls of felt the height of a London gallery all round the walls and across the entrance, leaving only a small three foot high gap from the floor up for the viewer to enter. Inside was a grand piano. A total damped silence was implied by felt and experienced by the deadened acoustic. Beuys also made a work consisting of a Volkswagen camper van with a number of wooden sledges emerging from its door, linked by a harness. Public works consisted of the planting of 5000 trees in a small town and the casting of a concrete public underpass by pouring melted lard into it and then cutting it out. Beuys made many works on paper, using charcoal, paint, ink and, in particular, a specific brand of rust coloured paint, the kind used to preserve sail cloth or paint door steps. The name of the paint and the colour was personally symbolic. In the first half of the century some artists were involved more directly in politics. 

!!ART AND POLITICS.

Artists have often used their skills in the service of their political beliefs, sometimes by working as official state artists, in which case they were well provided for. The Soviet government used socialist realist art as a propaganda tool and so artists who wanted to resist found other ways of working. Chinese state artists were similarly employed, some even worked at altering photographs to suit the government’s needs. Artists working under totalitarian regimes who had opposing political beliefs may have had no choice but to serve the state or risk punishment. In order to avoid this many defected or worked underground and in these situations art has often been a tool of resistance or subversion. Most of these artists did not achieve great fame due to the circumstances in which they had to live, but some did. Picasso’s Guernica is a well known work of political resistance. Some artists simply used their skills to record what they found abhorrent. 

!Kathe Kollwitz.

Kollwitz is an Austrian artist who made contemporaneous drawings in the concentration camps during the holocaust mainly in graphite and charcoal often depicting women. 

!Diego Rivera.

Rivera was a Mexican mural painter working in the 1930s and 40s who made political wall paintings celebrating ordinary people and workers in public buildings in the service of the socialists. He fell out of favour when the socialist government gave way to a right wing party who found him politically embarrassing and prevented him from finishing his last major public commission. Rivera was the husband of the artist Frida Kahlo and a friend of Leon Trotsky. The posters used by the demonstrators in the national strike and demonstrations in Paris and other cities in May 1968 borrowed the ideas of the Situationist International group of artists, who set up a poster printing workshop to make them. These bold graphic one colour screen prints had simple images and texts, for example ‘Under the pavement is the Beach’ and ‘Beauty is in the streets’. 

!!THE SITUATIONIST INTERNATIONAL.



!Guy Debord.

Founder of the Situationist International, a Paris based group of international artists, Debord led the group with his revolutionary ideas (and politics). Debord took Baudelaire's idea of the flaneur, the wanderer through a city, and developed it into his personal concept of the ‘dérive’, a directed walk through urban scenery. Debord believed that people should interact with their city, to which end he had various ideas including putting light switches on street lamps so that the people could turn them on and off at will. 

!Asger Jorn.

A Dutch artist and member of the Situationists, Jorn made collages with torn posters, for example a sixteenth century portrait of a woman in a blue brocade dress has her face defaced by the additions of paint and a collaged element. There was a strong performative element in what the Situationists proposed. Many artists in the 70s took conceptual objections to the status of the art object to its logical extreme and began to make performance art, dispensing with the object and any sense of permanence entirely, using their own bodies as both subject and material. Performance art was also a focus for many women artists who saw it as a way to make work about reclaiming of the female body by women. 

!!PERFORMANCE ART.



!Carolee Schneeman.

American artist Schneeman’s best known work is Meatjoy (1975), for which a large number of naked performers were instructed to perform an orgiastic celebration by wallowing in animal entrails and pieces of meat and blood. EyeBody was a solo work by Schneeman in which she sat on a swing, naked, in a space with white backdrop paper on the walls, holding an umbrella. During the performance she made a drawing by swinging as close as she could to the walls and drawing on them. For another work Interior Scroll, Schneeman read out loud a poem that she had written, from a long strip of paper previously inserted into her vagina and gradually extracted as she read. Schneeman’s Olympia, made in collaboration with sculptor Robert Morris, was a performance in which she posed as the woman in Manet's eponymous painting. Morris, dressed in a white robe and wearing leather work gloves, carried large plywood boards around, arranging them as a backdrop to Schneeman. Both performers wore white makeup. The piece was concerned with the ‘work’ of art, about the male artist and the female model, and about the translation from two to three dimensions, the boards representing ‘planes’. 

!Tseching Hsieh.

This Chinese artist made a performance in the early 1980s for which he was tied to another person by a rope 3 metres long for one year. 

!Allan Kaprow.

Kaprow is best known for his Happenings in New York in the late 1960s. These were site specific installations in which a performance would happen witnessed by an invited audience. The Happenings were one of the earliest examples of artists making live art in galleries or gallery like spaces rather than in a theatre or studio space. One Happening took place in a loft in which a structure of stud wall frames covered with transparent plastic sheeting created a box like space in which the performers could be seen but were obscured. Kaprow wrote a seminal book about the Happenings which was influential for artists making time based work. 

!Eleanor Antin.

Antin is an American artist best known for her work Carving: a Traditional Sculpture made in 1971, for which she put herself on a strict diet and had a male doctor weigh and measure her every day for a month. This is one of a number of works in which Antin investigated the effect of the ideals forced upon women in a patriarchal society. In this work she attempted to literally sculpt her own body, making a comment both on women’s control over their own bodies, on the centuries of depiction of the nude female body by men, and on women’s continuing need to make themselves attractive for men. The performance was documented by one photograph every day that were then placed together in a grid and framed, and by a video. During the 1980s Antin began to make more theatrical performances, using cut out puppets. She also developed an alter ego, Eleanora Antinova, a trans gender Russian ballet dancer who appeared in a tutu and a long beard. 

!Marina Abramovic and Ulay.

Abramovic first came to public attention when she invited visitors to a gallery in which she appeared naked, stating that the visitors could do anything they wanted to her and she would not resist or retaliate. She provided a number of implements with which to do this including knives and a gun. Abramovic narrowly avoided serious injury but discovered that her initial premise was correct: like the invisible barrier of etiquette surrounding an artwork, there was a barrier of propriety and embarrassment protecting her that was only broached when the beginnings of a mob mentality formed. Abramovic worked together with her partner Ulay until the early 1980s making performance ‘sculptures’ and endurance works. A work entitled Breathe linked them in a mouth to mouth embrace for as long as they could breathe using their combined exhaled breath. They braided their hair together and stayed sitting back to back for eight hours. For another work they used a full size bow and arrow and stood facing each other, with Ulay holding the string and Abramovic holding the arrow at the point on the centre of the bow string from which it is released. They maintained this state of equilibrium through tension until the strain of holding on meant they had to either stop or let go of the tension in the bow. This would have resulted in the release of the arrow, which was aimed at Abramovic. In Doorway they stood naked and facing each other on either side of the entrance to the gallery. This meant that those wishing to enter were forced to brush past them and had to choose who to face as they turned sideways to enter. These partner works focused on endurance through concentration and on the tension in partnerships manifested in physical mutual support. They are notable for the degree of politeness and gentleness combined with a power and implicit violence. The artists described them as sculptures. After the relationship with Ulay ended Abramovic continued to make endurance works. While these maintained a formal sculptural quality they also acquired a ritual aspect, as in the work in which she sat in a circle of salt surrounded by poisonous snakes. In another work created in an amethyst mining town in Brazil, she made and wore clogs from blocks of amethyst. Abramovic made a work for a tree sited in a public square in which she placed a number of speakers playing birdsongs so that it seemed that the tree was teeming with tiny birds. 

!Vito Acconci.

Acconci, an American artist, made several endurance works in the early 1970s, notably Seedbed, for which he built a false gallery floor which sloped up from the door to the rear of the space. Acconci was reputedly sealed in the void under the floor masturbating. In another work he constructed a shelf high up on the gallery wall in such a way that he could not be seen by those in the gallery from any angle and stayed on the shelf for the hours during which the gallery was open. In these works he made the gallery space and the viewer’s own feelings about being in that space into the subject of the work. A smaller scale work involved a video on a monitor placed on a furry rug on the floor. On the screen Acconci is seen, lying on his stomach, his face right up against the camera, talking to the viewer and inviting them to lie down on the rug and join him on the floor. 

!Chris Burden.

Burden became known in the late 1960s and early 70s for his performance work that mostly involved physical endurance. In ‘Shoot’ he arranged for a friend to shoot him in the arm in a gallery at a specified time in front of an audience. The shooting, which did not seriously injure Burden, was documented and like all his works of the period, the documentation became the work. In Locker Piece he had himself locked in a left luggage locker at a railway station for eight hours. In Freeway he lay in the middle of a freeway. He also had himself nailed to the bonnet of a Volkswagen Beetle through his hands and feet. Burden was interested in making formal sculptural work using his own body and in the visual power of pain and danger. The purity and simplicity of the works made them very powerful. In his later work he developed an interest in war and military capability through his pacifist beliefs and interest in ecology and the environment. He made a work involving hundreds of tiny model submarines, and a large meteorite like lump suspended from the gallery ceiling with model railway track running all over it and in and out of it. In 1999 Burden constructed a large and complex computer controlled machine for making and throwing paper aeroplanes. 

!Joan Jonas.

An American performance artist and musician who worked with Judson Church dancers Yvonne Rainer and Merce Cunningham, Jonas made a performance work in which she played the cello. 

!Dara Birnbaum.

Birnbaum is an American artist best known for her performance in which she dressed as Wonder Woman. Some women artists made work that although not performed was performative in its intention. They included Judy Chicago and Mary Kelly. 

!Judy Chicago.

American artist Chicago changed her name from the patronymic to that of her birthplace as a feminist gesture in the 1970s. Chicago’s work The Dinner Party from 1972 became an iconic feminist work. It is a narrow table arranged in a triangular format, with place settings for all the famous and unsung heroines of American life, for example Mrs Lincoln. The place settings are actually embroideries depicting plates, each one made by a different woman artist, similar to the way women make church kneelers. Chicago wanted to draw attention to the under representation of women’s achievement by staging this imaginary dinner party of these great women, using traditionally female but culturally undervalued materials and methods. She brought the women together by means of the dinner table because this is how women had traditionally brought people together. The work came to be seen as representative of the struggle for recognition of women from all areas of life and times, and was influential for several succeeding generations of artists. 

!Mary Kelly.

An American artist who made works dealing with feminist issues from the 1970s onwards. Kelly’s seminal work is the Post Partum Document, an extremely influential work made in 1973 documenting the birth and emergence into language of her son Kelly. The work takes the form of about thirty framed panels, each containing an object and a text. The object might be a soiled nappy (which helped to gather tabloid attention at the time), or an item of clothing. The text details the child’s weight and length, age in weeks, and describes his development with notes on his linguistic development. In this work Kelly documents not only the birth of her child but also her care for his life as a creative activity. This startlingly simple and obvious statement caused a great deal of criticism and publicity, and the Post Partum Document was declared not to be art, to be offensive and even cruel. Despite the criticism Kelly was widely acknowledged to have raised the issue of motherhood as a taboo, as work that is not to be considered work, not on a par with artistic achievement and not to be revealed in all its messy detail. The work’s form was important. It was predominantly a text work and this was another of Kelly’s points – that text, or language, is how the subject is constituted and that we are not subjects until we are speaking subjects. The emergence of a child into language is therefore the most important moment in our existence. Kelly drew on the works of Lacan, the French literary theorist who developed the theory of the other and the difference between the male and female subject, and of psychoanalytic writers such as Freud and Klein. Lacan's theories on the constitution of the subject were particularly influential for a whole generation of artists in the 1980s, mostly but not all of them women. Kelly’s later work developed into institutional critique and was always wall and text based. She made a work that consisted of highly crafted metal medallions or wall plaques. 

!Hannah Darboven.

Artist working in the 1960s and 70s who made works using text. Darboven was interested in catalogues, indexes and diaries, one work for example took hundreds of index cards on which the artist had written and displayed them carefully categorised. The text on the cards however consisted only of ‘x’s. This display of an organising system that contained no information served to visualise a practice which is in use in daily life, but which is never actually seen and rarely considered. Darboven’s obsessive writing projects are always made by hand. 

!Hannah Wilke.

American artist who became known in the 1970s for her photographic and performance work. Wilke often appeared naked, which was controversial at a time when feminist artists were reclaiming the female body, traditionally painted naked by men, for themselves. For one performance she appeared naked but wearing high heeled shoes on the steps of a public building. Wilke’s Chewing Gum Scarification Series is her best known work, in which she applied a large number of ‘scars’ made from chewed gum to her naked torso and photographed herself. These scars were also exhibited as sculptures. Wilke made a video work which shows her in her apartment, mostly on the phone to a man with whom it seems evident she is flirting. The film is a study in body language and how the viewer interprets it. 

!Martha Rosler.

American artist who began her career in the seventies making a number of photographic and text based works which juxtapose text and image to make work that highlights social inequity and challenges assumptions. She is best known for her seminal work the Bowery Project. This consists of a series of photographs paired with texts that juxtapose words or phrases used to describe people who live on the streets with images of the area in which they live. An image of a banking hall doorway, for example, is paired with the phrase ‘down and out’. An early collaborative work called Metal Box Factory is a systematic survey of a number of employees in a factory around 1970. Rosler asked the workers to write down their daily schedules both at work and home, displayed the results and made a comparison of the time spent working and at home. The schedules reveal the amount of work done in the home mostly by the women to be almost as much as the work done in the factory. Clocking on cards and production line rotas are also part of the display of material in the installation. Rosler made a series of collages showing domestic interiors that are (often humorous) comments on the division of labour in the home and the role of the housewife. Rosler is one to the earliest artists to make politicised feminist work which presented a critique of society. Performance art often combined video and installation. Some artists like Bruce Nauman had a multi disciplinary practice. 

!Bruce Nauman.

Nauman is an American artist who began his career in the 1960s making performance, installation, sculpture and video work. In fact Nauman made some of the earliest video art, in the days when the technology was new and only television broadcast equipment was available. This was bulky and heavy, and editing equipment was not readily available. Nauman was also one of the first artists to consider the television monitor as an art object in itself, as seen in his corridor installation. This consists of two 4 metre high freestanding white walls, placed next to each other with a gap in between. The walls are at a slight angle so that the gap between them narrows gradually until it is the width of a monitor, which shows only white interference. As the viewer approaches the work from the wider end, the light from the monitor creates a hypnotic effect so that the viewers feel compelled to enter the corridor and walk as far as they can before they no longer fit the space. This is intended to represent the tunnel with a white light at the end that is often described by those who have had a near death experience. In the 1960s and 70s, Nauman made several performed works that are usually viewed as video documents. The most well known is Taking a Line for a Walk, in which Nauman is seen walking around a square marked on the floor. Another work, a development of this piece, is a 4 metre high white box round which the viewer can walk. A closed circuit television monitor is placed at each corner, on which the viewer sees himself or herself passing the previous monitor. The film playing on the monitor is played back at slight delay, so that the viewers are following themselves. At the same time, since they are walking around a square, the implication is that they ought to be able to catch up with themselves. Nauman made other video works, one for example in which he is dressed as a clown and laughs uncontrollably for thirty minutes. The way the video is presented is always important; in this the monitor is in a wooden cubicle. In the 1980s Nauman began to make more static sculptural work, and began a series in which he cast dogs in rubber and displayed them in various ways, for example hanging them from the ceiling in a carousel like structure. In the 1990s Nauman made a large scale video installation for which he filmed himself, head and shoulders, talking to himself, and presented a number of these films on 2 metre high flat screen monitors placed upside down. Cinema and the work of experimental filmmakers had a great influence on many artists and with the advent of early video technology the distinction between art and film became blurred. 

!Nam June Paik.

This American artist was one of the first to make and show video as a sculpture. Paik was also the first artist to broadcast his work on national television, which he did by purchasing advertising slots. The work most often associated with him is a robot made from television monitors. 

!William Wegman.

Wegman is an American artist who made a number of videos in the 1970s featuring himself and his pet dog, a Weimaraner named Fay Wray. The dog performs a number of actions, such as licking up a line drawn in milk on the studio floor, which Wegman himself also tries to do, considerably less efficiently. Two similar dogs sit face on to the camera following something off screen intently in perfect unison, the object of the dogs’ attention moving sometimes slowly and sometimes dramatically and violently. The videos are filmed in black and white in a single shot with no editing. Wegman went on to make colour photographs of the dog dressed in clothes. 

!!EXPERIMENTAL FILM.



!Kenneth Anger.

An underground filmmaker born in the 1950s whose best known film Scorpio Rising had an influence on a whole generation of artists and filmmakers. A number of artists made site specific work that, like performance art, relied on photographic documentation for its dissemination and permanence. This became known as land art. 

!!LAND ART.



!Dennis Oppenheim.

Much of Oppenheim's work was site specific land art with a performative endurance element. For one of Oppenheim’s best known works, Trench, he suspended his outstretched body by pressing with his hands and feet against the top of two opposite walls of a purpose built trench. For another he lay in the sun until his skin was burnt and had changed colour, calling his sunburn the evidence that an artwork had been made. 

!Robert Smithson.

Smithson's work is nearly always site specific and involves him altering the natural environment, either by rearranging it or by placing mirrors so that the landscape reflects itself. He is interested in the perspective of the map in terms of making the ground his work surface or picture plane, and in creating ‘images’ which, like the topography represented by a map, are only visible from the air. In one sense, then, his work is intentionally invisible. Smithson’s use of mirrors is a development of this interest in making formal rearrangements of the natural world. The mirror works occupied a variety of locations, including rural ones and one in Manhattan in which large mirrors are stood on the ground at specific angles. Spiral Jetty is the best known work by any land artist. It was made by taking several tonnes of small rock to a salt lake and building a walkway, which leaves the beach and follows a very large spiral that is only visible form the air. Spiral Jetty has over the years been submerged by the lake and now ceases to exist. However, it was documented in photographs and on film, like all of Smithson’s work, and this is the form in which his work is most commonly experienced. In fact Smithson was very interested in the dichotomy between the reality of his work experienced in situ and in a photograph (which shows the work in an entirely different way). He made a number of other earthworks and plans for earthworks, and also made work using maps. 

!Mary Miss.

American artist born in the late 1940s who made a number of earthwork sculptures, for example a cube shaped void sunk into the ground which had its top exactly level with the surface, and could be entered on ramps from ground level. Miss subverted minimal sculptural forms being made by her contemporaries by making them absolutely concrete but invisible. 

!Agnes Denes.

The American artist Denes planted and cultivated a field of wheat in Manhattan on a two acre landfill site in 1982. Denes wanted to make a work about world trade, the economics of food production and famine. 

!Walter De Maria.

Known for his permanent installation Lightning Field of 1987 which is a site in Arizona, in a specific area that has the highest frequency of lightning strikes in the world. De Maria set out a square mile of lightning conductor poles linked by conductive wires which when struck by lightening animate the field in a spectacle of blue light and electrical activity. 

!!SITE SPECIFIC ART.



!Gordon Matta Clark.

American artist Matta Clark is best known for his work House, a suburban clapboard house that the artist cut in two vertically down the centre. The two halves lean outwards and away from each other. Matta Clark made a number of other works which involved cutting through buildings, for example a project at the opening of the Pompidou Centre in Paris in which he cut several circular and elliptical holes through a nearby building that was about to be redeveloped. Although major in terms of their execution, Matta Clark’s interventions are visually extremely simple. 

!Christo and Jeanne Claude.

Beginning in the 1970s French artist Christo and his partner Jeanne Claude began wrapping objects, to transform one thing into another, solidify forms, change a hard form to an apparently soft one, graduating to larger and larger objects. They began to work with fabric on a large scale, either wrapping buildings or the landscape. They used parachute silk, vinyl and other kinds of industrial fabric with the required properties. Several works exemplify what they do best. For example, Valley Curtain, made in the early 1980s, was a large orange curtain hung across a valley somewhere in California. Running Fence is a mile long fence of red fabric that follows the contours of a hill. The artists installed a large number of table umbrellas dotted over a hillside. The Island Skirts surround a number of small islands, following the shoreline and floating on the water for thirty metres or so. As with all their work, the proportions of the intervention always take the view from a distance into account. The work is by its nature temporary and is always made with the final photographic document in mind. Thus the island skirts were designed to be wide enough to be clearly visible in an aerial photograph, and the locations are very carefully chosen with the final photograph in mind. In the 1990s Christo and Jeanne Claude completed their most ambitious projects, the Wrapped Reichstag and the Wrapped Pont Neuf. These structures were wrapped in a fabric that was light enough to fold and gather around the forms they covered, and bound with rope. The Pont Neuf was wrapped in a gold fabric resulting in a transformation that was a triumph of planning, negotiation and construction. There are still postcards of the work on every Parisian postcard rack. 

!!FIGURATIVE PAINTING.

In Britain in the early twentieth century many figurative painters were engaged in a gritty realism that echoed the post war reality. 

!Stanley Spencer.

British artist Spencer painted English rural life and religious subjects, often combining the two. He often showed god or Jesus in a scene set in an English village. The paintings are sometimes large and the composition is crowded. Spencer’s figures are often solidly built with heavy limbs, almost statue like, and he kept to a palette of grey, brown and ochre. 

!Ivon Hitchens.

Hitchens’s English landscapes with vegetation and flowers are painted with loose flowing brushstrokes leaving parts of the canvas unpainted. He favoured subdued earth colours, purples, blues and greens. 

!John Bratby.

Bratby was one of a group known as the kitchen sink painters for their choice of domestic subject matter. Bratby's interiors showed his own home and family, painted with thickly encrusted paint often squeezed directly from the tube and applied with a palette knife, in an almost naïve style. 

!Cecil Collins.

British twentieth century visionary painter. 

!LS Lowry.

British artist Lowry lived and worked in the North of England and painted scenes of everyday and working class life in the cities of the North. He became known as a kind of people’s painter, a painter of ‘matchstick men and matchstick cats and dogs’ immortalised in a popular music song. Lowry’s paintings showed the grey light of northern winters and the relentless coming and going of daily life, with buildings and a multitude of tiny people walking past. The people are painted in two or three brushstrokes, suggestions of figures so that Lowry could paint a great number of them on one painting. The paintings are often predominantly grey or white, the figures in dark colours with little hints of brighter colours. 

!Lucian Freud.

A British figurative painter who was born in the 1930s, Freud began his career precociously in his teens and was still painting in 2003. He paints portraits, usually of models, sometimes of friends, family, and sometimes of well known people, although he rarely accepts commissions. His models are almost always nude, or semi clothed and painted in accurate detail, but with a psychological perspective. They are often lying down. The predominant colours of the paintings are consistent: skin tones, and the greys and browns of his studio are the background to all his works since he never paints anywhere else. A painting may take up to twenty or so sittings, and some sitters are not able to continue. He has often painted a favourite model repeatedly. Some of Freud’s paintings might be of two people lying together, or there may be a dog, often a greyhound. He favours gaunt loose skinned physiques but has also painted larger people. Freud’s subjects include himself, his lovers, his models (sometimes the two are the same), his daughters, artist Leigh Bowery, the Queen of England and the fashion model Kate Moss. 

!Francis Bacon.

Bacon was a very important British painter whose paintings are conceptual but theatrical figurative works with abstract qualities. They are nearly always portraits in which the figure is distorted, often to grotesque effect, perhaps looking as if they have been melted or brushed away, appearing to be in discomfort. The figures are rendered with rough brushstrokes, and are placed on a contrasting flat colour background, often within a delineated three dimensional space. Bacon made many self portraits. One of his best known works is the diptych Three Screaming Popes; three large canvases each with a portrait of a pope in a cope and mitre, within a kind of abstract geometric frame structure, on dark orange and red grounds. The faces of the popes are blurred and distorted. He also painted a dog that is either curled up or chasing its tail. As with Bacon’s other work, there is an impression of movement, as if a photograph has been taken on a long exposure and the sitter moved, leaving a trail of the movement on the image. Bacon was almost as well known for his lifestyle as for his work. A heavy drinking habitué of bars in Soho in London he lived a solitary and obsessive working life, rarely spending a day without painting. His studio was famously untidy and dirty. Bacon died in the 1990s. Painting had begun to aspire to the condition of photography, particularly in America. This was to become a major theme in the work of Richter and others later on, but initially there were only a few interested artists. Photorealist painters literally mimicked the appearance of a photograph. 

!Chuck Close.

Close initially made photo realist paintings but he is best known for his gridded paintings which, although they pre date digital technology, have the appearance of pixilated images. Inspired by the printing process and the way an image is broken down into dots of three colours, Close developed a way of placing a grid of squares of about 3 by 3 cm over the image he wanted to paint, very often a self portrait. He then produced the image by painting each square a different colour so that from a distance the image would be readable but close up the coloured squares would be predominate. In doing so he was investigating both the effect of photographic printing and painting, making a painting that looked photographic, as painting had been trying to do, yet not in the conventional sense. All this work was made before the digital revolution that has made Close’s paintings look exactly like pixels. As well as paintings, Close also made works on paper in watercolours. He made a series of self portraits using black and greys that emulate black and white photos. 

!Reinhardt Voight.

An American artist born around 1950, Voight makes paintings based on a grid. In Voight’s early work he translated a photographic image into a coloured grid, degrading the image almost entirely, an idea that prefigured pixellation by decades. 

!David Hockney.

One of the best known of all British twentieth century artists, Hockney was born in the 1940s and began his career in the 1960s, becoming successful while he was still at college. At this early stage he often added text to his paintings as in a work titled We Two Boys Together Swinging, where he added the title of the painting in white letters. Hockney’s most famous painting is called The Bigger Splash. It shows an empty swimming pool and diving board with a villa in the background. The pool is rendered in flat aquamarine and there is a white splash in the water as if someone had just dived in. This is the best known representation of a splash in the history of painting. Hockney went to live in Los Angeles and the swimming pool became his favourite subject. He became very good at painting water and also painted in watercolour on tiled sheets of watercolour paper. He continued to paint in watercolour in 2003, making large scale full length portraits. Drawings constitute a major part of Hockney’s oeuvre. He used a single confident line, pencil or pen, and also made a number of lithographs and etchings. His drawings are restrained and monochrome in comparison to his often bright paintings. He drew his friends and lovers incessantly, and his mother, as well as still lifes, flowers, landscapes and animals, in particular cats and dogs. In fact Hockney’s work is extremely traditional in terms of his subject matter, it was rather his means of execution that attracted attention. Another well known painting by Hockney is Mrs and Mrs Clarke at Home, a double portrait of Ossie and Celia Clarke, fashion designer friends of Hockney’s, seated on opposite sides of a bay window in their London home with their cat next to them. Hockney also designed a number of stage productions for opera companies, notably the Magic Flute and The Rake’s Progress, painting backdrops as well as designing costumes. 

!Edward Hopper.

An American artist who began his career in the 1940s, Hopper is one of the most popular painters of American life, mostly but not always choosing urban scenes. He was particularly good at painting night scenes. One painting shows a bar at night, a long and low corner building, green on the outside with mostly black on the canvas, but glowing with bright yellow light from its interior. Inside is a woman waitress and a few solitary male customers. Another painting shows a petrol station at night, another a person on a lonely road at by a payphone, caught in the headlights of an oncoming car. Hopper depicted the melancholy and loneliness that he found in modern life. 

!Norman Rockwell.
