American artist who made paintings that represented the American way of life, and who became synonymous with a certain idealised view of America. Rockwell painted rural scenes depicting life on farmsteads, domestic interiors, fireside scenes and landscapes. A particularly well known work is a painting of a meadow filled with poppies in which a little girl is sitting. Rockwell painted in a highly realistic illustrative style avoiding any reference to painting as object. He spent his career making an impeccably painted record of America between the 1940s and 60s, many of which were printed on the cover of Picture Post and became images that entered into the national consciousness. 

!Georgia O’Keefe.

O’Keefe is well known for her paintings of hugely enlarged flowers in very strong colours which also allude to the female anatomy. O’Keefe spent many years living in the desert in New Mexico from which she derived much of her inspiration, also making a series of works depicting bleached animal bones and skulls which she found there. Through her flower paintings O’Keefe succeeded in turning a traditionally delicate subject into large bold and even aggressive or threatening forms. 

!Frida Kahlo.

This Mexican artist was born in the 1920s and made figurative paintings, largely self portraits. Kahlo depicted the tragedy of her life in her paintings, which are graphic and detailed. She shows herself in many guises, with her distinguishing eyebrows that meet in the middle, and her long braided hair and native Mexican dress. She details her gynaecological problems and back problems stemming from a trolley bus accident as a young girl (which eventually took her life prematurely in the 1950s) in her paintings, in particular a number of miscarriages (she shows the foetus outside her womb, still attached by the umbilical cord, for example) and the restraining corset which she wore to support her spine in her last years. Kahlo's discomfort and anger at her body’s inability to fulfil its biological destiny, coupled with a feminist fury at the obstacles faced by a woman trying to succeed as a serious painter at that time, also fed into her work. Kahlo’s paintings are disturbing and often horrific but also full of bright colours and pattern. Kahlo lived with the Mexican muralist Diego Rivera for part of her life. 

!Philip Guston.

American Guston made abstract expressionist work at the beginning of his career in the 1950s and early 60s. He then had a crisis of belief in abstraction and began to paint figuratively. Guston developed a distinctive personal iconography that included cigarette butts, ashtrays, horseshoes, shoes, and Klu Klux Klansmen’s pointed hats, painted in a cartoon like style with heavy black outlines. The objects in particular can be seen as self portraits. Paintings include a pile of shoes, old and battered boots with the odd horseshoe, an ashtray overflowing with giant, very fat cigarette butts, and a series of paintings of figures wearing Klansmen’s hats in which the figures are often facing each other as if trying to converse. Guston typically used either a monochrome palette of grey, brown, black and white (for example in the painting of a pile of horseshoes), or a bright palette. His bright colours are blue, red and a fairly bright light pink, the pink often used as a background. Some of the Klansmen paintings are black on white. Guston depicts a lonely world, yet not without a bleak humour, spending long hours in the studio smoking and trying to communicate with the rest of the world. 

!Sam Fernandez.

American artist Fernandez made psychedelic paintings in the 1960s and 70s, which were sometimes used for record covers. They consist of a richly dense backgrounds of patterns and flowers flowing into the main element of the image. Fernandez’s work influenced graphic design and was re discovered in the 1990s by a new generation of artists. 

!Nancy Spero.

Spero’s narrative paintings include small figures. She also made a work called A Tribute to Anna Mendieta (the wife of artist Carl Andre who died when she fell from the 30th floor of a building, reputedly pushed by Andre). This work is a pair of red handprints at the top of the gallery wall, which are dragged in a red smear down the wall as if someone with bloody hands was trying to hold on. 

!Helmut Newton.

Although not a painter, German photographer Newton was very influential among artists. He made black and white or occasionally coloured photographs of women, naked or clothed, but always objectified as glamorous, made up and fashionable. Newton always photographed his models wearing very high heels. Newton’s images fell out of favour for a time in the 1970s when feminist critics claimed back the female form, but he continued to make work. Newton’s women often seemed like objects for male pleasure but in fact they have a strange autonomy, despite their nakedness. Sometimes several women might be photographed together as in a well known image of several models standing in a group staring at the camera. 

!!EARLY TWENTIETH CENTURY SCULPTURE.



!Henry Moore.

Moore, a British artist born at the beginning of the twentieth century, began as a figurative sculptor, but later became a pioneer of semi abstract sculptural form in Britain, and made many public commissions. Like his peer Barbara Hepworth, Moore’s sculptures often have holes through them and hollows; he too was interested in representing the ‘void’ sculpturally. He worked in stone, bronze and clay. Unlike his contemporaries, however, Moore did not make smooth surfaced works; his roughly modelled sculptures are like giant bones, and always refer clearly to the human form. For example a reclining nude (a favourite form for Moore) might be abstracted from a series of voids and hollows, as if it were a piece of driftwood that had been worn away. A standing figure might have a large space where the belly would have been. Heads are featureless and the proportions of the body adjusted to suit the form that Moore was trying to achieve. Moore’s sculptures are often sited in rural landscapes and parks, and indeed it was the way in which the human form could be made to look as if it were part of the landscape that interested Moore. A late example of this is an arch (around 1960) made for a lakeside site in Hyde Park in London which seems to be constructed of three monumental bones, two standing on their ends, the other balancing across the two. Moore made a large number of drawings, both as preparatory sketches and in their own right. His drawings are realistic and anatomically accurate, as shown in a series of studies of hands. As well as drawing the figure, he made a series of impressions of people sheltering from bombs in an underground station during World War Two. 

!Jacob Epstein.

A British sculptor whose best known work is Rock Drill, the head and torso of a futuristic automaton like creature with a head shaped like an animal skull. The breastplate ribcage is carved with angular geometric lines and the head is turned to one side. Some of Epstein’s work reflects the agony of war. Epstein worked mostly in stone although he did make some bronze casts. Jacob and the Angel is a massive male and female figure both with huge thick limbs embracing each other. It is carved from a tan coloured but translucent stone. Epstein made a similar carving of a prone figure, a little like an Egyptian mummy. 

!Elisabeth Frink.

British artist born in the 1930s who has made bronze heads, busts and equestrian statues from the 1960s onwards. Frink is particularly known for her distinctive thin figures riding horses bareback whose tiny heads have strangely flat features. The surface of the sculptures is usually roughly modelled. Frink’s work can be seen in many public sites in London. 

!Niki De St Phalle.

French artist who makes large and small (ranging from table sized to outdoor public sculptures) papier mâché and fibreglass sculptures of human and animal forms. These are somewhat like totem poles or other tribal art objects, being mainly highly stylised white smoothed forms that are richly decorated with hand painted patterns particularly on the head and limbs. 

!Louise Bourgeois.

Bourgeois is an American sculptor born in the 1920s. She has had one of the longest careers of any female artists of her generation. Bourgeois has made works that are both intimate and monumental in scale, and all her work has a strong female sensibility. She has made, carved and cast sculpture and installations. Notable are her carved marble works of the 1950s and 60s, they are often quite small organic curvy shapes suggestive of the inside of the body or of plant growth. One of these is made in pink marble set on a plinth. Installations include room structures that have furniture and objects in them. Most recently she has made a monumental 30 metre high bronze spider. Bourgeois repeatedly uses the same motifs such as the shoe and the spider. 

!Barry Flanagan.

A British sculptor who mainly made semi abstract stone sculptures until the 1980s when he began to make figurative work and took the hare as his motif. Flanagan achieved great success with a series of sculptures featuring the figure of a leaping hare roughly modelled in clay, its legs exaggerated in length, cast in bronze, often painted in ultramarine, set upon a plinth made of a crisscrossed rectangle of lengths of planed wood. This hare and plinth combination was repeated in many different materials. 

!Ed Keinholz.

Keinholz created several installations including the Beanery, a tiny kiosk set up as a bar that the viewer could enter to experience the disorienting scale of the space inside it. Keinholz's installations usually featured life size figures cast from plaster, unpainted with real furniture, fittings and fabric. 

!Duane Hanson.

An American artist who began making his very well known life casts of people in the 1970s. Hanson’s fibreglass casts of himself and others are the sculptural equivalent of photo realist paintings. The casts are painted, have real hair added and are dressed to look as if they are real, prompting a double take by the viewer. Hanson chose very ordinary subjects, for example a police officer, a museum attendant, a pair of overweight and badly dressed American tourists, a motorbike accident victim prostrate with his bike, and a cleaning woman. 

!Allen Jones.

Jones is a British artist who achieved notoriety in the 1960s and 70s for his sculptures of women as furniture. Feminists in particular found his work hard to accept and although his work declined in popularity during the 1980s the images of some of his sculptures are known all over the world. Jones’s sculptures are made by modelling idealised life size female figures. These are cast in fibreglass, spray painted realistically and dressed in clothes, usually underwear. A characteristic work is a coffee table – a glass top is placed on the back of a kneeling woman who is wearing a basque, stockings and suspenders and extremely high heels. Other furniture includes a similar table top placed on the shins of a woman lying on her back with her legs bent at hip and knee, shins parallel to the floor and bound with cords to form a ‘z’ shaped support and a stool (a woman crouched over and bound with a specially made upholstered leather seat strapped on to her back, for example). Jones also made paintings. 

!Daniel Spoerri.

Italian sculptor who was part of the Arte Povera movement in the late 1960s and early 1970s. Spoerri is best known for his upside down table wall piece, a table top strewn with the remains of a meal – wine glass with dregs, dirty plate and brimming ashtray all fixed to the table and defying gravity. 

!Joseph Cornell.

An American artist known for making work in boxes. Cornell’s boxes vary in size and materials, they sometimes have doors, or interior shelves or sections, are often painted, covered in fabric or leather, and contain found items and printed material. They are like three dimensional collages. Abstract sculptors found a wide range of new materials and working methods, expanding the definition of sculpture. 

!Lynda Benglis.

An American sculptor working in the 1970s, Benglis made sculptures from brightly coloured polyurethane foam, poured onto the floor and allowed to expand and cohere. 

!Lee Bonteco.

Bontecou’s wall based constructions made from metal, acrylic sheet, perspex and rivets have the appearance of flowers but due to their materials they also reference aircraft fuselages. 

!Eva Hesse.

Hesse was born in the 1940s and died in 1969 but her prodigious output is now seen as an important counterpoint to the new and radical minimal sculpture being made at the time, and as also as a turning point for women artists who were in the minority, particularly in this field. Hesse combined current sculptural preoccupations with unorthodox and traditionally feminine materials and methods. Her achievement was recognised very early and she was included in several seminal exhibitions including one curated by Lucy Lippard, defining the new work. Hesse began her career around 1960 as a painter, training under Joseph Albers. She made figurative paintings and self portraits, gradually turning to abstraction, until she had a crisis of confidence in her ability to paint. Hesse ceased to make work until she and her husband travelled to Dusseldorf where a German industrialist collector had offered them a studio residency in a disused factory that he owned. There she found an abundance of materials and machine parts lying around, and she began to draw these parts and experiment. Working in the factory Hesse produced a number of odd abstract drawings based on the machine parts. She began to make wall based three dimensional paintings, modelling the surface of the work and adding string following the shapes, as well as projecting elements, the whole of which she painted. Then she went on to make sculptures with the materials she found on the factory floor. Her first successful ones were cubes of steel mesh sheets, open on one side. Hesse pushed equal lengths of rubber tubing through the holes in the mesh in the manner of rug tufting, to create a furry effect on the interior of the box. This painstaking task produced two sculptures (one with black tubing, one with transparent tubing), which combined the (traditionally female) techniques of handicraft with those (traditionally male) of industrial fabrication used to make minimal sculpture. Hesse continued to make wall reliefs from modelling materials, string and paint including Rosy, a breast like form on a buff coloured round with a spiral of string painted in graduated shades from pink to red. Hesse’s interest in concentric circles was worked in these reliefs and in her Dot graph paper drawings, delicate pencil or pen drawings in which rectangles are filled with row of tiny small circles or rows of concentric circles coloured in with pencil. The string forms resulted in a wall work consisting of a row of about 25 hemispheres 30 cm in diameter covered with string spiralling round, placed in a horizontal line on a long strip about 40 cm wide, at picture height on the wall. From the centre of each hemisphere, cords project and extend to the floor where the ends lie. The whole piece is painted grey. Hang up (1967) is a seminal work by Hesse consisting of a two metre by one and a half metre empty frame on the wall, bound with cloth bandage and painted light grey. A heavy gauge wire loop projects outwards from the frame that is also wrapped with cloth bandage. The loop is attached on the bottom right and top left corners and looping out about two metres from the wall and back again. The viewer may stand inside the loop, in which case they seem to be lassoed by the work. This work is an abstract but concrete representation of the nature of looking at paintings. String was one of Hesse's favoured materials along with latex and fibreglass. She made a string mesh consisting of lengths of cord dipped in latex and strung from the ceiling to form an abject enlarged untidy web. She took net shopping bags and stuffed them with scrunched up plastic sheeting, hanging them in clusters, which are strangely anthropomorphic, on the wall. She photographed herself lying on a bed covered with a mass of string like spaghetti. Hesse also used rubber tubing that she filled with plaster to create sausage like forms. Her work often made allusions to the male and the female body. The preoccupation with tubular forms and repetition characterises Hesse’s later works, Accession and Accretion. Accession consists of two 50 cm square black panels mounted on the wall with a space between them filled with a mass of silver wires. Accretion is a row of one and a half m long fibreglass tubes, slightly crumpled, leaning against the wall placed at regular intervals. Similar works are a casual arrangement of caved in fibreglass cylinders about 70 cm high and 50 cm wide standing on their ends on the floor, and rows of latex spheres on a sheet. 

!Dan Flavin.

An American artist working in the 1970s and 80s, Flavin used fluorescent tubes and the coloured light they produce as his medium. He created sculptures by leaning the tubes against the wall, sometimes two or three, sometimes a whole row, sometimes placed casually at different angles, sometimes measured and at the same angle. Some tubes might be placed flat on the floor. The transformers and cables are very visible and are part of the work. Example of works are a cruciform formation of cold blue coloured tubes mounted on the wall and three tubes, one red, one blue and one green. 

!Maya Lin.

Lin, an American artist, was chosen by competition to design the Vietnam War memorial in Washington. She designed a long highly polished black marble wall, inscribed on which in columns are the names of all those who lost their lives in combat in the Vietnam War. The wall is recessed at ground level, and appears to be gradually revealed as the ground in front of it is excavated to slope down. The top of the wall remains level parallel with the ground and viewers descend passing along the wall, which becomes higher in relation to them the further along the wall they walk. The memorial is extremely popular, due to its simple formal qualities and its gentle interactivity with the viewer. The memorial invites participation and takes the viewer down below ground level to see the names symbolically buried but rising up to meet the light. Its surface reflects the viewers, so that a multitude of faces appear on its surface. The traditional methods of casting and carving continued to be popular, but many sculptors now began to use welded steel. 

!Ellsworth Kelly.

An American sculptor who is best known for his 1960s to 80s work that consists of wall mounted steel shapes, distorted rhomboids, parallelograms and triangles. They are immaculately spray painted in matt primary colours and black and white, and butted up together one colour and shape against another, to create abstract works that are both sculpture and painting and have a contemplative lyrical presence. 

!Michael Kenny.

British artist Kenny made wood and metal abstract sculpture. 

!David Smith.

British artist working from the 1960s onwards who made large welded steel sculptures. 

!Philip King.

King is a British artist who made large steel sculptures between the 1960s and 80s. King made a number of public commissions, many of which can still be seen, for example in Fulham in London there is a red painted tubular steel structure work. King also made the designs for the decoration of the walls of Embankment underground station in London. 

!Hubert Dalwood.

A British sculptor born in early 1930s who died in the 1970s, Dalwood made carved, cast and constructed sculptures from stone, bronze, acrylic resin, acrylic sheet and metal. He also made a few paintings. His early works were totemic stone objects and tabletop sculptures that were a bit like small abstract sculpture gardens, cast in aluminium or bronze. He used tubular or square forms and wavy lines placed on a rectangular base that were cast in one piece and also made sculptures from acrylic with metal wire threaded through. As he became successful Dalwood began to be commissioned and he made a number of public works. One example is a construction in a park made from brushed steel tubes of a large diameter fixed vertically to the ground in a cluster, reaching about 5 metres in height, curving around at the top like giant geometric plant stalks. Another is an egg shaped bronze sculpture about one and a half metres in length lying on its side with some detail on its surface. 

!Alexander Calder.

British sculptor working in the 1940s to 60s who became best known for his kinetic sculpture and mobiles, Calder is one of the very few artists to have used the mobile successfully as a form in sculpture. Calder’s sculptures generally use circles, spheres and triangular shapes and rods. They are made in sheet metal, painted typically in black and white with red, yellow and blue. 

!Antony Caro.

Caro is a British artist who became very well known in the 1960s for his welded steel sculptures. Caro was the most successful of a group of British artists who made large scale abstract steel sculptures in the 1960s and early 1970s. His were either cut from steel plate, or made of round and square cross section steel tubes, rods and I beams which were sometimes left to rust, but sometimes spray painted in primary colours. Caro was one of the first sculptors to make semi abstract works on such a large scale. Caro was influenced by the scale of ambition of his American contemporaries but his subject matter was initially the English landscape; his sculptures depict landscapes, although they appear abstract. Caro used steel to make very open structures, often rather long and low, about 12 metres long and 2 metres high describing a kind of vista in space. One might be painted bright green, another bright yellow. Smaller works might be made from shapes cut from sheet steel put together in which the ragged edge from the cutting tool is often left unfinished. In the 1990s Caro made a number of small steel sculptures of a chair based on the chair in Van Gogh's painting of his own bedroom. Caro had been synonymous with a style of sculpture that was predominant in Britain for a decade, not least due to his years of teaching at St Martins School of Art in London, then a leading sculpture school. 

!Lynn Chadwick.

Chadwick's figurative bronzes range from life size to very small, around 30 cm high, human figures. They are angular, with stick like legs, flattened irregular trapezoid bodies, and heads that are either pyramid or cube shaped to denote female and male figures. Chadwick was interested in the difference between male and female forms in terms of body language. The bronzes mostly have a blue green coppery patina. 

!John Chamberlain.

Working in the 1960s American artist Chamberlain made sculptures from compressed dumped cars that he either retrieved from the compressing press or took to be compressed. The resulting cubes were exhibited as sculpture. He sometimes extracted compressed parts of cars that he combined to make into sculptures. 

!Eduardo Chillida.

Mexican artist Chillida is a sculptor who made monumental steel sculptures which were usually site specific, often situated in natural environments. For example, one of his characteristic M or comb like structures is placed on a cliff top to rust and acquire its orange red colour. During the 1980s, artists began to have a far greater variety of media at their disposal. Home video made it possible for anyone to shoot and edit films and large colour photographic prints were affordable. Digital technology was in its infancy but desk top publishing, graphics programmes and digital printing began to make an impact on artist's practise. 

!!POSTMODERNISM.

In the 1970s modernism had reached an impasse. The art object was no longer considered to have an autonomous existence; its meaning was constructed by the subjective interaction of the viewer. The work of Walter Benjamin on the aura of art, of Jean Baudrillard, Frederic Jamieson and Roland Barthes on art, culture and the nature of reality and Ferdinand de Saussure on semiotics were influential. All cultural forms were re examined and language was subjected to a rigorous philosophical analysis by structuralism writers and psychoanalytic theorists. Feminist theorists argued for an entire redefinition of subjectivity on psychoanalytical and semiotic grounds, influenced by the work of Jacques Lacan. Conceptual artists had been making work that examined the condition of art and the status of the art object, and soon artists working in all media began to follow suit. The 1980s was the era of conceptualism, of theory based practise, appropriation and the real. For most post modern theorists, the real world was no more real than the reconstructed world of representation For many artists the act of appropriation was a way of understanding the point in its history that art had reached. They freely borrowed elements from the works of others and added these to their own. The paintings of David Salle are an example of this. Artists also investigated the nature of reality, known as 'the real' and suggested another 'hyper reality' through acts of mimesis. Postmodernism made it possible to argue that art was by definition conceptual and that subjectivity was essential in the construction of meaning of an art object. 

!!ACTIVIST ART.



!Act Up.

Act Up were a group of gay rights activists that included artists, working in America in the 1980s. Act Up created poster campaigns, subverted advertising and made use of official statistics to point out discriminatory practise. 

!Group Material.

A Toronto based group of artists and activists, producing collaborative works, solo works, publishing, running a gallery, and commissioning projects and books who included the painter Jochen Klein. 

!Guerilla Girls.

A group of anonymous feminist activists who were working in New York in the late 1980s and early 90s, the Guerilla Girls were named for their wearing of gorilla masks when they went to organised demonstrations. They also used the banana as an emblem, along with the gorilla suit a prop of slapstick humour and a visual pun on both the diet of gorillas and the offering of an opportunity to institutions who fail to represent women adequately to ‘slip up’ (on the proffered banana skins). The group is thought to have included a number of members who went on to become prominent artists, for example Sue Williams. The Guerilla Girls were a product of an era in which feminism was well established and attention was turned from campaigning against discriminatory practises in everyday life, work and the home, to those in culture. The group produced a number of seminal posters and postcards for example a list of reasons ‘Why There are no Great Women Artists’, and a list of statistics about the proportional representation of women artists in art museums. These texts achieved widespread distribution because they were clever in their use of graphics and advertising to get message across, and because the appearance of the gorilla suited members staging demonstrations at institutions and relevant events was always newsworthy. 

!!PHOTOGRAPHY.



!Berndt and Hilla Becher.

German husband and wife working together born around 1950 whose work became known in the 1980s, series of black and white photos of industrial sites, silos and water towers that imbue the subjects with a formal sculptural quality. 

!Nan Goldin.

During the 1980s and early 1990s American artist Goldin obsessively recorded the lives of her friends photographically. People are photographed sleeping, eating, sober, drunk, fixing up, having sex, getting dressed, in the bath, on the toilet, kissing, cooking, talking, singly and in groups, meeting the camera or looking away. Most of them are artists, musicians, actors and their friends; there are straight and gay couples, single parents, families and children. Goldin creates a sense of a window on private lives but never a sense of intrusion. A subject might have a black eye, or be menstruating. The subjects are always relaxed in the presence of the camera. The resulting hundreds of images were exhibited in groups, usually printed fairly small, approximately fifty cm high. The groups of images became larger and they were installed in grid formation. Ultimately they were shown as a continuously running slide projection of hundreds of images. Goldin’s work became a testament to and document of the lives of a generation many of her whom were of the generation that was most drastically affected by HIV. This body of work is a memorial, although it is not in any sense melancholic but experienced as a record of the intimate lives of one generation of New Yorkers. 

!James Casebeere.

Casebeere made a series of photographs in the late 1980s of scenes that he had constructed entirely from cut, folded and shaped paper, for example a rural scene with a river, trees and a canoe, photographed in black and white. There is no particular attempt at realism or trompe l’oeil, the scenes are a combination of fairytale and cartoon, given a sombre seriousness by the use of black and white. This relates them back to early twentieth century American landscape photography. 

!John Coplans.

Coplans, a British artist born in the 1950s, made black and white photographs of his own naked body and parts of his more or less average male body, including his genitalia, in detailed close up. The texture of his skin, hairs and wrinkles are sharp and nothing is left to the imagination. Coplans presents the male body as a counter to the over exposed female body. His presentation of the male body reveals its frailty, and significantly it is an entirely unsexualised presentation. 

!Philipe Lorca di Corcia.

Photographer who takes images of everyday scenes and shows them in groups. 

!Michel Francois.

Belgian artist who makes large scale photographic installations. 

!Gilbert and George.

Two British artists born in the 1950s who have lived and worked together in a longstanding partnership. They began to work together in their twenties, when they moved into a house in London’s East End and began their existence life as living art, their daily lives rigidly habitual. Gilbert and George became as well known for their lifestyle and their deadpan personae as for their work; in the early years of their careers rarely speaking about their work or lives together. They parried all interviewers’ questions by assuming an impenetrably eccentric manner so that was never clear whether or not they were having the last laugh. Gilbert and George lived together in a sparsely furnished house, wore tweed suits, hats and overcoats, ate in the same café every day of the year, and were never seen without each other. Their early work was performance: they held soirées with invited performers, conducted with an anachronistic formality, including letterpress invitation cards. They became well known for their work ‘Underneath the Arches’, made in about 1975, for which they became ‘singing sculptures’. They stood on a plinth, their clothes and bodies entirely covered in silver paint, underneath the arches of one of the bridges over the river Thames and sang the music hall song of the same name continuously. In the 1980s Gilbert and George began to make the collaborative photographic work for which they are now known, photo collages made by taking self portraits, in which they appeared full length, usually one on each side of the image using the kind of symmetry found in religious diptychs. Initially these were small black and white works, the subject matter of which included images of London, flowers and plants, often repeated in a mirror image. Later they began to photograph young men and boys that they had met around London and the compositions began to have a scatological and sexual theme and were often directly abusive. By the 1990s, Gilbert and George were making works that set out to shock, using images of human faeces and naked men, including themselves. The images are printed in square sections which are placed together in a grid to make a large image often three metres square. Each section has a thin frame which is usually red so that there is no pretence that the work is one large image. The Gilbert and George photo collage became an instantly recognisable trademark. 

!John Hilliard.

British artist who makes photographs of landscape and architecture, presented on wall mounted light boxes. 

!Sylvia Kowalski.

American conceptual artist. 

!Robert Mapplethorpe.

A photographer working in New York in the 1980s and early 90s, Mapplethorp became notorious for his photographs of gay men. The men are often naked or semi clothed, and the images are sexually unambiguous such as Man in a Polyester Suit which shows an African American man resting his penis on a stool, and another photograph showing a man urinating into another’s mouth, both men dressed in leather. Photographs like these led to Mapplethorpe being attacked by the Christian right and his exhibitions were denounced as pornography. The fact that museums spent public money on exhibiting Mapplethorpe's work led to a full scale row in the United States senate and other artists were dragged into the debate. Senator Jesse Helms led the attack supported by The Association for the American Family and religious organisations. The result was a substantial decrease in the amount of support given to artists by the National Endowment for the Arts, the United States government arts subsidy fund. The existing obscenity laws were reassessed and there was a call for a revision of the censorship laws in order to make it easier to close an exhibition or require special conditions to be met. There were many counter claims, not just by Mapplethorpe, of discrimination on the grounds of sexuality. Mapplethorpe said of his work that he photographed images of love and trust, and that even the most ‘sado masochistic’ of his images represented supreme trust, love and vulnerability on the part of the participants. His defenders claimed that if his images were of heterosexual couples there would have been no such outcry. The case went to court and a ruling was made that the photographs defined as obscene could only be exhibited in a specially constructed screened off area, with a clear indication to the public of the contents of these works. The case increased the public interest in the work and made Mapplethorpe one of the best known photographers in the world. Mapplethorpe’s themes were love, sex and death. He made many self portraits the best known of which shows him wearing a leather biker jacket with chains and a leather cap. Another shows Mapplethorpe as a young man standing on a fire escape with the singer Patti Smith. A photograph taken just before his death shows a visibly frail Mapplethorpe holding a cane topped with a skull and crossbones emblem. Mapplethorpe made a series of photographs of flowers, mostly lilies and other exotic flowers, which are also sexual images, sometimes reminiscent of Georgia O’Keefe paintings, usually in black and white but some are colour. He also photographed the body builder Lisa Lyon, whose physique he considered to be perfect. Robert Mapplethorpe died in 1994. 

!Andres Serrano.

Serrano, an American artist born in the 1950s, is notorious for his work Piss Christ, which caused a furore and demands for its censorship on the grounds of obscenity and blasphemy from American right wing and religious groups. Serrano immersed a cheap plastic figure of Christ on the cross in a tank of his own urine with a light behind it and took a photograph. Christ is seen submerged in a red and golden light resulting in an image of glorification. Serrano, brought up as a Catholic, continued to make controversial images such as the Last Supper and a Pieta (both black statuettes immersed in tanks, with the bubbles adhering to them adding texture. He made a series of photographs of the blending of blood and milk in a dish, and of spurts of semen frozen in mid air at the point of ejaculation – white streams on a black background, and a series of images made in a morgue of corpses titled by the way in which they died; drowned, poisoned, stabbed. Serrano stated that all his work was an entirely respectful investigation into sex, life and death and the relation of these to spirituality and religion. 

!John Stezaker.

British artist born in the 1950s who makes photographic collage and printed work. Stezaker has made photographs of landscapes reflected in water, which he presents upside down so that they appear to be the right way up at first. He has made a series of large black and white prints based on an old photograph of a nude, which he has distorted. 

!Susan Trangmar.

British artist born in the late 1950s who makes photographic work. 

!Hannah Villiger.

Villiger made a series of Polaroid photographs of her own naked body holding the camera at arms length. The resulting series of images (it took several to document the whole of her body in this way) are printed enlarged so that the parts of Villiger are life size, and exhibited in a row. 

!Jo Spence.

A British artist born in the late 1940s who made photographic work from the 1970s onwards. Spence, a large woman, used herself and her male partner as models to make a number of works that explore preconceived ideas about ideal beauty, masculine and feminine identity and relations. The photographs were sometimes printed life size. In the 1980s Spence developed breast cancer but continued to photograph her ravaged body during and after mastectomy and radiotherapy. Spence’s photographs are often accompanied with written text in a diary format. 

!!PERFORMANCE.



!Bobby Baker.

A British performance artist born around 1950 who made a series of performances based on food in the 1980s. Baker returned to art in her late forties after raising a family and training as painter, but began to make performance art when she found that a painter’s life was not compatible with bringing up a family. She found a parallel between painting and its history and the creativity inherent in domestic and family life. In her first performance, ‘Drawing on a Mothers Experience’ she made a ‘painting’ by telling the story of the night one of her children was born by presenting samples of the food that she consumed before and during her labour and using it to make a painting on a bed sheet. She dripped black treacle, splattered jam, and printed with steaks. The resulting work was intended to be compared to an abstract expressionist painting. Baker obliterated it by sieving icing sugar over it and rolled herself up in the sheet. An Edible Family in a Mobile Home was a family constructed from cake and icing and placed in a caravan. Baker also staged a performance in her own home, inviting the audience into her kitchen to watch her discuss menial domestic tasks and what she enjoys about them, for example the freshness of washed cabbage leaves and the fun of icing (she poses on a revolving cake stand). After each action she pins a symbolic item onto her white coat. Baker also did a performance in which she gets into a bath of chocolate custard. Baker draws on the work of 1970s feminist artists who made the domestic their subject but adds humour. 

!Leigh Bowery.

A British fashion designer, club promoter and artist whose work defies definition but was very influential, Bowery always wore his own designs, usually involving heavily padded lycra, to change his shape substantially. He was known for his mask like make up, often with big black eyes, rubber and fetish wear, extremely high heeled shoes and boots. He morphed between a male and female look, both sexual and asexual. Bowery's club night Taboo inspired a generation of people to dress up, and he became an icon, eventually exhibiting himself in the window of a London gallery as a living work of art. Bowery died in the 1990s in his late thirties. 

!Cosey Fanni Tutti.

British video and performance artist who has been working since the 1960s. Fanni Tutti’s most recent work is painting. The work for which she is best known is a series of live events made on her own and with the band COUM transmission (later to become Throbbing Gristle). Fanni Tutti often appeared half naked. One image is always associated with her showing her reclining on a bed, wearing underwear but bare breasted and wearing a blindfold. She made a number of photographic works imitating soft porn photography. 

!Karen Finley.

Karen Finley is an American performance artist whose work, like Mapplethorpe's, attracted the attention of the American Christian Right. Finley received public funding and became notorious when she was banned from performing a work in which she inserted pieces of yam into her vagina. When she brought this work to London the venue at which she was to perform was forced to bow to local authority pressure and ban it. Finley continued to make controversial performance work centring around themes of child abuse, sexual identity and the female body, often in the form of a monologue by a character who was in turns aggressive and profane. 

!Rose Finn Kelcey.

British artist born in the 1950s, Finn Kelcey made a number of performance works in the 1980s and early 1990s, one using shadow puppets. In the late 1990s she made Matador, a work for which she trained as a matador in Spain and took part in a bullfight, a work which existed only as documentation. She later made an installation titled Steam Bed, which consisted of a large square brushed steel trough from which copious amounts of steam were emitted which was sucked towards an extractor fan of the same dimensions as the trough, creating an illusory solid form, a cube of steam between the two. 

!Ukeles Mierle Laderman.

Laderman is an American artist who made a collaborative work with the New York Sanitation Department in the 1980s. She staged a performance in which she shook the hand of every sanitation worked in New York, an action that highlighted the social invisibility of the waste disposal workers, those who carry out a daily task without which life would become untenable. Another part of this project was the design and construction of a refuse collection cart with mirrored sides which was used on the daily refuse collection round. As it travelled the city streets the people that created the refuse contained in it were reflected clearly in its sides. 

!James Luna.

Born around 1950, Luna is a Native American living on a reservation who was known for his socio political art projects highlighting the discrimination against Native American Indians. The best known of his works is a performed installation for which Luna had himself tranquillised and placed in a display case in a museum of native American artefacts during museum opening hours. He remained in the case every day for a month, under close supervision. 

!Bruce Maclean.

Scottish artist MacLean was born in the 1940s who made his best known work in London in the 1970s and 80s. He made paintings, colourful gestural abstracts and performances, in which he was seen smoking a pipe and wearing a trilby hat. 

!Alistair Maclennan.

A Scottish performance artist working in the late 1970s and 80s who made a controversial work at the Institute of Contemporary art in London in 1981 call Still Tills. McLennan spent a week living in the gallery eating and sleeping surrounded by food, detritus and waste including bodily waste. He also sorted through and arranged bags of refuse, which as the press reported included sanitary protection and nappies. The title of the work was a comment on the end of consumerism and shopping and the beginning of a new self sufficiency and the artist was trying to show the possibility of this by living without purchasing anything for a week. Other works of McLennan's were similarly time and endurance based. 

!Hermann Nitsch.

Austrian performance artist who pioneered Viennese Actionism which is the name he gave to his live art practise which consisted of solo works and events or happenings that involved a large number of people. Nitsch's performances usually took the form of some kind of self devised ritual, echoing religious, pagan or tribal sacrificial events and usually involves a degree of self inflicted pain or endurance, and often large amounts of animal blood and carcasses. The events are conducted with great solemnity and conviction. The large scale events have taken place in various locations, sometimes outdoors. In one a number of volunteers were crucified and the participants covered in blood. Nitsch thought of the blood as paint which he used to record of the event, producing large numbers of paintings and works on paper as well as photo and video. Nitsch is perhaps best known for a performance in which he reputedly cut off (or at least mutilated) his own penis. This is apparently documented by a series of photographs, which show Nitsch bandaging various parts of his body. 

!Gina Pane.

British performance artist who made endurance works, most infamously one in which she continually bit her fingers in an attempt to loosen and remove her fingernails, periodically rinsing them in a bowl of milk. She also made a performance during which she inserted rose thorns into her skin. Pane was one of a number of artists working in Britain in the 1980s that resisted the idea of art as a commodity instead using their own bodies as medium and subject. 

!Annie Sprinkle.

An American ex prostitute, artist and performer who is best known and in fact notorious for a live work in which she reclines on an armchair and invites the audience to examine her vagina through a speculum, to masturbate her and to ask her any questions they want. 

!Station House Opera.

British performance art group led by Julian Maynard Smith who came to prominence in the 1980s with highly formalised repetition based works. Performers dressed in ordinary clothes and not speaking would carry out everyday actions repetitively, without making allowance for each other’s movements but avoiding collision. The performers obeyed a set of rules and together a counterpoint effect was achieved as in the work Table Move, which consisted of performers moving furniture obsessively around a stage. Later works built on the idea of physical risk, involving the performers in a variety of increasingly dangerous endurance based works. One involved the performers sitting on and using furniture including tables, chairs and cupboards, while suspended high above a pedestrian area from the outside of a building. Another, Bastille Dances, consisted of a day long construction and dismantling of a model of the Bastille form polystyrene blocks. 

!Stelarc.

Australian artist who made performance works involving physical endurance and technology. For one performance he inserted several hooks into his flesh and suspended himself from these, his body hanging horizontally from a bridge over Sydney Harbour. Stelarc was interested in the body as a machine and for another work he had a robotic arm constructed, which was connected to his brain via electrodes so that he could control it by thought. 

!Gary Stevens.

British artist born in the 1950s who made a number of performance works in the 1980s which took place in galleries. In his work Stevens adopted a stylised performing persona and often used large numbers of soft toys. 

!Andre Stitt.

British performance artist. 

!Steven Taylor Woodrow.

A British performance artist who made a number of solo and group performances in the 1980s, his solo work consisting mainly of theatrical poetry readings, in which Taylor Woodrow appeared as a kind of Victorian gothic character behind a lectern reading Dada poetry accompanied with pyrotechnics and fire breathing. Taylor Woodrow is best known for The Triptych Man and The Living Paintings, made between 1985 and 88. The Triptych man was a performance for three people who were inserted into a costume made from three men’s suits sewn together so that it had only three arms and legs each wide enough for two people’s limbs. The wearing of this forced the performers into a triangular formation, facing outwards, bound to and supporting each other but unable to see each other and only able to move by bouncing. The Triptych man performed for several hours at a time, never speaking but often approaching people and sometimes remaining still for long periods while the performers rested. The Living Paintings was static work for three performers, who were hung from the wall by a concealed harness wearing suits, painted a solid colour with matching makeup on any exposed skin and hair and contact lenses that featured an enlarged cornea with a white iris. These performers also remained silent, relying for effect on inspiring embarrassment, uncertainty, and a desire to communicate in the viewer. 

!Wolf Vostell.

Vostell, a German action artist working in the 1980s, organised large public performances in German cities, often involving pyrotechnics and explosives. His interventions into public life were deliberately meant to refer to terrorist actions, and were intended to be disruptive although the formal and sculptural qualities of his actions were as important as their political aspect. For one action he released a large amounts of smoke at a specific sites and times. He led a group of people about a city ending in a derelict excavated site. His ambitious works were extensively documented and Vostell wrote in detail about each one. 

!Sylvia Ziranek.

British performance artist who made a number of works in the 1980s and has also published written work. Ziranek's invented persona was central to her work and this was carried through into her appearance – she always dressed in bright pink and often has pink hair. 

!!INSTALLATION ART.



!Sophie Calle.

A French artist born in the 1950s who has made a large body of project and research based work. Calle's Following Piece is perhaps her best known work, a performative project made in Venice for which she selected a man to follow for a month. He never became aware of her constant presence until the project was over since Calle adopted the techniques of undercover surveillance in her project, keeping a detailed diary with photographs, which she made into a series of text panels and presented as a documentary installation. For another incognito work, Chambermaid, presented as text and image on the wall, Calle got a job as a chambermaid in a hotel. She searched the rooms and luggage that she cleaned and made lists of what she found and what she deduced about the occupants of the room – the kind of people they were, what their occupations were, why they were staying in the hotel. She photographed the rooms and things in them that interested her. Calle's interest in these works was in what the viewer could discover about an absent subject from the visual world that surrounds them and in how that subject can be reconstituted. The viewer of the work is made aware of their position as voyeur which they share with the artist. Calle subsequently continued her interest in subjectivity but began to work with people rather than observing them unseen. The Blind involved asking blind from birth and those who had lost their sight since birth to describe images. These descriptions are presented in place of images on the walls of a gallery giving a privileged place to the images of the mind's eye, images that would never normally become concrete. For a similar work made with museum attendants Calle asked those who worked in a certain museum to describe the paintings that they observed daily in the course of their employment when those paintings were not present because they were on loan. The resulting descriptions were placed on the wall in the place of the loaned exhibit. The discrepancies in the descriptions made the viewers subjectivity very obvious; descriptions of the same work would vary greatly, placing an object in a painting that was not there for example, adding something new to a painting or changing the composition of the colours. 

!Guillaume Bijl.

Belgian artist Bijl is known for his perfect recreations of very ordinary spaces. Each installation he creates in a gallery is a recreation down the last detail of a particular place, for example a gym, a supermarket, a beauty salon, another gallery and a bookshop. These spaces would be usable for their projected purpose, such is the attention to detail, but their remain functionless and empty, and such is their purpose. They are three dimensional facsimiles, all the more unreal for being so real. 

!Christian Boltanski.

Boltanski is a German artist who made large scale installation and site specific works and is particularly interested in socio political issues, in the memorial, and in the legacy left for the living by the dead. One work was sited in a gap in a terrace of houses, a bombed out house never rebuilt. Boltanski discovered the names of the people who had lived in the house and inscribed them on the walls that still retained details of the interior of the house such as fireplaces, wall paper and paint. Another work consisted of a larger number of leather suitcases that referred to the luggage left by those killed during the holocaust. 

!Sonia Boyce.

British artist working in the 1980s onwards who made work dealing with racial awareness and black identity. 

!Ian Breakwell.

A British artist whose career began in the 1970s, Breakwell is best known for his written diaristic work. He made a number of pieces with large amounts of densely handwritten text, often detailing his own life and observations about life. He also used photographs. In the 1980s he made a performance in which the audience was confronted with a stage curtain. When it opened they were faced with a painted audience and the back of a proscenium arch, and in fact they themselves were on stage. 

!Jan Fabre.

Belgian artist Fabre is very interested in insects and since the 1970s he has been making large scale theatrical performances usually featuring himself and others dressed as insects or in costumes covered with insects. Fabre also makes objects and paintings encrusted with insects, particularly beetles, the dark iridescence of their wings and shells fascinates him. 

!Ovind Fahlstrom.



!Sylvie Fleury.

French artist working from the 1980s onwards. Fleury is interested in consumerism and in the relation between fashion and desire. One work consisted of shopping bags from different high fashion shops, placed in a casual arrangement on a plinth so that the bags themselves took on a significance beyond their actual existence as carrier of luxury goods, to even transcend this, so clearly did their names, logos, colours and designs advertise a particular way of life, style and aspiration. Fleury also made works with shoes. 

!Susan Collins.

UK artist who pioneered the use of holograms in art in the early 1980s, Collins later worked with sound digital technology and the internet. 

!Ian Hamilton Finlay.

Scottish artist who since the 1970s has made, and continues to make site specific outdoor installations. Hamilton Finlay carves text into stone panels and blocks, inscribed in simple classical fonts, single words and phrases that relate to landscape in which they are placed. His intention is to integrate words with landscape. These installations are sometimes small scale but may also be installed over a large area of land, such as in a park often as a permanent work. Hamilton Finlay's work is probably best known as photographic documentation. 

!David Hammons.

Artist whose work investigates cultural and ethnic identity. 

!Mona Hatoum.

Hatoum is a British artist born in the 1950s in Lebanon who makes installation and video work. Hatoum’s work investigates the extremes of human nature and cruelty, alluding to the repressive practises of certain regimes in the Middle East and their effect on the life of women in particular. In an early video work the veiled face of a woman has the shadows of Arabic text falling across her face and a certain point a knife is drawn across her eyes as if to cut them open. A later installation consists of a long totally dark corridor constructed within a gallery, at the end of which is dimly visible a red glow. The curious viewer will walk along the corridor and the glow becomes brighter, revealing itself to be several vertical bars of red light. Only at close proximity does it become apparent that the light source is electric heater elements. The effect of this on the viewer is to give the sense that they might burn themselves by being lured towards the red hot bars. Another installation employing a similar economy of means consists of three banks of wire mesh lockers, arranged to create a ‘room’. Hanging from the ceiling is an unshaded electric light bulb which slowly descends and rises into and out of the locker space. The shadows cast by the wire grid construction of the lockers grow large and shrink in the most sinister fashion. In the late 1990s Hatoum moved on from the almost theatrical effects created by these installations to create more video work. In a video installation called Deep Throat displayed in a wooden kiosk, Hatoum showed a film made by a medical camera travelling through the various orifices of a woman’s body. Like all her work the visceral impact of this work leads to secondary reflections on imprisonment and torture. 

!Susan Hiller.

British artist born in the 1950s, Hiller makes installations usually with video which is often projected, works on paper and often three dimensional objects and structures. She is interested myth, religion, ritual, literature, grand narrative, and psychoanalysis. Belshazzar's Feast made in the late 1980s takes the biblical reference of its title as a starting point for one work. A sitting room ‘set’ with sofas, coffee table, lamps and sideboard contains a television where the fireplace would be. On the television is a film of showing a burning fire, as if to illustrate that television is the hearth around which we gather. On the wall is automatic writing (a reference to the writing on the wall by the unseen hand that takes place at Belshazzar’s Feast) done by Hiller. Another work features giant shadow puppets of Punch and Judy and other folkloric characters, made into a film projected to cover all four walls of a gallery space, the silhouetted characters looming and distorted. Hiller made a site specific work at the Freud Museum in London (the home of Sigmund Freud) for which she presented a series of cardboard boxes containing text and objects and made photographs of Freud’s ethnographic collection. 

!Jenny Holzer.

Holzer works in a variety of media using text. During the 1980s Holzer made a series of works featuring slightly altered popular sayings that she titled Truisms. These were political statements that subverted commonly used truisms to make points about consumerism, subjectivity and art. The Truisms featured on T shirts, till receipts in shops, park benches, digital display boards above Times Square and Piccadilly Circus, LED displays in public places, posters and leaflets. Holzer employed the various methods of public display and dissemination of information for the presentation of her work. 

!Stewart Home.

British artist and writer who has written about punk, art and performance, including writing and photographing performer Cosey Fanni Tutti. Home has also made work on paper and installation, such as the Art Bed, an ordinary single bed with a bedspread on which the viewer is invited to recline. 

!Rebecca Horn.

Artist born in the 1950s who makes sculptures that are largely concerned with the human body. Horn’s work is often presented as installations and she sometimes makes kinetic work. An example of this is apiece called The Conductor, which is a white non sighted person’s stick suspended in space over a metal plate. An invisible magnetic field causes the stick to dance spasmodically, making a tapping and scraping sound when it touches the metal plate. It seems to be wielded by an invisible person, and appears quite threatening to the viewer. A piano hangs from the ceiling, its insides exploding out periodically, catching the viewer unaware as the lid springs open and all the strings sound and the hammers slide out. Another work consists of a large number of hoses strewn over a whole exhibition space, all leading to something which is operated by an air bladder. The hoses are full of compressed air and the whole operation seems to be random. A work which is visually similar to this is a dressmaker's dummy holding a dress made from transparent tubing which is full of red liquid, which is pumped around the dress as if the tubes were an arterial system. Horn has also made a dress, again displayed on a dummy, from large pieces of meat sewn together. Much of Horn’s work involves the construction of strange garments, such as the gloves with one metre long pointed finger extensions. She often makes work which impedes and transforms the function of the body, or places its inner workings on the outside. Her work has a fairy tale atmosphere that is quite frightening. 

!Ilya and Emilia Kabakov.

The Kabakovs are a Russian artist couple who have made a large number of installations which comment on Russian (both Soviet and post soviet) social order, and on the position of the individual in a totalitarian society. The Kabakov's work is often optimistic, sometimes didactic and engenders a feeling of the possibility of escape in the viewer. A work made in the late 1980s consists of a bedroom constructed inside a gallery space, the structural walls entirely freestanding so that the viewer is presented with a wooden box. Through the open door the viewer can see into the room which is full of books and comic books and magazines, a bed and some rudimentary furniture. A life size realistic dummy of a aircraft pilot who has apparently ejected from a plane has parachuted through the ceiling, bring the ceiling down and spreading rubble over the tiny room. The pilot is suspended, his parachute harness caught in the roof. The Kabakov’s also made a very large scale installation based on Vladimir Tatlin's tower, a spiral construction. This installation consists of the tower, and several interlinked spaces with doors and windows, a kind of maze the whole of which is roughly constructed of unpainted light coloured wood. Viewers enter the structure and negotiate the spaces, eventually climbing to the top of the tower on its helter skelter like steps. At the top of the tower and in the rooms are various objects, images and texts to contemplate, often placed on school desks. There is quite a lot to read. 

!Karen Knorr.

British artist born around 1960 whose conceptual text and photographic image work is concerned with the interrogation of the institution. 

!Jannis Kounellis.

Greek artist who was very successful in the 1980s with a number of large installations to do with the relation of cultural history to the modern world. Kounellis used the image of the horse quite a lot, as well as working in steel construction and using soot or smoke like pigment. 

!Barbara Kruger.

Kruger made a seminal body of work feminist and political in the 1980s using photographic collage and text. Using skills developed in her paid job in graphic design Kruger began to juxtapose generic images with generic phrases and slogans that she slightly altered to make a political point, appropriating graphic styles from Russian constructivist art and socialist graphics. For example, a female hand with long immaculately manicured nails in black and white with the slogan in bold Helvetica black ‘protect me from what I want’ diagonally across the image. A closed eye appropriated from a surrealist photograph is captioned ‘I am your almost nothing’. One of her most copied slogans is ‘I shop therefore I am’. Kruger’s skill was in the juxtaposition of slogan and image producing a powerful effect. She rarely used colour but when she did it was almost always red. Her work was produced as postcards and achieved wide dissemination making her one of the best known artists of her generation. 

!Lucia Nogueira.

South American artist born in the 1950s and died in the 1990s who made installations from glass beads and blown glass placed on the floor and hanging. 

!Cady Noland.

American artist who is known for her scatter works in the 1980s in which she placed objects on the floor of a gallery and for her scaffolding pole constructions. 

!Cornelia Parker.

British artist born in the 1950s whose best known work is Cold Dark Matter an Exploded View made in the late 1980s. This work consists of the hundreds of fragments of a garden shed and its contents, destroyed by the artist in a controlled explosion. The fragments are displayed hung from invisible thread to form a shed shape, as if the shed has been frozen in the act of exploding and the fragments stopped in time. For Thirty Pieces of Silver Parker flattened silver ware and other silver plated objects such as a trumpet and suspended the silver on threads to hover just above the floor. A similar work used a steam roller to roll over a trail of silver ware. Parker’s interest in the transformation or destruction and representation of objects continued with a work in which she melted down a gun and recast it as a solider and therefore unusable form, an LP which she reduced to shavings by playing it with a blade instead of a needle, and other works involving dust and shadows. 

!Adrian Piper.

British artist who makes work about ethnicity. 

!Lawrence Wiener.

American artist born in the 1940s who is an influential conceptual artist working exclusively with text. Wiener made a replica of the Rosetta stone that is ten metres across. He made a great number of gallery installations that consist of transfer lettering applied to the wall to his specification, presenting a text that he had written, much or all of which is often crossed through. He also made typed and printed work on paper and paintings of text. Wiener’s work was an investigation into the nature of language and specifically into the written word, taking the postmodern debate about language as a system of signs and signifiers and making it the subject of his work. In Wiener’s work the meaning of the words, the action of reading by the viewer and the visual presence of the words combine to make a work of art. 

!Fred Smith.

American artist working from the 1980s onwards who makes socially engaged installation art. Smith is interested in ethnicity, cultural identity and institutional representations of different cultures. He has often been invited to make installations in museums, for example he has swapped exhibits from ethnographic collections with those from nineteenth century decorative arts collections. 

!Kate Smith.

British artist born around 1960 who makes installation and photographic work, which has sometimes featured shoes. Smith’s work is rooted in her interest in psychoanalytical and feminist theory. 

!Rosemarie Trockel.

Artist born in the 1950s who makes knitted paintings, panels made on a knitting machine using jacquard techniques placed on stretches, as well as balaclavas and gloves. Trockel uses various motifs such as the hounds tooth from the check pattern, and the playboy bunny, both of which appear incongruous when translated into knitting. 

!!FILM AND VIDEO.



!Cheryl Donegan.

Donegan is an American video artist whose work investigates the sculptural and painterly possibilities of everyday activities. In Sunflower, she spins on grass with a bottle of yellow paint attached to her waist, it sprays out and slowly paints a circle. In Kiss My Royal Irish Ass she paints her buttocks green and sits on sheets of paper, three times on each to make a clover leaf painting. In another work she suggestively and lasciviously drinks milk that is spraying slowly from a hole made in the side of a plastic bottle and when her mouth is full she sprays the milk on the wall. In another she holds a French stick between her knees and eats it as far down as she can reach. In many ways Donegan's concerns bear comparison to the work of Sarah Lucas, with her interest in how the banal and the domestic can be both sexually suggestive and formally sculptural and humorous. Donegan also makes reference to abstract expressionism and to the ejaculatory possibilities that can be seen in the work of Jackson Pollock, and mocks the machismo inherent in his work with her own abject attempts. 

!Jack Goldstein.

American artist born in the 1950s who has made a number of 16 mm films and videos. Goldstein’s work often has a formal simplicity that belies his choice of medium; he may choose to film something that is barely there or hardly moves. He adds animation to the film, for example in a film of a willow pattern plate, which simply shows the plate seen face on in a static shot, one of the birds in the pattern comes to life and flies off the plate. In another a figure of a person is outlined in tiny red lights and moves, the lights shining blurrily. 

!Chris Marker.

Very influential British underground filmmaker working from the 1970s onwards, whose work is often a collage of images and film clips. Marker’s films are presented as an installation. For example one work is shown on a tower of monitors placed one on top of the other, the same film playing on each but at a different point, so that the images are never the same. 

!Tony Oursler.

American artist who became known for his video installations in the early 1990s for which he filmed himself and other people talking. He made scarecrow like figures with white pillows for heads, some life size some doll sized. The videos are projected on to the pillows, the projector either wall mounted or placed on the floor directly in front of the figure and are very much part of the work. The figures then ‘speak’ to the viewer, usually stream of consciousness babble, often sounding as if they are talking to some kind of therapist or analyst. Sometimes they become distressed and scream. Although patently unreal, the figures seem very real and the viewer has a poignant attachment to them. In one case the projection is onto a heap of clothes as if the person who inhabited them has gone and there is just a soul remaining. In another a pillow on a sofa might carry the projection, or one squashed under a toppled chest of drawers, or stuffed into a suitcase. The general impression is of tortured souls whispering and babbling in uncomfortable positions. 

!Ulrike Rosenbach.

Artist who made video performance work in the 1980s, of which Spinning is an example. The viewer sees an wobbling apparently abstract circular image of spinning dots, which are reflective. On inspection it can be seen that the image is of a woman (probably the artist) wearing a wide net skirt with large sequins on it, who is spinning in one place, seen from directly above her head with her arms clasped around her torso so that only the skirt and the top of her head are visible. The effect is of a spinning glittering disc. As the spinning continues the unsteadiness of the image becomes puzzling as well as disorientating for the viewer. After ten minutes the woman falls down when she is too dizzy to continue. The viewer is also dizzy. When the woman is still but the image is still unsteady it becomes clear that the film is not shot from above but from the ground into a mirror placed above her head, and that it is the mirror that is wobbling. 

!!SCULPTURE.



!Richard Artschwager.

American artist who made sculptures of furniture in the late 1970s and early 80s, around the same time as Ettore Sottsass's Memphis group was making furniture from laminate in Milan. He made a table from a box covered in different printed laminates to indicate legs and a pink tablecloth. Artschwager also made a series of Chippendale chairs flattened out and laminated in unlikely patterns and designs. In these works he was interested in making unlikely combinations of generic form with generic material, forms from the classical era or from antiquity with modern materials. 

!Edward Allington.

Allington is a British sculptor who was part of a group of artists who were making large scale ambitious work in the 1980s and early 90s. His best known work is Horn of Plenty, a cornucopia of found objects. He uses the cornucopia extensively as motif, and was one of the artists invited to propose a work for the empty plinth in Trafalgar square in London in 2001. 

!Ashley Bickerton.

American Bickerton was part of the Neo Geo group of artists who worked in New York in the 1980s. He made a number of wall mounted sculptures that consisted of closed metal boxes, shallow but projecting some distance from the wall, which he covered with stickers and logos, giving them the appearance of both an item of luggage and a sign, its function entirely ambivalent. The stickers themselves were created by Bickerton and were generic types that had no actual reference in the real world to a company, an instruction or an advertisement. Bickerton also made a work in which a life size rubber shark was hung from the ceiling with block and tackle. Hanging from the ropes were also a number of small yachts fenders along with sealed transparent plastic film bags containing some kind of blue gel. 

!Scott Burton.

American artist who made work with furniture. 

!Tony Carter.

British artist born around 1950 made a number of installation works in the 1980s based around an existing narrative or historical events and heavily theorised, for which he created highly finished elements often including printed text and drawings. One such work contained Carter's own casts of several silver buttons from an eighteenth century character’s jacket. Carter gave detailed attention to the display of the elements of his installations, fabricating highly finished display cases and plinths. 

!Helen Chadwick.

A British artist born in the late 1950s Chadwick made sculptural installations and photographic work, often combining the two. Her work dealt with the female body and ideal beauty. One work consisted of light boxes on plinths with photographic images of luxurious and sensual arrangements of hair, ribbon and entrails, and for example length of intestine, braided together. Chadwick often associated the visceral with the luxurious and the interior of the body with its clothed exterior. She was regarded as a feminist artist and in common with other women artists of her generation produced much work dealing with the female body. Another work consisted of a series of wooden boxes of different shape and dimensions, produced to correspond with the size of her own body at various stages in her life so that the box for her birth is new born child sized, another is curved as if a child were curled up, others are standing adult sized. The boxes have photographic transfers in black ink of images of Chadwick at these different ages and they stand in a group. Around the walls of the space in which the boxes are installed are wooden panels with similar images with Latin texts describing the ages of a woman running around the four edges of the panels. The work for which Chadwick is best known is probably Piss Flowers. These are painted casts taken from the space made in snow when Chadwick and her male partner urinated in it. When cast as a positive this space is revealed to have a central column going down and a petal like circle surrounding it, made by the splashes of hot urine on the snows surface and the main stream melting deep into the snow. The male ‘flower’ has a much wider petal arrangement and a shorter ‘stalk’. The female ‘flower’ is the opposite, a result which fired Chadwick’s interest. The Piss Flowers are a nod to Duchamp’s casts of the negative space of male and female genitalia. Chadwick died in the 1990s. 

!Stephen Cox.

British artist born in the 1950s who made carved stone tondos with classical imagery and inscriptions in the 1980s. 

!Dorothy Cross.

Irish artist who makes work from leather and fabric, a kind of feminist surrealism, for example a corset made from leather tanned from a cow’s udders with the udders sticking out. 

!Grenville Davey.

British artist born around 1960 who made very large cast and lacquered fibreglass sculptures in the 1980s and early 90s. Davey created abstract forms that had a functionality about them, like giant lids, plugs or covers, often round, sometimes pairs that seemed that they might fit together. These were perfectly constructed, painted in shiny cream, grey, or white and placed on the gallery floor, often on their curved sides. 

!Jimmy Durham.

A Native Australian artist who makes junk sculptures, which appear to be fetish objects and often have text applied. Durham’s work is concerned with the displacement of the Aboriginal people and their culture. 

!Brian Falconbridge.

Born around 1950, Falconbridge made very small and detailed finely constructed sculptures, often relying on balance and tension in their construction. 

!Jochen Gerz.

Gerz and his partner realised several public sculptures most notably two holocaust memorials. The Sinking Monument is a lead covered square column which is gradually sinking into the ground on which it stands so that over a number of years it will eventually fill a hole exactly its size and disappear for ever. The soft lead surface invites graffiti, as it is very easy to scratch into and the public are invited in to add to it in a reversal of the customary civic practise of removing any graffiti or making it difficult to carry out. Some people engrave the names of those they lost and the monument will take these names underground and they will remain there forever, a subterranean plinth to a memorial plaque on the surface. The other memorial is sited in front of the Reichstag in Berlin. The Gerzs researched with help from student volunteers into a number of Jewish cemeteries known to have been destroyed and built over. Lists of the names of those buried in these places were obtained. At night they removed cobblestones from the street in front of the building and carved the name of one of the deceased on the bottom. The stones would then be replaced. Eventually all of those whose graves had been lost were symbolically reburied in a permanent and subversive act. 

!Laura Godfrey Isaacs.

British artist born around 1960 who made wall reliefs that eventually led to polyurethane foam sculptures reminiscent of the work of Lydia Benglis. The foam in Godfrey Isaacs’ sculptures is brightly coloured with forms often jokily alluding to the female body. 

!Andy Goldsworthy.

British artist born in the 1950s who makes site specific sculptures placed in the landscape. Goldsworthy began in the 1980s to make small sculptures from leaves and twigs, which he would photograph in the landscape. Sometimes he arranged leaves, for example grading the different colours of fallen autumn leaves and arranging them in a pattern or floating them on water. He also used growing plants, staking the branches of willow trees to form a living structure, weaving twigs into circles. He then also used snow to make sculptural forms. Goldsworthy’s work makes reference to the history of ritual and nature and the resulting artefacts.
