Gradually Goldsworthy’s work became larger scale as he travelled widely in search of new landscapes to work with, for example to Alaska. 

!Antony Gormley.

British artist born in the 1950s who became very well known in Britain when he made a large scale public commission called The Angel of the North. This thirty meter high sculpture is a standing figure with no facial features or detail. Its outstretched arms have become aeroplane wings, the arms spanning about forty meters. The sculpture, which is made from steel is sited by the motorway and railway line from London to Scotland. It can be seen for miles around, and is probably the most visible and best known piece of public art in Britain. Gormley made lead casts of parts of his body for a number of years in the 1980s and 90s. He then began to cast his whole body and make sculptures from beaten moulded and welded lead sheet. The resulting figures are life size and have no detail or features. Where the lead sheets meet they are seamed as if they are made of stitched together pieces of fabric. Some are prone and some are standing. Gormley has conducted a most thorough investigation into the male human form, using a visual anonymity to allow the figures to stand for every person. Gormley became known to a much wider public when he made the participatory work Field in the early 1990s. Field consists of hundreds of forty cm high clay figures, roughly alike, placed shoulder to shoulder in a mass on the floor of the gallery. Field was partly inspired by the terracotta army of seventh century Chinese sculptures found in a burial ground. Wherever field is exhibited it is created anew by local people, each person making their own figure from local clay. 

!Dan Graham.

American artist working in from the 1970s onwards, Graham makes architectural sculptures, usually site specific, from glass and mirrors, simple structures that invite the viewer to enter them. The reflections or the surrounding landscape and of the structure itself cause a sense of disorientation, in particular in terms of which parts of the sculpture are transparent and which are reflective so that there is a confusion about what is a real image and the nature of that reality. The structures are extremely simple and formal, usually human height, often no more that a series of vertical planes placed together at angles and ramps. They are usually placed in the open air as the light of the sky plays an important role in the reflectivity that is part of the work. 

!Thomas Grunefeld.

German artist born in the 1950s known for his stuffed animal sculptures in which different parts of animals are combined to form one hybrid creature that could never exist. Grunefeld uses highly skilled taxidermists to renders these creatures objectively completely convincing as if they were the result of some kind of perverse genetic engineering project. The animals, for example a roster’s feet on a furry mammals body with the tail of a lizard, could have no imaginable purpose, thus they seem like contemporary fantastic beasts. 

!Shirazeh Houshiary.

British artist who makes sculptures, often of metal, with references to Islamic architecture. The surfaces of the sculptures are often etched with Arabic text and Islamic patterning. 

!Bethan Huwys.

Welsh artist who makes very small scale sculptures from natural materials. 

!Neil Jeffries.

British artist who has made folded and painted sheet metal sculptures of people and vehicles from the 1980s onwards which have a toy like, mad inventor quality. 

!Anish Kapoor.

A British artist who began in the late 1970s to make works using pigment, Kapoor was first known for his varying shaped piles of very bright and densely coloured pure pigment arranged on the floor of the gallery. Kapoor then began to make spherical and other shaped structures that he placed on the floor and attached to the wall, the interior of which were coated with pigment. The intense matt quality of the dry powder sucked in light creating oddly disorientating effects and tricks of the eye, something like the effects created by the light works of James Turrell. He also made some work in which large boulders of rock were drilled with cavities filled with pigment. Kapoor made an extremely large installation for a London museum which consisted of a 50 metre high 200 metre long trumpet like structure that tapered into a tube running the length of the museums entrance hall and curved into a second funnel. The work was made with a steel frame attached to which is a dark red rubber skin stretched to a smooth tautness. 

!Jeff Koons.

American artist, formerly a banker, born in the late 1950s who became known in the 1980s for his photographic works based on advertisements. Koons invented magazine advertisements based on existing ones and had them professionally photographed featuring himself as the model. He then placed these ads in art magazines in effect advertising himself, a strategy was intended to show that an artist and a career could be invented and promoted. Having achieved this Koons began to make sculptural works. He first made a marble bust of himself, which he had carved by the best stone carver he could find in white marble with a slight sparkle to it. Koons was one of number of new York artists making work that became known as Neo Geo. His first works were objects in glass cases, for example a baseball, apparently suspended in mid air inside a vitrine on a plinth, and a number of pristine vacuum cleaners in a large case (a pun on the idea of the airless vacuum). Koons then began to design sculptures based on trinkets and souvenirs enlarged to many times their original size and presented on plinths, works which called attention to the status of the work of art, its value, the skill of its making and its authorship, because Koons rarely made anything himself. Instead he ordered his works to be made to his detailed plans by the best crafts people he could find in the medium that interested him. The first was a serried of porcelain sculptures for example one of Michael Jackson reclining with a poodle, some large flowers taken from a children’s model set, and one of an enlarged children’s Cabbage Patch doll. The sculptures were made by the best porcelain artists (in eastern Europe), hand painted, gilded and extremely kitsch but very striking due to their size (about one metre high usually). Koons was interested in how an object of little value other than sentimental value which may however have cost the owner quite a lot (the nature of souvenirs) might be transformed into an art object of immense value despite its bad taste, lack of traditional fine art values and lack of the artists hand in its making. Koons made several groups of works in a similar way, for example cast metal which was highly chromed (a train set which is a decanter glasses and coaster holder, a balloon rabbit), and polychromed wood vases of flowers, extremely detailed pieces of virtuoso carving made in Germany by master carvers. He later made a number of extremely large site specific works, the flower sculptures, for which he again borrowed the talents of others. The first of these was a twenty metre high Scottie dog covered with flowers the colours of which in some way functioned like the dots on a billboard poster. In the mid 1990s Koons married la Cicciolina an Italian porn actress and made a series of photographic works which were styled as stills from a porn film, using professional stylists and photographers once again. The images had a degree of shock value since some featured clearly visible penetration. Koons and Cicciolina stared out at the viewer. The images were odd hybrids of pornography and portrait. After a much publicised divorce from la Cicciolina Koons began in the late 1990s to make two or three metre high paintings of enlarged parts of things, such as balloons, mixtures of toys, household objects and natural things, so enlarged and partially visible as to be hard to interpret. These are painted by his studio assistants to Koons’s designs. 

!Richard Long.

British artist born in the 1950s, Long is a land artist and as such his site specific work exists mostly as documentation. Long began by making walks and documenting them with photographs. He would then mount these photographs relate them to sections of large scale survey maps showing the walk and the north south orientation, and print below them the distance walked as proportion of the total distance of the walk. In doing so he was developing ideas put into practice by American land artist Dennis Oppenheim in the 1960s and 70s, and anther artist who walked along coastlines, the walk, he declared, making a drawing of a line, the line being the demarcation between land and water. This idea of ‘taking a line for a walk’ has been explored by several artists. Long's walks were made all over Britain and then in other countries, they were mostly quite short. In Longs work the land itself, the ground on which we tread and the landscape it forms, is the subject as is artist's subjectivity in terms of choice of and experience of the walk. Long developed his interest in the ground underfoot into an interest in the materials this comprised of and brought clay, earth and stone into the gallery in various forms. He made a number of temporary wall paintings made of mud and clay, thrown at the wall or splashed on with handprints, the mess and drips on the surrounding floor and wall allowed to remain. Long made a circle of white clay splashes on a dark painted wall, or a horizontal band of dark mud on a white wall. He also made rock floor sculptures, which make reference to the work of Carl Andre and Robert Smithson. A piece of the rock from a particular landscape was cut into slices and then fragmented into enormous jagged shards laid out to fill a solid circle or band on the floor, in a sculptural reference to rock strata and man made intervention. 

!David Mach.

British artist born in the 1950s, Mach became known when he made large sculptures from meticulously stacked magazines. These monumental scale sculptures developed and Mach began to use other materials, for example a submarine made from scrapped car tyres, a train made from stacked bricks. Constructed with no fixings, the sculptures have a casual appearance that belies the complexity of their construction, needed to prevent them from collapsing. The sculptures gain their intrinsic strength from the way the stacks are formed and interlinked, but they remain potentially un fixed and are impermanent despite their bulk and weight. 

!Mario Merz.

Italian painter born in 1940s who made sculptures and wall based works which incorporated some illuminated neon tubing. For example and igloo like structure with neon on floor. Paintings with neon arrows. 

!Carl Plackman.

British sculptor who made assemblages with natural materials and steel, particularly favouring pebbles. 

!Sabin, Andrew.

British sculptor who made work with coloured hanging chains. 

!Kiki Smith.

American artist born in the 1940s who makes sculpture characterised by her use of ephemeral and delicate materials like paper, fabric, and beads. Smith often makes life size human figures, animals and birds from paper; sometimes the figures are wearing paper clothes. Her work describes the condition of femininity and of childhood. A naked female figure lies on her side on the ground, strings of glass beads issuing from between her legs as if toe represent blood. A peacock’s tail on the wall has the detail drawn on in pen. like all of Smiths work these are made from white paper stuck together and varnished, so that the final appearance is of a translucent yellowed skin, three dimensional forms which are hollow skins, fragile and ephemeral. 

!Haim Steinbach.

American artist born in the 1950s who became well known in the 1980s for his work including small sculptures on shelves, using cast metal, plastic and laminate. 

!Richard Wentworth.

British artist born in the 1950s who makes sculpture and photographic work. Wentworth observes the everyday life of and use objects and makes sculptures that observe the way in which one thing can be used for another using aluminium buckets, tools, ladders and rope. One work made in the 1980s is called Sinking of the Belgrano and consists of a model battleship in an aluminium bucket on a false surface. Another is a book with a large number of sweet wrappers inserted between the pages in an exaggeration of the potential use of one as a bookmark. For twenty years Wentworth has been photographing the area around Caledonian Road in London where he lives every day. He has a particular interest in the interaction of handcraft, artisanship and making do with the built environment. His collection includes documentation of changing shop fronts and names, appropriate, unusual or inappropriate uses for street furniture, railings and doorways, graffiti, litter and makeshift solutions to broken objects and parts of buildings. Wentworth has also made a large collection of photographs of the way one object is used for a new purpose, for example a saucer wedged in to keep a window open. 

!Alison Wilding.

British artist who makes sculptures from transparent acrylic sheet, slotted together to form complex three dimensional grid structures that take advantage of the way that the edge of the acrylic can be seen as lines in space. Wilding’s sculptures are solid forms that give the impression of being light and delicate. She also makes working drawings which are in effect technical perspective drawings for the sculptures. 

!Hermione Wilshire.

British artist born in the 1950s who makes installations and sculpture. Wilshire has often worked with glass. She blows large globules and droplets of glass and places them over photographs of parts of the human body which are then viewed through the glass, magnified and glowing. One work consisted of glass droplets extruded into stalks which were fixed projecting form the wall, on the bottom of which were fixed photographs of finger prints, as if these had been left in the air. Another consisted of a large number of variable sized glass pebbles on the underneath of which were attached photographs of penises, arranged on the floor as if they had been scattered in a allusion to ejaculation. 

!Bill Woodrow.

British artist born in the 1950s who made installations from collections of waste plastic bottles and containers, arranged by colour and shape on the wall or floor to create an image. Woodrow also made a series of works using discarded refrigerators, washing machines. He cut the form of an object for example a guitar from the metal walls of a washing machine and by bending and folding the metal coaxed it into a convincing representation which remains attached to the washing machine. He also fashioned a broken umbrella into a crow. 

!!PAINTING.



!Craigie Aitchison.

Aitchison is a Scottish painter best known for his 1980s paintings of his pet Bedlington terrier. The dogs are painted in profile on large brightly and flatly coloured canvases. 

!Michael Andrews.

British painter. 

!Basil Beattie.

A figurative painter of the Glasgow school born in the late 1930s Beattie painted fishing boats, ship builders and chip shops lyrically and colourfully. 

!Ida Applebroog.

Applebroog's paintings and free standing works from the late 1980s and early 90s are concerned with memory, childhood and narrative. She often uses the predella form, and uses an image of a film negative as a framing device. The paintings are often constructed from several canvases butted together and freestanding works are propped on the floor upright or placed horizontally. Applebroog made a film with her daughter, film maker and artist Beth B, which is a loose narrative of unhappy childhood with a dreamlike quality. 

!Art and Language.

British artist duo born around 1950 known for their conceptual work text paintings. 

!John Baldessari.

American artist working in the 1970s and 80s who made photo collages typically consisting of black and white photographed images with bold graphic lines and forms added in colour. 

!Jean Michel Basquiat.

Graffiti artist from the Bronx in New York, Basquiat was born in 1973 and died in 1998. He was discovered by a dealer who fostered him and provided him with studio and materials, enabling Basquiat to paint on canvas. He became hugely successful, his work commanded high prices and he was feted by the art world for his work, his looks and his rebelliousness. Basquiat used graffiti tags combined with idiosyncratic signs and symbols creating a way of making a painting that successfully combined street and Afro Caribbean consciousness with abstraction. The outline of a crown, a reference to his own self determined kingship, was his signature, the skull often appeared. He mostly painted in black and bright colours on white ground, drawing in outline and rarely filling in, sometimes used white on black to give a blackboard effect. The apparent naïve style used to issue a series of commands and demands on the world and his viewers, which are both egotistical and irresistible. Unable to cope with his success he became addicted to heroin and died of overdose before he was thirty. Andy Warhol befriended Basquiat towards the end of his life and they made large collaborative paintings together. Basquiat was later mythologised in a feature film of his life made by artist turned director Julian Schnabel. 

!Glen Baxter.

A British artist born in the late 1940s whose cartoon drawings accompanied by text depict events, happenings and people that illustrate a typical Englishness with self deprecating humour, and surreal, slapstick narrative. These cartoons were used by advertisers in the 1990s and Baxter’s work became extremely widely known. 

!Ross Bleckner.

Bleckner was one of a number of painters in working in New York in the 1980s and early 90s who were making monumental scale works. He had a solo exhibition at the Guggenheim in 1992 in which he showed a number of abstract works that looked like starry pieced domes, wavy stripes with ribbon rosettes dotted among them and fuzzy chandelier and flower like compositions. Bleckner's work is sometimes interpreted as having a memorial quality. 

!Eric Bulatov.

Russian artist working in the 1980s and 90s who combined socialist realist imagery, blue skies, and fields with large text painted across the image. 

!Simon Callery.

British artist born around 1960 who made a series of white paintings of banal urban landscapes in the early 1990s. These relied on impasto and tiny amounts of colour in the white to create an almost intangible image. 

!Vija Celmins.

Artist born in the 1940s who has made a large number of paintings and drawings of the sky and the ground. Celmins paints on small landscape format canvases about sixty cm wide or slightly more, using very small brushes or makes drawings of a similar size using pencil. In either case she will draw the most minimal view – a patch of dust on the ground with a few tiny chips of gravel becoming as important as if it were the surface of the moon. She has also drawn the surface of the moon. A tiny part of the night sky becomes a deep blue painting of tiny diamonds. Celmins work brings the minute and the gigantic together but the works have a deceptively modest unassuming appearance. 

!Francesco Clemente.

Italian American painter born in the late 1940s who was extremely successful in the 1980s. Clemente makes figurative painting and works on paper, as well as small scale sculpture and ceramics. His work shows his interest in Indian art, in its colours and in its often spiritual themes. 

!Beryl Cook.

British artist born in the 1940s, usually termed ‘naïve’. Cook paints ordinary women, often rather large both in size and in personality, going about their daily life. Her paintings are humorous and have a strong narrative and simple appeal. Cook is extremely well known in Britain. 

!Enzo Cucchi.

Italian artist who made large scale drawings of classical nudes using an obsessive cross hatched style for which he became very well known in the 1980s. 

!Rita Donagh.

British artist who makes geometric drawings. 

!Paul Eachus.

British painter. 

!Gerard Hemsworth.

British painter born in the 1940s who has taken generic graphic forms and visual jokes as his subject matter. Hemsworth's paintings are made with entirely flat smooth colour and hard edged lines. Typically he will choose one or several diagrammatic representations, such as a cloud, a rabbit or a vase for example, simplify this to the minimum number of lines required and paint it on a pale coloured ground in black. 

!Marlene Dumas.

Dutch painter who makes large figurative works on paper using water colour that usually depict women. 

!Carroll Dunham.

Abstract paintings made from the 1970s to the 2000s, brightly coloured but using a lot of white and the forms outlined in black, featuring blob and disc shapes, painted thickly, like lumps or pompoms. 

!Nicole Eisenman.

American artist who was one of the so called ‘bad girls’, women artists working in the 1990s in New York, using pornographic or otherwise potentially offensive imagery in their work. Eisenman made wall sized paintings depicting pseudo classical scenes. 

!Rainer Fetting.

German painter born in the 1950s who made large figurative paintings, for example one of a row of men in the shower, painted in blues and whites, in an expressionistic style. 

!Gunther Forg.

German artist born in the 1950s who makes paintings, photographs and bronze sculpture. Forg typically places a two meter high black and white photograph in a frame butted up against an abstract painting of a similar size, consisting of very broad straight single brushstrokes and rectangular areas of colour in for examples orangey red and green. His photographs are very often of women (head and shoulders) who look serious. Forg’s bronze sculptures are the same size as the paintings and photographs, in fact they could be described as sculptures of paintings. The bronzes are about thirty cm deep and two metres high rectangular panels standing, in portrait format, on bases which slope straight down from, and are one piece with the panels. The surface of the bronze is covered with impasto marks presumably made in clay with a palette knife, as if to indicate that this might be a cast of a painting. These monolithic objects are placed around the gallery space in relation to the works on the wall. 

!Rebecca Fortnum.

British artist born in the 1960s whose abstract paintings made in the 1980s and 90s have been described as feminist abstraction. Fortnum often painted a cut or slit as a metaphor for the female body, the interior, a metaphor employed by many women artists in the 1980s and early 90s. This arose from readings of Lacan, a French psychoanalyst whose ideas about ‘lack’ in the female psyche and about language and femininity were taken up by feminist artists and theorists. Fortnum was also interested in a general discussion about the female body as a ‘text’ (in the sense of the term used in literary theory) and the ‘writing out’ or performing of that text being essential to the representation of femininity. Fortnum’s paintings were intended to symbolise this lack, and the writing out of the female, by the painted slit or cut, (never an actual cut) and by text she wrote on the canvas, sometimes unreadable writing like marks, sometimes actual words. Fortnum’s paintings are very large, up to three metres high, and she predominantly used dark reds and browns, fairly flat colours except where she chose to paint the slit, which is made with very thick paint squeezed and brushed. Writing is done with a small brush in a flowing hand, usually a single line of text running horizontally across the painting. 

!Gillian Ayres.

British artist who made brightly coloured, abstract impasto paintings throughout the 60s to the 90s. 

!Pamela Golden.

British painter. 

!Maggi Hambling.

British artist who began painting portraits in the 1960s. 

!Keith Haring.

American artist, now dead, who began as a graffiti artist in the early 1980s in New York. Haring developed a distinctive graphic style, using bold lines to outline his figures, animals and symbols, and filling them in with plain flat colour. Once he became known, Haring began to make his work on canvas and made a lot of printed works, books and ultimately merchandise. Today Haring’s simple lexicon of symbols and figures is instantly recognisable. He always outlined them in black and filled the background in with small marks, painting hearts, hands, animals and figures which rarely have features. One of his best known images is of a red heart on a yellow background, the heart surrounded by lines as if to indicate that it is beating. Haring most often used the primary colours yellow and red, and black. Haring made a lot of political posters about AIDS, and his graphic style was particularly suited to campaigning on various issues. 

!Tim Head.

Since the late 1970s Head has made work in a variety of media. He has often focused on generic and graphic imagery which he has abstracted and reconstructed in various ways, both as sculptural form, painting and works on paper. In the 2000s Head began to work with digital animations, using the pixels that usually constitute the image of video and manipulating their colours to create abstract colour fields of constantly changing coloured squares. These are projected very large, and make immediate visual connection with pointillism and with the dot matrix printing process familiarised by advertising hoardings. Head has created a moving abstract work from pixels, literally the stuff that images are now made from. 

!Peter Howson.

British artist who makes paintings and drawings, travelling to several war zones as a war artist. 

!John Hoyland.

British painter who makes figurative paintings. 

!Jorg Immendorff.

German artist Immendorff achieved success in the 1980s with his expressionist twentieth century history paintings often featuring the eagle of the third Reich, nightclub and café scenes and musicians. Immendorff's earlier work had been performative: he invented a concept called Lidl, which consisted of an imaginary town and trailed a brick with the world Lidl on it in and out of shops. Immendorff was arrested by the police a few times for his performance activities. 

!Callum Innes.

Innes is best known for his work white on white abstract paintings made in the early 1990s. 

!Bert Irvin.

British painter born in the 1930s who made large abstract paintings usually by pouring very brightly coloured paint onto a white ground, sometimes only a few stripes of red yellow, green and blue paint across the bottom corners of a canvas. 

!Vanessa Jackson.

British artist born around 1950 who makes abstract paintings. 

!Ben Johnson.

British photo realist painter born in the late 1950s. 

!Joy Clair.

British painter born in around 1960 who painted skies with clouds in the 1980s and later made pattern paintings. 

!Alex Katz.

American painter who has made large figurative paintings since the early 1970s on the subject of landscape and his family. Katz paints with flat acrylic paint, making each work in a day applying the paint simply and with no texture. Katz painted a row of autumnal trees, trees in blossom, his wife (repeatedly), his children and groups of friends, all shown in casual poses. The photographic source of Katz's work is deliberately evident because he is interested in highlighting that conjunction of distance and intimacy. 

!John Keene.

British painter chosen to record the Gulf War for Britain. Keene later travelled to Afghanistan to record the war there, having become very interested in the depiction of armed conflict. 

!Bhupen Khakur.

Artist born around 1930 who is one of the few Indian contemporary painters to become well known outside of India. Khakur’s figurative paintings depicted his life, often intimately, and included sexual acts with male partners. Khakur showed India in a contemporary way unhampered by the history of Indian art but at the same time he made this a part of his work, which shows in particular the influence of Indian miniatures. Khakur died 2003. 

!Anselm Kiefer.

Keifer is a German expressionist painter who has made paintings and sculpture, both on a large scale, tending towards the monumental from the 1970s onwards. He is often considered to be one of the most important painters of the 1980s because he evolved a contemporary purpose for expressionistic painting and because of the operatic scale of his work. Keifer’s paintings generally depict bare depopulated interiors, for example one that shows an attic like space, which is entirely composed of painted wooden planks. He used a restrained palette of earth colours, black and white with dark reds and blues, and his work often has a atmosphere of darkness and foreboding. Keifer’s sculpture is often presented as an installation, for example a row of hospital beds, the sheets actually made of lead sheet, draped over the beds, in the centre of which are shallow depressions some of which are filled with water, as if to denote the transformation or passing of the occupant of the bed. 

!Martin Kippenberger.

One of the greatest German postmodern painters, born in the 1950s Kippenberger was extremely prolific, producing large and small paintings and objects, usually abstract with collaged elements. He often added stickers and found labels to his paintings. Kippenberger made a series of drawings on hotel notepaper every time he stayed anywhere. He designed all his own invitation cards and other publicity, seeing these as part of his artistic output. 

!Linda Kitson.

British painter and war artist. 

!Leapman David.

British artist who began working in the mid 1980s making paintings which used repeating motifs on a plain ground, the lines themselves removed from the surface of the canvas revealing under the plain flat colour very bright multicoloured painting. He later began to use fluorescent colours. 

!Rosa Lee.

One of generation of British women artists born in the early 1960s who developed a self defined ‘feminist abstraction’ that owed much to Brigit Riley. Their work defined itself by its relation to the 1980s and 90s feminist critique of abstract painting, in particular the claiming of the painting as the body, and painting as being an act of ‘writing the body’. Lee’s work employed repeated decorative elements such as bows and ribbons on a brushstroke ground. 

!Sherrie Levine.

American conceptual artist who became extremely successful in the 1980s and influenced a generation of younger artists with her postmodern appropriation works. Levine’s primary interest is in the status of the work of art and of the reproduction. In this interest makes evident her interest in the work of Marcel Duchamp. Levine made a number of chequer board works painted on wood, a number of paintings in which she simply reproduced a cartoon character in outline (such as Charlie Brown), a number of black and white photographs by famous photographers, and of a marble torso sculpture which she signed, declaring the work to be then authenticated by her and therefore made by her. She made some works using wooden ply in which she utilised the oval insert of ply that fills the hole where there was a knot, as part of the image. Some works using gold leaf dealt with another interest of hers in the perceived versus the intrinsic value of a work of art. Levine’s work is almost exclusively small, no bigger than eighty cm high. Levine’s work essentially borrows the work of other artists, which she either remade (as she did in a number of drawings and watercolours after certain artists, which are then signed and authenticated as by her), or transferred from one art form to the status of work of art. Through appropriation Levine made fakery, plagiarism and false authentication into the subject of her work. 

!Simon Linke.

British artist born around 1960 who became known in the late 1980s for his post modern conceptual painting. Linke reproduced advertisements from art magazines on small canvases about sixty cm high, painting the text very accurately but using thick impasto. He chose to reproduce mostly advertisements printed in black and white, the paintings appearing from a distance to be exactly what they represent. The texture and application of the paint is what makes them paintings, reducing painting to its fundamentals. These works became iconographic, and were seen to represent a particular moment in conceptual art when artists engaged with issues of reproduction, the condition of reality and the status of the subject. Following this work in the late 1990s Linke began to work with text for its own sake, making the paintings in the same way but not reproducing anything. 

!Nicholas May.

A British painter born 1962, May made a series of large paintings by pouring large pools of house paint, while the canvas was on the floor, and tipping them to manoeuvre the paint and allowing them to dry. He often used different colours together, pouring one into the other, wet on wet, including metallic colours and powders, the resulting slick of paint forming the painting. May made a number of these during the 1990s, after which he ceased to make art. 

!Len McComb.

British artist born around 1940. Len McComb mainly makes large scale pencil drawings, for example a sequence of cliff faces on the Anglesey coastline drawn on a number of pieces of paper tiled together to make a wall sized drawing. 

!Humphrey Ocean.

British artist who makes semi abstract paintings. 

!Therese Oulton.

British artist who became known in the 1980s for her thickly encrusted dark coloured semi abstract paintings. 

!Sydney Nolan.

Australian twentieth century painter who is well known for painting the outlaw Ned Kelly, who he represented with a black square for a head. 

!Richard Prince.

Prince is an American artist who took developed the legacy of pop art, from the 1970s onwards to make a variety of types of work. He is most usually associated with two particular works: the Marlboro add and the joke paintings. These illustrate Prince’s prime interest in generic forms. The Marlboro work is a reproduced advertisement for Marlboro cigarettes with the cowboy on the horse removed so that remains is still instantly recognisable because of the brand name, and the type of landscape. It is a while before the viewer realises that the cowboy is not there, since the image is so familiar and this is what they expect to see, revealing clearly how embedded in visual memory the advertisement is. Prince has made joke paintings throughout his career, very simple text paintings telling a joke on a plain coloured canvas in a san serif typeface. The colours, typefaces and size of painting varies up to four metres high. The jokes are mostly quite funny. Prince has also made a large number of paintings using photographic screen printing mixed with painting, and abstract pattern paintings. His prolific output is distinguished by its variety, but also by consistency within that variety. 

!Arnulf Rainer.

German painter ! Paula Rego. British artist who since the 1980s has made semi autobiographical narrative figurative paintings. Rego has a direct style of drawing, and paints loosely but boldly using a generally subdued palette. She often adapts scenes from her own childhood to represent collective rites of passage, or taboo subjects such as incest. Little girls feature in many of her paintings, often with a man wearing military dress, a father figure. Rego is interested in the psychology of her chosen subjects as well as representing an era. 

!Gerhard Richter.

One of the most important twentieth century German artists, Richter made paintings in a variety of style and on a range of subjects but is best known for his exploration of the relation of painting to photography through his execution of 'the blur'. He is highly skilled at making photo realist paintings, which form a large part of his work. Richter’s colour photo realist works include one of his most popular works, a portrait of his daughter seen from behind, wearing a red floral print top with her blonde hair tied back. This painting is made very realistic and the paint is smoothed soft so that there are no visible brushstrokes, emulating a photographs even in its physical surface. He made similar paintings of a lighted candle and a baby. A number of monochrome paintings of blocks of flats are given a more exaggerated blur, as are his numerous black and white paintings. These include one of a chandelier, many landscapes and some trees. Richter made two notable groups of painted photo portraits, all in the same format, one of the Baader Meinhof Gang and the other of German industrialists. These are painted as direct copies from their source, including any text that was attached to the photo and the white edge of the photo. Most of the photo based works are smallish, around one metre high or less. A series of views of mountains are made to look like colour by numbers paintings, as if the image has been reduced to the essentials of light and dark. Richter made some colour chart works, which are immaculately painted multi coloured shiny rectangles of colour within a white grid. Taking the blur to its physical extreme his later work is a series of abstract paintings done with a giant squeegee dragged across the width of the canvas, spreading the various colours of paint into each other in one sweep. Richter wrote a book called The Daily Practise of Painting. 

!David Salle.

One of the best known postmodernist painters, Salle’s paintings during the 1980s defined the term for painting of that period. Salle borrowed elements and styles from paintings from any period in history and mixed them all up on the canvas. His paintings are very large. 

!Julian Schnabel.

Schnabel is an American artist known for the very large paintings (at least four metres high) made in the 1980s onto which he stuck broken crockery, mainly white dinner plates. Schnabel has always worked on a large scale and in 2000 he made a ten metre high painting, painted on the floor, which had footmarks on it. 

!Mira Schor.

American artist who makes abstract paintings that have feminist and psychoanalytic theoretical concerns. Schor’s paintings often contain a painted slit or vaginal motif. 

!Adrian Searle.

British painter and critic who made small abstract paintings until the mid 1990s when he gave up painting to concentrate on writing about art. 

!Philippe Taaffe.

American painter who first became successful in the 1980s with his large three metre high paintings using repeating patterns based on printed and woven fabric designs and geometric abstract drawings. Taaffe’s work exhibits a combination of precision, in the drawing of the pattern, and painterliness, in the application of paint over the whole and uses strong colours, for example yellow with black. 

!Mo Throp.

British painter born in around 1950. 

!Tom of Finland.

Pencil drawings of gay men variously dressed in leather, naked, sometimes in a state of sexual arousal often couples or threesomes, but sometimes alone. They are realistic and detailed, made in the style of the drawings that used to be found in barbershop windows to illustrate hairstyles. Tom of Finland's drawings document specific pleasures and codes of dress in a straightforward manner, but for all their sexually explicit subject matter they have a strange ambiguity in terms of their purpose. 

!Amikam Toren.

British artist born around 1950 who makes works from found paintings onto which he stencils words, cutting out the letters to leave the wall behind the canvas visible. 

!Kyffin Williams.

British artist born in the 1930s who spent his life painting the welsh landscape. Williams’ impressionistic work is made with a very thick impasto, mostly paint applied with a palette knife to create a loose gestural interpretation using bright colours as well as a natural earth palette. The paintings are rarely more than one metre high. 

!Sue Williams.

American painter born around 1960 who makes figurative painting. Her best known work is from the 1980s when she was known as one of a group of women artists described as Bad Girls for their strongly feminist, aggressive and angry work. Pro Porn and Anti Porn, a painting from this period, shows a little girl's prone under a kneeling man seen from the rear, his erect penis and scrotum in full view. There are other similarly explicit drawings on canvas with text. The outline drawings in the paintings are done in black of grey on a coloured grounds. 

!Paul Winstanley.

British artist who makes paintings of shadows cast by window frames, in which the structural elements of roofs, usually in black and white, are softened, the image becoming abstract. By the 1990s digital technology and video had become commonplace. Artists were increasingly working across a range of media, and were not necessarily confined to their studios. Research based practises led artist to engage with institutions and with social groups. Telecommunications and cheap travel made it possible for young artists to have international careers. The art market grew. The number of practising artists increased dramatically. Painting, which had been said to have suffered a dip in popularity in the 1980s, became fashionable again. Artists no longer sought to demonstrate the theoretical concerns underlying their practise. The early part of the decade saw the emergence of so called 'dumbing down', art made as a reaction against the high production and intellectual values of 1980s art. 

!Faisal Abdul’allah.

In 2003 Abdul’allah made an installation that invites the viewer to enter a large box like structure, people with light coloured eyes entering to the left, those with dark eyes to the right. Once segregated by a genetic characteristic, the viewers find themselves in one of two spaces. One is a small room with highly polished metallic floor walls and ceiling in a copper coloured metal that functions like a mirror and creates a glowing light. On the floor is an inscribed text that addresses the viewer personally and asks them to consider their feelings on arrival in this space. The other space, which is the larger remainder of the box like structure, is pitch black. On entry it can be seen that the small room projects into this space, and is in fact constructed from a material that allows one way vision. From the dark space the viewers in the small space can be seen clearly. Although this would seem like a privileged space due to the materials of its construction, the people in it seem to be imprisoned, regarded secretly by the watchers in the larger space. 

!Tomma Abts.

German painter born around 1970 who paints small abstract geometric works using very specific and carefully chosen combinations of colours and shapes. The surfaces of the works show traces of intensive reworking and over brushing, evidence of the time taken to make the paintings. 

!Reverend Ethan Acres.

Ordained clergyman from California who also works as an artist, making live works that combine a religious service with performance, usually involving a sermon, and other performers. 

!Add N to X.

A partnership of two musicians, both born in the 1970s, who made video works in the late 1990s and early 2000s, on example is a continually changing colour grid was controlled by the soundtrack which accompanied it. 

!Shahin Affrassiabi.

A British artist born in 1960, Affrassiabi’s conceptual installations employ household items and materials to make formal sculptural constructions which reference other works of art. 

!Doug Aitken.

American film and installation artist born in 1975. Aitken first worked in commercials and used the experience gained in this field to make a series of multi screen and projected video installations. Diamond Sea featured a deserted mining town in Namibia, which is not marked on state maps to ensure that its location remains secret. A sense of the people who worked there is evoked (and their exploitation implied). Aitken makes his own soundtracks, which form an integral part of the installations, sometimes several films are shown at once resulting in a blend of soundtracks. Another work, a film of the audience attending a travelling cinema in India, was screened in a tented room with sand on the floor. Aitken’s work deals with location and presence, with a sense of place being a priority. 

!Philip Akkerman.

Akkerman makes a self portrait every day of his life, each one in a different style. The paintings are about fifty cm high. 

!Philip Allen.

Allen is a British painter born 1970s whose almost abstract paintings of the early 2000s mark a change from his previous representational work. The paintings have a roughly painted background and a multicoloured scallop motif on top, referencing landscape and abstract painting. 

!Darren Almond.

Almond, a British artist born in the 1970s, is interested in time. He made a large clock of the type that has the 24 hour time display in figures that flip over. It shows the time going very fast so that a minute is a second and an hour is reached in a minute, and is to be mounted in a public institution or railway station. He also made a large aeroplane propeller painted white and mounted it horizontally on the floor of a gallery so that its blades nearly touch the walls leaving just enough room for a person to pass. He set up a live video link between a gallery and an empty prison cell, permitting the viewer a fly on the wall visit to a prison cell. Almond also made a video titled Ghost Train, which is filmed in black and white in a ghost train at a fairground somewhere in Austria. Shot from one of the cars, the film is grainy and visually lyrical as the low light conditions of the video affect what can be seen dramatically. The sound of the train and the sound effects of the ride are edited with a musical soundtrack. Another work is a replica of a bus shelter from a town where near a Nazi concentration camp. 

!Kai Althoff.

Artist born around 1960 who makes bright water colour paintings on paper. 

!Francis Alys.

Born in the 1950s, Alys is a Brazilian artist who made a series of works in the early 1990s by commissioning a sign painter to paint a sign, which he then placed in his studio and made a painting of the scene including the sign. 

!Janine Antoni.

American artist born in the late 1970s who has made a series of performance works and installations. Chocolate Gnaw and Lard Gnaw involved the artist chewing as much chocolate as she could from a one metre cube and melting the resulting chocolate to make chocolate pieces. The lard chewed from a similar sized block was made into lipsticks and the residual blocks are displayed as part of the work. She also cast busts of herself from the chewings. The chocolate and lipstick products were displayed in luxurious packaging in a mirrored and gilt edged display case of the kind that might be seen in a department store. She thereby made a connection between the basic act of chewing and eating, female relationships to food and body image (fat and chocolate) and cosmetics, and the role of packaging in the stimulation of desire. In ‘Harmony Hair Colour’ she used her long dark hair as a brush to make paintings with hair dye on large pieces of paper on the floor, referencing Yves Klein’s blue paintings made using women’s bodies as brushes. ‘Sleep’ was an installation in which Antoni slept at night, wired up to a machine which recorded her REM patterns. She transferred the resulting line on the graph drawn by the machine on a continuous sheet of paper onto a specially constructed wooden loom in the same room and wove on it a white fabric with the sleep patterns as a blue line. The resulting fabric length was as long as the sleep graph, defined by the number of days Antoni slept in the gallery. Antoni made the fabric into a nightgown. 

!Reza Aramesh.

A British Iranian artist born in the 1960s who works in mixed media, made paintings of the branches of trees, and installations with furniture, wallpaper and real stuffed animals. 

!Liz Arnold.

British artist born 1963 who painted women and anthropomorphised cats, very often with flowers, inhabiting story book universes, in strong colours with lots of black in a neat way. Arnold’s creatures inhabit the real world in an unreal way and often seem to be undertaking serious but mysterious business. They are fashionably dressed and wear jewellery and have long eyelashes. In one painting cats are wearing lab coats and there is a fiction atmosphere. Arnold died in 2002. 

!Conrad Atkinson.

British artist. 

!Attila.

Russian figurative painter, born in 1960s who is one of the few Russian artists to be commercially successful in the west during the early 1990s. 

!Fabienne Audeoud.

French artist born in the 1960s, Audeoud’s work is often derived from her interest in classical music, for example a video works shows Audeoud as a diva singing to the point of collapse, and in another she appears as a fading diva on a self destructive binge in her bedroom. There is a spare and often painful quality to these works, which are both abject and comical. For one project Audeoud set herself the task of unlearning everything she knew about playing the piano which took many months. Like many of her works, this was a daily practise. She filmed a brass band rehearsing, whose discordance is at odds with their calm appearance. She has also made several collaborative painting exhibitions with former Bank artist John Russell. These are room sized narrative figurative paintings, quickly and badly made, with text added. 

!Donald Baechler.



!Alex Bag.

American artist who worked in video in the 1990s and made a video about being an art student. 

!Stefan Balkenhol.

German artist who carves large figures from whole tree trunks that are often over three metres high. The figures are carved roughly and all the chisel marks are visible with no attempt being made to smooth the surface of the wood and they are then painted. Balkenhol has made site specific work such as the six metre high figures he placed on wooden piles in the river Thames in London. His carvings depict contemporary people whilst referring both to folk art and to historical polychromed sculpture. 

!Bank.

British collaborative artists John Russell, Millie Thompson and Simon Bedwell, born in the 1960s, worked as Bank in the 1990s until Russell left in 2001. Thompson and Bedwell continued to work as Bank until 2003. Bank produced a large body of painting, sculpture, printed matter and texts as well as curating several seminal group exhibitions. They directed their attentions at the art world making ironic and satirical work underpinned by a detailed knowledge of and love for art history, a sense of humour and a disrespect for the establishment (which could be seen as Bank’s trademark). Bank’s painting spanned a range of styles including highly realistic still life featuring the detritus of their studio done in old master style, ‘bad’ figurative painting, ‘serious’ abstraction, and self portraits. They explored the world of painting as iconoclasts. Most of Bank’s exhibitions were accompanied by a printed text in the form of a polemic on the state of art, of the world and of artists. Bank Newspaper was self published for ten years, featuring news about the art world, drawings and photographs on a tabloid model. Irreverent and downright rude, those (especially their targets) who failed to get the joke found nothing in Bank’s work for them. In 2000 Bank published their ongoing fax back series as a book. For several years they had been returning press releases to the gallery that sent them, edited, corrected and with suggestions. These served to highlight the pomposity, inaccuracy, bad grammar and inflated claims made about artist by their dealers to hilarious effect. Bank also curated exhibitions the first of which was in a bank giving the group their name. But most were held in Bank’s studio in London’s East End. Other shows were titled Gallerie Poo Poo and Cocaine Orgasm which featured a life size self portrait sculpture of the group members on crucifixes. In 2003 Thompson and Bedwell posed semi naked for a series of photos dressed as classical figures from paintings but using their untidy studio as a background and anything to hand as costumes and props. 

!Fiona Banner.

Banner is a British artist born in 1965 who uses text as her material, first becoming known for a work called ‘The Hunt for Red October’ in which she wrote out the entire synopsis of the film of that name by watching it. She typeset this text and printed it, two metres high on paper in landscape format. The text represented the entire film but was not possible to read due to the line length. The viewer is forced to read vertically, skipping from line to line, producing their own unique reading of the text. Banner followed this with several similar film synopsis works, culminating in The Nam, a huge paperback book which contains her synopsis of the film Apocalypse Now. She also made an audio recording of herself reading this synopsis, which is produced in a flat vocal style. Banner’s work has most often referenced film, the book and the printed page, but she has also made work about songs and music, including a Bob Dylan song produced as a large work on canvas. The words of the song are spelt out in large stencil style letters on unprimed canvases three metres high, then cut out so that the canvas is mostly gone. The song stretches over several canvases. Since 2000 Banner has made several sculptural works, three dimensional versions of punctuation marks which are carved from polystyrenes and painted or cast in bronze. She has also made a number of works dealing with pornography, including some in which she removed all the text from a narrative leaving only the punctuation. 

!Guy Bar Amotz.

Bar Amotz is an Israeli artist born in the 1970s who lives in London. His work deals with sound and music in the form of sculptures that often incorporate speakers made from household objects, or especially made fibreglass structures which house audio and computer equipment which have a futuristic appearance. He made a giant karaoke machine which can entered by the viewer and an amplifier which is worn as a backpack housed in a rigid fibreglass lacquered shell. Bar Amotz sometimes uses metallic car spray to finish his sculptures. In 2003 he made a machine to be worn by a dancer whose movements triggered various sensors so that the movement created the music. 

!Sally Barker.

Barker made a performance work in the early 1990s for which she assumed an alter ego; Tela, a goddess or warrior queen. Barker performed as the goddess sitting on a throne, with acolytes, dressed in a cross between Roman and Viking costume with gold sprayed greaves, wristbands and a helmet. 

!Matthew Barney.

An American artist born in 1960s, Barney’s prolific work includes video, film, and sculpture. He made a performance work in 1993 with called Drawing Restraint in which he was tethered to the back wall of a squash court with a rubber bungee and used his own strength to get himself to the opposite wall, leaving marks on it to gradually make a drawing. He also scaled a lift shaft in a multi storey car park naked, using climbing equipment, with a surgical implement or machine tool inserted in his anus. These performances were documented on video. They are followed by a series of sculptures made from solid petroleum jelly. This is known both as a medicinal aid and a lubricant with sexual associations, and Barney used it to cast ambiguous sculptures that resembled gym equipment, rendered very realistically and shown in refrigerated spaces. Petroleum jelly became a significant element of Barney’s work. Barney made a series of five feature length films called Cremaster I – V, shot in 35 mm to commercial cinema standards, but made by a tiny crew and with the exception of the final film on a shoestring budget. The series explores a surreal world of symbolism, perversion, fetishism and onanism. The five films are conceived as a whole and were made over a period of 10 years. One of the films is set on an American football pitch. There is a long sequence of synchronised marching shot from above on a crane and from pitch level, featuring women dressed as glamorous grown up cheerleaders moving in tightly choreographed formation. They begin at the edges of the pitch and gradually form the shape of one of the Cremaster logos, a kind of jewelled harp. Another is filmed on a frozen lake on which a kind of snow fort has been constructed. Cremaster Four is filmed on the Isle of Man and takes the Manx symbol of the three legs of man and the annual TT motorbike race as its main subject. Barney’s character is a kind of fawn with red furry legs and pointed ears, and there is a goblin and a peasant girl and her parents. There are other exotic creatures all of which have strange mutant genitalia (a consistent theme in Barney's work). Four is filmed on Giants’ Causeway and is based on the Celtic myth in which a pebble is thrown into the sea and becomes an island. Another of the Cremaster series takes place both in the Guggenheim Museum in New York and in the interior of an airship with stewardesses in very chic uniforms seated round a table with a large display of grapes. One of the stewardesses is under the table on a small shelf curled round the pedestal of the table. She clings on precariously, while trying to and succeeding in making a hole in the table cloth above, using hairpins, and then gradually pulling grapes through. The grapes are red and white. She eats the grapes. This is intercut with animated film of grapes in lines forming various symbols. In the Guggenheim Barney, dressed as a Scottish piper in full ceremonial dress, slowly ascends the outside walls of the huge spiral stairway using climbing ropes and shackles. On each level different people and groups are encountered, for example a long line of can dancing women who place one extended leg on the edge of the balcony for Barney to crawl under, an extremely beautiful woman, and a small child. There is a section of the film which takes place in an empty warehouse and lots of water, Norman Mailer and Ursula Andress in a black jewelled outfit. Cremaster Five is shot in a bath house in Budapest, and features an opera singer. The last in the series to be made, Cremaster Three, is shot entirely on a set and features the Chrysler building in New York. The film opens with a long sequenced involving a hermaphroditic almost dead person recently disinterred from a living grave being deposited in a black sedan in the centre of a hexagonal room which also contains a crow. The arena is eventually recognised as an empty floor high up in the Chrysler building, and the corpse to be Gary Gilmore. As the viewer imagines the crow to be pecking out the eyes of the corpse, five large apparently driverless cars emerge from the corners of the space and begin a kind of ballet, repeatedly hitting and gradually crushing the black sedan until it is no more. A bar is staffed by a stereotypical Irish comedic barman who pours Guinness and becomes overwhelmed by foam. Barney’s character is a construction worker on the building. He is a young handsome Irish character who has some mysterious secrets, revealed when he visits two ‘doctors’ in a room in the building who reveal under his tattered clothes (including a leather apron with Masonic items in the pocket) his mutant genitalia. He excretes a petroleum jelly ‘turd’ which immediately mutates. As the top of the building is constructed the man is involved in a kind of wedding at the pinnacle, under a floral arch with ribbons which are finally unfurled and thrown over the side of the building to float down until they are caught by the workmen and drawn in through the windows. This is carefully choreographed to create a spiral of ribbons on the exterior of the skyscraper as if it were a giant maypole. One the pair who is married has their head crushed by falling steel. The mutant genitalia, gym equipment, logos and symbols of all the films relate to the title of the sequence, Cremaster. The Cremaster is a muscle is located somewhere between the scrotum and the anus, it is significant in both sexual performance and procreation, but most people are unaware of it. Barney treats the muscle as if it were one that could be worked, and as a metaphor for his own ideas about creativity and the individual. 

!Massimo Bartolini.

Italian artist born second half of the twentieth century. 

!David Batchelor.

British artist born in 1950s who uses coloured acrylic sheet to make strange utilitarian low trolleys in a multitude of colours which are then arranged to make an installation, or stacked to make a tower of coloured light. 

!Lolly Batty.

British artist born in the 1960s who made a series of abstract sculptures of three dimensional polyhedrons, carved in polystyrene and painted look smooth and heavy as if they had been carved in plaster. They have the appearance of one metre high geometric snow flakes smooth, clean and white. 

!Breda Beban.

UK performance artist born 1950s. 

!Vanessa Beecroft.

American performance artist Beecroft uses professional models to make her room sized installations, chosen for their perfect bodies and dressed alike, usually in underwear or naked but always wearing shoes and make up and usually wigs. In one early work they are wearing yellow bobbed wigs and items of school uniforms, whilst in a more recent work they have natural hair but identical face and body makeup giving them an ethereal gleam, and high heels. The models stand, sit or lie, however they feel comfortable, for the duration of the work usually four hours, and may engage the viewer's eye or not as they choose. They do not speak. Although the viewer is free to wander among them, no one does because the experience is intimidating. It is Beecroft's intention to show supposedly objectified naked beautiful bodies of women rendered very powerful simply by their presentation and context. The control of the situation is given to women by their very muteness, their make up and their beauty, traditionally seen as means of oppression. 

!Tony Benn.

British figurative conceptual painter born late 1950s. 

!Bik Van der Pol.

Dutch artist partners who make collaborative sociological investigations, setting up forums for investigation into contemporary art practises and in particular encouraging and facilitating cross nationality projects and interaction. 

!Richard Billingham.

British photographer born around 1960 who has made a series of photographs of his own family which reveal a chronically alcoholic father who is either comatose or the life and soul, and an overweight mother who likes to laugh. The pair watch a lot of television, drink, smoke and play with their other children and their dog. Their house is very untidy. The photographs are graphic and show Billingham's parents asleep, happy, sad, playful, and drunk. The degree of intimacy permitted to the viewer is startling, asking the viewer to reconsider their preconceptions. The photographs are all taken in the house with ambient light. To get the image he wants, Billingham often places the camera low down or high up, using any angle necessary to get a certain part of the room or a certain number of people. Billingham followed the photographs with a film, made in the same way, which reveals that his father is evidently going to die of a smoking related disease, and that the younger brother is very good at speaking on camera. Billingham’s work is characterised by its combination of casualness and thoughtfulness. It could not have been made by anyone other than a family member, as it otherwise would have been intrusive and vicarious. Equally it could only have been made at a time when the convention of fly on the wall documentary had been established and a fascination for the minutiae of ordinary lives become commonplace. 

!Olaf Bjornsdottir.

Scandinavian artist. 

!Katrin Bohm.

German artist born in 1970s who made social environments as gallery installations. Bohm designed a colour coordinated social space, with various printed paper items, paper cups and pens to match. She placed literature, questionnaires and other printed material in the space. 

!Nick Bolton.

British artist born in the 1960s who has made installation and video work. A telephone box with etched glass has a sensor attached to the mouthpiece so that when someone speaks on the phone, the light in the phone box becomes brighter or dimmer related to the pitch of the speakers voice. 

!Christine Borland.

Scottish artist born around 1970 who made a number of works featuring hand guns, presenting a police blanket with a bullet hole in it and a caravan in a gallery, containing some evidence of a crime or the suggestion that a crime could be committed, with a handgun lying on a table. Borland’s work displays an interest in how material evidence can be represented as an art work and in how that visual evidence can be used by the viewer to construct a narrative. 

!Philippe Bradshaw.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes large scale installations often using chain curtains. These have photographic imagery on them and are made by a commercial technique using different coloured links to make the image. An installation in a darkened room contained several of these curtains, some with photographs on, one with a nineteenth century landscape on, another with a Franz Hals painting on, placed around the space in the centre of which was a trampoline. Video monitors displayed a montage of imagery and also a film of Bradshaw jumping on the trampoline naked, which had been performed as part of the installation. The effect was that of multiple images, hard to grasp (the chain curtain images are hard to see at close quarters) and disorientating, but visually rich. 

!Stan Brakhage.

Late twentieth century Canadian film maker, Brakhage has made a large body of video work. He is interested in visual narrative and its disruption. One work consists of a fictional tale performed by actors of the infidelity of a modern sea captain’s wife, shown as one projection but which in fact consists of several plot lines woven together, leading the viewer to make choices about the direction of the narrative. Another work takes twenty four hours to view. A film set in a deserted house, shot in the dark by the light of one torch, is presented on a freestanding screen so that the viewer must walk around it. 

!Candice Brietz.

Artist born in 1970s. 

!Cecily Brown.

British artist born in the 1970s who paints very large splashy, dribbly and apparently messy works with tightly controlled colour choices. At first glance these appear to be abstract expressionistic paintings but almost immediately they reveal themselves to be figurative, for hidden in the mass of brush marks and paint splashes is a meticulously constructed figure, or figures often in pornographic poses. The works set up an expectation for the viewer and then almost immediately confound it, also making an analogy between abstract expressionism and sexual gratification. 

!Glenn Brown.

Brown is known for his excessively meticulous paintings that reproduce exactly other works of art, or more accurately that reproduce reproductions of other works of art. Brown scales up a reproduction, chosen from among many, all of which are different in colour due to the printing processes. He then paints this reproduction faithfully in oil paint, using the tiniest of brushes, reproducing not only what the work depicts but also the actual brushstrokes, giving the painting a trompe l’oeil quality. In fact it is debatable whether Browns paintings are copies or trompe l’oeil since they do seduce the viewer into thinking that their surface is thick paint textured when in fact it is flat and smooth. This is particularly evident in a series of de Kooning copies, where the thickly encrusted paint is entirely realistic. However on closer inspection the ‘impasto’ has a plasticised quality. Brown's de Kooning series culminated in a number of painted ‘lumps’ – apparently lumps of paint palette scrapings like those de Kooning reputedly had in his studio, but actually bronze casts painted by Brown to give the illusion of paint scrapings. Brown subsequently copied Fragonard's Boy in a Hat, and girl on a swing, which he showed upside down, and a number of science fiction paintings by Chris Foss, which were the subject of a legal copyright action. Brown's work deals with reproduction and its importance in the dissemination of the images, questioning whether what people know best is the image itself or its reproduction, and questioning the importance of the hand of the artist. His main idea is to make painting the subject of itself. 

!Angela Bulloch.

British artist born in the late 1960s. Bulloch's sculptures and installations deal with the social structures of society, the rules by which we live and the deconstruction of art and image making. Bulloch’s 1990s works include a mudslinging machine which created a mud spatter ‘painting’ on the wall, a drawing machine which drew a blue spattered line along a wall. These machines were viewer activated in some way. Often a piece of furniture designed by the artist would trigger the machines to work if sat on. The machines would be presented in an environment which invited contemplation, the artist filling a space with bean bags made to her design, often large enough to seat groups of people. The bean bags symbolised communality, and were a striking alternative to the customary formality of art galleries. Bulloch was one of a number of artists in the 90s who were explored the relationship of the viewer to art but more particularly to the institutional context in which they found it. She questioned the normalising structures by which we live by presenting such structures in new ways. Her Rules works are an example of this – presenting rules designed for specific purposes as if they are general rules to live by, rules that may be written in a small book are put on a wall using transfer lettering, giving them a perverse authority In early 2000s Bulloch made some small sculptures from chewed gum which she then cast and painted. She continued her series of works made from pixel boxes, her own invention which consists of a wooden box with one opaque acrylic face, inside which is a light source with the capacity to change colour infinitely. This is linked to a computer, which is able to control the change of light colours, using as its instructions of the changes the pixels in a video image, or a piece of music. Bulloch stacks the pixel boxes vertically in a variety of formations and also horizontally to create the effect of an illuminated dance floor and minimal abstract sculpture. In the latter case the pixels were controlled by sound. Bulloch often works with sound, and in 2003 she made a number of small sculptural objects, smooth amorphous shapes sprayed with white lacquer, which on handling revealed an inbuilt and incongruous means of generating sound, perhaps in response to light levels for example. ] ! Tom Burr. American artist working in the 1990s onwards. 

!Jane Bustin.

British painter born 1960s who makes severe abstract works that are both investigations into the effect of colour and pigment, surface and also into votive painting and the painting as symbolic or spiritual object of contemplation. Bustin uses natural colours, dark reds, blues, black white. There is never a figurative element in her paintings, merely subtle shifts in tone that may hint at a form. 

!Caivano Varda.

Still life painter working in the early 2000s. 

!Ingrid Calame.

Calame's apparently abstract paintings are in fact taken from the outlines of stains she finds on the street and in public places. She photographs the ground, draws the outlines of the stains, then paints the canvas in subtle colours to create an abstract image from something very mundane and ephemeral. 

!John Callan.

British sculptor born in 1960s who pushed paint through pin pricks following the lines on pages of text in an open book to create painted text. Callan also built a small house or dog kennel out of bricks and mortar. 

!Ellen Cantor.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes video and drawings. 

!Janet Cardiff and Georges Bures Miller.

Canadian artist and musician/sound engineer partner working from the 1990s onwards. Cardiff made a work for the Whitechapel Library in London which took the viewer on a walking tour – the viewer was given a personal CD player which directed their steps and used sound to interpret and accompany what they were seeing. She made a gallery installation with a choir in which the choir members, performing a medieval choral work, were recorded individually, and then played through individual speakers placed on speaker stands at ear height in a circle in the gallery. Thus the viewer could choose how to experience the work by where they stand, and hear each voice individually. This gave a sense of what it is like to be part of a choir, and also a sense of the physical space which a choir occupies and how that space is experienced aurally. The gallery space is also articulated by where the sound is coming from – this varies according to whether the whole choir is singing, or just the sopranos in one segment of the circle, or a few of each type of voice dotted round the circle. The work gives the non participant a participatory experience and inverts the performer listener relationship. 

!Gillian Carnegie.

British artist born in the late 1960s who began her career in the 1990s. Carnegie makes paintings in two distinct ways. She paints her own and other female bodies nearly always the back view, mainly featuring the buttocks in a realist way using muted lifelike colours. These paintings are small, about forty cm high. She also paints landscapes in extremely thick impasto, the canvases ranging from forty cm to 150 cm high. These landscapes are usually woodland scenes, sometimes with lakes, rarely featuring people. The paint is sometimes squeezed straight from the tube so that close up the image is nearly impossible to read, in particular in the paintings which are predominantly one colour, often black or very dark colours, as if it were night. In these paintings the image is revealed by the sheen on the squeezes and smears of the paint, the texture and the shadows created. In other landscapes more typical colours are used, blues, browns and greens, but in all the landscapes the vegetation is rendered in detail, as is the bark of trees and the ripples on water. There is a sinister atmosphere in the images, although they appear timeless there is somehow the implication of a contemporary narrative. Similarly with the nudes, although a traditional subject, the fact that they are often self portraits coupled with her obsession with bottoms, gives a contemporary twist to the sexual projection of these images and hints at both onanism and voyeurism. Carnegie subsequently began to paint floral still lifes, using a much lighter palette although still restrained. In these the background and any vessels holding the flowers are painted smoothly and the flowers are rendered in her style of thick impasto and squeezings, a few judiciously placed ones making them very convincing as flowers. Carnegie's paintings make an investigation into the history of painting styles and subject matters, subverting traditional subject matter by making anachronistic and unexpected juxtaposition of style and content. 

!Melanie Carvahlo.

British artist who is interested in how people conceptualise their world. In a work from 2002 she invited a large number of people from all walks of life to make a painting of ‘home’. She displayed the resulting works in a gallery as part of an installation. Carvahlo also makes complex photo collages that include people, buildings and brightly coloured exotic flowers and sunsets. 

!Maurizio Cattelan.

Sculptor and installation artist who uses stuffed animals in his work. Cattalan hung a dead stuffed horse from the ceiling in a sling, made to look as if it were either dead or tranquillised. He made an effigy of the pope toppling to the floor felled by a meteorite. A table football game for two teams of eleven to play simultaneously. A squirrel slumped at a tiny table with a gun indicating that it had committed suicide. A small boy wearing a hooded sweatshirt sitting at a school desk who has speared a pencil through his hand. Cattelan’s work is often intended to be disturbing and provocative. A large format photograph of the back of the Hollywood sign reversed so that it reads the right way, apparently installed in a mountainous area, it is unclear whether a huge deconstruction was undertaken to make the photograph or whether it is digitally created. 

!Brian Dawn Chalkley.

British artist born 1950s, Chalkley is a transvestite who works as both a man and a woman. He uses video and photography to investigate identity, for example in a film in which Dawn is seen in various dangerous scenarios, alone in isolated places having possibly been attacked. 

!Jake and Dinos Chapman.

British artists born in mid 1960s the Chapmans are brothers who work together. They became known in 1993 for Disasters of War, a sculpture that recreated Goya’s disasters of war as a 1:100 scale model. This grisly scene was presented on a plinth the size of a table top, realistically made and leaving nothing to the imagination. Flayed corpses hang from trees, severed limbs are strewn about, headless torsos are propped up, heads stuck on bayonets. They later made a series of etchings based on this work in homage to the original. Another early work was Little Death Machine, a working machine built in model scale and presented in a vitrine on a plinth. It was a rubber model of a brain that functioned as a pump to spurt out white liquid. On one hand the Chapmans' work is deeply rooted in art history, politics and subversion with implied criticism of Western capitalism and of the failure of art to deal with important issues. On the other hand the artists delight in the puerile and a childish humour and set out to deliberately and defiantly shock. It is the Chapmans' life size sculptures of mutant children that are most notorious in this regard. These children, which are adapted from shop window mannequins, are made in what has become their main medium of cast fibreglass with realistically airbrushed skin and features. They have genitalia on their face and smooth skin where their genitals should be. A girl might have a vagina as a mouth, or several orifices on her body where none would usually be. A boy may have a penis as a nose. The children are posed in apparently compromising positions and situated in a beautiful garden. There are trees and plants with large fantasy flowers and grass made from a material that resembles a scaled up version of the green sand like material used as grass by model makers and model railway enthusiasts. in this work, as in all their work The Chapmans have taken a basic human urge, such as the urge to kill, the sexual urge and its many perversions, and urge to power and represented it in order to confront their viewer with the possibility of discovering such urges in themselves.
