In this sense their work is highly politicised. The artists subsequently created The Disasters of War as a life size work. The Chapmans made a porn film featuring people with mutant prosthetic genitalia attached to their bodies. They continued to make etchings and other works on paper, and to make large scale installations and models. Their McDonald’s Concentration camp, made in 2000, is a very large 1:50 model of a fictitious concentration camp, which has McDonald's logos. The camp processes people, and the viewer can see tiny figures being brought in, living in squalor, being killed and ‘processed’ in various ways. Much of what the model depicts is familiar to us not through experience but through fictitious depiction on film and television and the model relies on this to animate it. The work makes a literal analogy between a fast food company bringing cheap food to the masses with no concern for their detrimental and social impact, and the Nazis. They have also made larger scale sculptures again in their usual medium of cast fibreglass, realistically painted with an airbrush, which represents a kind of gothic heavy metal concentration camp monument with massed skulls, very black and dark. In 2003 the Chapmans' presented a darkened room apparently full of African tribal art, painted wood carvings which have added grass, beads and teeth. The room is like a museum, darkened to lend a familiar reverence to the sculptures set on plinths. There is a deadened acoustic. On closer inspection the viewer can see that the carvings all in some way feature the MacDonald’s logo, the golden M, or represent a burger or Ronald McDonald the company’s clown mascot. These are cleverly worked as ‘African’ looking sculpture, Ronald McDonald a totemic figure, the burger often the mouth of a figure, the M appearing as a decorative element. 

!Lali Chetwynd.

Artist working in London, Chetwynd made several performance works in the late 1900s and early 2000s. 

!Lisa Cheung.

Korean artist born in the 1970s who makes photographic works often shown as slide projections. 

!Andrew Child.

British artist born in the 1960s. 

!Billy Childish.

British artist, poet and musician born around 1960. Childish has made a large number of naïve figurative paintings often of himself and his friends on found wooden boards, the sides of boxes, and other unconventional materials. He is known equally for his poetry which includes, and makes a virtue of, his dyslexic spelling errors, and for the punk band which he fronts, Thee Headcoats. Although he has worked in this way since the early 1980s, in the late 90s Childish achieved a notoriety beyond his cult following when he was immortalised by ex girlfriend Tracy Emin in several works, including her embroidered tent ‘Everybody I Have Ever Slept With’. Since then Childish formed the Stuckists, a self proclaimed movement that promotes the idea that British art in particular the work of the artists that make up so called Brit Art, is simply ‘stuck’. The Stuckists set out to critique it, by means of pamphleteering, demonstrating, fly postering and other public protestation and interventions. 

!John Chilver.

Born in the 1960s the British painter Chilver has made paint itself the subject of his conceptual paintings. He uses paint squeezed or piped in the way icing would be to decorate a cake, through a square nozzle, to produce a three dimensional line on the canvas. He also adds paint from a traditional round nozzle. The paintings depict simple scenes, for example a fence with a bird on it. Or a style with a clump of grass. The fence or style will be piped in square lines often black or dark grey, the bird in many colours of thinner round lines, the whole giving the impression of casualness and speed of execution when in fact the reverse is obviously the case. Chilver often uses puns in his title, for example 'style' and 'stile'. He investigates the nature of painting the painted mark, without actually painting at all since the backgrounds are entirely flat and the paint is never touched by a brush or worked at all. 

!Adam Chodzko.

British artist born 1960s. Looking for Jesus is a work made in the 1990s. Chodzko placed a classified advertisement requesting people who thought they looked like Jesus to send him passport photos. Often they bore no resemblance at all. Chodzko made a work with Afghan refugees living in Hastings in 1990 in which he asked them to sort through and select from the archive of Cubitt, an artist run organisation in London. The selection was placed in a watertight box and buried below the high water mark on the beach at Hastings. Another work from 2003 was site specific, Chodzko placed a billboard in a field in Texas on which it was printed the directions to a billboard outside a gallery in London, which again had printed the directions to the billboard in Texas. A kind of transatlantic endgame with the idea of encouraging a sense of place, geography, time and space. A photo of the American billboard was displayed at site of the British one and vice versa. Chodzko's work is usually socially engaged and often involves research, correspondence or mass participation. 

!Anne Collins.

American artist born in the 1970s who makes photographs, for example of women in urban and rural landscapes, with a precisely controlled banality. 

!Matt Collishaw.

British artist born in the 1960s who among other works has made a large format photograph of a bullet entrance hole in a person's scalp. 

!George Condo.

American artist born around 1960 who makes paintings that use art historical references, often nearly remaking a particular work but with his own often fantastic twists and distortions so as to create an entirely contemporary version of a painting from history. The paintings use a variety of styles and usually bright colours, and although they have a clearly historical reference they often have a science fiction or fantasy reference too. Condo also paints LP covers, turning them into large paintings around a metre high. 

!Melanie Counsel.

British artist born around 1960. 

!Keith Coventry.

British artist born in 1960s who paints white on white paintings that reproduce famous paintings in oil paint without colour. 

!Martin Creed.

Scottish artist born around 1960 who makes sculpture and installation from almost nothing. All his work is assigned a number and a description for the title, for example, A Door Opening and Shutting, The Lights Going On and off, One A4 Sheet of Paper Screwed Into a Ball. Further works include a cube made of layers of elastoplast, a cube made of layers of masking tape cubes, a bulging protrusion from the wall of a room which is plastered and painted to be as part of the wall. Creed's work is sometimes active, for example one which consists of a room full of white balloons through which the viewer must push their way. This references Yves Klein’s ‘Void’, and empty gallery space. The door that opens and shuts is operated by a door closer on a timed switch. The lights going on and off is similar. This work has also been ‘performed’ by Creeds. band Owada – whenever the lights came on the band played, whenever they went off the band stopped, as if the electricity supply for the amplifiers were linked to the lights. Or the other way around. Owada which can be described as making minimal conceptual music gained success as an art band in the late 90s and early 2000s with tracks such as One to A Hundred, which featured Creed speaking these numbers in order and the band playing rising scales one note per number. 

!Gregory Crewsdon.

American artist who makes photographs of staged events that appear to be crime scenes. There is something of the supernatural about the images, which have an unreality due to the highly controlled lighting and decisions made in the printing. A small town petrol station is seen from above at night and the disposition of people and vehicles seems strangely static, leading the viewer to guess at possibilities, perhaps to wonder whether there has been or is about to be an alien invasion. Another work might show a person looking at the sky, presaging some unknown possibly extra terrestrial event. The photographs are complex and ambitious in their construction of a subtly warped reality, shot on location rather than in a studio. 

!Russell Crotty.

American artist born around 1960 who makes obsessive biro drawings sometimes on large sheets of paper, also in very large books, at least eighty cm high. The drawings are made by colouring in with blue biro, using the pen for the purpose for which it is least suited, on a scale which is plainly absurd. 

!Juan Cruz.

British artist born in the mid 1960s who works with sound and text. Cruz made an installation performance in which he translated the text of Cervantes' great Spanish novel Don Quixote in real time over a number of days. He also made a work based on the Duende, an untranslatable Spanish word that describes the ‘feeling’ needed to perform flamenco. Cruz is chiefly interested in language and translation. 

!Shane Cullen.

Northern Irish artist who made a large scale text installation for which he printed or stencilled or used transfer lettering to inscribe the letters written to him during a correspondence he had with political prisoners on remand in the H block, a notorious prison in Northern Ireland. These covered the walls of the exhibition space. 

!Stewart Cumberland.

British painter born in the 1970s. 

!John Currin.

American painter born in 1962 who became extremely successful in the late 1990s and early 2000s. Currin made his name by painting women with unfeasibly enormous breasts, made in a mixture of kitsch and old master style. The women have exaggerated cartoon like features yet the colours used are entirely realistic, seeming to be there to hold the male gaze and fulfil male desire. They are however often painted in pairs, absorbed with each other, ignoring the viewer. Currin also paints portraits of real people, still lifes and interiors. His knowing and anarchic use of virtuoso painting techniques and undeniable skill in painting and drawing is allied with subject matter that would have been entirely unacceptable in the 1980s or early. 

!Brian Cyril Griffiths.

British artist born in the 1960s who has made a number of installations from cardboard, discarded materials, empty plastic containers and any number of banal materials. Griffiths’ space ship command module control panel made in 1997 was an adult sized version of a child’s dream. Griffiths has made many objects that seem like things that the viewer may think that they could make themselves but for the scale. In 2003 Griffiths made a hansom cab from a selection of second hand furniture, piecing specific parts together to make a three dimensional wooden collage. He also made a large striding warrior figure in the same way. 

!Dexter Dalwood.

British artist born in 1960 who makes figurative history paintings. Dalwood began his career painting expressionistic autobiographical narratives, often depicting himself and members of his family, particularly his father. He spent a year working in India and the influence of Indian miniatures is clear in his work of this period. Dalwood began to use the photograph as a source for his work around 1989, when his work became less narrative. He used images of multiple reflections in windows, grids and all over lattice patterns to create veil and screen effects in which appeared various motifs, for example a painted wooden toy bird. Dalwood also began to make groups of small paintings which were hung together. One such group depicted various personal histories, including paintings based on photographs of his father as a young man. His style of painting remained eclectic using a variety of textures, methods of application and thickness of paint. In 1995 Dalwood began a series of paintings of interiors which marked a change in his work. These mainly featured ceilings and light fittings, and are the only works of Dalwood’s that come close to abstraction. Dalwood began to paint fictitious interiors, making a collage from magazine cuttings and then replicating it on canvas, including the cut edges of the paper and differences in colour of sky, for example, taken from two different places. The interiors are imagined for a variety of well known people, such as Elvis’ Living Room, Bill Gates’ Bedroom, and also famous venues such as Studio 54 night club, Liberace’s Drawing Room. These collaged interiors function as portraits of a person but they began to include the work of other artists. Dalwood combined the work of an artist whom he connected in some way with the person he was making the painting about and added a part of their work to each composition by collaging. Mary Shelley is combined with Munch and Friedrich, Nixon with late Braque, Che Guevara with Piero della Francesca and Mao with Antonello da Messina. A painting of the greenhouse in which Kurt Cobain committed suicide has cherry blossoms from a Van Gogh painting. In this way Dalwood has made a series of portraits of famous people from a variety of walks of life. In 2000 he began to make the connection with history painting more explicit by making paintings that referred to a date rather than a person. Dalwood’s paintings are between one and four metres high and he uses a range of techniques according to the artist to whom he is making reference. 

!Adam Dant.

British artist born in the 1970s who makes wry satirical cartoon style drawings about art, artists, and the art world. Dant’s project is serious and comprehensive in its ambition to comment upon everything that happens but to make sure that art doesn't take itself too seriously. He began by making a weekly self published pamphlet, much in the vein of eighteen century pamphleteers soapbox speakers. The pamphlet was hand drawn and written by a pseudonymous character invented by Dant to represent himself (who was often shown in the drawings). It was a bit like a diary and featured topical news about art and advice written in mock serious style. It was photocopied and folded into a pocket sized booklet which Dant himself distributed wherever he went free of charge. He did this every week for several years, and it was the long term nature of the project that made it successful. Dant constructed a portable booth from which to distribute the pamphlet and became a presence at art fairs and museum exhibitions. By a simple weekly action Dant produced a major work. 

!Ian Davenport.

British artist born in the 1960s who began to make the drip paintings for which he became known in the 1990s. Davenport poured liquid paint onto a canvas from the top, allowing gravity to take the drip to the bottom of the canvas in a perfectly vertical line. He continued in this way across the width of the canvas using different colours until he had achieved a striped painting made without a single brushstroke, or any touch of the artist’s hand. At first sight the stripes would appear to have been painted with a brush perhaps even using masking tape, but on closer inspection tiny irregularities in the pour and the quality of the edge of the stripes made it obvious how the painting was made. Sometimes the paintings were in sombre colours, other times they were rainbow striped. Later Davenport developed various ways of making the pouring easier (fixing the paint pot to a runner for example) and he was able to make bigger canvases and even to pour directly onto a wall. Davenport also made paintings by pouring a coating of one colour of paint onto a canvas placed horizontally, and then adding in a stripe, a line, and arc in a contrasting colour by pouring directly into the wet surface. The two colours when dry formed a perfect flat shiny surface, in the way that the technique of enamelling can be used to combine colours. 

!Enrico David.

British Italian artist born in early 1960s who made a series of very large embroideries mounted on stretchers in the late 1990s. These were taken from collages and drawings he made from fashion magazines depicting women in fetish wear, extremely stylised clothes, very exaggerated bodies, very high heels, masks, drawn in a geometric style often with a 1920s or 30s feel. Although the images were of women they depict a non specific sexuality, the women often have large masculine bodies and may seem more like cross dressed men. David used woollen thread to fill in the figures with satin stitch, and due to the scale of the image (often two metres high or more), the stitches sometimes needed to be over a metre in length. Sometimes the stitches represented rays of light, too, or in one case silver thread was used on a blue canvas to represent the tight binding on the body of a model. David uses any craft materials or fabric that he likes to create these creatures. One has a butterfly entirely concealing her face, like a mask. Another is like an African queen. They are decadent and often sexual images, their handcraft materials at odds with the subject matter. He also made items of furniture and lamps. David began to work on some photo collages in 2000, using photos of himself wearing coloured paper constructions – red and blue, black and white, something like the costumes designed by the dada artists for Cabaret Voltaire. Other collages used images from magazines and often the background paper was cut in a certain way so that the found image could be raised from the background. He used brooches and other found jewellery and began to use carpentry, furniture making and decorative arts materials. In 2000 David began to draw men. A man in evening dress appears before the closed curtain of a theatre in a drawing in back and white that looks very much like a woodcut. A sculpture of a male figure is made of plywood coloured with wood stain; the profile of the figure is cut twice from sheets of ply and slotted together at right angles to each other. Turned wooden knobs are added to represent testicles. A large rag doll three metres high is made of canvas, stuffed, and embroidered with facial and bodily features and decorative patterns, and presented sagged against a plywood cabinet and lying on the floor. A gallery door is replaced by an etched glass and metal door that has an art nouveau style. A very long plywood screen covers the length of a gallery wall, on it a repeating interlinked pattern of a stylised male figure that has a 1930s look is made by incising the wood and using wood stain to create a kind of faux marquetry. A giant leather medal hangs on the wall. David was briefly a member of Donatella a group of artists and musicians who produced music videos and CDs, and made work in collaboration with fashion designers Eley Kishimoto for catwalk shows. 

!Peter Davies.

British artist born around 1970 who began his career making paintings consisting of many coloured dots on a white background on huge three or four metre wide canvases, creating patterns of waves, like an abstract pointillism. Davies always uses acrylic paint applied as flat solid colour. He went on to make text paintings in the later 1990s. For example The Hot 100 is a numbered list drawn up as a table in two columns with the lines painted in white, of the artists personal hundred favourite artists, painted in white on a multicoloured ground, a different colour for each line of text. The list functions as an illustration of subjectivity presented as a formally considered abstract painting. Davies subsequently made a diagram painting describing twentieth century art in terms of interlinked artists and movements. The text is painted in white on coloured elliptical or circular shapes to fit each name or title, which are linked or cross linked by coloured lines, the whole on a white background. Davies then began to make a series of very large pattern paintings at least four metres wide. The paintings are abstract three dimensional patterns that cover the picture plane and create a surface pattern, some painted in bright colours, some in monochrome, a bit like geometric doodles that play with simple perspective and convex and concave illusion. Like the text paintings these are painted in flat acrylic paint, with hard edges to the colours painted by hand rather than masked. Some of them allude vaguely to Robert Delaunay’s paintings and to 1930s textile patterns, others to futuristic cityscapes. 

!Peter Davis.

British artist ! Verne Dawson. American artist whose paintings depict an apparent utopia, a kind of modern pastoral world, with tiny people on the scale of the people in a Bosch painting populating rural and woodland scenes. 

!Angela De la Cruz.

British artist who makes sculptures from collapsed and destroyed paintings. De la Cruz's method is to construct a canvas which may be any size (from tiny to the largest which was a large scale installation of a canvas about fifteen metres squared). She paints the canvas in a plain colour, and then destroys it by breaking the stretcher and then twisting the whole into a heap. This is then fixed and either displayed on the wall or the floor. These deceptively humble objects assume the presence of a sculptural object, thus a painting is transformed. 

!Richard Deacon.

British artist Deacon began making very large sculptures in the 1980s at a time when sculpture was at a zenith of popularity. Deacon and his contemporaries Bill Woodrow and Grenville Davey made abstract works largely from cheap easily available materials. Deacon used reclaimed wood which he cut into strips, plied and glued and bolted together to make large hollow snail shell like structures, which he later began to design on a computer. The nature of the materials belied the complexity of the shape and the effort required in their construction. The means of construction were very evident, for example glue spilled over from the joins where they had been compressed. What would usually be hidden or on the inside of such a large work, was actually the surface of the work. 

!Tacita Dean.

British artist born in 1960s who uses 35 mm film to make installations and simple projections. One film is made inside a lighthouse. A series of photographs examines the beached yacht of a sailor, Donald Crowhurst who faked a transatlantic voyage. She made a number of drawings of film story boards featuring a ship in a stormy sea on blackboards. 

!Jeremy Deller.

British artist born in the late 1960s who is interested in different social groups and how they are creative. The authorship of Deller's work is always in question (an essential part of Deller’s work) – the creative work is as much the participants’ as it is Deller’s, who acts as the facilitator of an idea. In the mid 1990s Deller began working with groups of people brought together by a particular shared passion. One of the first of these was his Manic Street Preachers project. Deller asked young fans of the band to write and draw their feelings about them and their music. The resulting document was collected into a book and is a testament to the power of music to represent the feelings and hopes of a generation. Deller subsequently drew a chart linking acid house music with brass band music via many other forms of popular music. The chart was hand drawn in white on black with circles containing text linked and cross linked by arrows. Acid Brass was a project in which Deller commissioned a brass band to play acid house music. The resulting hybrid was performed in concerts halls and galleries, and released as a CD. Deller’s most ambitious work, Battle of Orgreave, was a full scale re enactment of a battle fought between the police and striking miners at the peak of the miners strike in the early 1980s in Britain in which the police charged the miners on horses. Deller arranged for several historical re enactment societies to come together to stage a full scale re enactment of this event. The re enacted battle was filmed by director Mike Figgis and intercut with original news footage. The resulting film, shown on national television, was a very accurate portrayal of what happened that also revealed the lengths to which the governments went to ensure a media cover up of the brutality of the police and their determination that the scale of the protest should be played down. Guest list was a work made for Imprint 93 an artist pamphlet series published by Matthew Higgs, which reproduced the guest list for an exhibition opening at the Institute of Contemporary Arts in London. The list which featured a number of celebrities revealed a degree of social ambition and perhaps sycophancy on the part of the institution. The Folk museum was made by Deller with Alan Kane. Together they collected hand made craft and folk objects of no intrinsic value and displayed them as if they were valuable collection of artefacts. At the Venice Biennale in 2003 Deller made button badges with a road stop sign (a red circle with diagonal line bisecting it on a white ground) with the words No Vogue across it, which referred to the magazine – Deller commented of the ubiquity of the magazine’s presence at any art event, and the ever increasing tendency of the art world to collide with the world of socialites and celebrities and be photographed doing so. 

!Thomas Demand.

British artist who makes photographs of complex paper ‘sets’ that he constructs himself. The sets replicate a real space, for example an office, which Demand then remakes entirely in card and paper. The resulting construction is carefully lit and then photographed in large format and presented as a very large (about two metres high) print. The image provokes a double take – it seems to be a plausible space although oddly empty. Once the viewer begins to try to decode the image, and look for clues, it becomes apparent that for example the surfaces of the furniture are too homogenous, without detail. Even then it is difficult to establish why the space depicted seems unreal. When the process behind the work becomes clear the work can be viewed more as an achievement in terms of its construction. 

!Xenia Dieroff.

German artist living and working in Britain, Dieroff has made photographic work. A slide installation projected onto a hospital screen uses silhouetted figures and clever dissolves to create the illusion of a patient undressing that morphs into a striptease. 

!Mark Dion.

American artist who is interested in anthropology, material culture, ecology and natural history. Dion's usually large scale multi part installations are the result of research projects into specific locations or subjects. For example in London he searched an area of the muddy Thames riverbank above and below the timeline with the painstaking care of an archaeological dig. The resulting ‘artefacts’ or trash was cleaned up and exhibited in a number of vitrines and display cases in the Tate Gallery. 

!Rineke Djikstra.

A Dutch artist who became known in the late 1990s for her photographic portraits of young people. Like Thomas Struth and Thomas Ruff Djikstra asked her subjects to look straight at the lens and not to smile. The resulting images are a true document of a generation. In 2000 she began to make video portraits. A film portraying young people at a club shows them standing against a white backdrop, moving slightly. They are listening to music which the viewer cannot hear, but Djikstra has asked them to stand still. Their body language reveals a lot about them, as do their clothes. Although it is a film, the participants have been captured in time as if it were a photographic portrait. Their youth is very expressive. 

!Willie Docherty.

Northern Irish artist, Docherty made a number of photographic series and video works in the 1980s and early 90s at the height of the troubles in Northern Ireland. These featured empty roads, empty landscapes, cars and checkpoints. A video work in his Turner prize nomination exhibition was filmed from a car driver point of view and purported to be a night drive with sinister intentions, although this was merely suggested with the greatest economy of means. Docherty's work has an oneric quality although it deals with political issues. He gives a glimpse of life on the border and of political strife in urban and rural landscapes. 

!Peter Doig.

British painter who grew up in Canada, born 1957. Doig is chiefly a landscape painter and was among those British artists who created a resurgence of interest in painting in Britain in the early 1990s. His landscapes are often oneric, usually populated by a single figure if at all and have a strong sense of the unknown and of adventure. Doig paints the seasons, the mountains, forests, lakes, vernacular architecture, and occasionally sports such as fishing, skiing, snowboarding. He paints thinly and loosely, scattering the surface with blobs of thicker paint when the image is complete. This often serves to represent light, for example the white blobs in an early work The Architect's House. He also painted a Corbusier housing block in a similar way. Both are seen through trees and use a lot of green brown, back and white with touches of yellow white and red. Often the touches of paint on the surface do not read as part of the image but become very apparent on close inspection. They may even be bars of colour that are not part of the image at all, or splatters. Another early work, the Bean Eater, shows a boy walking in a field empty except for a red wooden house or barn. A leafless branch of a tree with some kind of seed or flower painted like glowing orangey white bobbles. Later works from 2000 onwards depict a steamboat apparently steaming past a jungle on a river reminiscent of (apocalypse). This is a very green and lush painting. A tree in the moonlight with a girl sitting on a branch high up is painted in greys and lilacs, as if it were a night scene or a nightmare. A lake with a canoe on it paddled by a man with long black hair and a beard, the canoe is orange and the lake very blue. This image is taken from an album cover. A painting of a mountain ski slope called Red Ski Jacket is very white with only the minute people bringing in colour. One of a snow boarder shows the boy in the air upside down at the top of the canvas with a group of people watching at the bottom. Doig became extremely influential in the mid 1990s. 

!Kay Donachie.

British artist born in 1970s who made process paintings during the 1990s, using a roller to transfer an image to a prepared canvas, resulting in a shadowy painting, the image always seeming slightly removed from reality due to the technique. Donachie painted isolated houses, a tent, a Christmas tree, the interior of Paul Klee's studio. She uses a monochrome palette, choosing a predominant colour for a painting. The transfer technique brings the concept of the photographic print into the painting although no such process is involved. The viewer’s uncertainty about how the image is made is paralleled by the uncertain status of the subject of the painting. Following this Donachie began to paint figures, for example groups of young women, and although not necessarily using the same technique, she gives the image the same once removed quality that places it in an uncertain time. Lighter colours begin to appear. Donachie's paintings are small, mostly under a metre squared. 

!Fiona Drummond.

British artist born 1962 who takes portrait photographs and still lifes. She often uses flowers and references seventeenth century Dutch painting especially in terms of light, using studio set and lighting, also paints dogs and flowers. 

!Dubossarsky and Vinogradov.

Russian painters born in the 1970s who paint collaboratively. Dubossarsky and Vinogradov are among the few Russian artists to gain commercial success in the America and Europe. Their work alludes to both history painting and to Russian Socialist Realist art. They often ask other artists to paint parts of the work for them and they may make several versions of the same painting or small versions of sections of larger works, for example a book on a snowy muddy ground with a frog diving into a hole. 

!Keith Edmier.

American artist born in the 1960s who makes resin cast sculptures of plants and flowers from scaled up models of a plant, such as a water lily up to four metres high, in pale pink or yellow resin. The sculpture has very realistic detail and despite its size looks very fragile. Edmier has used a variety of plants in this way, and sometimes makes very small sculptures. 

!Volker Eichmann.

German artist born in the late 1970s who makes photo collages using interior design magazines and strips of text. 

!Olafur Eliasson.

Norwegian artist who is interested in the effect of the environment, both built and natural, on humans and the reciprocal effect of humans. Eliasson makes minimal and sometimes barely visible interventions, as in his installation at Museum of Modern Art in New York. This consists of frosted transparent tape applied in vertical stripes, covering the whole glass curtain wall in the foyer, subtly but substantially altering the visitor's experience of this public area. Eliasson has also coloured the water in a harbour bright green, and in 2003 he made a work called the weather project for Tate Modern in London which consisted of a thirty meter diameter semi circular yellow panel representing the sun, hung at the top of the museums entrance hall, with a ceiling panelled with silver mirror fabric to create a reflection of the sun as if it were in water. A theatrical mist made of sugar and water is fed into the hall. Viewers lie on the floor to watch their reflections and to look at the sun. 

!Tracy Emin.

British artist who was at the forefront of the Brit Art group that came to prominence in the 1990s in London. Emin was born in the 1960s in Margate, a British seaside town which features prominently in her work. Emin's work is essentially a diary of her life, reflecting on her state of mind particularly in relation to her love life and her emotional life. Emin relates experiences that mostly remain personal, such as her abortion, her rape, her breakdown, drunken and regretted sex, good sex, the break up of relationships, her problems with alcohol and her feelings about her absent father. These are represented in a wide variety of media: video, drawings, mono prints, text, neon signs, textiles, embroidery, and installation. Emin’s consistent poetry and diary writing underpins her work. Her video works include ‘Why I never became a dancer’ made in the early 1990s shows Emin dancing in Margate. In 2000 Emin made a video in which she appears (achieved with editing) twice, as both good Tracy and bad Tracey, sitting together and chatting on a couch. The two dress differently and behave differently, both are likeable in different ways, one is drinking a cup of tea and the other a glass of wine throughout what seems to be an interview of some sort, or perhaps a therapy session. Emin achieved notoriety when she embroidered and appliquéd the inside of an ordinary one person tent with the names of all the people she had ever slept with. This was quite a long list of mostly men including several male artists, but also siblings and women friends who she had shared a bed with. In order to view the work the viewer had to crawl into the very small tent (which had only room for two), and recline, since there was no room to stand, in intimate proximity with both Emin’s work and the other occupant of the tent. Emin also embroidered an upholstered chair and made a series of appliquéd and embroidered quilts. The quilts bear text legends of life and love and art, for example ‘I need art like I need god’ and patterns, and are made using patterned and plain fabric and basic embroidery stitches onto bedspreads and blankets. The quilts were usually dated. Emin had a number of works made in neon, either from her writings or her poetry. The neon letters either a single sentence or a verse, often in pink, were mounted on the gallery wall. Emin consistently made drawings and mono prints, often accompanied by descriptive sentences or titles as part of the image. These are often explicit, showing her menstruating, lying in a drunken heap, happy and in love, masturbating. They are very simply drawn in a childish hand in pen, pencil or a blue or black ink for the mono prints, that belies their effectiveness. In the mid 1990s Emin opened a shop with another artist, Sarah Lucas, selling trinkets, souvenirs and t shirts all of which were made by the artists, for example an ashtray with a picture of Damien Hirst's head (which has since become a collectors item). When the shop closed Emin established the Tracey Emin Museum, essentially a live work space which she kept open to the public regularly each week and sold work from. Her insistence on non exclusivity as manifested by the shop and the museum, coupled with her appropriation of ‘anyone can do it’ craft methods and materials captured the zeitgeist of the time, and contributed to a surge in public interest in contemporary art. Before this was seen as elitist and even if this interest was often prurient, Emin was ultimately persuasive. She also took up many of the areas of investigation of women artist in the 1970s and updated them and has been extremely influential to a whole generation of artists, especially women. The work that crowned Emin’s reputation (for notoriety or otherwise) was My Bed, exhibited in the 2000 Turner Prize exhibition in London’s Tate gallery. This is the bed in which Emin spent many days during her breakdown, drinking, smoking, eating, having sex. The bed and the surrounding detritus, empty bottles, food plates, glasses, pill packets, dirty clothes including underwear, overflowing ashtrays, a soiled tampon, books and magazines, was exhibited on a plinth to great public outcry. Since 2000 Emin has continued to make works on paper and also to make more sculpture, including a rickety wooden tower which can be climbed, and a stuffed bird on a plinth. Birds have been a part of her personal iconography throughout her career. 

!Pepe Espaliu.

South American artist born in the 1960s who made a piece of work with a large quantity of old clothes, involving him cycling round the trunk of a tree on a platform that encircled the trunk just below the canopy. 

!Jonathan Faier.

German artist who made a video with Johannes Rust and one other artist in which they put together a large number of clips from news footage and advertisements featuring corporate logos. They edited it and added an ironic and humorous soundtrack to represent their appropriated material as a fast paced and amusing look at global capitalism and consumerism. This video is an example of a way of working that emerged in the late 1990s as video editing technology became universally accessible so that anyone with a camcorder and a TV could edit video. 

!Angus Fairhurst.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes drawings and collages and digitally animated drawing videos. 

!Mark Fairnington.



!Simon Faithfull.

British artist who has made a number of installation and works for the web. 

!Keith Farquhar.

German artist who makes drawings in marker pen on found surfaces such as pieces of laminate. The drawings reference the graphics on the covers of 60s and 70s text books. 

!Vincent Fecteau.

Artist working in the 1990s onwards who makes sculptures from wood, sometimes painted, with strong references to modernism. 

!Rachel Feinstein.

American artist born in the 1960s who makes sculptures from particle board and other timber sheet material, polystyrene, paint and a variety of other media. The sheet material is cut into shapes and assembled as the framework of a sculpture. Sometimes it is spray painted. Feinstein's work has a theatrical feel to it, as if she were making a kind of prop or stage set. She made a number of works that looked like futuristic flowers. Later works are made only of painted board cut into shapes, often silhouettes of people. The sculptures are usually no more than two metres in height. 

!Peter Fend.

Artist born in the 1960s who made a film of himself jumping around with only a jumper and a pair of shoes on. 

!Fischli and Weiss.

Swiss artists born in the 1950s who work in partnership. Peter Fischli and David Weiss make installation, photographic and video art. They are interested in nature, animals, cause and effect and the human condition. Early works consist of the artists dressed as animals filmed walking in the countryside, a series of realistic replica interiors for example a store room, the artists’ studio in which every item and piece of furniture is modelled from clay and cast in rubber, in a variety of solid colours, producing a simultaneously convincing and perplexing version of reality. Fischli and Weiss made a number of photographic works in which they created art works with food. For example, Still Life with Cold Meats shows an arrangement of sliced sausage, frankfurters and gherkins. Photographic work is always part of their practise, most usually presented as a slide projection or light box. Indeed much of Fischli and Weiss’s video work is very static and closer to photography. A slide projection featuring hundreds of images of flowers, shown grouped into seasons, each slide dissolving into the next so that a complex double image is created, takes about an hour to view. It has a mesmerising effect. An illuminated table, about fifteen meters long and one metre wide is used to display hundreds of images of land and city scapes, each one about six cm wide. At first off putting, this work also draws the viewer in as they try to make connections between the images and inadvertently construct their own narrative. Video work is often similarly open ended, as in one installation of about twelve monitors placed in a room, all showing different images of landscape, but not simultaneously so that some screens were blue while others are playing. The viewer is directed to move about the room by the monitors’ apparently random showings. Fischli and Weiss’s best known static video work is Pussy, a single shot film of a black and white cat drinking milk from a saucer. This was made to be projected on a huge LED screen above Times Square in New York, where it achieved a monumental quality. Perhaps their best known video is The Way Things Go, a film of the progression of a series of inanimate objects caused to move by a cause and effect chain of events. Using simple science, physics and common sense, and materials such as wood, metal, a variety of vessels, balls, weights, fire, steam, salt, batteries, and ice, the artists made a series of structures, balances, ramps, slopes, water channels, tubes, pipes and levers that are set in motion be each other over a period of about half and hour. Basic principles of physics utilising steam for expansion, melting, burning, balancing, rocking, tipping and velocity. This is for example used to propel water down a slope into a bucket which tips filling a tank with something floating on the surface which when it reaches a ball cock pushes a weight down on a platform which sets a ball rolling and so on. This piece is filmed in an empty warehouse space. Fischli and Weiss often make questions part of their work, as if they are involved in some kind of research. The question with which they are most often associated is ‘Are Animals People?’ At the Venice biennale in 2003 they presented a work in which they simply projected a series of questions on a wall in enormous letters, one of which was ‘Will Happiness Find Me?’ ! Ceal Floyer. British artist born in the 1960s who has made many works using light as her main medium. However Floyer's earliest works are a pair of brass door fingerplates with the inscriptions pushed and pulled on them, and a red dot of the kind galleries use to indicate that a work has been sold, which in reality was a hole in the wall filled with red pigment. Both are poetic interventions into the mundane, which also characterises Floyer’s whole body of work. A lot of Floyer’s early works are slide projections. Light switch, made in the early 1990s, is a slide projection of a photograph of an ordinary household light switch, projected onto a wall where a light switch might be. The projector is not concealed at all. The projection looks exactly like a real light switch prompting a double take, and the viewer, surprisingly, does not readily associate the projector with the image. Floyer achieved full suspension of disbelief by presenting something utterly ordinary in a magical way. Light Under The Door consisted of a projector placed in front of a door, projecting a strip of light the exact dimensions of the space under the door, at this space. The effect was of an incredibly strong light was coming from behind the door, enough to make the viewer want to investigate, when in fact the light source was readily visible at the viewers feet. In a similar vein, a photograph of an illuminated light bulb was projected on to a switched off light bulb, making it a light source in a new way. A light was projected down through a light bulb, projecting the text on the bulb (brand name and wattage) onto the ground below, again prompting a kind of double take since when this work is viewed it seems like something that should happen every time a light bulb is switched on. Floyer began to work with sound in the late 1990s, presenting a work in which the sound of a slide projector is recorded and pressed onto vinyl, which is played on a turntable on a plinth. The viewer looks for the slide projection, which is not there, and eventually associates what they are hearing with what they see. This slippage, between what is heard and seen, or what is seen and understood is Floyer’s main interest. Around the same time, she made a video of a dog just outside the frame, jumping into the frame regularly leaping for an invisible ball. Once again it takes a certain amount of watching to work out what is happening. An apparently abstract static image suddenly moves and turns out to be a white net curtain against a window which occasionally flaps in the breeze, and when it touches the window becomes more transparent. A piece of screwed up white paper, fluttering in an eddy of wind in an empty cul de sac, resembles, when the film is looped, a white butterfly forever fluttering in the same place. Floyer’s interest in light extended to an interest in all things white, and indeed she has not used colour at all unless the object she wishes to use is coloured. A till receipt is placed on the wall and seems to be nothing more than that, until the viewer realises that every object on the list is white in colour (milk, flour and lard for example). In 2003 Floyer made a work for a museum in London. She installed a bell ringing at regular intervals, a bit like the bell that summons an audience into an auditorium. This created a sense of expectation, and a feeling in the viewer that they ought to obey the abstract summons causing them to look for a direction and finally to look towards where they perceive the sound to be emanating from. This act of looking is what the artist wanted to achieve. Another sound work is a recording of a drip in a bucket, played on a cassette player placed in a bucket, causing the viewer to look to the ceiling or if not to think about the sound they are hearing as being generated by a drip coming from the ceiling which they know is not there. Floyer’s project has been to reveal the beauty in the nearly intangible. 

!Laura Ford.

British artist born in 1960s who made a number of installation works in the 1980s concerned with fairy tales and the imagination, including one performance featuring a person in a bear suit. In the 1990s Ford made a large installation of actual size stuffed toy children, made in chintz fabrics of different colours, in a range of poses. 

!John Frankland.

British artist born around 1960 who makes sculpture and installation. Frankland's early works were a series of site specific installations in which he used plastic sheeting, stretched over battens, to line the walls of a space. In such a way he created a second skin inner wall that appeared to be very solid and shiny but was in fact only a thin layer of cheap plastic. He did this in black plastic in a cellar, in silver grey plastic on a freestanding form that looked like a giant metal wall, and in gold metal along a wall in a gallery. In this last work, Lift, he arranged the battening and supports so that there was the illusion of minimal gold metal lift doors set into a gold wall, with a hemispherical convex form pushed out from the back of the plastic to represent the button for calling the lift the only detail. Following this, Frankland made a work in which he painted a section of a tree trunk in bright yellow high gloss paint, using so many layers that the details of the trunk are smoothed out, giving it a first impression look of a model tree trunk. The trunk is mounted on the wall at chest height defying its tremendous weight. There is a contradiction between the possible lightness of the trunk if it is a fibreglass cast, and the reality of the weight of the wood. Frankland later made a site specific work for a sculpture park which consisted of his selection of a one ton boulder from a quarry placed in a site of his choice. 

!Katharina Fritsch.

Artist working in the late 1990s and early 2000s. 

!Bernard Frize.

German artist who makes process paintings, using multiple clear and bright colours of thin paint in several distinct and apparently continuous brushstrokes, wet into wet, to create a simple repeated pattern. The means of execution of the painting is puzzling as it appears that it would be impossible for the brush strokes to be juxtaposed in the way that they are. 

!Anya Gallaccio.

British artist born in the 1960s who has made a number of installations since the 1990s. One of her first installations involved paining the walls of a gallery with chocolate. The colour was reminiscent of a dirty protest, the smell in contrast overpoweringly sweet. She also made a curtain to hang in a gallery window from dark orange gerbera flower heads strung on threads the height of the window. 

!Gang Chen.

Chinese artist born in the 1960s who makes paintings of luggage seen on airport x ray machines. 

!Vidya Gaston.

Artist working in partnership with another artist who has made work in the 1990s and 2000s consisting of woollen pompoms placed on Perspex boxes. The pompoms are made in the way that children make them, by threading wool around two circles of card with a hole in the middle and then cutting between the circles. They are made in a range of sizes and colours, usually muted, from about 2 cm to 60 cm in diameter. The plinths are various sized too. The two artists have made other kinds of work together. 

!Ana Genovese.

British sculptor born in the 1970s who make small scale models of large environments or tracts of land, for example deserts. A desert complete with an oasis is be modelled on a domestic scale. Genovese also makes fibreglass ‘puddles’. 

!Isa Genzken.

German artist who makes sculptural objects and collages, for example concrete forms reminiscent of building materials placed together to form rudimentary architectural structures of about three metres in height, or long wooden spear like forms four metres tapered at both ends. Other works include collections of metal cooking utensils, such as sieves, whisks, knives, colanders, graters and peelers, strung together with chains and hung in clusters on the wall. Presented in this way these banal domestic objects seem somehow sexual and perverse. Genzken also made a series of thirty cm square collages of nudes cut up and re joined to make strange human forms. In 2002 Genzken made a series of small sculptures that look like maquettes for skyscrapers, made from found materials such as fruit crates, cellophane, printed matter, cardboard and glass, and roughly formed with copious amounts of glue oozing from the joins. This work is about the re building of Berlin, the changing from a faded modernist architecture to the new skyscrapers ! Ori Gersht. Artist working in the 1990s and 2000s who took a panoramic photo of a London football stadium and presented it on 25 metre gallery wall ! Liam Gillick. British artist who born in around 1960 who works in a variety of media. Text is integral to Gillick's work both visually and because he has written a number of books which he also designs, for example Erasmus is Late (with a yellow cover, the title punched through in ticker tape lettering), and published texts which are part of his practise. In both texts and visual work he explores the legacy and iconography of modernism, in particular its architectural and graphic forms, and urban and social space. There is a strongly liberal political aspect to Gillick’s work. He often uses quotation relating to politically significant historical events (such as the English Civil War) in his work, an approach which ultimately gives Gillick’s work a didactic quality. For Gillick the design of printed material is of paramount importance, equal to the construction of his sculptural works. For example Gillick’s ‘Conversation Platforms’ are panels made from squares of apparently random coloured Perspex sheets set in an aluminium framework to make a grid. The panel is then hung above head height and the viewer will be standing under it. These works have expanded into more complex and architectural structures, featuring sections of wall as well as platforms, and even a huge suspended ceiling, always using different colours of Perspex. Gillick has also made a large number of works on paper, many of them proposals for graphic design projects or architectural designs for the facades of buildings. In addition to his ideas for transforming public spaces he makes proposals for social events, some of which are carried out, as for example when he scheduled a programme of yoga classes to take place in a gallery under and around his Perspex platforms. Gillick’s approach is ideological; part social scientist, part philosophical theorist, part artist, part liberal political. He presents a vision of the world which has clean lines, perfectly considered typographic style, well designed building, where history can be reconsidered and there is a lot to read. 

!Kersten Glass.

British artist born in the 1970s who paints images of female models from magazines, juxtaposed in the manner of a collage, with a text title running across. The women are painted realistically, and are fashionably dressed and made up often in punk and Goth style. The paintings echo 1980s design. 

!Margarita Gluzberg.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes very realistic detailed pencil drawings of women, about two metres high. 

!Robert Gober.

American artist born in the 1960s, Gober’s work consider the human body and landscape. He has made a series of works using wax casts of his body parts, for example legs and hands. These are realistically finished down to the last hair, and installed in odd ways, for example a leg and foot may project from the wall at a right angle. He has made many installations featuring body parts, for example a room papered with the artist’s own design of wall paper, a forest of trees with sinister things going on among them and leg sculptures sticking out of wall. Another theme is that of ‘orifices’. Gober will often make circular openings with metal facings and plugholes, highly finished but ambiguous in terms of position and function. It is as if the external world is seen as a metaphor for the human body, and the orifices in the world might lead to a mysterious interior which provokes both curiosity and repulsion. Everything Gober makes has a highly detailed finish and exactly specified materials. In the early 2000s he began to make huge scale installations, for example a large gallery space which is empty apart from a small grill in the floor. Looking through this grill it is possible to discern an apparently real river bed and pool stretching under the floor, creating the impression that the whole gallery floor is covering a piece of nature. Somewhere there is a figure of Christ. Gober also made a piece of work featuring a bridal dress. 

!Steven Gontarski.

American artist born in the 1970s who makes figurative sculptures. Gontarski began in the late 90s to make life size figures made from fabric sewn tighter in the style of a stuffed toy on top of an armature. The figures were distorted human forms made from shiny plastics and vinyl, elongated and featureless, their limbs often joining and intertwined. The combinations of fabric alluded to garments. Sometimes a part of a garment might be incorporated into the sculpture. The lumpy feet might have a stripe referring to, but looking nothing like a trainer. Gontarski's soft sculptures are dancers, made by combining modernist sculptural forms, for example Henry Moore, with elements from club wear. Along with the sculptures he made drawings on acetate, and later on Perspex sheet. These were simple outlines in fibre tip marker featuring people that he had met and odd lines from their conversation. In 2000 Gontarski began to make similar figures from cast fibreglass, finished in one colour of a high gloss lacquer often pearlescent or metallic. The figures began to take on a molten morphology, like a mixture of Rodin, Boccioni and the molten baddy in the film Terminator 2. The figures twist and dance, and are placed on plinths made of the same material and of a part with the sculpture. The plinths are sprayed with graffiti, by graffiti artists that Gontarski has invited. Gontarski also made a series of busts in a similar way. These refer to the generic bronze bust which one might see anywhere. In Gontarski’s smoothed out and rendered version, there might be a hole right through the face. One is a virgin Mary figure painted in white. 

!Douglas Gordon.

British artist born in the 1960s who works in a range of media, including photography, video and text. Gordon is fascinated with the visual representation of psychological states, particularly in film. He made an installation for which he covered the walls with a list of all the people he knows, their names applied in self adhesive lettering in columns. Gordon often uses this type of lettering. A small work appeared in a book consisting of a text about looking which ends by stating that by the time the reader has read the text, they will have been reading for 3 minutes. He makes a comparison between the time taken to read and the time taken to look at images. Gordon has made a number of video and film works. He often uses back projection onto freestanding or hanging screens in the gallery. One work shows a film from the thirties of two psychoanalysts talking to and subsequently restraining a hysterical female patient, finally pinning her down. The film is presented played forwards and backwards, resulting in very different interpretations on the viewers part. This deconstruction of found film led eventually to Gordon’s best known work, 24 Hour Psycho. The film of the same name is projected the gallery wall, slowed down so that it takes 24 hours to play from start to finish. The gallery remains open during this time. The Resulting film provokes all kinds of new readings, and the extreme slow motion renders the famous shower scene entirely different. Gordon has made many works on paper including a number of photographs of film stars with the eyes cut out or replaced with plain paper, and a number of photographs of apparent actions such as his black finger tattoo, a photograph of his finger which is entirely black. 

!Felix Gonzales Torres.

American artist born around 1960 who made sculptures and installations. Gonzales Torres died in the late 1990s. During his lifetime his father died as did many of his friends from AIDS related illness. His work is preoccupied with death. Gonzales Torres used a variety of media, usually with minimal intervention on his part. The work he did was in choosing his material, decisions about quantity and placement. For example he presented a simple stack of his own weight in sheets of newsprint. The weight of his dead father in ivory coloured sweets, is laid out in an amorphous shape on the floor, the sweets are the same colour as bone and the visitor is invited to take one away. A way of giving out the body of his father in an obvious parallel with the Transubstantiation. This is one of a series of candy works, using different colours of sweets arranged in different abstract shaped heaps on the floor according to the subject of the work (given in the title) always to be taken away so that the work changes through interaction. Another series of works are bead curtains, floor to ceiling, strung across the width of a gallery to bisect or divide it creating a new space, partially transparent, the beads are of certain specified colours. One work included a performative element. A Male dancer naked except for silver shorts and boots danced on a podium surrounded by lights. When the dancer wasn’t there the podium remained as the work, a plinth for an absent sculpture, the dancer, symbolic of death, the final curtain and the performance of life. Gonzales Torres also made a series of light bulb works, strings of standard light bulbs to be strung in specific locations, in a tree, a stairwell for example. He also made photographic works, the most well known of which is an empty bed with white sheets each of the pillows bearing the impressions left by a head. Another is a photograph of flowers growing in bare soil, which is titled The Grave of Gertrude Stein. Gonzales Torres’ work deals with death, the fleeting nature of life, and the idea of the memorial, by using the simplest sculptural forms. 

!Rodney Graham.

Canadian artist born in the 1950s who since the 1980s has made works in film, video, installation, photography and sound. Graham made a video showing himself as Robinson Crusoe lying beneath a palm tree sleeping until a coconut drops onto his head from the tree. He sits ups, rubs his eyes, tries to focus, feels dizzy and lies down only to have the same thing happen again, and in fact the film is a loop. This work portrays the endless loop of time lived out by Crusoe while stranded. Another film shows a house burning down. Graham made a sound work in which he took a segment of a piece of classical music which is not usually played when that piece is performed, analysed it and added it to make a playable loop. He then arranged for an orchestra to play it and had it recorded. Graham took a photograph of a tree in winter, and presented it upside down. The branches of the tree become the roots, and the roots the branches. On the theme of the inverted or retinal image Gordon also installed a pinhole camera in a horse drawn carriage, giving the viewer the opportunity to enter the carriage and view the scene outside through the camera upside down. 

!Gregory Green.

American artist born in the 1960s who makes installation and sculpture. In the early 1990s Green made an installation in a small room for which he constructed an imaginary IRA bomb factory from scratch by reading books and information freely available to the public. The process of bomb making is shown taking place in a small kitchen, the materials needed strewn everywhere, empty cups and ashtrays, food and food wrappers, plates the evidence of long hours of work taking place. The drain gin board contains canisters of weed killer and bags of sugar, the materials used to make explosives, and there are five litre plastic bottles with liquid undergoing some process of transformation. A table is crammed with electrical equipment and tools, mostly ordinary domestic materials such as light switches, plugs, fuses, wire, insulating tape, cable connectors. There is a bomb maker’s manual open on the table. The verisimilitude is such that the viewer cannot avoid the feeling that they are trespassing in a place where an illegal activity is taking place. The viewer feels jumpy, as if they might be discovered by the perpetrators who they feel might be dangerous. The effect of the installation is to show how close to daily life, how enmeshed in the everyday is the potential to kill. Green also made several bombs complete but lacking explosive and detonators. These attracted the attention of the tabloid press and in some cases the gallery that exhibited was forced to remove them. One was a suitcase bomb, one was even a nuclear device, complete except for the plutonium. Subsequently Green set up a pirate radio station and broadcast illegally to a free state that he had created. 

!Renee Green.

American artist born in the 1960s, Green is known for her large scale interactive installations which have a socio political genesis. In the late 1990s Green made an installation that filled a gallery with a maze like partition board structure consisting of a series of spaces, the walls painted in different colours, sometimes plain, sometimes striped. Viewers walked through the structure, choosing to stop where they wished. Videos are presented on monitors on the floor with bean bags and headphones strewn about. Written participation in a survey is invited. Photographs are on the walls. One space has sitting room furniture. One has a record collection and a record player. Overall the viewer enters a microcosmic world that represents daily life and is asked to consider and participate in activities that illustrate how identity is constructed and how it might be possible to think in new ways about its construction. 

!Johan Grimonprez.

Belgian artist who made a video work in the late 1990s D I A L History. This projected film consists of vast amount of excerpted footage from newsreels, film and television that concerns terrorism, specifically the hijacking of an aeroplane. Grimonprez has edited this footage to make a short film showing the history of hijacking and the imagery that we have of such events. 

!Lucy Gunning.

British artist born in the 1970s who made a video titled Climbing Round my Room (mid 1990s). Gunning negotiated the perimeter of her room without touching the floor, which involved climbing to the top of a wardrobe and balancing precariously on various items of furniture and using door handles and steps. The resulting video presented a re articulation of an ordinary domestic space. 

!Andreas Gursky.

Swiss photographer who takes pictures of banal scenes in which the minute is contrasted with the gigantic, creating a formal image which in some cases becomes almost abstract. Gursky heightens this effect by manipulating the image in the darkroom, often altering the perspective so that the image reads as a flat picture plane rather than a representation of perspective. There is usually a repetitive pattern created by the presence in the images of a mass of something, for example people or windows. These effects are subtle and subversive. Examples of Gursky's work from the 1990s include a container park at the port of Ferrara, where the bright colours of the containers form a patchwork pattern on the background of red soil and glowing pink light. The floor of a Siemens factory is a mainly white image in which the black lines for the production line furniture and the bright colours for the electrical cable supplies create a pattern. The Japanese stock exchange is a pattern in yellow and black with white added by the presence of cascading tickets and the striped jackets of the trader. An expanse of plain carpet seems to be vast, skiers on slope are sticks of colour in a white space, a race course in Japan can be read as horizontal bands of colour, the display case in a Prada shoe shop is alive with glowing jewelled colour, a shopping centre is presented as bands of brass and greenery as the escalator travel down through the main atrium, a vast apartment block is a pattern of squares, a canal and dyke are horizontal stripes ! Graham Gussin. British artist born 1960s who makes drawings and sometimes installations. Around 1999 Gussin made a sound sensitive floor that made sounds in response to the viewer’s footsteps, producing a re articulation of the viewer’s action in looking. Gussin made some drawings of the sound produced in this way using a computer programme that represented the sound waves as a perspective drawing. 

!Fabrice Gygi.

Swiss artist born in the 1960s who makes installations. These consist of structures in metal wood rubber and plastic, which apparently have practical function but in fact are difficult to decipher. The structures or groups of structures might fill a room. They have frames made from some kind of metal pole, put together using clamps and off the shelf heavy duty fixings. There might be a screen like structure, a bench like one, a platform. The frames might be filled in with plastic or rubber sheeting, hemmed and edged with large eyelets and then laced to the frame. A bench might have an upholstered top. The elements reference the gym and the club, which here seems to be an arena that implies sex and possibly violence. Yet they are benign mysterious objects that invite human interaction. 

!Matthew Hale.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes performance videos and assembled sculptures from household objects that hint at performative action. Hale was one of the founders of City Racing, an artist run gallery in London in the 1990s. 

!Siobhan Hapaska.

Irish artist born in 1965 who makes sculptures and installation. An early work was eight identical life casts of a clothed young man, painted in buff colour, bearing a coffin. Made in 1990s, this work seemed like a comment on the number of young men who were dying from AIDS related illnesses at that time. He has also made a vaguely heart shaped wall mounted ‘speaker’ constructed from teak veneer and purple felt, with a heartbeat sound playing from it. St Christopher Legless was included in a room sized installation featuring a fibreglass body cast or lifelike sculpture of the saint in rags and long black beard, sitting against the wall with amputated or cut off legs, an irony for this patron saint of travellers, or a cruel blow dealt to him deliberately. Echoes of kneecapping punishment in the conflict in Northern Ireland. There was also a fibreglass sculpture of a motorbike, whose contours and mechanics had been smoothed out and filled in so that the whole bike turned into a kind of futuristic vehicle. This was indeed reminiscent of the sculptures of Boccioni but also somehow of Rodin. It was sprayed in a pearlescent white and highly polished. Also a large bedlike structure, a futuristic sleeping platform made from shiny white lacquered fibreglass, the platform with ergonomic curved base, with a slit running all round the inset mattress area, from which water was pumped and ran over the sides of the base in a constant water curtain. The mattress of shaggy fur, with a harness and headphones suggesting that one could strap oneself in and be transported, not literally but mentally, by the comfort and the sound and the trickling water. The bed, like the motorbike, seemed to come from science fiction. 

!Mark Harris.

British artist born around 1960 who makes conceptual work. 

!David Harrison.

British artist born in the 1950s who makes autobiographical sculpture, paintings and drawings. Harrison’s work has a naïve quality, the paintings are small, around 60 cm high, and sometimes awkwardly painted. The drawings are made in pencil. In his early work in the 1980s Harrison concentrated almost exclusively on painting cats with a highly stylised 50s appearance. Later Harrison began to describe his own life and lives like his more literally in terms of encounters with people, animals and landscape. For example, several paintings feature a caravan in a rural setting, and include the name of a purported guest to the caravan in the title, for example British footballer Paul Gascoigne. In some of the caravan paintings a mysterious white rabbit hovers, ghost like, as if it represents the spirit of something. Harrison’s sculptures are assemblages of objects to resemble animals, in the spirit of Picasso. Forks, table legs and any kind of discarded objects are given new roles as the legs of a heron, a bird’s beak. The sculptures junk like appearance belies their accurate resemblance to the animal they represent. 

!Marcus Harvey.

Marcus Harvey is a British painter born in the 1970s who is interested in using paint and its application to produce a hidden image, or conversely to produce an image by an unusual means of application of paint. Harvey made a series of works in the 1990s, which on first inspection appear to be abstract paintings with lots of impasto and brushstrokes. In fact superimposed are outlines of women taken from pornographic images painted in thin black lines merging into the abstraction. Once recognised, however the images are very clear. In 1999 Harvey made a painting of Myra Hindley, a convicted child murderer still serving her sentence at the time, which became notorious. This painting became famous by the constant press interest in the story. Harvey made the ten metres high portrait, taken from the police mug shot, from the handprints of children. The handprints in varying shades from white to dark grey form a kind of pixel effect, creating and image that only appears in full detail from distance, much like the effect of a billboard poster. 

!Jun Hasewaga.

Korean artist working in Britain, born in the 1970s. Hasewaga makes paintings of people on board, in shiny gloss paint with solid outlines, and then cut out. 

!Thomas Helbig.

German artist born in the 1960s who makes paintings on hessian. 

!Matthew Higgs.

British curator and artist born in the early 60s who makes works on paper. Higgs’s dry witty conceptual work re presents book covers, pages from books and titles, making plays on words and puns. He will often make a text work in which a book, or a title is transformed by minimal intervention into a reference to the visual. One series of works involved obliterating all the words on a page except the words ‘There is a painting on the wall’ and placing this framed page next to a painting by another artist. Higgs also made an artists publication series called Imprint 93 (from 1993 onwards) for which he invited artist to make a small pamphlet. This activity bridges his practise as an artists and his work as a curator. 

!Roger Hiorns.



!Thomas Hirschhorn.

German artist born around 1960 who makes large scale environments and installations. Hirschorn’s work evolves from a life as art philosophy that leads him to create environments and make related publications. He uses found imagery, and all kinds of materials in his work, building large architectural structures in a homemade way from cardboard, silver foil, plastic bags, and any number of other found items and materials. 

!Damien Hirst.

British artist born in the early 1960s who became one of the best known contemporary artists in Britain when he exhibited a shark preserved in formaldehyde in a glass tank titled The Impossibility of Death in the Mind of Someone Living. Hirst is considered to have been the impetus fro a generation of British artists known as the Young British Artists (abbreviated to YBAs) because he curated an exhibition in an empty warehouse titled Freeze. This featured his contemporaries (they were all still students) at a time when it was nearly impossible for young artists to exhibit their work in London galleries. The term YBAs went on to become the collective name for all of Hirst’s generation, including Jake and Dinos Chapman, Tracy Emin, and Sarah Lucas. Hirst’s work was purchased by the London Collector and advertising executive Charles Saatchi, who financed the shark and subsequent formaldehyde works. Saatchi had the only gallery in London at the time big enough to exhibit these works. Hirst made a number of formaldehyde works including a cow and her calf sliced right through into two vertical halves, exhibited in two pairs of tanks so that the viewer could walk throng the centre of the cow and the calf. He also made a work consisting of a glass wall case filled with rows of fish of different species preserved by taxidermy, all facing the same way (Fish Swimming in the Same Direction) Hirst has made many paintings which fall into three series, the spin paintings (done on a circular canvas spinning horizontally which has multicoloured gloss paint thrown at it), the spot paintings (multicoloured gloss paint spots on white arranged in rows) and the butterfly paintings. The first butterfly paintings were canvases covered with gloss paint in plain colours in the surface of which adhered many different types of butterfly. Most recently Hirst has been covering the canvas entirely with the wings of butterflies, arranged in geometric patterns. Hirst’s paintings are made by assistants to his instructions and produced in large numbers. The spot paintings have become Hirst’s logo, and these signature spots adorned first the restaurant that Hirst opened with Charles Saatchi in London, The Pharmacy, then a Thames riverboat which takes visitors between the two Tate galleries, and finally an unmanned space vehicle which has been sent to Mars. Life and death are the major preoccupations of Hirst’s work made evident by his use of formaldehyde, insects, and medical drugs. Following his work with butterflies, Hirst used flies; a cow’s head was placed inside a three metres high glass tank, and maggots were allowed to breed in it. These turned into flies, which could fly around inside the tank and an interconnected one in which there was a desk and a chair. The flies eventually died, having lived their entire lives in the sealed tank. Medical drugs are used by Hirst to represent both life and death and he has made several works using bottles, cartons, boxes, tubes, and other containers of drugs. These are arranged in glass wall cases showing their predominantly white packaging with blocks of a huge variety of colours giving a particular visual effect when seen in the context of a gallery. Hirst made a room sized installation titled Pharmacy, which contained these cases on all the walls as well as some of the oversized red, green and blue coloured glass decanters that are seen in pharmacist’s windows. Hirst also made a feature film with many of his friends as actors, shot on 35 mm with high production values. In 2003 Hirst continues to make formaldehyde works, for example glass tanks containing animals’ heads with knives plunged into them, or with blood spattered on the wall around them. Damien Hirst’s work is often lampooned by the national press in cartoons. 

!Hobbypop.

Group of German and British artists who work collaboratively. Hobbypop are based in London. Their paintings are often installed both on the wall and propped at angles in the centre of a gallery space. The group also initiates events including guided walks, and theatrical performances. 

!Nicky Hoberman.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes large (up to three metres high) paintings of children. These are done from photographs taken by the artist of children she meets. They are painted as if there is a strange, often green or bluish light cast upon them. The children have exaggerated features and seem to exist in some other world. There is something sinister yet saccharine about the children. Hoberman applies paint smoothly and thinly, all brushstrokes softened out. 

!Candida Höfer.
