Photographer born around 1960 who has photographed zoos, libraries and various other institutions. Her images often show glamorous ornate surroundings yet have a cool restrained atmosphere. 

!Evan Holloway.

American artist who makes skeletal models of buildings from twigs and skeletal frameworks from multi coloured metal rods. 

!Georgie Hopkins.

British artist born in the late 1960s. 

!Roni Horn.

American artist working from the 1980s onwards. 

!Kate Howard.

American video artist born in the 1960s. 

!Douglas Huebler.

Canadian conceptual artist. 

!Des Hughes.

British artist born in the 1970s who makes resin cast sculptures of junk. For example Hughes might model old nails in clay and cast them, painting them to look exactly like the real thing. 

!Marine Hugonnier.

French artist who made a film over several months spent in Afghanistan. 

!Gary Hume.

British artist who has represented Britain at the Venice Biennale. Hume is one of the artists in the group known as Young Births Artists or YBAs who emerged in the early 90s. Hume began his career making paintings from hospital doors, which he painted all over with white or cream coloured gloss paint. In fact gloss household paint has become Hume’s trademark and he never uses anything else. Hume went on to make figurative paintings mostly portraits in which he vastly simplified the elements of a face, so that it consisted of one or two areas of colour and no features. The paint was poured on, allowed to dry, and then the next colour added. The edge of the dry part acts as a barrier to the next colour. Hume painted the model Kate Moss, a virgin Mary and other images taken from famous paintings, and a baby. He featured plants, and vague interiors, but all in his vastly simplified blocks of shiny colour. The paintings nearly resist interpretation, which is Hume’s intention. He made a video in which he portrays himself as King Canute. Around 2000 Hume began to make sculptures, bronze snowmen, very smoothed out shapes painted in shiny pale colours. 

!Per Huttner.

Swedish artist who make socially inclusive work in which he often invites the viewer to participate. 

!Pierre Huyghe.

Swiss artist who makes photographs and videos. One photograph shows a stone frozen in mid air over a motorway. The stone looks like a boulder implying impending tragedy as it will surely cause a terrible accident, until the viewer realises that it is a tiny pebble that has been photographed as it drops from a bridge over the speeding cars. 

!Cathy de Monchaux.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculptures and installations. De Monchaux made a number of wall mounted works in the early 1990s from machined metal templates, bolted together with velvet, fur and leather often red or purple or black which was clamped between the metal but spilled out in carefully contrived folds. These constructions had a sexual fetishistic quality, they hinted at a purpose but remained ambiguous. They have a slightly gothic appearance but also a heavy metal aesthetic. Put simply they contrasted the supposedly masculine with the feminine, the functional with the luxurious to create decidedly feminine and visceral forms, which often alluded to the female body and genitalia in particular. De Monchaux continued to develop this way of working by expanding the scale of her sculptures, making repeating units and larger more complex forms, using a wider palette of coloured fabric and other materials, and sometimes making floor based installations. 

!Yasu Ichige.

Japanese artist living in London, Ichige makes videos usually featuring himself. In one he places a car directly in front of a wall and puts his foot on the accelerator, driving into the wall without moving until the tyres catch fire and the engine gives up. Another shows Ichige enter an amusement arcade dressed in full racing driver gear. He sits in a seat in front of a racing game and plays the game, winning the race without dropping any points. We see the game from his point of view. 

!Inventory.

British artist group who make socially engaged and political work and publish a journal, Inventory. Inventory’s work usually consists of installations, often some kind of stand or kiosk like structure, with a variety of photographic and text works. They take the position of proselytisers for a new way of life, highlighting the dystopian elements in everyday life and imagining collective actions. An example is a photo collage of London in 2002 showing the city as if it were about to collapse. Inventory have spray painted anti American slogans on the walls of a museum in which they were invited to show. 

!Jim Isermann.

American artist who makes multicoloured tapestry quilts and cubes. The tapestries hand stitched in repeated geometric patterns. 

!Runa Islam.

Twentieth century artist who makes films that are concerned with ethnicity. 

!Christian Jankowski.

German artist born in the 1970s who makes video, installation and sound work. In Bank, Jankowski hired security guards to keep people out of a empty disused bank, the result of which was that people wanted to enter a space they ordinarily would have ignored. In another work Jankoswki recorded a board meeting at a national art museum, and then got children to speak the transcript. Because the children had no understanding of the issues discussed they gave the transcript an entirely new and rather odd emphasis. Jankowski often uses actors. In another work he asks actors to record the reading of the extended email correspondence between himself and his girlfriend when they were apart for several months. Again he was interested in the different inflexion and emphases given to the transcript. Jankoswki is interested in what happens if slight subversions are made to everyday materials and locations. 

!Chantal Joffe.

British artist born in the 1960s, Joffe paints children and women in loose expressive style. Her paintings are usually very small, no more that 20 or 30 cm high, made on board with oil paint. She uses photographs as her source. Joffe often exhibits groups of paintings together arranged in a line or on a grid pattern. 

!Alison Jones.

British figurative painter who began working in the 80s and 90s. at the beginning of 2000 Jones began to make site specific installations by painting directly on walls using projections of shadows (for example of people who might inhabit the room someone on their hands and knees vomiting, or a cleaner going about their work, painted in flat black silhouette) and by wallpapering architecturally complex sections of the interiors of buildings using multicoloured striped wallpaper. 

!Gareth Jones.

British sculptor, formerly a critic and curator, Jones makes singular and highly crafted objects, a red yellow and blue cube with one side missing, a paint handkerchief made by painting several layers of white paint onto a handkerchief and peeling off the paint, a peacock feather stuck into a wall, works using unspoiled cassette tape, stainless steel minimal box floor pieces, a series of plinths made of different materials, some with words on, commenting on the display of art and turning the plinth in art object, a cape made of raffle tickets, works using LP covers, drawing pins, tape, and architectural model like constructions. Often a homage to a musician, for example David bowie. 

!Sarah Jones.

British artist who makes hard edged abstract pattern paintings that take the facades of modern buildings as her subject. She minimises the detail in these and produces brightly coloured grids which have a perspective to them, the grid is in white. The high gloss paint is meticulously neatly applied. 

!Zebedee Jones.

British painter born in the 1970s who makes abstract paintings by putting a vast quantity of very thick oil paint in a single on a canvas (usually under one metre high) and spreading it across the canvas leaving the brush marks readily visible. His paintings are really no more than thick layers of paint and as such they draw attention to the physicality of painting. 

!Ben Judd.

British artist born around 1970 who takes photographs that allude to glamour photography, using amateur techniques such as Vaseline on the camera lens. In 2002 he began making short video films that show groups of people involved in photo shoots and film making watching the actors and models, who are never shown. Judd’s work thus becomes a study of the viewers, of vicarious pleasures, revealing these men in their absorption. 

!Isaac Julien.

British artist who makes films, usually presented in two or threes screen forma. Julien uses 35 mm film and projects his films in especially exhibition spaces. He is interested in masculine identity and sexual and ethnic specificity. His films have a still, dream like quality, with high production values. 

!Alan Kane.

British artist who worked with Jeremy Deller on a work called the Folk Archive, a collection of British folk and craft objects. 

!Ann Teresa Keersmaeker.

Dutch artist ! Seydou Keita. An African artist who photographed the extraordinary qualities he found in ordinary people. The photographs are black and white. 

!Mike Kelley.

US artist making sculptures and drawing, Kelley has a particular interest in history, politics and the state. Often uses unwanted and second hand materials, car boot and charity shop purchases. Series of formal sculptural works using soft toys, for example a large wall relief made off found soft toys sewn onto a found blanket, which uses abject objects to makes reference to abstract expressionist painting, and highlights craft and homemade qualities, drawing a comparison between different values assigned to cultural production. Similar floor pieces – two soft toy dogs face each other across a small blanket, somehow an encounter that seems serious and eloquent. A number of different sized toys under a large grey blanket entitled Lumpenprole a jokey reference to the greyness and poverty of ordinary working lives and a pun on the German – a lumpy grey blanket is just what the work appears to be. A large collection of soft toys fills a room neatly laid out on trestle tables, all accompanied by measuring sticks, as if in some kind of statistical survey, post mortem, or the aftermath of a mass disaster like an air crash. The soft toys used to represent human kind in its lowliness and vulnerability. Kelley manages to use these materials in such a way as to produce a formal sculptural works that have a seriousness that belies their existence as discarded and undervalued objects. In fact Kelley eschews conventional art materials on the whole. A series of felt banners made in a homespun way, around six feet by four feet, in which he replicates flyers found on the street usually made by grass root organisations bearing populist messages, or even just advertisements. An installation called Plato’s cave which has large panels with drawings of American leaders like Lincoln accompanied by substantial text all round the wall. Drawing with marker pen and use of handwritten text also common in Kelley’s work. 

!Janice Kerbel.

Canadian artist working in Britain, born in 1969. Kerbels’ work is conceptual and usually research based, and is carried out in variety of media, united by her interest in desire and its link to deception. Kerbel’s research often involves a degree of controlled risk, and elements of detective work. The place of desire in Kerbels work is elucidated by her invention of utopian places and ideal conditions. Kerbels earliest works from the early 1990s concern subterfuge and malicious intent. A table is laden with jam made from poisonous plants. Snippings is a series of minute snippings taken from the clothes of strangers, who remain unaware of this affront. The snippings are mounted and titled with the date and the place on the garment and type of garment from which the snipping is taken. Trust Me (Remington) is typed on a blue sheet of letter paper, folded once, with a ring stain from a coffee mug on it. The letter s has jumped and is higher than the rest as if it had been typed on a manual typewriter in the days when the author of a letter could be traced by mechanical faults in the way a typewriter functioned, such as a raised letter. Accompanying this is a disc containing a digital typewriter style font with a raised s that Kerbel has created. Kerbel’s interest in deception led her to invent four Magic Tricks fro which she designed the apparatus as if it were flat pack furniture. The tricks included levitation table and disappearance box. Like much of Kerbels work the plans and drawings are the work, which is never actually realised. This is intrinsic for the success of the work as it hinges on Kerbel’s interest in the desire to do unacceptable or impossible things. Places to Bury a Body illustrates this. A series of photo pHs show very ordinary places such as a front garden, where there is no obvious hiding space but the acknowledgment that a hole could be dug. Or a cliff top over which a body could be thrown. Bank Job (1999) consists of two cork board panels onto which are pinned detailed plans for a bank robbery from an existing bank in the City of London. There are reconnaissance photographs, detailed floor plans, a map of the getaway route, and an exhaustive time line of the robbery, listing all the participants, entrances and exits, times and vehicles involved. Bank Job is a work which presents the inconceivable as something which anyone could carry out. A number of Kerbel’s works are concerned with how to cheat. Home Fittings is a series of floor plans, made for existing rooms and buildings, on which Kerbel indicates where a person might walk in order to avoid any creaking floorboards or flooring, and where a person might stand in relation to doors to avoid casting a shadow that might give away their presence. These places are indicated in the space by the insertion of thin brass rods flush with the floor. Marked Decks are prints showing the backs of packs of playing cards, the patterns on which have been subtly altered to allow a person in the know to deduce the suit and number of the card. A letter typed on blue paper seems to allude to a failed tryst, an unfulfilled desire. A number of Kerbel’s works deal with this explicitly, for example Bird Island (2000) a web project and book. Kerbel invented an island paradise based on a real uninhabited Caribbean island, and designed a website that invited the purchase of timeshare in the island. The topography, flora, fauna and the style of the buildings on the island are explained and illustrated in detail. The site persuade the viewer to buy into a dream which doesn’t exist. Any applicants would receive a brochure. Kerbel even invented a bird, the Exuma Emerald, which she later illustrated by commissioning a drawing from a bird illustrator and the creation of its potential song from a bird song expert, presented as a cd. Further to this Kerbel made a number of drawings of fantasy islands in which she played god, taking control of the climate and thus the flora and fauna. In 2003 Kerbel made a series of drawings of hypothetical planters for specified but unnamed buildings. The planters and the plants they contained are designed to grow the most suitable plants for the buildings climactic conditions in the best possible way, thus the plant lists take account of light quality and direction, temperature and humidity, the planters make the most of available light and maximise the planting relevant to the size of the space. The floor plans indication the light source and type, the scale and disposition of the planters, the function of the building and the climactic conditions There is a respiration garden for a Gym, for example, planters for low light in a Victorian house, a shelf garden for a student room, a hanging garden for a launderette and one for a bathroom both making use of high humidity, and circular garden for a revolving restaurant. 

!Karen Kilimnik.

American artist born around 1960 who is primarily a painter, although she has begun to make photographic work in the 2000s. Kilimnik’s practise is project based. She makes work around a particular theme and usually presents it as an installation. One such project dealt with the fantasy life of teenage girls, and featured a gallery with purple painted walls, drawings on the walls with lots of text which has a diaristic quality, paintings and autumn leaves strewn on the floor. Another had a Russian theme, and has paintings that have qualities of Russian icons, with Russian themes, and snow scenes, objects and stuffed animals and drifts of artificial snow on the floor. Kilimnik’s paintings are rarely more than 60 cm high. Kilimnik has many pets and animals are a constant in her work, in particular a pet cockatoo, a crow called Ralph, and dogs and cats. Ralph’s portrait, Ralph in Ectoplasm, shows him both as he is and covered in ectoplasm. Another installation is based on a detailed study of ballet, in particular Giselle, and features gallery walls painted dark red, the paintings and drawings in black frames depicting scenes from the ballet, imagined related scenes, and a sculpture on a plinth. Some of the paintings have black velvet ribbon bows on the frame and silk flowers. Kilimnik’s work hovers close to kitschness and sweetness which is held in check by her skill as a painter and the darkness of some of her subject matter. In 2001 Kilimnik made a series of photographs of central park in New York showing the park in winter in low light conditions. By careful framing, the light conditions, and aided by the appearance of a horse drawn carriage used to convey tourists on sightseeing tours, Kilimnik showed the park as if it had been photographed in the nineteenth century. These were shown alongside a number of landscapes and cityscapes that are also painted in an anachronistic style. Kilimnik’s work is distinctive for its combination of rich reference to the past and the history of art with an autobiographical and contemporary approach to painting. 

!Scott King.

King is a British artist born in the 1960s and publisher of an magazine called Crash!. Crash! is a broadsheet sized magazine written and designed by King and another person, which features anti establishment satire and polemic mixed with cultural criticism and appreciation in the form of poster style text, graphics and images. It is witty and uncompromising, and is a hybrid of magazine and art work which is implicitly critical of the magazine publishing industry. King’s solo work draws on these approaches. He has made collages from magazine images and a large number of text works often in the form of numbered lists. In the 2000s King’s texts became more autobiographical and often self deprecating. They are designed and typeset in Kings very visual style. One work is entitled ‘Why I Never Became A Punk’ and consists of a list of the reasons for this. 

!Joanna Kirk.

British artist who makes large scale figurative pastel drawings. 

!Michael Klega.

German artist born in the 1960s, Klega makes wall drawings form unspoiled cassette tape wound round map pins, sculptures from plastic water bottles. He utilises everyday materials like adhesive tape to make his minimal but often dryly humorous works. 

!Jochen Klein.

German artist born in the 1960s died in the late 1990s. Klein’s water colour and collage paintings have an oneric atmosphere, as if the protagonists were lost in time. The collage, usually an image from a magazine often a person, is often the starting point for a painting. Klein extended the collaged image by painting out from it. He used thin washes of colour on a white ground, often leaving areas deliberately unfinished, to make translucent and light filled images. He uses Klein also made works on paper including a pop up book made from collaged images featuring a pop up penis as its centrefold. 

!Ulli Knall.

Swiss artist living in Britain who makes ceramic sculptures. Knall has made a series of small busts of characters from television programmes such as Star Trek, and other invented characters, as well as portraits of her friends. These are modelled in a simple almost naïve style. Other works include small fountains. Most of Knall work is on a domestic scale, ranging between 30 to 80 cm high. The sculptures are painted and glazed carefully in realistic colours. 

!Jutta Koether.

Koetther is a German artist born in the 1960s who makes large messy paintings and drawings with text added. These are apparently autobiographical and personal but remain mysterious. The paintings are large and scruffy, often appearing to have been made on the floor over a period of time. 

!Tania Kovats.

British artists who uses model making materials to construct landscapes on shelves mounted on the wall, grassy hills and valleys with trees, rivers and lakes, microcosmic three dimensional landscapes. 

!Elke Krystufek.

Austrian artist born in 1970s whose subject is herself and her own body portrayed in videos, drawings and paintings of herself in her daily life. However Krystufek often appears naked, usually made up with the red lipstick which is characteristic of the videos and the drawings, sometimes in an abject state and her work is also a portrait of neurosis. Her emotional state is usually unexplained and never indulgent. A video self portrait, for example is shot with a hand held (thus never more than arms length away) as she spins around dancing. Her own apartment is often the setting for the work. 

!Wolfgang Laib.

artist working from the 1990s onwards, Laib makes installations. He made a floor work by sifting bright yellow pollen over a large rectangle shape in an even layer, the colour startlingly bright. The effect of this work lies in the contrast between the minimal quality of the work, the quantity of the material and the viewers knowledge of the organ and potential of the material and the work required to obtain it. 

!Jim Lambie.

British artist working from the 1990s onwards who made a number of floor installations in which the floor of a gallery or museum is entirely covered in stripes made of plastic tape in different colours and widths, the stripes following the floor plan of the room, running round the edge of the room and moving inwards towards the centre of the room concentrically until the centre is reached. Sometimes Lambie might use black and whiter tape, or metallics. Lambie also made wall sculptures from ordinary domestic objects such as record player turntables, mattresses, mirrors which are covered in glitter. 

!Sean Landers.

American artist born in the 1960s who made a number of very large text paintings in the 1990s. the text on these paintings is essentially a diary of his life as an artist. In these works Landy tries a new way of carrying out the traditional task of the artist to make life into art. The canvas of one 3 meter high painting rolls onto the floor at the bottom, reaching toward the viewer implying a physical inclusion in the work, and in the viewing and reading space. Landers went on to make a series of very large paintings after famous artists such as Picasso, copying works and adding to them, so that they are instantly recognisable but recognisable different. 

!Michael Landy.

Landy is a British artist who was born in the 1960s. He began career making obsessively detailed cartoon like drawings in black pen featuring a multitude of images and text. These drawings created an alternative world of social and political comment and from them emerged a large scale installation, Scrapheap Services in the late 1990s. Scrapheap Services is a fictitious company which carries out the collection and disposal of unwanted members of society. What Landy showed was a collection of the trolleys, uniforms, printed literature, signage, bins and waste bags, all bearing the companies logo and livery. The paper literature was stern all over the floor as if it were itself rubbish. He also made a flower stall. Landy's next large scale work further developed his interest in human waste and the value of inanimate objects, sentimentality and materiality. This involved the professional construction of a large waste disposal plant consisting of a conveyor belt for sorting, various grading machines and a compressing, shredding and grinding machines, reducing everything to a pulp. Landy passed every single item had every owned through this machine: the contents of his flat, his car, his clothes. The item he found most difficult to destroy was his deceased fathers coat. The machinery was installed in a temporarily empty department store ground floor and open to the public every day. The list of items destroyed remains as the document of the work. In 2003 Landy made a number of botanical drawings of weeds species and varieties that might be found growing in the streets and waste areas of London. 

!Abigail Lane.

Born in eh 1960s Lane has made a number of installations. For one of these she papered a gallery space a wall paper printed with bloody hand prints. In the centre of the room a female figure constructed from a collection of clothes, shoes and a wig hung from a rope. 

!Langlands and Bell.

Two artists who work together producing works that investigate architecture and the built environment and its relating to the lives of people. In the late 1990s they made a work which consisted of a series of white chairs with models of buildings set into seats visible through a glass top to the seat. They made a series of works about the panopticon plan for prison buildings. 

!Matthew Leahy.

British artist born in the 1970s died in 1999. Leahy made landscape paintings in particular snow capped mountains. 

!Mark Leckey.

British artist born 1976 makes video and sound installations. Leckey’s work comes out of club and street culture, but has a serious intent. His video Fiorucci made me hardcore shows intercuts footage of the British northern soul scene with hardcore house clubs, edited to make a compelling testament to transcendence through dance and music. Other works come out of his experience as a dj. Member of the art ban Donatella. 

!Marysia Lewandowska and Neil Cummings.

British artists working together who make theoretical conceptual investigations into economies, culture and knowledge, resulting in a socio political project based practise which may be presented in many different forms. They made a project in London with the Bank of England which was the result of a period of research. They presented documentation of their research and made it possible for museum visitors to interact with their work. 

!Mark Lewis.

British artist born in the 1950s who works in 35 mm film and video. Lewis’s work since the 1980s has stemmed from his interest in and knowledge of the history of cinema. He uses the structure and visual language to construct short looped films presented as installations and longer more narrative scripted films using actors. Lewis presents everyday scenes and situations through the lens of cinematic production, prompting the viewer to consider the inconsequential as a moment of grandeur, and to question what they are seeing. A short looped film made in the late 1990s shot from inside a café A woman is talking to a man outside on the pavement right in front of the window. But there is something odd about their conversation. The man doesn’t seem to be listening to the woman. Eventually a car passes and disappears mid screen. Then other traffic does the same, coming from both directions, as do pedestrians. Now the viewer begins to think that they are watching a split screen, a convention occasionally used in cinema to show a telephone conversation or to show two things happening simultaneously, and that in fact the couple are not talking to each other but to others. Finally it becomes clear that in fact the dividing on the screen is in fact the edge of a mirror on the wall of the café, which exactly reflect the street outside. The woman is in fact reflected in this mirror and is talking to someone else out of view. The man is seen through the window and is on his own, waiting. A film made in a city housing estate shows a concrete walkway with tower blocks rising above it, the vast empty space punctuated and enlivened by children playing games. Another film shows a derelict building of which only the façade and floors. The camera moves towards the building extremely slowly. As it moves the viewer becomes aware of the trajectory of the camera, something that is largely unnoticed usually. It is moving in and down from a great height and the shot is continues. The camera reaches the building and enters a window. On a table in the empty roofless space is a child’s spinning top. This short looped film places the viewer in the position of the camera and makes the process of filming evident and the subject of the work. The camera is finally understood to be on a very high crane which is also moving on a track, usually only associated with big budget film making. Another work shows A section of an black and white silent film showing an elevated cast iron railway upside down. A landscape work shows an iced over lake in a snowy landscape with a solitary skater on the ice. Lewis has made narrative films using actors, including one shot in a garden in California which uses porn actors, one shot in a Californian desert hotel, Peeping Tom (a homage to the feature film of the same name) and The Pitch, which shows Lewis himself standing in the middle of the main concourse of a railway station talking to the camera, oblivious to the people around him. By the end of his monologue it becomes apparent that he is pitching the idea for the very film that the viewer is watching directly to the viewer. 

!Cedar Lewisohn.

American or Canadian artist who makes abstract pattern paintings and related drawings which reflect his work as a dj. 

!Glenn Ligon.

American artist born in the 1960s who made work about black American identity. A lot of Ligon's work took slavery and its legacy as a starting point. He made installations and added text to them and also made photographic works. A canvas punch bag hanging from the ceiling on a rope has a poetic story of a slave prize fighter stencilled on its white surface in black letters, as if it were both a painting and a sack. This object symbolised the body of the slave, captive to be treated as an object, subjected to punches, traded as goods. A boxing ring has text inscribed around it in a similar way. Ligon made a version of the US Flag drawn in black with words where the stars and stripes would be. In his work he drew attention to the objectification of black Americans, particularly black American men. 

!Hilary Lloyd.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes video films that she terms sculptures. Lloyds films are always presented on a specified size of monitor anything ranging from a portable personal TV with head phones to a large monitor. The monitors are placed on monitor stands of a particular type, in particular relation to each other. Some works may consist of two monitors places back to back. The viewer is never a typically passive receiver, since they have to walk round the monitors and Lloyds work is never shown projected. Lloyds films have a minimal sculptural quality. She is chiefly interested in people and banal repetitive activities. For example, in Colin, made in the mid 1990s, a young man standing on some derelict land between two buildings proceed to slowly pick up, examine and discard the items that he finds there, pieces of wood and metal and other kinds of refuse. He does this slowly, very deliberately, as if there were some kind of purpose to his actions, which become quite compelling and graceful in repetition. Colin, like most of Lloyd’s work involving people, met the artist in a club and she persuaded him to allow her to film him. She takes pains to allow her subjects to make their own decisions about what they will do as she films, arrived at through social interaction. In this sense she is emphatically not directing the action. Other works have featured a man pulling a jumper off over his head played in forward and reverse on back to back monitors so that it becomes a kind of dance. There is one of a young man sitting on the floor cutting images out of magazines endlessly. There is a film in which two teenage girls hold a length of string between them, one has ball and slowly unwinds it the other winds the string back into a ball. The end of the film the girls have transferred the string from one to the other. Another film shows a woman in a white trouser suit sitting on a chair one leg resting on the other knee, smoking a cigarette. On the point of extinction of the cigarette the film loop begins again. Most of Lloyd's work is looped, including the works that do not feature people such as one which shows a reflection on the surface of water which is somehow read, the film becoming a shimmering abstract pattern. The films are always static camera single continuous shots, even one in which Lloyd attaches a camera to a skateboard and asks a skateboarder to skate. The resulting film of pavement, ground and then graffiti covered walls as the skateboard goes vertical is accompanied by the sound of the wheels. Similarly a film shot again form a static camera placed on the windowsill of the revolving restaurant in the post office tower show 360 degree panorama of London in the time it takes for the restaurant to revolve – this is quite slow so the image only changes imperceptibly. Lloyd made some films in which two djs are in a taxi going from one job to another, and a text and photographic work which features a dj called princess Julia. Later works from the early 2000s show the spray from a fountain against backdrop of trees, ebbing and flowing with the wind and the jet of the water, and a slide projection showing waiters at a café in St. marks square in Venice. Lloyd became interested in groups of people observed rather than single people invited to cooperate. Lloyds work deals with the still moments in everyday life that can be considered as formal visual and discrete. Andy Warhol is a reference point for her work. 

!Hew Locke.

British artist who made Cardboard Palace, a maze that fills a gallery from floor to ceiling made from corrugated cardboard sheets cut into shapes a bit like a Frank Stella wall relief, with light bulbs creating shadows. The cardboard is left brown with only the black felt pen outlines as adornment. The maze leads to a kind of sitting room with a tea table. There is lots of documentation about the British Royal Family pinned to the structures of the maze. 

!Sharon Lockhart.

American artist born in the 1960s who makes films and photographic work. Lockhart’s work has an anthropological vision, she is interested in how the cultural life of different society groups is portrayed by those who study it, and in how this can be made visual, or even in how the artist can make a similar portrayal with very different results. Lockhart made series of photographs of the inhabitants of dwellings that had been visited and described by anthropologists. The dwellings are shown uninhabited, and the inhabitants shown separately. Lockhart spent some time in Manaus in Brazil making a film set in the town theatre. Teatro Amazonia is a hour long single shot film showing the auditorium of the theatre seen from the stage. The audience in the theatre watching Lockhart’s film are put in the position of performers on a stage watching the audience in Manaus. The performance of a piece of contemporary classical music begins, sung by an invisible choir on the stage in the position of the viewers. The music begins with the tuning up of the orchestra and then takes the form of a single note of orchestral noise building into a continuously mutating wall of sound, then fading. The audience on the screen watch, listen fidget, get up and go out occasionally. The minute detail of their behaviour becomes interesting. The viewer is able to watch something that would be unwatchable without the suspense created by the voices of the choir. The films titles list the audience participants, every single one of them, in groups dictated by the barrio that they live in. So that the image of the audience can be seen representing the people of the city of Manaus, and in fact they do this proportionally. The numbers of people from each barrio representing the relative population of each barrio. This list of names also reveals the family connections of the people of the city. Lockhart subsequently made a film in a Japanese gymnasium where a group of women are doing physical drill. This is very regimented but balletic in the way that the space seems to be used choreographically. Lockhart films in similar choreographed shots to create a mesmeric document of a strict regime, in which the scrutiny of uniformity yields fascination for minute details of difference ! Bridget Lowe. British artist born in the 1960s who works with text. Lowe made a work in which she took the titles of books and typeset them as a continuous text. She also made a work fro the web in which a text is presented to the viewer at a controlled pace. 

!Colin Lowe and Roddy Thompson.

British artists who work together, both born in the 1960s. Lowe and Thompson have for a number years been conducting tongue in check correspondence with businesses, public agencies and institutions about art and artists. They make outlandish requests, complaints or suggestions but these are made in absolutely conventional ways, most often in the form of letters which themselves constitute the artist’s work. They have also made site specific work including street banners. 

!Sarah Lucas.

Lucas is a British artist born in the 1960s, one of the so called Frieze generation of artists who were working in London in the early 1990s. She was a close friend of Tracy Emin’s at this time and the two artist’s opened a shop together. Lucas’s work shows a preoccupation with sex death and female identity within a framework of humorous abjection. The work with which she became known was a mattress from a double bed placed against a wall, hall of it propped up, half on the floor. Inserted in openings cut in the mattress were two melons at the top on one side and a bucket on the part resting on the floor below them, whilst on the other side a cucumber was inserted at an angle of 45 degrees from the mattress, at the same level as the bucket. This was a crude but very expressive representation of the male and female forms reclining in bed and like a lot of Lucas’s work a crude humour is used in a sophisticated way to represent universal sexual desires by formal but unconventional, and by implication not traditionally male sculptural means. Another work in similar vein A Kebab and Two Fried Eggs consist of an old wooden table with two fried eggs placed on it at one end and a pitta bread containing doner kebab at the other end. Plainly suggestive of female sexual anatomy, this work also represents the female form lying on a table, suggestive of sexual intercourse in simultaneously humorous and slightly threatening ways. Other early works have become iconic, in particular a photographic self portrait, one of a continuing series, of the artist sitting in a chair with feet up on a table at the front of the image nearest the viewer, clad in work boots. This uncompromising image presents Lucas as a female artist in male pose and clothes, a subject to which she returns often in these photographs. She holds a beer can between her knees and pulls the ring tab, for example, and the beer which has been shaken, spurts upwards. She sits on a toilet, her trousers round her ankles. Her work boots which she is wearing in all the photos, are shown on their own. Lucas often has a cigarette hanging from her lips. These and other images portray the artist as a working man, bringing the viewers own preconceptions to their attention. Small sculptures, such as a cast of the artists genitalia in a homage to Duchamp (a major influence), Lucas’s own work boots with razor blades inserted into the sole at the toes also explore the theme of masculine and feminine identity as well as traditional artist’s themes such as the nude body and the self portrait. A collage of newspaper images from the Sun newspaper featuring vastly overweight women shows a distorted image of femininity. In later works Lucas began to model forms more literally as in her series of sculptures which are covered entirely in cigarettes stuck on in a mosaic pattern, such a s motorbike and an enlarged garden gnome. Quite apart from the characteristic inappropriateness of the materials the apparent cost of materials is striking. The display of the materials of addiction and over consumption is in tune with the associations with drink in particular beer in the earlier work. A car is used in a large installation. The car has a mechanism in it that makes it bounce rhythmically, to give the impression that a couple are having sex inside and bouncing on the seat. The Bunny Girl is another later work, this is a representation of a playboy bunny girl made from stuffed pantyhose draped on a chair to suggest arms and legs, and a pair of ears and a fluffy tail. This utterly abject object conveys both the idea of a bunny girl astride a punters lap, or straddling a chair and the idea of a woman as flesh encased in nylon tights or even of a sex toy, a toy woman. 

!Jeff Luke.

British artist born around 1965 died 1996. Luke’s sculptures are made essentially from rubbish and throwaway materials, yet they have a strongly formal quality. His sculptures are often on the floor, usually installed and involving a large number of parts, reminiscent of American scatter art of the late 1970s. A number of rubber band balls some about 60 cm in diameter, are arranged on a low plinth, as if they were a Henry Moore or Barbara Hepworth carved stone sculpture. Instead the abstract forms, pebble like shapes, are the result of hours of obsessive activity. Luke took a mindless doodle like activity and expanded the scale to make a sculpture. Cosmetics and toiletries such as hair shampoo and conditioner, cleaning fluids, and other household liquids are places in tiny 2 cm high sample tubes, which are numbered to refer to a key telling the viewer what they contain. These are then placed on a shelf in a row, creating a formal work from the great range of colours of the liquids. Other works consist of unidentifiable bits of small rubbish (this quality of anonymity, a sense that the object is a part of the unknown larger whole seeming to be the criteria for selection), placed in a careful arrangement on the floor. 

!Ken Lum.

Canadian artist ! Christina Mackie. Scottish artist born in 1960 who makes sculptures and video, which are conceptual, cool and restrained and have a poetic minimalism. Mackie might place a monitor showing a piece of road with traffic sounds next to a railway sleeper, or use a telephone which the viewer can pick up and listen to a sound. Monitors are often placed on or situated in constructions made of old timbers. 

!Goshka Macuga.

Polish artist living and working in London born 1970, Macuga makes environments in which she curates exhibitions. She may create a cave within a gallery from crumpled brown paper, or a snowy mountain scape from fabric, and fill this space with the work of artists that she has invited to participate. In 2003 she built a wooden cabinet with many doors and shelves to display works of art. Her work as an artist is the exhibition environment that she has created. 

!Elisabeth Magill.

British painter born in 1960s who made a series of paintings of luggage seen through an airport security x ray machine. Later work includes paintings of trees. 

!Martin Maloney.

British artist and former curator who pioneered ‘bad painting’ in the late 1990s, making very large scale monumental figurative works often based on other paintings for example Poussin, for which he used friends and acquaintances as models in a highly ambitious kitchen sink meets Alex Katz style. He also painted still lives and animals, as well as a painting cycle of gay club scenes. Maloney’s work is distinguished by the speed at which he works painting wet on wet with large brushes at speed, rarely spending more that 6 hours on a work. The resulting faux clumsiness was highly controversial but Maloney is representative of a generation of artists who began to make work in hitherto scorned media such as textiles, the so called ‘dumb’ art which combined a knowing intellectualism with great enthusiasm and joy in materials and a freedom from the tyranny of conventional artistic skills. Maloney curated several seminal exhibitions in his own home, including Lost in Space and Die Yuppie Scum and his ‘you can do it if you really want to’ attitude to success in the art world, combined with a willingness to combine the classical with the trashy made him very successful in the 1990s. He works chiefly in oils, but also in watercolour, sticky tape and ceramics. 

!Margarita Manzanelli.

An Italian painter born in 1970s who paints young women, often emaciated but fashionably dressed, in the middle of a large canvas with a plain flat background. The women are characterised by their monochrome flat backgrounds, thin bodies and large often bulging eyes. 

!Christian Marclay.

American artist born in 1957, Marclay worked as a musician and DJ before becoming an artist as well. Starting from his interest in music, Marclay makes video, sound and works on paper. A 2003 installation consisted of six screen projection arranged in a horizontal row, composed of film clips. These were chosen from a huge range of films, documentaries, news footage, and chosen for a particular sound (pitch and quality of sound) as well as for the image. Thus a singing Marilyn, marching feet, shouting, piano player, almost any thing is sampled and used. Marclay built the resulting samples into a complex musical score, fifteen minutes long, which is ‘played’ by the film clips. The viewer is engaged in a compelling set of responses to what they hear and see, having to match sound to image and also to process responses to the image, many of which are consciously or subconsciously recognised. The urge to find a narrative is continually denied, and the viewer is forced to recognise the sound as the narrative instead. 

!Maria Marshall.

A British artist born around 1968 who has made a number of video films featuring her own children, including one of her son smoking. 

!Mike Marshall.

A British artist born in 1970s, Marshall has made a number of films of banal everyday scenes, including a film of a water sprinkler in a garden. The viewer watches the green leaves of the hedge and the waxing and waning of the spray droplets as they sparkle in the sun. Marshall brings a kind of everyday sublime to the viewer’s attention, producing moments of abstract beauty and contemplation from unexpected sources. 

!Jason Martin.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes paintings by applying and thick layer of a single colour of oil paint and combing it with a specially designed tool the width of the canvas (up to three metres high) to add a wavy ridged pattern. This catches the light giving rise to an optical effect. 

!Simon Martin.

British artist born in 1968 whose highly finished conceptual painting and sculpture is concerned with the nature of art. He has made a photo realist painting of a bright red tree frog, a painted fibreglass replica of a carved and painted wooden bird in relief, that is based on an original wood carving, and a lacquered fibreglass sculpture of a toy monkey’s head, enlarged to about four feet in diameter and sprayed in off white gloss paint. His work is laborious and labour intensive, the results of his labour producing luxurious objects that have no clear purpose, making the viewer think about them as art objects. He has also made other conceptual work which is opposite in its choice of more ephemeral materials such as cardboard boxes. 

!Karl Maugham.

Painter born 1960s who paints intensely detailed flowerbeds, every flower given individual attention, on large canvases. 

!Paul McCarthy.

American artist who is well known for his performance work, which is always shown as a video documentation, usually displayed as part of an installation containing the props, set and costumes used in the production of the video. The actual one off performance may be done in front of an audience, but they watch it on a monitor in another room, Macarthy always performing in private. Macarthy appropriates folk tales and popular icons, subverting them by reacting the tales transplanted into his own bizarre and usually scatological world. He draws a parallel between everyday activities and the making of art through his parody of fiction. Macarthy himself usually features as main protagonist/artist. He always uses theatrical costumes and sets, often using masks and props. For example a work titled Heidi and the Grandfather is loosely based on the story of Heidi, but in McCarthy’s hands becomes a tale of child abuse and perverse practices involving faecal obsession. In part of the performance Heidi and the grandfather make sausages from fake faeces extracted from Heidi’s prosthetic bottom. Bossy Burger is a horror story involving a chef character that wears a mask and makes burgers. The film makes reference to Sweeny Todd and a lot of tomato ketchup is used (a development from an earlier video in which Macarthy smears himself liberally from head to foot with tomato ketchup). A film of a Pinocchio character is shown on a monitor in a large box construction with peepholes, which are apparently holes for Pinocchio’s nose, and there are Pinocchio costumes complete with noses for the viewer to put on. As ever, in this work Macarthy makes obvious and puerile associations with his chosen subject and lets the various elements collide to disconcerting but humorous effect. He takes the Freudian claim that art is at base no more than playing with ones own excreta absolutely literally. Macarthy notorious for his wittiest creation, De Kooning, also his cruellest and funniest parody of art and the art world, featuring McCarthy visiting a blue chip dealer and also dressed as a tube of paint throwing him self mindlessly against the studio walls, chanting a tuneless ditty ‘De Kooning, De Kooning’. He mocks the art world's pretensions and contrasts them with the still feted work of the supposedly senile de Kooning. This is perhaps the most extreme example of the way Macarthy focuses on breaking taboos. In 2003 McCarthy made an installation for a London gallery which included President Bush of America and the Queen in the interior of a royal palace. He also made a fifty metre high outdoor inflatable black vinyl sculpture of a figure with a Pinnochio nose. MaCarthy’s comic and clownish portrayal of senility, ketchup and fake faeces smeared over his body and surroundings to mimic blood and shit, scenes of child abuse and indecent or murderous practises: all these are grist to McCarthy's comic tragic mill, his interpretation of the darkest side of human nature as a form of slapstick. 

!Lucy McKenzie.

Scottish artist born in the late 1970s, Mackenzie’s practise is extensive, ranging from painting, drawing, printmaking and writing, to video, installation, dj ing, and event organising. She has also used her work to promote the activities of charities, such as one that supports women in Eastern Europe who are trafficked as prostitutes. Mackenzie's polymorphous practise is the result of an ideological and socially engaged approach to art as a medium with the power to transform daily life. At the same time her work references early twentieth century art, particularly that of Russia and Germany both iconographic ally and in the creation of social events. Her paintings and works on paper often borrow apparently anachronistic historical graphics style, and have featuring athletes, Olympic rings, and graphic and geometric patterns. Or they may be graffiti like, borrowing from classical art and include text. They are painted in faded out colours, drawn roughly or printed on, often on cheap paper which in itself is a symbolic act, as cheap paper and printing methods seem entirely anachronistic in western Europe today, where they are still the norm in many parts of the world. In some sense Mackenzie’s work reflects the state of the body politic, in other senses it is a reflection on the practise of art. She started a club night in her native Glasgow that was and remains a focus for artists and musicians. She also ran a club in Warsaw with Pauline Olowskowa, a long term collaborator, at which they presented events including music, poetry and readings. A video made in 2003 shows Mackenzie and Olowskowa working together in an empty space. Mackenzie is drawing Olowskowa who seems to be doing nothing but talking on the phone. Mackenzie also uses text in her large paintings and painted wall sized murals. A recent printed work features a year planner poster for 2004, blank, with all the weekends blocked out in yellow highlighter pen. 

!Steve McQueen.

British artist born 1960s who works in film and Video. McQueen’s films have a wide range of subjects but share basically sculptural concerns. Bear, made for his post graduate show in 1993, shows two men wrestling naked, shot from below in slight slow motion. Another shows a tightrope walker shot from below, so that we focus on the soles of the feet in special shoes, wobbling on the wire. In both these films by choosing an unusual viewpoint McQueen changes our perception of these spectator activities and shows the effort required to carry them out. In 2001 a film shows McQueen standing before a wooden barn, which subsequently falls apart, the roof collapsing and the walls falling outwards. As it falls, it seems that McQueen will be squashed by the front of the barn but in fact he is standing at the exact spot over which the window falls, leaving him standing with the window opening around him, unscathed. In 2003 he made two more ambitious works The Deep, which he filmed in a mine in South Africa, and the Leap, which showed young boys jumping off a cliff. The Deep takes us down to the coalface in the lift and films at eh coal face, using available light and ambient sound. The result is often extremely and hard to see, with no illumination save the miners headlamps and creates the impression of a kind of hell. The film culminates in a sequence shot in the miners' shower and clocking out hall where each mans temperature is taken. As well as seeking new ways to look at things and investigating the visual perceptions associated with sound, McQueen’s work deals with race and the representation of race in art cinema and photography although he does not claim this for his work. The Deep does this explicitly, but Bear and the Falling Barn are subtler. The barn is based on part of a Charlie Chaplin film in which the same thing happens. 

!Jonathan Meese.

German artist born around 1970 Meese made a number of works in which he covered the walls and ceiling of the exhibition space with a vast quantity of found material, photographs, drawings, images from magazines, in fact any visual material. Discernable themes might emerge. The effect was that of a teenage bedroom vastly expanded, with adult subject matter. Sometimes the material might be sent by post to the gallery by the artist, and the gallery left to mount it on the wall as they wished. 

!Dawn Mellor.

British artist born 1960s who paints women. For a long time Mellor’s paintings were very small and displayed in groups, subsequently she began to make bigger works. 

!Tania Merz.

British painter born 1960s ! Alain Miller. British artist born around 1960 who paints highly realistic images (for example a heart or a brain) which from a part of a larger abstract composition. 

!Lisa Milroy.

British artist born around 1960 who makes figurative painting. Milroy’s work is essentially still life but there is an element of process painting in her work. She often paints multiple versions of the same type of object, set out in rows that cover the whole picture plane, for example shoes or blue and white porcelain plates, with the effect of standardising everything she chooses to paint both in terms of the genre of thing, and in terms of the relation of one type to another. So that car tyres might be treated in the same way as jewellery. The objects in Milroy’s paintings are painted in consistent way, with simple flowing brushstrokes on a flat colour ground, in realistic colours. They look very real viewed from a distance, close to the canvas the paint and brushstrokes are what the eye focuses on. 

!Aleksandra Mir.

Polish artist born in the 1960s who live in America. Mir’s work comprises socially engaged installation and events, combined with the construction of large site specific or temporary sculptures. Mir often chooses to respond to a location or a situation. For example she sited a military tank painted pink on a disused lot in London, where local children play. The tank was photographed with women and girls dressed in pink draped over it in an ironic take on the female military mascot. Mir has made a work based on the Mir space station, and most recently has made an almost life size inflatable jumbo jet which can be sited anywhere. 

!Tatsuo Miyajima.

Japanese artist working from the late 1980s onwards who has made a large number of works using LED displays. A series of works use small LED units placed on the floor or on the wall, showing a very high number, which is constantly changing. This number is in fact the date and time displayed in fractions of a second. 

!Sarah Morris.

American artist born in the 1960s who paints process based geometric pattern works based on the facades of modernist buildings. Morris photographs these mostly glass and metal framed buildings whose main feature is windows, and reduces the resulting grid, distorted due to the perspective of the original photograph, to its basic elements in a computer programme. The grid is painted in one colour, often white, and the ‘windows’ in a number of colours, usually very bright, to create a grid pattern with a nod to Mondrian and to Bridget Riley, although it is more complex and colourful. Morris uses high gloss paint very precisely applied. The paintings (and similar screen print panel series) are about 3 m high and 4 m wide at their largest. Morris also makes films showing the kind of architecture and city that inspires her work, for example a film shot in Miami. 

!Paul Morrison.

British artist born in the early 1960s who began his career in the late 1990s painting trees, flowers and vegetation in silhouette. Morison’s paintings are nearly all made in black on white, showing meticulously drawn plants and trees of varying scale made into a composition, which are painted by hand absolutely flat and hard edged in black acrylic paint on white. He has occasionally made negative versions in white on black. Morison’s paintings are usually around 2 metres high, and he has also made round ones. His wall paintings often span 20 metres. 

!Anna Mossman.

British artist born about 1960 who takes photographs in which she represents sensory experiences or thought processes. In a photograph of a rose the rose is out of focus as if the viewer had leaned close enough to smell the roses perfume. A series of photographs show people who are out of focus to the most extreme degree. For these photographs Mossman asked her subjects to stay in a room alone with the camera, which was set to take a very long exposure. The sitters were to tell the camera a secret. The resulting image is blurred due the movement of the sitter during the telling of the secret, the blurring varying from person to person. 

!Ron Mueck.

British sculptor born in the 1950s who after a career in film industry prosthetics began to make sculpture. Dead Dad brought him to public attention, a life size model of apparently of his own dead father, made with such detail that it appeared to be real. Mueck’s work bears comparison with that of Duane Hanson in the 1960s but the difference is that Mueck experiments with scale and his figures are often naked. Mueck makes his sculptures from fibreglass, which is painted, and human hair applied strand by strand. He has also made a baby, a 6 meter high pregnant woman, a school girl (clothed), a post partum woman lying on her back with her child placed on her stomach, the umbilicus emerging from her vagina still attached to the baby and a man in a boat. Mueck’s sculptures are so realistic as to cease to provoke amazement after a while, since there is no doubt of their perfection. Instead Mueck wants the viewer to confront the human state, which each work represents – death, pregnancy, childbirth, and childhood – by presenting something as close to life as possible. 

!Dave Muller.

American artist who makes paintings in water colour on paper, some of which are quite large, for example a panoramic country scene made on several pieces of paper. Muller stages three day weekend exhibitions in his own house and in galleries which he considers to be part of his practise. In 2003 Muller made a series of paintings of the titles and the names of artists printed on the sleeve edges of the record collections of a number of people. 

!Vik Muniz.

Brazilian artist who makes unconventional paintings. Muniz’s paintings are usually made from newspaper or other photographs which he collects obsessively, for example he made a series of works in which he used treacle to copy well known images such as Jackson Pollock making a painting, which try to be the most basic form of illusion in terms of making a recognisable image. 

!Juan Munoz.

Spanish artist born in the 1950s who died in 2001. Munoz, who was Spain’s best known artist of the end of the twentieth century, made sculpture and installation. One of Munoz’s last works was a very large scale installation at Tate Modern in London. Several ‘elevators’ were constructed which ascend and descent through a false floor. Viewers can see the elevators from the basement level and from above, and watch them ascending in space, in a way that they would never usually be seen. The movement of the elevators echoes that of the pistons of a huge slow engine. 

!Muntean and Rosenblum.

Artist partners working in the late 90s and early 2000s in London who make collaborative paintings depicting usually young people in social situations conversing. Their speech is written on the painting. Muntdean and Rosenblum’s style is strongly graphic, with flat colours, and black outlines and white backgrounds are characteristic. 

!Takahashi Murikame.

Takahashi is a Japanese painter who makes highly decorative intricate and stylised floral paintings made in a flat style which make reference to traditional Japanese painted or woven screens, but are also distinctively modern even futuristic. Brightly coloured, meticulously painted and highly controlled, the paintings have a strongly graphic quality, which makes them difficult to contextualise. Murikame is notably for being one of the most commercially successful artists at the turn of the twentieth century and has a large business empire. 

!Sadie Murdoch.

British artist born in mid 1960s who began making large paintings in the early 1990s based on modernist architecture, specifically interiors, which she drew out on a white ground, usually in black and grey, partially painted using very thin paint, and then partially destroyed by pouring thin paint over the whole. In the 2000s Murdoch began to make architectural models from card and paper, which she again partially destroyed. Later models are more abstract and remain intact. Murdoch's interest in the modernist project in art and architecture has led to explore both abstract expressionism and architecture particularly the work of Le Corbusier. 

!David Musgrave.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes installations; Musgrave's work is essentially a sculptural representation of paint and the painted surface. He cuts out various thicknesses of plastic and other sheet materials, transparent and opaque, to look as if they are poured pools of colour and places them on the floor, overlaying them. The cut outs are complex shapes, as if liquid has been splashed or dropped, creating multicoloured floor pieces. Musgrave has also made a number of wall reliefs which are enlarged brushstrokes, modelled and cast and painted in high gloss. The mostly incidental shape and texture of the paint, the blob, the stroke is made into a very deliberate object. 

!Mike Nelson.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes installations that are like mazes. Nelson constructs a series of rooms and corridors within a gallery space, which the viewer enters through a door in the gallery. Inside they are free to wander in the interior space that nelson has created but there is only one way out. The first of these installations created a series of rooms that seemed to have been recently abandoned. Some were like offices in a factory, others empty rooms, all had a feeling of recent presence but also of possible sinister events. There are clues in the form of items pinned to the wall, furniture, but very few objects. The environment seems timeless and the viewer has the disorientating experience of not being able to tell what period, country or type of building they are in. the spaces are extremely real. At a central point in the maze it seems that the viewer has reached the point at which they started but this is in fact a replica of that room and the determined participant carries on to discover several more rooms. There are hints of the psychological pressures that are sometimes brought to bear on prisoners. Another example is a maze Nelson created specifically a museum in London, in which the viewer enters what seems to be a series of back rooms at the museum, storage spaces for works of art and wrapping and packing areas. It is very difficult to believe that this is an installation that has been constructed in the gallery and that the viewer has not entered behind the scenes by mistake, but for the peculiar absence of life and activity. 

!Shirin Neshat.

Iranian artist who has made several large screen projected video installations featuring women wearing the veil and music. 

!Mariele Neudecker.

Artists born in the 1960s who makes installations in which she creates model landscapes such as a sea, or a mountain range. 

!Jacques Nimski.

British artist who makes intentsley detailed pencil drawings of plants that entirely fill the paper. 

!Noble and Webster.

British artist partners born in the late 1960s who make sculpture and installation. Noble and Webster’s work is an unashamed unapologetic ode to money, creation, fame and ego combined with traditional forms, crafts and materials. Their illuminated signs, which lean against the gallery wall or are mounted on it, are an example of this. These may take the form of a dollar sign entirely made of white light bulbs, or of tattoo designs such as a heart stabbed by a dagger with a word on a ribbon, Las Vegas style. Noble and Webster made an installation fro which they made life size models of themselves as apes, cast in fibreglass and walking on a pristine white undulating gallery floor. Another consists of an enormous pile of rubbish, with a strong light projected at it, casting a shadow on the wall behind to reveal the profiles of the artists, created from careful arrangement of the rubbish but only visible as a shadow. 

!Paul Noble.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes pencil drawings, video and animation. Noble’s earliest work consisted of book jackets made for existing books, which altered or repositioned the book in some way, usually through humour or puns, echoing the British playwright, Joe Orton’s illicit alteration of library books. The main body of Noble’s work to date depicts his creation of a fictitious town called Nobson New Town. The architecture of Nobson is constructed on the blueprints of a typographical font created by Noble whose capital letters are three dimensional blocks, enabling Nobson to draw arrangements of building that spell out words. These blocks have the appearance of modernistic concrete structures and the letters are hard to read but decipherable. The Nobson project is an indictment of the way of life in the west, a Swiftian satire describing an urban and rural dystopian with utopian elements. The name Nobson is descriptive of the phallic forms of some of the buildings, and the phallic form is put to use everywhere from tree stumps to drawbridges. There are no people in the drawings. Noble’s drawings have been made since 1997 and show every aspects of life in Nobson from the dwellings, the park, the cemetery, the factories, the sewage works, the school, the hospital, in fact every amenity and faculty a small town would have. There is also a large house for the artist, which has a number of rooms and a tower. The drawings are made in pencil and are about 3 metres high, extremely detailed and finely drawn so that the whole drawing can take a long while to peruse. Any words spelled out by the buildings are readable from a distance only. Noble also makes small drawings showing details from Nobson. The words in the drawings usually spell out a political or ideological statement or quotation that deals in some way with the life of ordinary working people. Noble has also represented Nobson as a photomural, for which he made a tent in the shape of a letter from the Nobson typeface and pitched in a forest. Woodland animals are added to the photograph digitally as if this were a rural idyll. He has made a digitally animated drawing too, which shows the factories of Nobson in a static single shot view, with very little happening save for smoke plumes emitting from chimneys and a train travelling across periodically. 

!Nils Norman.

British artist, who makes installations, works on paper, models and written texts. Norman’s work presents ecological and sustainable models for human existence, in particular urban existence. Norman draws on 1960s and 70s models for resistance, tailoring them to the new century and a new era of globalisation and anti capitalism. In particular he focuses on the modernist myth of creating quality of life for every one through good practise in design and the built environment. His investigations of how to put simple ideas for more socially responsible living into practise take various forms. He has made a number of architectural models that demonstrate for example acts of urban resistance or reclamation such as the conversion of the flat roof of an apartment building in New York into a vegetable plot to enable the residents to be self sufficient. He has also designed and built a sustainable bus called Geocruiser (2001). The bus runs on converted waste fat from the catering industry and has solar panels enabling it to actually return energy to the national grid. It collects rainwater and part of it is a green house with a composter enabling the bus to be used to grow food efficiently. The bus also houses a library of books and texts on ecology and self sufficiency and a computer terminal with internet access, a painter and a photocopier available for public use wherever the bus stops. Visitors can photocopy any texts they wish to take away. Geocruiser visited several cities in the UK and in Europe. 

!Tina O’Connell.

Irish artist born in the early 1960s who makes large scale architectural installations often using concrete, and usually site specific. 

!Chris Ofili.

British artist born in the late 1960s who became known and initially notorious for his pattern paintings and paintings of black women and men with elephant dung attached in the mid 1990s. Ofili's figurative paintings are complex in their execution. A psychedelic pattern is painted first, then a figure added, picked out and filled in exclusively with concentric lines of tiny dots of paint in many colours which follow the contours of the figure. His earlier works have cut outs from pornographic magazines collaged on to the surface. The whole painting is then covered with a layer of poured plastic resin, adding a physical depth to the surface. Elephant dung soaked in resin is attached tot eh canvas, sometimes with words and names picked out in coloured map pins on them. The paintings rest against the wall on two lumps of dung. Ofili mostly made paintings of women in this series which he described as goddesses, highly stylised head and shoulders portraits of woman wearing jewellery, including a black Madonna which created a furore amongst the American Christian right for it juxtaposition of the virgin Mary with elephant dung. He also made a number of full length portraits of male characters including one called Captain Shit, a black superhero. Ofili continued to paint women and to make a large number of watercolours on paper, drawings and prints, until the early 00s. He then made a series of paintings depicting a man and a woman, as lovers, but also as iconographic symbols of Africa and its people. Ofili's continued to use the dung to represent his connection with his mother country (he had begun to use it after his first trip to see where his family had come from). The lovers paintings are made in the same way, but there are fewer collaged elements. Flowers and leaves are added to the backgrounds and the paintings are more reflective. In 2002 he made a series of 13 works featuring a monkey, painted in the same way. These are installed in an especially constructed timber lined room resembling a darkened chapel. Illuminated so that they have an icon like quality each paintings is based on a different colour and titled Mono Azul, Mono Or for example, meaning Blue Monkey Gold Monkey. The twelve monkeys, six on each side of the room, depict the apostles, and the gold monkey at the end of the room is Christ. This work is typically both irreverent and reverent. All of Ofili’s work is characterised by a sense of the profound combined with a highly decorative surface and a dry humour. He plunders popular culture and mixes influence in the same way that hip hop music does. Hip hop is often cited as an importance influence on Ofili’s work. 

!Saskia Olde Wolbers.

Dutch artist born in around 1970 who makes videos based on models that she constructs in her studio which have a spoken narrative voiceover. Olde Wolbers takes a true story, often from a newspaper; a tale of someone’s life or something that happened to someone, and elaborates it, writing a first person narrative which Olde Wolbers chooses a person or an actor to read it. She imagines a scene in which this tale might unfold and constructs a three dimensional model, which she then films. The models are made from a variety of materials – one model of an underground car park also resembles the frosted interior of a fridge but in fact is made from textured plastic sheeting and plastic bottles and cups. Another of a hospital ward is made from a pet cage dipped in household gloss paint and submerged in a tank, filmed underwater. The paint drips but stays in place, creating an eerie white scene. Olde Wolbers films have an eerie often futuristic quality, which is at odds, both with the prosaic materials of their construction and with the true story narrative of the voiceover. The narrative becomes extraordinary both in the writing and in the reading, Watching, the viewer has to grapple with interpreting what they see, which seems very real yet palpably is not and relating it to what they hear. 

!Pauline Olowskowa.

Polish artist who makes drawings and collages, sometimes working collaboratively with Lucy Mackenzie. 

!Hans Op de Bek.

Belgian artist who makes photographs. 

!Julian Opie.

British artist born around 1960 who makes works in a range of media. Pie’s early work consisted of painted cut outs made in folded and bent sheet metal. They stand on the floor and represent objects such as a pile books, telephones, even a painting. The objects are painted in a cartoon style with black outlines. They are Opie’s way of making painting into sculpture. This work demonstrated an interest in generic representation, which has sustained throughout his career. In the late 1980s and early 1990s Opie made a large number of sculptures fabricated from industrial materials. These fall into two groups – case, chest or freezer like constructions and glass screen constructions. The case or chest works are made from sheet metal with aluminium fittings – for example edging, feet and ventilation grilles. They are ambiguous since they are evidently non functional, with no discernible means of opening them, yet they refer visually only to functionality. In them Opie refers both to modernist design and to minimal sculpture in particular referring to the work of Donald Judd. The glass screen works are arrangements of toughened glass in aluminium frames to resemble doors and glass walls that might be seen at an airport or other public space. The glass often has discs etched on it in a row at eye level. These arrangements engage the viewer in a familiar experience of the negotiation of glass barriers, whilst considering the formal arrangement as a sculpture. Opie made a number of works taken from the symbols and pictograms used in signage, developing an idea of the point at which the individual becomes generic and vice versa. Opie has made many paintings and prints, usually screen prints. The road paintings, Imagine You Are Driving, (around sixty cm high) were made after he drove across America, and show roads reduced to the minimum of detail – a black road, with white lines and green verges with blue sky, each rendered in absolutely flat acrylic paint with hard edges. These paintings conflate the experience of driving with the experience of the computer game and make the case for the roadscape being the contemporary landscape painting. A series of night paintings are made in black with blurry circles of yellow, white and red light indicating road lamps and car headlights and rear lights. Opie’s Computer game paintings (similar sized and also two metre high versions made) are a development of the road paintings, representing the same experience of driving but this time through a version of a computer generated urban environment smoothed out so that there is no detail at all. Opie took this approach into three dimensions with his model village sculptures, immaculately made and spray painted buildings with almost no detailing so that they become generic In the late 1990s continuing to work the idea of the generic Opie made a series of digital drawings of people he knew, removing all the detail from a head and shoulders photograph to produce a generic image in black on a brightly coloured background that actually retained a great deal of individuality. These images were used on an album cover and became known to a much wider public. He also began to make digital animations, taking the same principle and applying it to the moving image. He filmed a person moving and reduced this image to the bare minimum, resulting in a moving pictogram, which still retained some of the characteristics of the individual on which it was based. 

!Gabriel Orozco.

Brazilian sculptor who made a work called Ping Pond Table, consisting of two ping pong tables cut in half where the net would be and arranged in a cross shape with a fishpond in the vacant square area in the middle.
