Four players can play simultaneously, the pond replacing the net as the barrier to be crossed by the ball. 

!Pepon Osorio.

South American artist born in the 1960s died in the 1990s. Osorio made a work in which he cycled around a platform built at the top of a very large tree encircling the trunk. 

!Silke Otto Knapp.

German artist working in Britain born in the late 1960s who makes paintings using watercolour on canvas. Otto Knap’s earliest work in the 1990s was a series of female portraits taken from fashion magazines followed by a series of American landscapes sometimes in panoramic format often taken from Marlboro advertisements and form her own photographs. These became more impressionistic until she began to paint in watercolour on a white ground, making a number of groups of landscapes between 40 and 80 cm high. The watery paint is applied in a gestural manner pooling on the surface creating an often illusory image. A Los Angeles series shows the city by day and by night, as does a group of Las Vegas paintings. Otto Knap then made a number of ‘vegetation’ paintings depicting the greenness of parks, gardens and glasshouses such as Kew with enough detail hinted at for the viewer to interpret the image as a landscape, as well as some yellow desert paintings. More recently Otto Knap has made a number of paintings of show girls, again in watercolour, the colours predominantly red. 

!Laura Owens.

American painter born in the 1970s who makes large semi abstract works using bare canvas as a ground to which she adds blobs of colour and small painted areas. Owens paintings often include flowers and patterns and hint at landscape and nature. 

!Jorge Pardo.

Artist whose designs interiors, furniture and lighting with a distinct reference to modernist design for specific sites. Pardo became known in the 1990s for his displays of lamps and lampshades. In 1999 Pardo gained a commission for a major site specific work enabling him to build a house for himself in California, which he has declared an artwork and opened to the public. The house is a minimal modernist open plan building, filled with furniture and objects designed to Pardo’s specification. 

!Philippe Parreno.

Artist who commissioned the conception and design of and bought the rights to a Japanese Manga cartoon character named Annlee. Parreno then lent Annlee to a number of other artists (including Liam Gillick and Angela Bulloch) inviting them to make work with her. Some artists used her in their own digital animations. 

!Janette Parris.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes drawings, digital animations, writes produces and directs musicals and sings in a band. Her performing activities are part of her practise as an artist. Parris made a series of semi autobiographical cartoons featuring a character called Plank, and also a number of video installations featuring a rag doll called Janette. 

!Richard Patterson.

British painter born 1965, painted hyper realist figurative works, notably a very large landscape format landscape with a massively enlarged plastic toy minotaur figure perched on a motorbike handlebar, the whole painting given a slight blur by the softening of the paint but parts in sharp focus. The effect is one of double take. 

!Toby Patterson.

Scottish artist who makes plan and design based work, and has designed skateboards. Patterson is also a curator. 

!Daniel Perfect.

British artist who makes small decorative paintings and works on paper, landscape like patterns that are reminiscent of the 1950s. 

!Simon Perriton.

British artist born 1962 who makes paper cut outs on a large scale. These are often but not always like doilies made by repeated folding and cutting, and although at first glance they appear as lacy patterns, they are in fact thematic, for example a mushroom cloud in circular symmetry with CND symbol, barbed wire, mixed in with floral patterning to create complex intricate and skilled interwoven patters of great delicacy and fragility. The cut outs may be single or multicoloured and are made by sticking paper together to the required size and then cutting out the design with a scalpel. Perriton sometimes layers versions or the same cut out one colour on top of another. The cut outs are always displayed unframed and unmounted and hang from pins in the wall. 

!Grayson Perry.

British artist born around 1960 who makes coiled ceramic pots and vases richly decorated with drawn images, collaged photographic and printed elements, huge variety of glazes and gold and silver leaf. The pots seem like precious decorative objects but on closer inspection are revealed to bear highly politicised messages and images about issues that concern the artist for example child abuse, torture and a variety of social ills. This juxtaposition of luxury objet d’art with hard hitting message makes the vases transcend their genre. The text and images on the vases describe the grimmest of childhoods and allude openly to incest, paedophilia and child abuse. As an artist, Perry often appears in public as a transvestite called Claire who has a variety of dresses, including a Victorian costume and a dress embroidered with machine guns. She often carries a porcelain doll, Clara, dressed in a matching costume. Perry has also made photographic works of herself as Claire, and embroideries. 

!Raymond Pettibon.

American artist who makes pen and ink drawings. Pettibon's main inspirations are William Blake, surfing, film and cartoons. His drawings often use the cartoon format of a black outlined box, with text at the bottom hand written in capitals, as well as thought bubbles. There is rarely a drawing without text. The transient medium and sometime jokey style he uses and the subject he chooses to explore belies the seriousness of Pettibon’s intent. He often alludes to the larger issues of life, death and the sublime. Surfing is Pettibon’s passion and the surfer in his work symbolise the insignificance of human kind in the face of nature. The surfer might for example be shown at to top of a huge wave, and have a thought bubble with an existential thought written in it. Another drawing might feature a gun, or a light bulb with a caption. Some of the captions read like lines from a forties film noir. Pettibon’s drawings are either around 25 or 60 cm high, made on white paper, usually exhibited pinned to the wall unframed. 

!Elizabeth Peyton.

Artist born in the 1960s who paints portraits of her friends and of famous people. These are often pop stars, and have included the British royal family. Peyton’s work in the 1990s often captured the zeitgeist, yet her work also refers to the history of portraiture. The paintings are all done from photographs. Peyton always paints on board, quite small (around 50 cm high maximum) in portrait format, using translucent colour rapidly and simply applied. The white paint used for the ground and the coloured paint often drips over the edge of the board. 

!Daniel Pflum.

German artist who makes sculptures that appear to be corporate logos and signs. 

!Vong Phaophanit.

Vietnamese artist who made an installation for which he covered the floor of a gallery with rice which concealed a red fluorescent tube running the length of the gallery which sent red glow through the rice. 

!Sarah Pickstone.

British painter born in the early 1960s. 

!Jack Pierson.

American artist who makes photographs, paintings, sculptures and installation. Pierson’s work is diverse – his photographs are of everyday scenes given a fairy tale twist. His paintings are really photographs printed on to the canvas using a three colour printing process that transfers pigment in discrete dots of colour. His installations are various, examples are a section of wall and floor painted yellow with a cabinet with a record player on it, some LPS which the viewer is invited to play and a chair, and a section of the wall and floor painted purple with a made up single bed placed on the floor well away from the wall on which the viewer is invited to lie. Pierson’s sculpture in the main consists of phrases, sayings, and truisms spelled out with found signage lettering on the wall. The lettering is all in different styles materials and sizes, some old, some new, some Perspex, some moulded plastic, and some painted or gilded wood, some cast or cut metal. 

!Hadrian Piggot.

British artist best known for a body of work he made in the 1990s. Piggot crafted porcelain sculptures that took the form of bathroom sinks, for example, their forms changed to smooth rounded abstract shapes, convex where they should be concave, entirely without function yet with the addition of chrome taps and other fittings they became surreal objects. A plughole might be placed on a bulging shape where no water could ever collect. Piggot had leather cases made with satin lined moulded inserts to hold the sculptures, adding to their surrealism but inferring a useful portability. He also made some carvings from bars of soap. Piggot late began to make photographs of landscape. 

!Keith Piper.

British artist. 

!Michelangelo Pistoletto.

Italian sculptor working from the 1980s onwards who makes installations usually featuring mirrors made of steel. The mirrors are deployed as a literal way of making the viewer part of his work. Sometimes the mirrors have something printed on them. He also made an installation consisting of a mountainous pile of second hand clothes which viewers were invited to help themselves to. 

!Lari Pittman.

American painter who became extremely popular in the 1990s for his intensely intricate and complex abstract paintings. Pittman draws on a multiplicity of sources, borrowing with impunity and putting together intense compositions that owe something variously to fabric design, the 1950s, architectural drawing, pop art, cartoons, and post modern painting. It is impossible to locate the source of the elements of a Pittman painting because there are just so many elements and they are so perfectly graphically intertwined. But what makes Pittman’s work particularly distinctive is the method he uses to make them. The main design is outlined and painted in flat colour. This in itself produces a complex very brightly coloured painting. Then dots of paint, squeezed onto the canvas (the dots no more than 2 mm across) are used to outline some areas, fill in others. The dots are three dimensional and it becomes hard to believe that the painting could have been made by hand, although it clearly has. Pittman’s paintings function as a bold design with an overall structure as well as a surface of patterns within patterns that seems to have more in common with embroidery than painting. 

!Sigmar Polke.

A German artist who began working in the 1960s. Polke is famous for painting on tea towels (also on bed sheets, table cloths and curtains) In fact he tends to eschew canvas as ground altogether, sometimes working on resin panels as well as a huge variety of woven and printed textiles. Much of his work has photographic screen printing as well as hand painting. The screen print is usually a fragment of a vastly enlarged image from a newspaper, the dots that make up the image as big as a coin, printed in black. Polke’s hand painted is usually black outlines of figures and objects. Polke’s paintings depict such subjects as war, classical myth, weaponry, and rural idyll and are notoriously resistant to interpretation. Many of the images are taken from newspapers as if to address the proliferation of imagery and the difficulty of assimilating it. The fabric and pattern of the background is carefully chosen. Sometimes two or more contrasting panels are stitched together. Polke is a painter who is concerned with surface and image, what is real and not real, how an image is read, in what order and how narrative reading can be imposed and obstructed. In the late 90s and early 00s, Polke pursued his interest in enlarging printed images by making use of digital images and large format printing to produce 20m wide enlarged newspaper images, printed on vinyl coated fabric. 

!Rona Pondick.

British artist who makes sculpture by reupholstering and altering furniture. A chair might have legs like an animal for example. A sofa might have its shape altered to allude to the female form. Pondick’s work is influenced by surrealism in the way she transforms an everyday object. 

!Larry Poons.

American artist working from the 1980s onwards, Poons’ meticulous cartoon like paintings usually depict landscape with text on panels within the composition. For example a gravestone or the pages of an open book might carry the text, which is painted as if it were a printed typeface. Poons paints in a precise way using flat shiny colours and hard edges. 

!Kathy Prendergast.

British artist who has made a series of abstracted map or plan drawings of cities. These are extremely detailed pencil drawings about sixty cm high, showing the basic street plan and zones of a city, but without using any conventional cartographic. They have the appearance of cobwebs, or organic forms. 

!Monique Prieto.

American artist who paints large flat coloured abstracts which look at first as if they are accidental ‘pours’ of colour but which in fact are drawn on a computer first. Prieto says that she evolved this method of working after the birth of her children to speed up the production process, enable her to do some preparatory work at home and make the most of her time. The paintings are cool, calm and reflective. 

!Lisa Prior.

American artist who makes video works featuring her alter ego who is an expert in any chosen field. Prior’s films borrow the style of corporate or promotional video, and she appears as a psychiatrist fro example, telling the viewer about a certain psychiatric condition. Or about the mental health of Vietnam veterans. Or about her own dating experiences. Prior’s persona is compelling but ultimately fallible and not without humour. More recently Prior has made a work which has more formal concerns. She filmed a dog shaking itself, then looped this short section and slowed it down. The sound of the bell on the dogs collar is transformed and the dogs shaking appears graceful and deliberate. 

!James Pyman.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes pencil drawings on paper. Pitman’s drawings are often large, up to one and a half meters high, and depict a variety of subjects ranging from groups of people to landscapes or even just a view of waves on water. They are drawn in a soft but precise way, the pencil smudged to create soft shaded areas. 

!Mark Quinn.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculpture. Quinn’s best known work is Blood Head, a cast of his own head made from several pints of his frozen blood. This is displayed in a refrigerated vitrine on a plinth. Quinn’s work is mostly concerned with the human body, life and death. A companion work to Blood Head is Bread Head, a bust modelled from bread dough and baked. Quinn pursued his interest in preservation through freezing in other works. He used a species of frog, which hibernates, at sub zero temperatures in suspended animation, displaying the frog in a block of ice in a refrigerated vitrine. He also made a larger work using a mass of frozen flowers. Quinn also cast his own body and used it to produce a thin latex skin, which he hung form a hook in the ceiling, as if it were his own flayed skin. More recently Quinn has been pursuing his work with flowers and making photographs. 

!Fiona Rae.

British artist born around 1960 who makes abstract paintings and works on paper. Rae uses the brush stroke and the mark as her subject, and in her paintings from the late 1980s to the 00s she has consistently explored the possibilities of the gesture as a formal element of composition. Rae uses a huge range of colours and type of mark, from rough wide brushstrokes to thin solid lines. These do not follow any figurative logic but are discrete elements that make up the whole. The paintings are coloured grounds with decorative marks arranged densely over them, the marks refer Rae’s work invokes post modern ideas of mimesis and quotation, since she is not just making a mark but reproducing generic mark making, as well as borrowing from the history of painting for her sources. The paintings are usually about 2 metres high. In the 2000s, Rae has introduced photo collage of found images and uses digital technology to reproduce and rescale painted areas that interest her. 

!Michael Raedecker.

Dutch artist born in 1961 who makes figurative paintings that incorporate thread. Raedecker, a former fashion student, uses thread as a drawing medium, to describe outlines, textures, surfaces and light. He first paints the background, very often using thick and even pooled paint, and then hangs the canvas so that he can sew through it. Sometimes the thread is pushed through wet paint, or paint applied over it so that it becomes impregnated. In the late 1990s Raedecker’s first series of thread paintings featured the interiors of hotel rooms in grey, ochre, pale pink and beige. A lampshade might be filled in with satin stitch, a bedcover with a textured thread, and a rug with tufts of wool. There is also a painting of an empty cinema auditorium. However thread is used more unconventionally to represent light and shade. Another group of works made at the same time show odd figures, often wielding guitars, their hair done in thread. These figures seem as if they might be the inhabitants fop the hotel rooms. They also refer obliquely to the rock group Kiss, who wear costumes and face paint. The rooms are small paintings but the following group of work depicting modernist suburban houses are about 2 metres high. In these, a picture window at night might be filled in with lengths of thread across its width to simulate reflective light. Small knotted threads indicate shrubs and trees. Like the earlier works these are based on found photographs. Raedecker’s use of thread to depict plants, shrubs and trees led him to make a group of landscape paintings, often very large. These include a number of scenes of barren desert as if from a western, with stunted trees and parched earth. The thread detail is used to describe plants and also shadows. At this time Raedecker also made a number of more abstract works based on the shapes of male and female genitals, thread representing pubic hair, as well as a series of portraits of old men. The occasional appearance of a bird in the landscapes is transferred into a number of still lives made in the early 00s. These use thread more literally. Areas of stitching and tufting vie for attention with increasing use of very thick paint, applied horizontally and left to dry. These often include a duck or a swan like bird. 

!Neo Rauch.

German artist who makes paintings that have both figurative and abstract elements. The figures and buildings in Rauch’s are painted in a socialist realist style, but the are part of a larger, geometric abstract composition of coloured lines and shapes. The paintings are up to three metres high. 

!Tobias Rehberger.

German artist who makes site specific sculptures and models for these which are based on garden design and horticulture. Rehberger might for example make a modular planting unit in which he plants the crops grown in the area, for example maize, artichokes and sunflowers, and place this in a university concourse. Or he may reconstruct municipal flowerbeds and through the planting and shape of the beds echo a municipal building. 

!Steve Reinke.

Canadian artist who make video work. Reinke’s work 100 Short Videos, made in the mid 1990s, is just that – 100 short films made or found by Reinke, which include film of himself as a child, film of a group of children making a news broadcast at the Canadian Broadcasting Corporation and film of gay men meeting for sex in a forest. 

!Nick Relph and Oliver Payne.

British artists born in the late 1970s who make videos about urban culture, for example one about public toilets in London which combines music with a written narrative and poetry edited with images from footage they shot in public conveniences. 

!Jason Rhoades.

American artist. 

!Carol Rhodes.

British artist who makes small paintings of aerial landscapes, usually fairly ordinary areas such as airport terminals. 

!Pipilotti Rist.

Swiss artist who makes video installations. Rist, who used to be a musician, collages together still images, video footage and music, which she composes herself to make dream like sequences. She uses digital effects to create saturated colour, colour changes, dissolves, blurring, mirroring, and other optical effects. Rist’s videos sometimes feature herself. The films are non narrative and are looped. Rist employs a variety of means of presentation; double projections that meet in a corner for example. In one exhibition she provided a 2 metre high 4 metre long sofa for viewer to clamber on to and watch a video wearing headphones. 

!Matthew Ritchie.

American artist who makes work based on charts, graphs and plans. Ritchie makes large scale drawings, up to 5 metres long. An example of which is his diagram using circles around the names of individuals, corporations and institutions which are connected by arrows, showing the extent to which the US government and the family President Bush is linked to the oil business !Ugo Rondinone. Italian artist who makes circular target paintings with concentric circles in blurred colours that create an optical shimmer. 

!Eva Rothschild.

British artist born in the 1970s who makes sculpture and wall based work. Rothschild has made a large number of works by weaving together two contrasting wall posters, cutting them in to strips and reassembling them, turning two images into an abstracted third. She often leaves part of the image as unwoven strips which hang down the wall. Rothschild’s sculptures are minimal abstract objects which have the quality of small monuments. She made a work by placing hundreds of burning incense sticks into a rubber car tyre, juxtaposing the spiritual contemplation associated with the incense sticks with a reference to the burning necklace (a car tyre filled with petrol) placed round the necks of persecuted Buddhist monks in the far east. 

!Thomas Ruff.

German artist born in the 1960s who makes photographic portraits. These are head and shoulders images printed much larger than life size, the subject looking directly at the camera, expressionless. Every detail of skin and hair is in sharp focus. 

!Karin Ruggaber.

Brigit artist born in the 1960s who makes sculpture. Ruggaber takes elements of furniture and architectural fixtures and fittings and transforms them into minimal sculpture, for example a hand rail upholstered in leather effect vinyl. 

!Neil Rumming.

British artist born in the 1970s who makes large paintings up to 3 meters high and works on paper. Rumming juxtaposes imagery such as cross sections from medical books, tattoos, graphic design for rock band album covers, or graphic images of flames or flowers, for example, to make a complex abstract but decorative composition painted in hard edged bright acrylic colours. 

!John Russell.

British artist, formerly a member of Bank, who makes large scale representational paintings which are very messy and badly painted. Russell also makes performances. Art, artists and the art world are the subject of his often polemical work. Russell sometimes collaborates with Fabienne Audeoud. 

!Johannes Rust.

Austrian artist who made a film with Jonathan Faier !Anne Ryan. Irish artist born in the 1960s who makes cowboy paintings, each one based on a still from a Hollywood western. Ryan uses a palette of green grey and brown to depict men and horses in landscapes, or men in saloons. 

!Giorgio Sadotti.

British artist born in the 1960s who has made work in a variety of media and staged events. He arranged a dinner party for selected artists, dealers and curators at which the guests conversations were recorded. For a work entitled Be Me he sat in a gallery all day every day and invited anyone to come and take his place. 

!Jenny Saville.

British painter born in the late 1960s who makes monumental paintings, at least four metres high, of nude women and photographs. Saville’s subjects are often very large women and the subject of her work is really flesh. A painting will consist largely of expanses of painted flesh, onto which in the early years she inscribed text as if it were cut into the flesh. In the 1990s Saville made paintings of women lying on a sheet of glass so that their flesh squashed and distorted. 

!Kathy Schimert.

American artist born in the 1960s who makes installation, video and ceramic sculpture. Schimert made a film of a boy under water with wings on who represents drowned Icarus. 

!Andreas Schlaegel.

German artist born in the 1960s who make installation and sculpture. Schlaegel made a nude portrait from French fries, a sofa from washing up sponges and an arm chair from toilet rolls. He became an Elvis impersonator which he considered part of his practise as an artist. 

!Adrian Scrivener.

British artist born in the 1960s. 

!George Shaw.

British painter who makes small landscape paintings using shiny enamel paint which depict housing estates, garages, car parks and streets in suburbia. Shaw paints in a detailed and realistic way. 

!Jim Shaw.

American artist born in the 1950s who is best known for his collection known at the Thrift Store Paintings, an expanding collection of found art from which he select for exhibition, and the Dreams drawings, daily hand drawn records of his dream life, in the form of cartoon strips with text. The dreams very often feature art, artists, museums and dealers !Elsa Sighicelli. American artist born in the 1960s. 

!Roman Signer.

A German live artist who describes his works as sculptures, Signer is best known for works from the 1980s onwards. Signer’s materials are chiefly his own body, furniture, household and DIY objects and materials and explosives and his performances and actions are documented as short films and photographic sequences. They nearly always take place in a field or other natural environment. For example Signer stands on three boxes stacked one on the other in the middle of a field. Each box is rigged to explode in sequence. Signer remains standing as each one collapses, bringing him eventually to the ground. Something is placed in a stream, and the action of the running water eventually causes an explosive reaction. In both cases the resulting puffs of smoke is visually important to signer. He also used a bright orange ball in a number of works. Signer uses the formal language of sculpture, the body and the plinth, the landscape, and subverts it by adding the destructive power of explosives in a highly controlled way. The works seem slapstick and humorous but in fact they are precisely organised, even the puffs of smoke are carefully planned. Signer has also made some site specific firework displays. 

!DJ Simpson.

British artist born in the 1970s who make very large (at least 4 by 5 metre) machine carved panels. These are made as if they are abstract paintings. Simpson uses an electric router to gouge out channels to create lines, which loop and curve over the surface, as well as other tools that can inscribe the surface that he works on. He works on prepared and on found surfaces, such as laminate table tops, as well as building materials such as ply and silver insulation board, in fact any sheet material that is composed of layers so that the lines Simpson cuts out reveal the contrasting colours of the layer beneath ! Jane Simpson. British artist born in the 1960s who is best known for her ice sculptures, in which she uses refrigeration and ice as a construction tool and material. One of the earliest made in the early 1990s consists of two halves of a heart made from metal and brass sheet, the separated surfaces refrigerated and water added so that ice forms, sticking the heart back together. Another work from the same period is a small refrigerated metal shelf on which is placed a block of butter. These works are conditional – they only remain in their intended state as long as the refrigeration process is taking place. From the beginning of her career Simpson has also had an interest in vessels and containers, starting with a collection of Tupperware, which she displayed in vitrines, and photographed, the photographs enabling the plastic mass manufactured containers to transcend their reality and appear far more precious. In 2000 Simpson began a series of works in which she modelled vessels depicted in paintings, for example those in Morandi paintings, casting these in rubber, and later making them from scratch in glazed porcelain. These vessels are exhibited in groups on shelves and plinths. This interest in functional mass produced containers led Simpson to begin to cast similar containers in rubber, and then to cast furniture, leading to a series of floppy furniture works. Simpson cast stools, chairs, tables and sideboards in rubber, creating objects that absolutely refuse their function and flop in a heap on the floor almost seeming to be more like curled up or collapsed four legged animals. There is an abject side to Simpson’s work, which is contrasted with the grandeur of ambition in some of the ice sculptures. An antique sideboard is cast in plaster and its top refrigerated, inviting contemplation of the function of such a piece of furniture, and highlighting the appearance of a layer of ice as a decorative surface. A structure somewhat like a cross between double sided train seat, a fountain but chiefly resembling a urinal, has steel sides curving down into a trough. These are refrigerated and the water flow is continuously frozen. These transitional works marking the beginning of a series of works in which Simpson refrigerated already existing objects, often in situ, in order to explore the qualities of ice as a decorative surface. These works include a refrigerated chandelier and ornate cast iron gates. 

!Daniel Sinsel.

German artist who paints watercolours depicting young men and boys often naked or engaged in sex acts. The figures are often shown in rural landscapes. There is an idyllic atmosphere yet the figures in the paintings are clearly contemporary. Sense’s medium is traditionally associated with art of the past, with amateur art or with graphic art and illustration gives the images an ironically innocent quality. 

!Smith and Stewart.

Artist partners, born around 1970, who made a number of performative video works before turning to installation. Kiss is a video in which one partner covers the other with red lipstick kisses until there is no skin left untouched. The gesture of covering someone with kisses becomes sinister as the flesh begins to take on the appearance of having been beaten, bruised or flayed. In another work one partner is submerged in a bath, and the other periodically place their face into the water and breathes air into the submerged partners mouth enabling them to stay submerged. These simple acts of partnership, intimacy and trust have violence and control as their counterpoint. Smith and Stewart moved on to investigate ideas about control and implicit violence, for example in an installation made in 2003. This consisted of a 29 metre long 4 meter high breezeblock wall constructed diagonally across the gallery, not meeting the wall at either end. On one side of the wall the gallery was empty and dark. On the other side the space was also dark but a chair, various amplifiers speakers and cables could be seen. Projected onto the brick wall is a film of a hand covered in a black round shape, its surface bound with layers of shiny black plastic tape, knocking against a wooden board leaning against the wall. The film is made on the actual wall at the actual place where it is projected, leading to a double take in the viewers mind about what is real (the board is not really there). The lights turn on and off apparently in a random fashion. They are pendant light bulbs. The scene becomes sinister, the knocking like the sound of some possible torture or unspecified but unpleasant event. 

!Bob and Roberta Smith.

The pseudonym of British artist Patrick Brill, born in around 1960. Roberta is in fact the name of his sister, but he does not work with her. Brill makes painting, sculpture and installation using basic materials such as house paint, cement, clay, and timber. He has also made participatory works, inviting visitors to use clay provided to add to his collection of clay vegetables. His work draws attention to the role of the artist, the status of the art object and the role of the viewer through the copious use of hand painted text, and knowingly clumsy modes of construction. The text is always capitalised, painted in bright colours, a different colour for each letter, on a white background. Brill’s work is socially engaged in the sense that he acknowledges that the viewer is not to be deceived by art, also questioning the legitimising role played by commercial success and highlighting the mundane reality of most artists lives. He portrays himself as a fallen hero, a failed romantic busy with the gritty reality of the important job of the artist. Brill operates a kind of grass roots art politics, often creating events or action accompanied by promotional material and badges. An example is his 2002 initiation to all artists to bring their old work to be destroyed in a massive purgative action. His hand painted slogans are a feature of these, and by the 2000s these became the predominant form of his work. 

!Brigit Smith.

British artist born in the 1960s. 

!Kerry Smith.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes moulded painted fibreglass figurative sculpture. She has made a pregnant school girl holding out her hand in the style of a charity collection box, and small girl who is fixed to project horizontally form the wall just about floor level. 

!Pierrick Sorin.

French artist who makes work about knowledge and information, including a film in which he purports to be an expert explaining theories wit the aid of a white board, which he is fact making up as he goes along. 

!John Spiteri.

Australian artist who makes figurative paintings and autobiographical videos. In the videos he challenges himself alone with the camera. The videos are usually a response to a song and a location. For example in one film set in a bathroom Spiteri sings a rap song so fast that it is barely believable. It seems clear that this is a film that has been speeded up, but in fact Spiteri has practised until he could do it perfectly. In others, he samples a part of a song and uses this as the soundtrack for a self interrogation, in which he faces the camera and talks to it, such as I Can’t Do That, a film based on a sample from a song by the pop singer Cher in which Spiteri is seen regarding himself in a mirror repeating ‘I can’t do that’ in a litany to hopelessness. The alter ego that Spiteri has created for his work is a blend of a character who is not very good at life but is extremely good at the tasks carried out in the films. Spiteri has also made small detailed narrative paintings that seem to be illustrations for a 1950s children’s story. These are painted in enamel paint in bright colours. Spiteri has made a video of each painting that adds a narrative, consisting of an overheard conversation. Since 2000 Spiteri has been making oil paintings. 

!Fergal Stapleton.

British artist born around 1970. 

!Frances Stark.

American artist born in the 1960s who uses a variety of media. Stark also writes as part of her practise. Her book The Architect and the Housewife is about her practise as an artist and the difficulty of making art at home. Stark ahs made a series of videos of her cats at home in her apartment. She also makes sculptures, including one of a small Perspex grandfather clock. Stark makes quantities of drawings, fro example her text drawings made using carbon paper which feature a word or a number of words written out in rows each row being added to. The low tech transfer quality gives this work an ephemeral quality. 

!Simon Starling.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculpture by turning one thing into another. For example a bible into a chair – Starling will dismantle the bicycle, strip it down, grind, melt and otherwise reduce it to its constituents which he then recasts as another object entirely. Pursuing his interest into how one thing can be made into another, Starling has also made a group of works, which are based on United Nations blueprints for agricultural implements. These are instructions in how to make essential implements from freely available materials, such as oil cans, bicycle parts, and pipes. Starling constructs these fully functioning implements such as ploughs, harvesters and irrigators, often painting them, and displays them as sculpture. 

!Georgina Starr.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes video and installation art. Starr creates a variety of personas and worlds and weave a fiction around herself that is sometimes quite like a children’s story. One installation in the early 1990s created a fairy tale or cartoon like environment in which the viewer could wander. In 1999 she made the Bunny Lakes trilogy, a multi part work about teenage girls from a place called Bunny Lakes, based on a true story. Starr’s earliest works were very simple. Crying, a video, shows Starr bursting into tears and crying sitting in front of the camera. Another is a record on which a whistled tune is recorded. The tune was made by chance composition – Starr threw torn pieces of paper in the air, noted where they fell and translated this spacing into musical notation. 

!Jemima Stehli.

British artist born in 1962, Stehli makes photographic work centring on her own body. Stehli began as a sculptor whose early works included scarves several metres long knitted on very large diameter needles, which were presented folded on the floor, and wool embroidered padded panels, around sixty cm long featuring words. In the early 1990s Stehli began to make resin casts of isolated parts of her own body, such as a hand complete with watch or a breast, moving on to making casts of vessels and bottles which she presented in groups, and objects such as a purse or a tray. These small domestic scaled sculptures are lacquered and highly polished, often in deep reds, blues and black. Stehli made a performance work for which she lay naked except for a pair of high heeled shoes motionless on the floor of a gallery, titled Shoes Chosen by the Curator in the mid 1990s. She presented her own body as a sculpture and documented this by photographing it. This led her to make a series of photographs of herself naked. Stele’s photographic work since this point has concentrated on the relation of the viewer to the female body. She made two groups of images that alluded to bondage, in which parts of her body appear naked, the cable release of the camera highly visible. One group of photographs is black, in deep shadow; the other is white and bright. She then made a series based on the work of the 60s artist Allen Jones. Stehli recreated Jones’ famous fibreglass sculptures of women in high heels and underwear acting as the support for glass table tops or stools, photographing herself recreating the poses, without the underwear but with the shoes. The photographs are printed so that she appears life size, in black and white. Stehli work has sometimes involved other people. She invited women to be photographed by her naked, with herself also in the photograph. She then made what has become her best known work a group of photographs taken by men. She invited a number of curators and critics to come to her studio and be photographed sitting or standing as she faced them and removed her clothes except for a pair of high heels. The image is composed to show her back view in the foreground and the man looking outwards. Some of the men are looking at Stehli, some at the camera, some elsewhere. The men hold the cable release. A series of works based on a particular photograph of Helmut Newton’s followed. Stehli recreated Newton’s black and white image of four fashion models standing naked staring at the camera in her studio, placing herself to the far side of the image, leaving space where the others are in the Newton original, on a white photographic backdrop. She stands in the exact pose of the original, naked in one photograph and clothed in jeans and a t shirt in the other, in both cases holding the cable release, the cable trailing in from the camera at the bottom of the image. A number of works made on this basis included a series based on the paintings of Francis Bacon. For these colour photographs Stehli moves in front of the camera set on a long exposure. The result is a blurred image of intense colour, distorting features and body parts in the same way that Bacon does. In a similar series she uses a red silk scarf moving it around her body so that the red blurs and makes a virtually abstract image. Since 2000 Stehli has made images in which she is increasingly absent. A series of self portraits in her studio show her often lying on the ground at odd angles to the camera, her gaze not directed at the viewer. 

!Myra Stimson.

British artist born in the 1960s who make paintings. Stimson's early work was figurative. In 2000 she made a series of paintings in which she reproduced shopping lists she had found in a supermarket. 

!Jessica Stockholder.

American sculptor born in the 1960s who makes very large installations. Stockholders early work consisted of small assemblages of timber, carpet, household objects, wool and fabric. She began to make larger works – a transitional work consisted of painting areas of a garden shed, including painting the side it blue, extending the colour over the grass in front of the shed. A similar work involved a painted mattress. Stockholder’s mature work consists of much larger scale site specific installations. These are described by her as paintings and are often based on landscapes or still lives, for example by Cézanne. The installations are large sprawling constructions, often emerging from or including in their structure a partial demolition of the gallery wall. They appear like unfinished construction work which has a life of its own. Stockholder uses lengths of timber and board, or spreads items and material over the floor to build an environment, which is transparent, can be walked through and around, and which presents multiple views according to the angle from which they are observed. She adds flat planes in the form of sheet material, transparent lighting gels, fabric and carpet, and then paints certain areas. She uses light in the form of light bulbs in holders, and has also added miniature fans to create movement. In one installation a row of oranges sits on a shelf. In another a large carpet twists as if it is flying and has collided with the installation. Paint, timber and rope spill onto and extend over the floor. One work is made from a bank of baths, arranged in rows then cut into with a blowtorch and painted, the orange paint extending right across the floor. Another work uses a number of refrigerators arranged in a wall like structure. A work made from a large pink sheet of plastic pulled up at various points by wires attached to the ceiling ahs a long title including the words ‘big rock candy mountain’. Most of stockholders works have very long impenetrable titles that seem as though they may explain the work but in fact do not. 

!Tim Stoner.

British artist born in the 1970s who makes figurative paintings of people and groups of people. Stoner paints figures in silhouette with a halo of light behind them and adds a light coloured layer of paint, which serves to blur the image so that it appears as if it is viewed against the sun. 

!Thomas Struth.

German artist who makes photographic portraits, landscapes and interiors characterised by their apparent stillness as if time has stood still. 

!Michael Stubbs.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes paintings and sculpture about painting, for example his squashed canvases. These are wall mounted works consisting of several small square canvases about fifty cm square sandwiched together with very thick layers of oil paint in a variety of colours. These are bolted together through the stretchers so that the paint is squeezed out all around the edges, giving the impression that there are several hidden paintings. The whole object is mounted on the wall, the top canvas blank. 

!Daniel Sturgis.

British painter born in the 1960s who makes brightly coloured hard edged pattern paintings based on flowers. 

!Hiroshi Sugimoto.

Japanese photographer who makes photographs of a variety of subjects, often focussing on representing light, as for example in an number of works showing the light from candles. 

!Ricky Swallow.

Australian artist born around 1970 who makes sculpture, for example a group of works made using record turntables as their base, on which Swallow placed model making figures and constructed space ships and other futuristic vehicles, habituations and buildings, incorporating the parts of the record player in to the whole. Some of the sculptures make use of the spinning of the turntable or the movement of the needle arm. The whole sculpture is sprayed with grey paint and presents a very convincing and ultimately unnerving scene from a future envisaged by swallow. 

!Sarah Sze.

American artist born in the 1960s who makes site specific installations in which she arranges a vast amount of objects and materials over an entire room and its walls and ceiling. Sze chooses discarded materials, plastic bottles, cotton buds, cable, wire, paper, mass produced plastic vessels, toys, beads, sugar, glitter and hundreds of other items and materials and deploys them in a formal abstract composition also choosing to paint the walls, taking into account their form and colour. The vast scale of the undertaking is part of the effect of the work producing an effect of visual satiety in the viewer. 

!Neal Tait.

British painter born in the 1960s who makes relatively small paintings sometimes semi abstract, sometimes depicting objects. He paints in a loose gestural style. 

!Tomoko Takahashi.

British artist who makes large scale installations from discarded furniture and junk. An early work in a gallery basement seemed at first sight not to be art but to be simply an excessively untidy storeroom that had perhaps been burgled. Takahashi has made numerous gallery installations; one consists of office furniture and equipment, which is strewn over the space in apparent chaos. In fact the arrangement is deliberate and painstaking. Filing cabinets, cupboards, desks, chairs, computers, pens, telephones, faxes, lamps, kettles, lengths of electric cable and just about anything that might be found in a modern office are arranged to look as if a cataclysmic event has taken place, perhaps an earthquake, a search by government agents, creating a sense of unease. The destruction of an everyday environment on such a grand scale underlines the temporality and built in in obsolescence of our surroundings. Takahashi has also make a website called Word Perhect which is a hand drawn and written interface in which the viewer can select from a variety of notes, post its, scraps of paper and jottings on the back of envelopes and paper bags found in the artists studio and add read her comments about the contents. The reader can type on the interface and on the notes in a font based on Takahashi’s handwriting. 

!Sam Taylor Wood.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes photographic and video work. Taylor Wood is one of a number of artists who emerged in the early 1990s who are known as young British artists or YBAs. One of Taylor Wood’s early works, Brontosaurus, is a video of a young man dancing to disco music, naked, swinging and flapping his penis energetically in the process. Another early work is a four screen video projection showing four opera singers performing La Boheme. The screens on which they appear are placed in a box shape, creating the sense that they are in a room which the viewer has entered, and that they are singing together. It becomes apparent that the films were shot separately and the singers are singing together but not together. A similar work, showing a couple having a row in a kitchen, is projected onto the walls in a corner, one showing the man and one the woman. The frame is composed so that it appears that one half of the kitchen, in which the man stands, is on one wall and the other half, in which the woman stands, is on the other. This gives the illusion that the space in which the filmed event is taking place has been partly reconstructed. It becomes clear that the row is in fact performed by actors who are not in the room at the same time. In the late 1990s Taylor Wood began to take panoramic photographs using a camera, which is set on a slowly revolving tripod. This enabled her to take a 360 degree view of a room and its occupants. She choreographed a series of group portraits featuring people she knew, taking place in borrowed apartments and other places. The long duration of the exposure made it possible for some of the subjects appear twice in the photograph. These colour photographs are about 3 metres long. Taylor wood has also made a large number of conventional photographs including landscapes portraits and self portraits. One shows her sitting on the steps of a building holding a young man, who is limp in her arms, a reference to paintings of the Virgin Mary holding Jesus. 

!Juergen Teller.

British artist, formerly a fashion photographer, who has made work about the fashion world, including photographing hopeful models at the moment the door of the agency is opened, a group of images relating to Bertolt Brecht’s life in Berlin and the city after the wall and autobiographical work. He photographed a frozen dead dog sticking out of a refuse bin. 

!Kathy Temin.

Australian artist born in the 1960s who makes photographic, video and installation work, centring on the creation of domestic interior spaces in the gallery, fashion and fetishism. Temin made an installation of Polystyrene room dividers crudely cut out in a 1960’s lattice pattern. She has made a variety of deliberately clumsily fashioned models of dwellings and spaces, and well made domestic items such as lamps and furniture. Temin also made a video documentation of two people dressed in koala suits, and has done work about shoe fetishism and celebrity. 

!Thomson and Craighead.

British artists born in the 1960s who work together. 

!David Thorpe.

British artist born in the late 1960s who makes complex collages from coloured paper, as well as specialist paper such as bark paper. Thorpe’s early works are based on photographs of 1960s and 70s modernist buildings, seen by day and by night. The image is built up from layers of paper cut to match the graduations of colour on the original. A night view of a house high on a hill is made from a great range of dark blue, grey and black paper, with bright orange for the illuminated windows, in which tiny black silhouetted figures are seen. Thorpe’s views of modernist architectural ideals led him to begin to create images and to illustrate a Utopian vision. The collages began to depict invented buildings with symbolic overall structural design. He also designed an indoor planter which consisted several levels of mirrored glass, a small water feature and meadow plants. 

!Matthew Tickle.

British artist born in the 1960s. 

!Wolfgang Tillmans.

German artist born in the 1960s who makes photographic images of everyday objects and interiors, people, still lives, and abstract images made in the darkroom. Tillmans, formerly a fashion photographer, pioneered the influential everyday life, snapshot style of fashion photography that are so characteristic of the 1990s in Britain. Tillmans always exhibits his work in groups, unframed, pinned to the wall, printed in various sizes from postcard size to three metres high. The images are produced as inkjet prints. The groups are not usually obviously related in terms of subject matter but are formally related (there are exceptions such as a collection of re photographed images of young soldiers cut from newspapers). Thus an image of Kate Moss, a fashion model, might be placed next to one of some socks drying on a radiator, a naked man, some rotting fruit and an ashtray, a landscape. The images might be intimate or observational, and Tillmans’ work covers the range of categories including still life, landscape, cityscape, skyscraper, group and single portraiture. He photographs what is close to him and does not set out to find locations or subjects. A particularly well known image from the 1990s is of two friends sitting on a branch of a tree, naked except for raincoats and boots. Sometimes Tillmans returns to a subject repeatedly, most famously in his work Concorde, a collection of photographs, also published as a book, of the aircraft in flight made over a period of weeks mostly at Heathrow airport where the plane spotters gather. In 2000 Tillmans began to make more abstract images, using darkroom techniques and dyes to make colour images for example a pool of blurred red colour on a white ground that seems like a rose so that the connection with painting in his work, which has always been present, has become more obvious. 

!Padraig Timoney.

British artist born in the 1960s. 

!Mark Titchener.

British artist born in the 1960s. 

!Rirkrit Tiravanija.

Thai artist known for his installations that are constructed to be social environments. Tivrana became known in the 1990s for his noodle bar, which he designed and built from ply and timber as counter with seating, where he cooked and served bowls of noodles to viewers. This is an idea which he has developed and which has taken place in a variety of locations and forms. 

!Grazia Toderi.

Italian artist who made a digital video collage in which a model of a satellite is seen apparently floating in zero gravity inside a space consisting of a curved ceiling decorated with plaster relief which is digitally doubled to create a vault like space. 

!Fred Tomaselli.

American artist who has made a number of works mixing painting and drawing with collage, which characteristically feature patterns made by placing dried leaves and flowers under a thick layer of poured resin. 

!Chris Tosic.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes paintings, drawings and sculpture. In 2003 Tosic made a work consisting of a group of heads carved on the end of pencils. 

!Gavin Turk.

British artist born around 1960 who is known for his work in mixed media centring on his signature and the idea of authorship. An early work of Turks is a men’s urinal signed by him and placed on a plinth. This work is a copy of a work by Marcel Duchamp. Turk has made a variety of paintings of his own signature, something which is usually a tiny detail in the history of painting yet very important in terms of authentication and therefore of value. Turk has painted his signature in oil paint, screen printed it and made a relief from polystyrene balls. Perhaps Turks best known work is a life size wax work of Turk dressed as Johnny Rotten in the pose of Elvis Presley. He later made a waxwork of himself as a person living on the streets, with filthy skin dressed in ragged clothes and shoes. A companion work is an empty sleeping bag. 

!James Turrell.

American artist who makes large scale installations, either in museum, on selected outdoor sites, or to commission. Turrell combines architectural structures with natural or artificial light to create visual tricks that give the illusion of a depth or height that isn’t there, of to focus the viewers vision on the sky. An example is a gallery installation, which consists of a dark room in which there seems to be a red glowing wall panel, which seems to have some kind of activity on its surface. It is only when the viewer steps right up the apparent panel that it can be discerned that there is a rectangle panel cut out of the wall, behind which is a void which is lit with red light, and in fact there is nothing there. Another gallery installation is made by making window where no window is expected so that the viewer is fooled into thinking that there is a sky coloured panel on the ceiling when in fact it is the sky. Turrell's outdoor installations are sometimes very large scale. His observatory is a sunken earth work at the top of a specially constructed mound that is designed to harness the changing light for that geographic location at all times of year to create effects fro the viewer inside the work. The viewer’s view of the sky is controlled by the structure of the space that Turrell has designed for the observatory. 

!Luc Tuymans.

German painter born in the 1950s who makes a large number of paintings nearly always the same size about 80 by 50 cm. Tuymans’ uses a sparse palette of subdued colours, rarely mixing colours, but using a range of tones of one or two colours in each work. His paintings are very simple representations of everyday subjects such as the pattern on wallpaper, flowers, furniture and objects. He has been very influential for a generation of artists. 

!Keith Tyson.

British artist who makes installations and drawings. Tyson become known in the 1990s for his Artmachine, a sculpture that produced random artworks the description of which would be ejected from the machine on a card. Tyson would then construct the artwork. One installation consisted of a whole gallery filled by these art works linked by cables. The artworks usually juxtapose household, found objects and building materials in unlikely combinations and sometimes have a kinetic or sound feature. His interest in multiplication of ideas is always documented in his prolific drawing practise. Tyson produces drawings often with text which address any number of scientific or eccentric inventions, everyday objects, astronomy, geography, physics and chemistry. Typically these drawings are always the same size, 120 cm by 80 cm, but Tyson also produces much larger paintings. Tyson also produced a black monolithic sculpture which emits a low hum as if it is a powerful minimal futuristic machine of some sort. 

!Udomski.

Artist who makes large paintings up to 3 metres high by cutting out single letters from newspapers and magazines, sticking them onto the canvas and painting around them. The resulting dense pattern, perhaps of the letter ‘O’ in upper and lower case hundreds of times painted around with very dark brown paint, gives the impression of a pattern o dots. 

!Piotr Uklansky.

Polish artist born in the 1960s living in America who works in a variety of media. One of Uklansky best known works is a nightclub dance floor made from squares of coloured plastic illuminated from below, which he has installed in a number of galleries and which is permanently installed in a bar in New York. Uklansky has made other floor works, including a puddle of a certain amount of water poured onto the floor, and a floor skating drawing, black rubber marks made by the artist’s shoes when he ice skated on a gallery floor. Uklansky has an interest in the ephemeral and the unnoticed. An early work consisted of a number of printed colour advertising flyers, which in fact advertised nothing sensible, pushed through the door of the gallery and the surrounding area as if they were junk mail. Uklanksy also makes photographic work. Nazis is about 40 head and shoulders photographs of actors playing Nazi soldiers in cinema films. 

!Jeffrey Vallance.

American artist born in the 1950s who makes installation work and writes extensively about art both as part of his practise and as a critic. Vallance lives in Las Vegas where he has established The Liberace Museum to house a collection of memorabilia and artefacts related to the pianist. This is in part ironic and in part a genuine tribute. Vallance travels widely and part of his practise is an ethnographic investigation into the sacred objects of other cultures. Vallance has made a body of work that investigates the religious relic, making relics of his own and investigating existing ones. He made a Shroud by covering his face with coffee and imprinting it on a cloth. He shows that the images on the Turin Shroud are in fact images of nuclear mushroom clouds and explains why he thinks this is the case. He adopts a frozen chicken, calls it Binky and shows that it can be resurrected after death inn the manner of Jesus. He researched in detail the whereabouts of the various relics of nails and wooden shards of the True Cross and wrote about them. Vallance travelled to Tonga. Before he left he corresponded with the King and asked him what gift he would like. The king requested a pair of size thirteen snorkelling fins which Vallance provided. The correspondence is published. 

!Lily Van der Stokker.

Dutch artist born in the 1950s who makes wall paintings, screen prints, and self adhesive stickers. Van der Stokker’s paintings are usually made with accompanying especially made or upholstered furniture. The paintings consist of flowers and other decorative elements linked by curling lines, with a few words of text, for example ‘Lily is 43 years old and Jack is 62 years old’, which surrounds a two sweater sofa with a pale beige covering. Self adhesive stickers might be designed to be applied to a wall where it meets the floor. A screen print has an orange green and white plaid pattern on which is a bunch of flowers with the words Thank You. Van der Stokker’s painting is usually in pastel and pretty colours, painted precisely by hand with hard edges. Any text is usually in a hand writing style of lettering. 

!Julie Verhoeven.

Artist who makes pencil drawings of women with text that take the style fashion drawings. Verhoeven is also a fashion designer. 

!Bill Viola.

America artist working in Video born in the 1950s who came to prominence in the 1990s. Viola marries an interest in the representation of the human form in sculpture with a fascination for and skill in video technology. Early works include an arrangement of different sized monitors from the tiniest one inch square to the more usual domestic size, removed from their casings, strewn on floor or in on work on a shelf. On the monitors films of different parts of the artists body like his ear, the crook of arm or the side of his neck. It is as if the monitors represent a fragmented body, making a comment on the possibility for classical figurative sculpture in the age of video. Also a work installed in a corridor shaped room. When the viewer enters the room and walks down it they trigger back projected videos installed behind the false walls. Each one projects a person who appears to walk towards the viewer from the far distances, getting gradually larger until they are life size and stand, facing the viewer confronting them. Viola used non artist as models and asked them merely to confront the camera. The work has a spooky quality and leaves the viewer with an erroneous sense of control. Violas best known work is a triptych large screen video projection showing his partner giving birth to their child, his mother dying and himself apparently drowning underwater. Simultaneously projected with a breathing/water soundtrack, the work draws obvious parallels between birth and death, birth and being underwater, death and being underwater. This work has a powerful impact not least because it features an actual birth and death. The work hailed as a religious masterpiece fro the 20th century. Continuing his investigation of the sculptural representation of the human from Viola has since gone on to make a series of works on smaller wall mounted plasma screens. These works take famous paintings often old masters or renaissance works and recreate them in modern settings using actors and highly controlled lighting. Although they are video and therefore exist in time they appear static and little happens, therefore they can be read as paintings. In 2003 Viola made a work titled The Passion. 

!Sophie Von Hellerman.

British painter who makes loosely painted images of scenes or events that imply narrative, or appear to have a literary reference. Hellerman paints with great economy, using the minimum brushwork required to produce dreamlike, hazy images. The figures in her paintings often appear as if they are on a stage because most of the canvas is left bare, the thin paint soaked into the canvas. 

!Inez Van Lamsweerde.

Dutch artist who makes digitally manipulated images, drawing on her experience in fashion photography. Her highly glossy stylised portraits engender a sense of unease in the viewer. A beautiful blonde reclining in a swimsuit has male arms, a smiling male model has female hands created by digital alteration that is so skilful as to be initially imperceptible. 

!Jessica Voorsanger.

British artist born in the late 1960s whose work is concerned with the mentality of the fan. Her video and photographic work often has a participatory or collaborative element – she may invite answers to questionnaires, or include peoples responses in her own work. Voorsanger has made a number of works about being a David Cassidy fan and about being a football fan. Voorsanger has a web site that is integral to her practise. 

!Jeff Wall.

Makes very large scale photographic works, usually presented on light boxes. Wall remakes works from art history including Hokusai's ‘Wave’, interpreting the works into a modern setting. The wave is a group of people on a dyke casing paper blown into the air by the wind. He made a work showing dying and wounded men in mud of a battlefield. The works are recreated in the most painstaking manner to ensure absolute apparent veracity and accuracy, and photographed in larger format to ensure the sharpest highest quality image. It is difficult to fault the quality of the production. 

!Mark Wallinger.

British artist born around 1960 who has made work in a wide range of media. Wallinger’s practise includes video, sculpture, photography and painting. There is a strong socio historical aspect to his work and he is interested in football, horse racing and religion. Wallinger’s early works are mostly sculpture. He placed a table football game on dark green marbled plinth, made a tapestry of Munch’s painting The Scream and made a work with a bird in a cage. Another football work is a photo of Wallinger in the crowd leaving Highbury, a London football ground, carrying a banner bearing some significant words. He made a series of full length portraits of his friends presented as if they were homeless people. One of Wallinger’s early videos was a comparison between the television coverage of the opening of racing at Ascot by the Queen on three successive years. This is played simultaneously on three separate monitors and shows that the camera angles, shots, and commentary varies very little. Wallinger has made many works about horse racing. He made a number of almost life size paintings of horses in the style of Stubbs. Each horse is seen in profile, standing with no saddle or bridle, on a dark green background. They are painted very realistically, and composed from the front half of one parent and the rear half of the other, joined in the middle across the body. In the late 1990s Wallinger and a syndicate purchased and began to race a horse named A Real Work of Art. Wallinger has also made a number of works about religion. One features himself carrying a blind person’s white stick walking down an upwards escalator chanting the lords prayer which is played backwards. Wallinger contributed a proposal for the empty sculpture plinth in Trafalgar Square in London. Ecce Homo, a one metre high statue of a naked man (who could be a Christ figure) is made in white marble and stands on one corner of the plinth close to the edge looking outwards. 

!Rebecca Warren.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculptures from clay, ceramic and bronze. Warren models human figures very roughly, using lumps of clay barely fashioned, the surface not smoothed at all. Figures are sometimes engaged in sexual acts. Female forms are large breasted and monumental and male forms have oversized penises. The clay is usually left unglazed, sometimes and the sculpture is presented on a plinth. They range in size from life size down to table top size. Warren has made a series of large sculptures which are homage to both the cartoonist Robert Crumb and the painter Philip Guston (who was himself influenced by Crumb). Each one is a Crumb style figure (with an enormously exaggerated bottom) combined with elements of the work of a famous artist from history. Warren has also made works in bronze, sometimes consisting of no more than a lump or a phallic object. 

!Gillian Wearing.

British artist born in the 1960s whose work in a variety of media deals with identity. Wearing’s earliest work to gain popularity is called Dancing In Peckham, a video of herself with a personal stereo dancing in the middle of a busy shopping centre. She also walked around her local streets with her entire head bandaged, noting the comments and attitudes that she attracted. Signs That Say What You Want Them to Say, is a video and a series of photographs. Wearing asked people on the street to write down, on a piece of paper that she gave them, something that they would like to say but never had. She then photographed them holding the sign in front of them. The resulting contrast between the expressed desire or emotion and the authors face is subject of the work. Continuing her exploration of how people see themselves, Wearing made a video of herself in bed with a number of transsexuals and transvestites, and a single shot video of a local police force, standing in rows. The officers remain motionless and so does the camera, so that what is a moving image becomes almost a still image. Individual movements, blinking, and any tics assume the same importance as gesture and facial expression in their absence. In 2003 Wearing made an number of photographs of herself dressed as members of her own family, using masks, prosthetics, wigs and make up. 

!Gary Webb.

British artist born in the 1970s who makes abstract sculptures from a variety of building and fabrication materials, plastic, acrylic, blown glass, laminate, rubber, metal and fibreglass. Webb’s sculptures sometimes have an audio addition, piece of music, sound or a song emanating from part of the sculpture. Webb draws on the legacy of 1960s and 70s sculptors like Antony Caro and David Smith, but his choice of materials is fundamentally different since he chooses more fragile or delicate materials. An early work consists of a small wood veneered unit on legs with music coming from it and a plume of wood veneer. Another consist of an amorphous giant pebble form lacquered in bright yellow high gloss paint with various other elements. Webb used representative objects in some of his early work, such as a black skinned female doll attached to a sphere and decorated. There are often references to furniture, for example the coffee table is a favourite form. Sometimes a specially made blown glass element is added. Webb has made moulded rubber floor tiles as part of another sculpture. Webb’s sculptures have the look of giant three dimensional collages of abstract elements that are brought together formally in the manner of traditional sculpture but do not share this appearance. 

!Nicole Wermers.

German artist born around 1970 who makes collages. 

!Franz West.

German artist who makes sculpture and installation and works on paper. West’s work generally features furniture, papier maché timber and plaster or bronze ‘lumps’ and painted collage. A sausage, turd or penis form appears frequently in his work whether made in papier maché, cast in bronze or painted into collages. West has a democratic approach to art, believing that an art objects value is created as much by the viewing subject as by the artist. To this end most of his sculpture is made to be touched or sat on. West is probably best known for his interactive sculptures. These are ungainly white plaster objects, organic amorphous white shapes that sit awkwardly on a plinth and look like joke sculptures. The viewer is instructed on the label accompanying the work to pick up the object and interact with it. There may be a video monitor showing a number of people doing this. A curtained off area with floor to ceiling mirror may be provided, and the viewer invited to enter with the sculpture and interact with it. Some of the sculptures have wire extrusions enabling them to be ‘worn’, or areas which allow then to be balanced on a shoulder. The interactive sculptures each have a specific plinth, which may incorporate a mirror, or a seat and in on case a bed on which the viewer can lie with the sculpture. West has made a large number of items of furniture, on which the viewer is free to sit or lounge. In particular he makes chairs and sofas, simple square shapes with unconventionally deep and wide seats. The furniture is made from unfinished steel concrete reinforcing rods with thin cushions upholstered in utilitarian materials such as canvas, sacking and silver insulation fabric. The furniture is sometimes placed in direct relation to a sculpture, for example a chair with a Hessian cushion is placed near to a pink papier maché lump which is hung from the ceiling. Or a pair of chairs with silver cushions are placed with a one and a half meter high sphere covered with brown parcel tape between them. The furniture is designed to involve the viewer physically in West’s work, to provoke thoughts about the idea of contemplation of art works. Through his large number of lump sculptures West investigates the idea of the autonomous and intrinsically valuable art objects. He create essentially lumpen abstract objects that could be said to be ugly and presents then in the same way that any sculpture would be shown. The lumps range in size from around fifty cm high to two metres high and more. They are made in papier maché on a plaster and timber frame work, sometimes with a gap left through which the inside can be viewed, and painted. They are also made in bronze, cast from papier maché originals and painted often in pink or pale blue, sometimes multicoloured. The forms of the lumps seem arbitrary although West has made a series of lump portrait heads. The phallic objects which West deploys in his work a sausage, penis or turd shape appear as sculpture made from papier maché, bronze or resin. Some are fabricated in sheet steel, cut in pieces and welded together in a patchwork. These are painted in colours that seem inappropriate to the form and material such as pale pink and blue, ochre or gold. West has made sculpture in this form in sizes ranging from 30 cm long to 20 metres long outdoor sculpture. The phallic shape appears in West’s collages frequently. These consist of magazine cut outs usually of men and women, which are painted over in poster paint to alter their body shape. They often feature half obscured sexual acts. West adds sausages cut from magazine advertisements emerging from a man or woman’s mouth, or from a man’s trousers. 

!Martin Westwood.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes mixed media wall work using collage, drawing, painting and papier maché. Westwood often makes silhouette cut outs and uses spray paint over these or through doilies to create shadow effects. In general his work is wall based, but he has also made installations, for example one in which he hung a number of papier maché forms made over balloons, which he had painted, collaged and drawn on. Westwood often chooses unconventional paper for his drawings, such as newsprint, or printed material. 

!Rachel Whiteread.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes large scale sculpture. Whiteread casts the interior and the underneath of buildings, rooms and furniture in concrete, plaster, rubber and resin, making a solid form from negative space. She began by casting mattresses in rubber and translucent silicon around 1990. these solid forms were placed on the floor half bent to lean against the wall. She also cast a bath, producing a solid block with a negative bath shape set into it which looks like a sarcophagus. A basin done in the same way, and some tables and desks which are simply the solid blocks produced from the space under a table. Whiteread’s first major work is Ghost, 1993, a cast of a room in a house. This work appears as a life sized plaster block (made in sections) which has the impressions of the details of the room on its surface. For example a fireplace projects from the surface instead of being cut into the wall, door and window frames which are negatives set into the surface, as are door handles, skirting board and light switches. Whiteread continued to make smaller objects, and in 1997 she made House, her best known work and one which became notorious. House was the cast of the interior of a two storey Victorian terraced house in the east end of London. The cast was made by spraying the interior with concrete and reinforcing it, then demolishing the exterior carefully. The row of houses was condemned and was to be demolished to make way for a new park so Whiteread’s work was to be temporary. However it was so popular that there were calls for it to remain as a permanent public sculpture in the park. There ensued a long public battle between arts bodies and the local authority with press and local residents both for and against the sculpture. The sculpture was finally demolished. Whiteread was commissioned to make a holocaust memorial for Vienna. Her cast of the interior of a library sited in a pedestrian square was finally unveiled in 2001 after several years of wrangling between commissioning bodies, public bodies and the authorities. The library is a cement block with the spines of books covering it. These ‘books’ are removable, each representing a person from that city who died in the holocaust. Whiteread has continued to cast furniture throughout her career. She began to use acrylic resin at the end of the 1990s, casting the spaces underneath a classroom full of chairs, for example. 

!Cathy Wilkes.

Scottish artist born in the 1960s who uses found objects which she juxtaposes to make allusive sculptural works. One work uses a camp beds and a man’s tie, another a small ornament on a shelf. There is an solitary and pensive quality to Wilkes’ work. 

!Stephen Willats.

British artist born in the 1950s who makes installation, text based and graphic work. Willats has also self published a number of books and pamphlets. Willats investigates how people live from a social, political and geographical perspective, employing survey questionnaires, plans and charts, statistics and photography. He has produced a magazine for a number years. An exhibition of his work would typically feature photographs and newspaper clippings with accompanying text and detailed captions, posters printed by him, often with painted or drawn additions, video and a publication. 

!Erland Williamson.

Born in 1965 Scottish artist Williamson made socially engaged work, before he died in 1995. Williamson customised a van that he used to take the road show that he created to many different places.
