The van contained a collapsible kiosk which the artist would set up and open as a kind of drop in centre. He might set it up on a housing estate and ask residents if they wished to fill in questionnaires about where they lived, have a cup of tea, make flyers and posters themselves. He acted as a grass roots activist with the intention of encouraging people to mobilise for change through his work. His work, the van and the kiosk has a strongly homemade aesthetic, hand painted signage on the van and kiosk done in an amateur style in gloss paint on hardboard, hand printed posters and flyers, a heavy dependence on the photocopier, and even t shirts for sale printed with his own slogans. 

!Jane and Louise Wilson.

British artist sisters born in the late 1960s who make large scale projected video installations. An early work from the beginning of the 1990s involved them filming themselves under hypnosis to see if they were telepathic because they are twins. At this time they also made a series of photographs showing parts of their bodies in empty offices, which were printed in a square format and hung at odd levels in the gallery relating to the architecture of the space. These photographs were an investigation into the nature of deserted space and the human traces left in them by default. The Wilson’s then began to make the work for which they are known. This usually involves the selection of a location, invariably an empty building with a specific purpose or history. Examples are Greenham Common nuclear base, a law court, and a casino. They film these empty spaces and add a soundtrack. Sometimes the film features the artists themselves, more recently there are no people in the work. The films are multi screen projections, often shown on adjacent walls so that they meet in a corner. The consequent interaction of the moving images is controlled and deliberate, creating a multiple view of the same space and refusing the viewer a fixed viewpoint. In some works the screens are placed at angles, freestanding, with projections on front and back. The Wilsons make free use of mirroring and doubling in their work, often seen as an obvious consequence of the fact that they are twins. They make run the same film both backwards and forwards so the that the images create a symmetrical pattern. The way the films are edited often leads to an abstract patterning effect. 

!Richard Wilson.

British artist who is best known for a work that was permanently installed in the Saatchi gallery in London. This consists of a steel tank which is waist height. The tank fills the room but there is a narrow projection into the tank allowing viewers to walk into the middle. The tank is apparently filled with black oil. The oil is no more than ten cm deep. Its opacity, viscosity, slight movement and reflectiveness create a feeling of vulnerability and disorientation in the viewer, paradoxically coupled with an atmosphere of contemplation. 

!TJ Wilson.

American artist who makes video using found footage, his own footage and often uses subtitles. He has made a film about a roman villa and another about a baby elephant and its mother, the subtitles for these are not intrinsic to the images ! Richard Wood. British artist born in the 1960s who made a series of sculptures by arranging coloured plastic storage baskets, trays and other items and melting them with a blow torch. They meld together, colours mingling and are transformed into spiked and dripped structures. Woods also made a cast of a rolled carpet rug and painted it to resemble the real carpet. 

!Wood and Harrison.

British artist duo born in the 1960s who make performance videos in which they use their own bodies to create physical sculptures. The artist rely on the weight and motion of their bodies and use each other as counterbalances in a series of interactions with simple props such as planks, boxes and sheets of ply. They create ramps, fulcrums, towers and execute sharply performed and choreographed manoeuvres that exhaustively explore interaction of human body with flat plane and with three dimensional object. The videos are shown on monitors, often each monitor shows a single work looped. The ambient sound of Wood and Harrison’s activities is often loud as sheets of timber are walked on, dropped for example. Wood and Harrison aim to apply the principles of minimal sculpture to live art, reminiscent of Robert Morris’s activity in Olympia with Carolee Schneeman and the early video work of Richard Serra and Vito Acconci. 

!Richard Woods.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes wall paintings. These consist of abstract patterned elements positioned in unconventional places, perhaps high up, across a corner, along the floor, or in some place that relates to the architecture of the building. The works sometimes cover a wall, but are more usually quite small, around one metre high or wide. The designs have many references: historical architectural decoration, tattoos, textile designs, architectural drawing and typography. Woods refines them until the references all but disappear into his sharp edged, precise, often monotone works. The works are hand painted in black, or one or two of the primary colours. 

!Christopher Wool.

American artist who makes large abstract paintings on a heavy gauge woven paper which are mainly printed, often using fragments of found patterns, with areas of paint added, predominantly in black and white. 

!Elizabeth Wright.

British artist born in the 1960s who makes sculpture and installation that is concerned with the relative size of objects. In the mid 1990s Wright made an installation which consisted of a floor strewn with over sized cigarette butts about 40 cm long, and Marlboro packets about 30 cm long. Both objects larger that life size but the wrong size in relation to each other. The objects are made from cardboard and paper which is painted to look realistic. Wright also made scaled down telephone directories which are actually printed and bound, supermarket carrier bags and crisp packets also printed, as well as a bicycle one third larger than life size. 

!Cerith Wyn Evans.

Welsh artist born in the 1950s who uses a variety of media. Wyn Evans’ work often includes or is based on literary or philosophical text. Many of his works are subtle alterations of a pre existing object in order to transform the everyday into the sublime. For example his neon exit sign in which the word is written backwards, his metal crowd barrier which is covered in gold leaf. Some works of Wyn Evans works are very simple in execution, for example a large circular convex mirror and a transparent chandelier. A luminous painting to read by is a 50 cm high canvas coated with luminous paint, to be hung at eh head of a bed, which is charged with enough light during the day to provide reading light at night. Sometimes his work takes the form of an unobtrusive site specific intervention, for example altering the pitch of some bells strung across a stairway to bar public access in the John Soane museum in London. Much of Wyn Evans’ early work is 16 mm film. His film about the death of the director Pasolini shows a group of small boys playing on a beach, as behind them a text, a quotation from Pasolini, outlined in fireworks on a specially constructed frame ignites, blazes and burns away. He ahs also use neon text running round a light fitting, a phrase that reads continuously with no end. Wyn Evans has made a number of neon works using text that reads in a loop, or can be read forwards and backwards, as well as small neon arabesque shapes. He has also made text etched onto Perspex panels that hang from the ceiling. This use of quotation is given its most complex rendering in a work from 2001. Wyn Evans hung a mirror ball in a gallery and projected a bright light at it. The light flashes on and off in a Morse code pattern, spelling out a quotation from Oscar Wilde. In 2003 Wyn Evans made a work consisting of several crystal chandeliers the bulbs of each flashing the Morse code for a particular extract from a philosophical or literary text. 

!Huang Yong Ping.

Chinese artist working from the 1980s onwards who made a sculpture by putting books in a washing machine and moulding the resulting pulp into a sphere. He also created a number of outdoor pyrotechnic events. 

!Andrea Zittel.

American artist born in the 1960s who makes functional sculptures. Zittel's series of portable living units includes a kitchen, a bedroom and a lounging area. The units are made so that they can be folded away and easily transported. They are made to a high specification with polished wood exteriors and upholstered interior with special fittings (lights, drop down tables, cupboards, handles and mirrors). The lounging area is a purple upholstered sunken seating area, the walls of the case functioning as the walls that enclose it. The kitchen is fully functional if minimal. Zittel has also designed a number of items of clothing and has made a site specific work in the desert near to where she lives.
